# PORPHYRY
## THE PHILOSOPHER
### ALSO CALLED MALCHUS <note>original: "Malchos"; a Semitic name meaning "king."</note>
<margin>5</margin>
#### or King,
# THE LIFE
<margin>10</margin>
# OF PYTHAGORAS.

<margin>15</margin>
IT IS AGREED
among the majority
[of authors] that he was
the son of Mnesarchus.
But regarding the lineage of
<margin>20</margin>
Mnesarchus, there is disagreement.
Some say he was
a Samian. But Cleanthes, <note>A Stoic philosopher (c. 330–230 BCE).</note>
in the fifth book of his *Fables*,
says he was a Syrian
from Tyre in Syria.
<margin>25</margin>
When a famine had
struck the Samians,
Mnesarchus sailed
for trade with grain to the island
and sold it, and was honored
<margin>30</margin>
with citizenship.
Since Pythagoras was
naturally gifted from childhood
for every kind of learning,
Mnesarchus brought him
to Tyre. There, having
<margin>35</margin>
introduced him to the Chaldeans, <note>Ancient Babylonian astronomers and priests renowned for their mathematical and spiritual wisdom.</note>
he had him participate
extensively in their studies.
Having returned to
Ionia

# PORPHYRY
## THE PHILOSOPHER
### OR MALCHUS
#### Concerning the Life
# OF PYTHAGORAS
# THE BOOK.

->Translated by Lucas Holstenius<- <note>original: "L. Holstenio interpr."; Holstenius (1596–1661) was a noted German humanist scholar and Vatican librarian.</note>

CONSENSUS indeed
is the opinion of the
majority, that Pythagoras
was the son of Mnesarchus:
but about Mnesarchus's origin
not all agree. For
some affirm he was
a Samian. But Cleanthes
in the fifth book of *Fabulous Matters*
recorded that he was a Syrian,
from Tyre, a city of Syria.
But when a great famine
arose in Samos, Mnesarchus
landed at the island for the
purpose of trade, and in the
place of a reward he was
presented with citizenship.
As for Pythagoras, who from
boyhood possessed a genius
most suited to all disciplines,
he was later led by Mnesarchus
to Tyre, and was there
entrusted to the Chaldeans,
with whose teaching he
desired him to be especially imbued.
Having returned from there in

# ON THE LIFE OF PYTHAGORAS.

The same author <note>Referring back to Apollonius, mentioned on the previous page.</note> testifies that he not only studied under Pherecydes <note>Pherecydes of Syros, an early thinker who taught the immortality of the soul.</note>, but also devoted himself to Hermodamas and Anaximander <note>Anaximander (c. 610–546 BCE) was a pre-Socratic philosopher from Miletus, known for his theory of the 'Apeiron' or the Infinite.</note>. <margin>5</margin> Duris of Samos <note>Duris (c. 340–281 BCE) was a historian and ruler of Samos.</note>, however, in the second book of his *Annals* <note>original: *Horarum* (Latin) / *ὡρῶν* (Greek); these were chronological records or chronicles.</note>, writes that he had a son named Arimnestus, and says that Democritus <note>The famous 'laughing philosopher' and early atomist.</note> was his student. <margin>10</margin> Arimnestus, upon returning home from exile, set up a bronze offering in the temple of Hera <note>original: *Iunonis* (Latin) / *Ἥρας* (Greek). Hera was the patron goddess of Samos.</note>; its diameter was nearly two cubits <note>A cubit is an ancient unit of length, approximately 18 inches or 45 centimeters.</note>, and it had this epigram inscribed upon it:

<column-break/>

The same author testifies that he not only studied under Pherecydes, but also devoted himself to Hermodamas and Anaximander. Duris of Samos, however, in the second book of his *Annals*, writes that he had a son named Arimnestus, and that Democritus studied under him. Arimnestus, truly returning home from exile, set up a bronze offering in the temple of Hera, the diameter of which was nearly two cubits, with this epigram inscribed upon it:

---

->Arimnestus, the dear son of Pythagoras, dedicated me,<-
->having discovered many wise things in discourse.<-

->*He who brought to light many and excellent discoveries,*<-
->*Arimnestus, son of Pythagoras, placed this here.*<-

---

But Simus the Musician <note>original: *Simum Harmonicum*. A 'harmonicist' studied the mathematical ratios and proportions of music.</note> stole this very thing, <margin>25</margin> and attributed the musical <term>canon</term> <note>The 'canon' refers to the fundamental mathematical rule or principle of musical intervals, often demonstrated on a monochord.</note> to himself, publishing it as his own. There were, in fact, seven wisdoms inscribed; but because of the one that Simus stole, the others that were written upon the offering were also <margin>30</margin> destroyed. Others write that Pythagoras had a son, Telauges, and a daughter, Myia, by Theano, the daughter of Pythanax, who was of Cretan descent; <margin>35</margin> some add Arignote to these, whose Pythagorean writings still sur-

# PORPHYRY

<column-break/>

[The texts] are [preserved]. Timaeus <note>Timaeus of Tauromenium (c. 345–250 BCE) was a Greek historian whose work focused on the history of Sicily and Italy.</note>, indeed, is the authority who states that Pythagoras’s daughter, while still a maiden, led the choir of virgins, and as a woman, she led the group of women. The inhabitants of Croton <note>A Greek city in southern Italy where Pythagoras founded his influential school and community.</note>, moreover, turned his house into a temple of Ceres <note>original: *Cereris* (Latin) / *Δήμητρος* (Greek); the goddess of grain, known as Demeter in Greek.</note> and named the narrow street the Museum <note>original: *Musæum* (Latin) / *Μουσεῖον* (Greek); a place dedicated to the Muses, the goddesses of the arts and sciences.</note>. But Lycus <note>Lycus of Rhegium, a historian and father of the poet Lycophron.</note>, in the fourth book of his *Histories*, recalls that there are various opinions among some regarding Pythagoras’s homeland, saying: "As for the homeland and the city of which this man happened to be a citizen—unless you have seen it yourself—it matters little to you. For some say he was a Samian <note>From the island of Samos.</note>, others a Phliasian <note>From the city of Phlius in the Peloponnese.</note>, and some a Metapontine <note>From Metapontum in southern Italy.</note>." Furthermore, concerning his education, it is commonly handed down that he learned the mathematical sciences, as they are called, from the Egyptians, the Chaldeans <note>The Chaldeans were inhabitants of Babylonia, renowned in the ancient world for their skill in astronomy and astrology.</note>, and the Phoenicians. For the Egyptians were most studious of geometry from ancient times; the Phoenicians of numbers and proportions; and the Chaldeans of astronomy and the movements of the heavens. They say he received the sacred rites of the gods, the worship, and the other practices of life from the instruction of the Magi <note>original: *Magorum* (Latin) / *Μάγων* (Greek); Zoroastrian priests of ancient Persia known for their wisdom and occult knowledge.</note>. And these things [are confirmed] by many indeed, almost...

<column-break/>

[The texts] are preserved. Timaeus relates that the daughter of Pythagoras, while a maiden, led the maidens in Croton, <margin>5</margin> and as a woman, the women. The people of Croton made his house a temple of Demeter, and called the narrow street the Museum. Lycus, in <margin>10</margin> the fourth book of his *Histories*, also mentions his homeland, as some disagree, saying: "Concerning the homeland and the city of which <margin>15</margin> this man happened to be a citizen, if you do not happen to know, let it make no difference to you." For some say he was a Samian, others <margin>20</margin> a Phliasian, others a Metapontine. Furthermore, concerning his instruction, most say that he learned the so-called mathematical sciences from <margin>25</margin> the Egyptians, the Chaldeans, and the Phoenicians. For from ancient times, the Egyptians were diligent in geometry; <margin>30</margin> and the Phoenicians in matters concerning numbers and calculations; and the Chaldeans in the observations of the heavens. Regarding the holy rites of the gods and the rest <margin>35</margin> of the practices concerning life, he says [Pythagoras] heard and received them from the Magi.

### ON THE LIFE OF PYTHAGORAS.

# PORPHYRY

<column-break/>

[These things] are well known, since they have been recorded in written monuments; however, his other practices are less familiar. Nevertheless, Eudoxus <note>Eudoxus of Cnidus (c. 390–337 BCE) was a renowned Greek astronomer and mathematician who traveled to Egypt to study with priests.</note>, in the seventh book of his *Description of the Earth*, writes that Pythagoras practiced such purity, and so greatly abhorred all slaughter and those who kill, that he not only abstained from living things <note>A reference to the Pythagorean diet, which famously excluded meat and, according to some accounts, beans.</note>, but never even approached cooks or hunters. Antiphon <note>Likely Antiphon the Sophist, a contemporary of Socrates, known for his work on the lives of virtuous men.</note>, moreover, in his book on the *Lives of Men Illustrious for Virtue*, commemorates his extraordinary endurance in Egypt, saying that Pythagoras, wishing to take up the practices of the Egyptian priests and being very eager to become a participant in them, asked Polycrates <note>The powerful and ambitious tyrant of Samos (reigned c. 538–522 BCE).</note> the Tyrant to write to Amasis <note>Amasis II (reigned 570–526 BCE), a Pharaoh of the 26th Dynasty known for his friendly relations with the Greeks.</note>, the King of the Egyptians, who was his friend and host, so that he might be admitted to the teachings of the priests mentioned before. Indeed, when he had come to Amasis, he received letters from him addressed to the priests. He first went to the people of Heliopolis <note>The "City of the Sun," a major religious center near modern Cairo.</note>, but he was sent away to Memphis on the pretext that he was being sent to the elders there; but in truth, the Heliopolitans were merely making excuses of this sort. From Memphis, he was sent away under the same pretext and went to the people of Diospolis <note>Literally "The City of Zeus," the Greek name for the Egyptian city of Thebes.</note>. But they, being unable to put forward any excuse due to their fear of the king,

Antiphon, in his work on the *Life of Men Distinguished for Virtue*, <margin>15</margin> also recounts his endurance in Egypt, saying that Pythagoras, having accepted the discipline of the Egyptian priests and being eager <margin>20</margin> to share in it, requested Polycrates the tyrant to write to Amasis the king of Egypt, who was a friend and guest-friend, so that he might <margin>25</margin> share in the education of those previously mentioned. Having arrived before Amasis, he received letters to the priests; and having <margin>30</margin> met with the Heliopolitans, he was sent to Memphis as if to the elders; but in truth, the Heliopolitans <margin>35</margin> were making such excuses. ### ON THE LIFE OF PYTHAGORAS.

# PORPHYRY

...[the priests] could offer no excuse, they thought that he would withdraw from his purpose because of the magnitude of the labor and hardships; and so they enjoined upon him tasks that were exceedingly harsh and entirely foreign to the religious practices of the Greeks. But when he carried these out most readily, he became an object of such great admiration to them that he was permitted both to perform sacrifices and to take part in their studies—a privilege which is found to have happened to no other foreigner. 

Upon his return to Ionia, however, he opened a school in his homeland, which even now they call "Pythagoras’ Semicircle" <note>original: *Pythagoræ hemicyclum*</note>; it is there that the Samians gather when they are about to deliberate on public matters. He also established a private cave outside the city for his philosophy, and in it he spent most of his nights and days, devoting his time to conversations with his friends. 

Aristoxenus <note>Aristoxenus of Tarentum (4th century BCE) was a philosopher and music theorist who wrote one of the earliest biographies of Pythagoras.</note> relates that when he was already forty years old, and saw that the tyranny of Polycrates <note>The ruler of Samos whose initial letter of recommendation helped Pythagoras get to Egypt.</note> was more severe than a free-born man could bear under his rule and dominion, he took this opportunity to depart for Italy. Moreover, since Diogenes, in his book *On the Incredible Things Beyond Thule* <note>Antonius Diogenes (c. 2nd century CE) wrote a popular but lost work of "traveler's tales" that included details on the life of Pythagoras.</note>, has set out the affairs of this philosopher with accuracy, his [account] also...

<column-break/>

...on account of King Zeus <note>original: Διὸς τοῦ βασιλέως. In the syncretic tradition, the Pharaoh Amasis was often identified with Zeus-Ammon.</note>, they thought he would desist from his purpose due to the magnitude of his suffering. <margin>5</margin> They ordered him to endure harsh commands that were separate from the Greek way of life. When he performed these things eagerly, <margin>10</margin> he was so admired that he received the authority to sacrifice to the gods and to attend to their observances. This is not found to have happened to any other foreigner. <margin>15</margin> Having returned to Ionia, he established a school in his fatherland, still called even now the "Semicircle of Pythagoras." In which the <margin>20</margin> Samians, coming together, deliberate about common affairs. Having made a private cave for his own philosophy outside the city, in this he <margin>25</margin> spent most of the day and night, being present for the discourses of his companions. Having reached forty years of age, says Aristoxenus, <margin>30</margin> and seeing the tyranny of Polycrates being too intense, so that it was not well for a free man to endure his supervision and mastery, <margin>35</margin> he thus made his departure for Italy. Diogenes, in the *Incredible Things Beyond Thule*, [records] the things concerning the philo- <note>The word is truncated at the end of the page; it likely reads "philosopher" (φιλοσόφου).</note>...

### ON THE LIFE OF PYTHAGORAS:

...[of that] philosopher <note>original: *philosophon*. This likely refers to Antonius Diogenes, mentioned on the preceding page.</note> having accurately detailed [these things], I decided by no means to pass over his account. He says that Mnesarchus was a Tyrrhenian <note>original: *Tyrrhenon*. The Tyrrhenians were a non-Greek people often identified by the Greeks as the ancestors of the Etruscans.</note>
<margin>5</margin> by race, from among those who inhabited Lemnos, Imbros, and Skyros. And having moved from there, after visiting
<margin>10</margin> many cities and many lands, he once happened upon an infant child, lying under a large and shapely poplar tree. Standing over him, he saw him lying on his back, looking up
<margin>15</margin> into the sky toward the sun without blinking <note>original: *askardamyktei*. In ancient lore, the ability to stare directly into the sun was often a sign of divine or heroic nature.</note>, and having in his mouth a small, thin reed, like a flute.
<margin>20</margin> Marveling at this, and seeing him nourished by the dew dripping from the poplar tree, he took the child up, believing the child’s birth to be of a divine nature. When the child had grown up
<margin>24</margin> in Samos, he was taken in by Androcles, a native, who entrusted the management
<margin>27</margin> of his household to him. Living in abundance, he raised the child,
<margin>29</margin> naming him Astraeus <note>Astraeus: Literally "Starry," a name fitting for a child found gazing at the heavens.</note>, along with his own sons, who were three: Eunostus, Tyrrhenus, and Pythagoras. Androcles adopted him [Pythagoras] as his son, he being the
<margin>34</margin> youngest. While he was a boy, he sent him to lyre-players, physical trainers, and painters; but when he became a young man...
<meta>catchword: ma-</meta>

<column-break/>

...this opinion also I decided I should by no means pass over. He relates that Mnesarchus was a Tyrrhenian of that race who inhabited Lemnos, Imbros, and Skyros; and when, departing from there, he traveled through many cities and various regions, he once found an infant placed under a large and tall poplar, and standing nearby he watched the boy with his face turned upward toward the sky, looking at the sun with unblinking eyes <note>original: *inconniuctibus oculis*. This is likely a printer's error for the Latin *inconniventibus*.</note>; and a small, tender reed like a flute was inserted in his mouth: and when he saw, not without wonder, that he was nourished by dew dripping from the poplar, he took the child up, because he believed him to have sprung from some divine race: (they say that when this boy reached manhood, he was co-opted into the family in Samos by Androcles, a native, who entrusted the administration of the family property to him:) but Mnesarchus, since he abounded in wealth, educated the boy, giving him the name Astraeus, together with his own three sons, Eunostus, Tyrrhenus, and Pythagoras: whom, as I said, while still a boy Androcles adopted as his son. Therefore, he sent the little boy to a lyre-player, the wrestling school <note>original: *palæstram*. The palaestra was the site of physical education and wrestling in the Greek world.</note>, and a painter: but afterward as a young man to Anaximander <note>Anaximander of Miletus (c. 610–546 BCE) was a seminal pre-Socratic philosopher who taught that the beginning of all things was the "Infinite" (*Apeiron*).</note> at Miletus...

<summary>This page presents an alternative origin story for Pythagoras, detailing how his father Mnesarchus discovered a divinely-favored infant named Astraeus and raised him alongside his own three sons. It highlights Pythagoras's early education in the arts, athletics, and his eventual apprenticeship under the philosopher Anaximander.</summary>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of Pythagoras, specifically detailing his education under the philosopher Anaximander and his subsequent travels through the ancient Near East. It follows the narrative of Mnesarchus (Pythagoras's father or benefactor) arranging for his tutelage and the origins of the youth Astraeus.</meta>

# PORPHYRY

->Page 8<-

[Mnesarchus] sent [him] away to Miletus to see Anaximander <note>Anaximander (c. 610–546 BC) was a major Pre-Socratic philosopher and a student of Thales; he is credited with being the first to write a book on the nature of the universe.</note>, so that he might learn geometry and astronomy. Pythagoras, however, he says, also went to the Egyptians, the Arabs, the Chaldeans, and the Hebrews; among whom he devoted himself most accurately to the science of interpreting dreams; then he himself was the first to use divination made with incense. And in Egypt indeed he lived with the priests, and thoroughly learned the wisdom and language of the Egyptians, as well as the three-fold type of writing: namely the epistolary <note>original: *epistolicum*. This refers to 'Demotic' or 'Hieratic' script used for letters and common business.</note>, the hieroglyphic, and the symbolic. Of these, the first imitates the proper and common custom of speaking; the others express their meaning through allegories under certain coverings of riddles. Then he also drew a more accurate and perfect doctrine concerning the Gods. In Arabia he spent time with the king. In Babylon he lived with other Chaldeans; then he also frequently visited Zabratum <note>original: *Zabratum*. Scholars identify this figure with Zoroaster (Zarathustra), the Persian prophet, whose name was often Hellenized in various forms.</note>, by whose help he was purged from the filth of his former life, and learned from what things a serious man must abstain; then he heard him teaching about the nature of things and the principles of the universe. For from his travels to these nations, Pythagoras acquired the most important part of his wisdom. Moreover, Mnesarchus gave Astraeus as a gift to Pythagoras, who, having received [him], [explored] his nature...

<column-break/>

This was because a bear skin had been thrown over him at birth, and the Thracians call a skin a *zalmos*. <note>original: *zalmon*. ...[he] had buried <note>Context from the previous page suggests this refers to the burial of his teacher, Pherecydes of Syros.</note> him, then he returned to Samos because he desired to enjoy the companionship of Hermodamas, the descendant of Creophylus <note>original: *Creophylij*. Creophylus was a legendary epic poet of Samos, said to have been a friend or host of Homer.</note>. And when he had lived with him for some time, he devoted his attention to Eurymenes, a Samian athlete. This man, although he was of small stature, surpassed many of tall physique through the wisdom of Pythagoras and won at the Olympic Games. For while other athletes were still eating cheese and figs according to the old custom, he, on the advice of Pythagoras, was the first to eat a fixed weight of meat every day, thereby acquiring great bodily strength. Pythagoras, gradually imbuing him with his own wisdom, encouraged him to compete, but in such a way that he would not win; for he believed that while one should endure the labors, one should flee the envy that is produced by victory. Indeed, it often happens that victors, and those showered with leaves <note>Referring to the *phyllobolia*, the ancient Greek custom of showering victorious athletes with flowers and leaves.</note>, become defiled <note>original: *pollutos* / *ἀγεῖς*. In a Pythagorean context, this suggests that the pride of victory and the jealousy of others result in a loss of spiritual or ritual purity.</note>. Later, when the Samians were held oppressed by the tyranny of Polycrates <note>Polycrates was a famous and powerful tyrant of Samos (c. 538–522 BC) known for his patronage of the arts but also his absolute rule.</note>, Pythagoras, judging it unseemly for a man who is a philosopher to live in such a state, began to think of moving to Italy. Having put in at Delphi during his voyage, he inscribed certain elegiac verses on the tomb of Apollo, in which he indicated that Apollo was the son of Silenus, but was killed by Python...

# ON THE LIFE OF PYTHAGORAS

...and in the tripod, as it is called, [the god] was buried; it received this name because three maidens, the daughters of Triopus, gave birth to Apollo there. But when he reached Crete, he approached the priests of Morgos, one of the Idaean Dactyls <note>The Dactyls were mythical archaic smiths and healers associated with Mount Ida, credited with discovering iron and ancient mystery rites.</note>, by whom he was purified with the thunderstone <note>original: "ceraunio lapide" / "κεραυνίᾳ λίθῳ". These were often prehistoric stone tools believed to have fallen from heaven during storms and were used in ancient purification rituals.</note>: in the morning he lay stretched out face down by the sea, and at night by the river, crowned with the fleece of a black lamb. Then, having descended into the place called the Idaean cave while clothed in black wool, he spent the customary twenty-seven days there <note>original: "dies ternouenos" / "τριττὰς ἐννέα ἡμέρας", literally three times nine days.</note>, performed the sacred funeral rites for Jupiter <note>original: "Ioui" / "τῷ Διΐ". In Cretan mythology, Zeus/Jupiter was believed to have died and been buried on the island, a concept that shocked other Greeks.</note>, and saw the throne that is prepared for him each year. He even carved an inscription upon the tomb, with the title: PYTHAGORAS TO JUPITER. Its beginning was:

->**"Here lies Zan, deceased, whom they call Jupiter."**<-
<note>original: "Ὧδε θανὼν κεῖται Ζᾶν, ὃν Δία κικλήσκουσιν". Zan is an archaic or dialectal name for Zeus.</note>

But when he reached Italy and arrived in Croton, Dicaearchus <note>A student of Aristotle and a respected historian and geographer.</note> says that upon the arrival of a man who had traveled through many regions and was so extraordinary—one who, by his own nature, had been well-favored by fortune—for his very appearance was noble and grand...

<column-break/>

...and in the tripod, as they call it, [he] was buried: to which this name was given because three girls, the daughters of Triopus, brought forth Apollo in that place. But when he reached Crete, he went to the priests of Morgos, one of the Idaean Dactyls, by whom he was purified with the thunderstone: in the morning, indeed, lying face down near the sea, but at night near the river, wreathed in the fleece of a black lamb. Then, when he descended into the Idaean cave dressed in black wool, he passed three-times-nine days there according to the accepted custom, and performed the funeral rites for Jupiter, and saw the sacred couch <note>original: "puluinar". A cushioned seat or bed used in the "lectisternium" ritual where images of gods were laid out to "eat" and "rest."</note> which is spread for him every year. # 12 PORPHYRY

...he was of a noble character <note>original: "liberali". This implies the bearing of a free-born, educated man.</note> and of tall stature, and there was a great deal of grace and decorum both in his manners and in the other gestures of his body. The city of the Crotoniats <note>The inhabitants of Croton, a powerful Achaean colony in Southern Italy known for its doctors and athletes.</note> was so moved that after he had captivated the minds of the elders (of whom the city's magistracy consisted) and held a long and brilliant discourse among the young men, he then, by order of the magistrates, delivered exhortations to the youth. After this, he spoke to the boys who flocked to him eagerly from the schools, and finally even to the women; indeed, a women's assembly was even gathered for his sake. Once this was done, his fame spread widely and he gained many listeners, partly from that city—not only men but also women, among whom the name of Theano <note>Theano is often described as Pythagoras’s wife or daughter; she was a philosopher in her own right, credited with writing treatises on mathematics and ethics.</note> is the most illustrious—and partly from the neighboring barbarian region, including even kings and dynasts. However, what he actually handed down to his listeners, no one can say for certain, for they maintained a great and diligent silence among themselves. But these things became well-known to everyone: first, that he affirmed the soul to be immortal; then, that it migrates into different kinds of living beings; and in addition to these, that after certain cycles of time, the things that once happened...
<meta>catchword: ...happened once</note>

<column-break/>

...He possessed the greatest grace and beauty in his voice, in his character, and in all other things. He so influenced the city of the Crotoniats that after he enchanted <note>original: "ἐψυχαγώγησε" (epsychagōgēse), literally "led the souls."</note> the council of elders, having spoken many fine things to the young men, he again gave exhortations to the youth at the request of the rulers. After these things, he spoke to the children who had come together in a crowd from the schools; then to the women, for whom a women’s assembly was organized for him. When these things occurred, a great reputation grew around him, and he took many followers from the city itself, not only men but also women, of whom the name of one, Theano, became famous. He also drew many from the neighboring barbarian land, both kings and rulers. Now, what he said to his associates, no one is able to state with certainty, for silence was maintained by them of no ordinary kind. Nevertheless, the following things became most known to all: first, that he says the soul is immortal; then, that it changes into other kinds of animals; and in addition to these, that at certain periods of time, the things that once happened happen again...
<meta>catchword: ...again as new</meta>

# ON THE LIFE OF PYTHAGORAS.

...nothing is simply new. And furthermore, one must believe that all living beings are of the same family. For it appears that Pythagoras was the first to bring these doctrines into Greece. He so turned everyone toward himself that with only a single lecture, as Nicomachus <note>Nicomachus of Gerasa was a mathematician and follower of Pythagorean thought, living around 60–120 AD.</note> says, which he gave upon landing in Italy, he captured more than two thousand people with his words. As a result, they no longer endured to return home, but together with their children and wives, they established an immense communal assembly <note>original Greek: "homakoion". This term specifically refers to a shared place of listening or a co-educational school where students lived together.</note> and founded cities in what became called by everyone Great Greece <note>original: "Megalen Hellada," or Magna Graecia, the coastal areas of Southern Italy settled by Greek colonists.</note> in Italy. Receiving laws and instructions from him as if they were divine precepts, they did nothing outside of these. They also held their property in common and counted Pythagoras among the gods. For this reason, whenever they brought forward any of the secrets from their studies—something elegant and applicable to many natural results—they swore by the so-called tetractys <term>tetractys: A sacred symbol consisting of ten points arranged in a triangle of four rows (1+2+3+4=10), representing the harmony of the universe and the four elements.</term>, all of them addressing Pythagoras as a kind of god. In the things confirmed by them, they would say:

->not<-

<column-break/>

...had once been would happen again, and that nothing at all exists as new; and that all living things must be considered related to each other and of the same kind.
<margin>5</margin> For Pythagoras seems to have been the first of all to bring these doctrines into Greece. And so he converted all to himself that in a single discourse, as Nicomachus reports,
<margin>10</margin> which he held at his first arrival in Italy, he took more than two thousand men with his speech. So that they might not be away from home any longer, together with their
<margin>15</margin> children and wives they established a certain communal assembly <note>original Latin: "auditorium". In this context, it refers to a place for "hearers" (acousmatici) to live and learn together.</note> of huge size; and they occupied that part of Italy which is commonly called Great Greece <note>original: "Magna Grecia".</note>,
<margin>20</margin> by founding cities. They also received laws and statutes from him as divine precepts, and it was a sin to do anything besides them. They also brought their
<margin>25</margin> goods into a common fund; and they numbered Pythagoras among the Gods. Therefore, whenever they were to hand down some skillful and elegant point from the
<margin>30</margin> secrets of their science, from which many physical conclusions might be drawn, they especially swore by the tetractys (as they call it) or the quaternion <term>quaternion: A Latin-derived term for the Tetractys, emphasizing the number four.</term>,

->By him who handed down the Tetractys to our soul,<-

---

Indeed, whichever cities he encountered on his journey through Italy and Sicily—some of which had been oppressed by mutual servitude for many years, while others were only recently so—he filled them with a spirit aspiring toward liberty. Through the students <note>original Latin: "auditores," literally "listeners" or "hearers," the first grade of Pythagorean initiates.</note> he had in each city, he restored them to freedom: specifically Croton, Sybaris, Catana, Rhegium, Himera, Agrigentum, and Tauromenium, as well as several others. For these cities, he even established laws through Charondas of Catana and Zaleucus the Locrian <note>Charondas and Zaleucus were celebrated semi-legendary lawgivers of the Greek colonies in Italy; Porphyry credits their wisdom to Pythagorean influence.</note>; because of these laws, their neighbors for a long time desired to follow and emulate their institutions. Furthermore, Symichus, the tyrant of the Centuripae <note>An ancient town in Sicily, now Centuripe.</note>, after hearing Pythagoras, resigned his power and gave part of his wealth to his sister and part to the citizens. Moreover, as Aristoxenus <note>A Greek philosopher and pupil of Aristotle who wrote extensively on music and biography.</note> relates, the Lucanians, Messapians, Picentinians, and Romans also came to him. He utterly removed all discord—not only from his acquaintances and close friends and their descendants for several generations, but from all

<column-break/>

<margin>10</margin> Those cities which he noted throughout Italy and Sicily that he found enslaved by one another—some for many <margin>15</margin> years and others recently—he filled with a spirit of freedom through his students in each place, and he set them free: Croton, Sybaris, Catana, Rhegium, Himera, Akragas <note>The Greek name for Agrigentum, modern Agrigento.</note>, Tauromenium, and certain others. To these <margin>20</margin> he also gave laws through Charondas of Catana and Zaleucus the Locrian, by which they became enviable to their neighbors for a long time. <margin>25</margin> Symichus, the tyrant of the Centoripini, upon hearing him, laid down his rule and gave some of his money to his sister and some to the citizens. As <margin>30</margin> Aristoxenus says, the Lucanians, Messapians, Peucetians <note>The Greek text mentions the Peucetians (Παυκέτιοι) whereas the Latin column lists the Picentini.</note>, and Romans approached him. He completely did away with factional strife, not only among his followers, but also among <margin>35</margin> their descendants for many generations, and generally among all the cities in Italy

<meta>This page continues Porphyry's account of Pythagoras’s travels in Southern Italy. Having already freed several cities from tyranny, Pythagoras now addresses the moral character of the citizens and demonstrates his mystical influence over nature.</meta>

# THE LIFE OF PYTHAGORAS.

...all the cities of Italy and Sicily, both toward themselves and toward one another. For he frequently repeated to everyone, whether many or few, this <term>apophtegma</term> <note>A concise, pithy saying or maxim intended to convey a moral truth.</note>: "We must flee by every means, and cut away with fire and sword and every device, disease from the body, ignorance from the soul, luxury from the belly, sedition from the city, discord from the home, and together, excess from all things." And if one must believe those who have recorded his history—ancient and noteworthy authors—his admonition reached even to irrational animals. For having caught the Daunian bear <note>Daunia was a region in ancient Apulia, Italy.</note> which was harming the inhabitants, as they say, and having stroked it for a long time, and fed it with bread and fruit, and having made it swear never again to touch a living thing, he let it go. It immediately departed into the mountains and woods, and was never seen again attacking any living creature. And seeing an ox in Tarentum <note>A major Greek colony in Southern Italy, modern Taranto.</note> in a mixed pasture eating green beans, he stood by the herdsman and advised him to tell the ox to abstain from the beans. When the herdsman mocked him and said he did not know how to speak "ox-tongue," Pythagoras approached it himself, and into its ear...

<column-break/>

...all the cities of Italy and Sicily without exception, both internal and external. For frequently he had upon his lips, before whoever might be present,
<margin>5</margin> whether they were many together or few, this <term>apophtegma</term>: "One must drive away with all effort, and with fire and sword and whatever devices at last,
<margin>10</margin> cut away disease from the body, ignorance from the soul, luxury from the belly, sedition from the city, discord from the
<margin>15</margin> family, and finally, excess from all things." But if faith is to be given to those who wrote about him—authors of ancient and indeed established authority—his precepts and
<margin>20</margin> admonitions extended even unto brute animals. For a Daunian bear, which was causing the greatest damage to the inhabitants, he captured;
<margin>25</margin> and after handling it for a long time, and feeding it with morsels of bread and certain fruits, and binding it by oath to no longer touch anything living,
<margin>30</margin> he sent it away again. It indeed hid itself in the mountains and forests, and afterwards was never seen to attack even a brute animal. And when near Tarentum
<margin>35</margin> he saw an ox feeding recklessly and indiscriminately, and that it had touched green beans, he approached the cowherd and urged him to proclaim to the ox...

to abstain from the beans. But when the herdsman replied mockingly that he did not know how to speak "ox-tongue" <note>original: *bouinari*; a rare Latin verb meaning to speak to or act like an ox.</note>: Pythagoras himself approached and whispered into the ox’s ear,
<margin>5</margin> so that it not only left the field planted with beans, but never again in the future touched a bean. For many years afterward, it lived around the Temple of Juno <note>The Roman equivalent of the Greek goddess Hera.</note> at Tarentum
<margin>10</margin> until it grew old, and was commonly called the "Sacred Ox"; it ate whatever food those it met might offer. Then, during the Olympic Games, he is said to have drawn an eagle
<margin>15</margin> down from the air as it flew over his head—at a moment when he happened to be discussing omens, symbols, and divine signs with his companions, and how the gods signify and foretell certain things to humans
<margin>20</margin> endowed with true piety—and after stroking it with his hand, he let it go again. And when he stood by some fishermen as they were drawing a heavily laden net
<margin>25</margin> from the depths, he predicted the exact quantity of fish they were pulling in, defining the specific number. And when they promised to do whatever he might command if this turned out to be the case, he ordered them
<margin>30</margin> to return the fish alive to the sea, though only after counting them precisely first. And what is much more
<margin>35</margin> wonderful, during that whole time they were being counted, none of the fish died while out of the water...
<meta>catchword: himself</meta>

<column-break/>

...whispering to the bull, [he caused it] not only to abstain from the bean, but also never again to touch beans thereafter. For a very long time it remained in Tarentum at the Temple of Hera, growing old, being called the "Sacred Ox," and being fed by the food that passers-by offered. And an eagle flying over at the Olympic Games, while he was conversing with his acquaintances by chance about bird-omens, symbols, and signs from Zeus <note>original: *διοσημειῶν* (diosemeion); specifically portents or signs from the sky/Zeus.</note>—that there are certain messages from the gods, and these specifically for those men who are truly dear to the gods—he is said to have brought down, and after stroking it, let it go again. And standing by some net-haulers while a great burden was being dragged up from the deep in a dragnet, he foretold how great a multitude they were drawing in, showing the number of fish; and the men having submitted to do whatever he ordered, should it turn out so, he ordered them to release the fish alive again, after first counting them accurately. And the more marvelous thing was that not one of the fish gasped its last <note>original: *ἀπέπνευσεν*; literally "breathed its last" or died.</note> during such a long time of counting while remaining out of the water, while he stood there. And to many of those he met, he reminded...

# ON THE LIFE OF PYTHAGORAS. 17

...of their previous life, which their soul had lived long ago before it was bound to this body <note>This refers to the doctrine of metempsychosis, or the transmigration of souls, a core Pythagorean belief that the soul is immortal and inhabits different bodies over many lifetimes.</note>; and he revealed himself through undeniable evidence to be Euphorbus, the son of Panthous. And of the Homeric verses, he would especially sing those, and most melodiously performed them to the accompaniment of the lyre.

<column-break/>

...expired while he was standing by. He also brought back to the memory of many who approached him their previous life, which their soul had lived once before, before it was bound to this body: and by irrefutable arguments he showed that he himself had been Euphorbus, the son of Panthous <note>original: *Euphorbum Panthi filium*; Euphorbus was a Trojan hero in Homer's *Iliad*. Pythagoras claimed that his soul had previously inhabited Euphorbus's body during the Trojan War.</note>. And from the Homeric verses he especially praised these, and most sweetly sang them along to the lyre:

<note>An oval institutional stamp on the right side of the page reads "VITTORIO EMANUELE NATIONAL LIBRARY, ROME".</note>

> <note>The following is an English translation of the Greek verses from Homer's *Iliad*, Book 17, lines 51–60, describing the death of Euphorbus.</note>
> His locks, which were like those of the Graces, were soaked in blood,
> And the curls that were fastened with gold and silver.
> Like a thriving olive sapling that a man raises
> In a solitary place where water bubbles up in abundance,
> Beautiful and flourishing; and the breezes of 
> Every wind toss it, and it bursts with white blossoms;
> But suddenly a wind comes with a great storm,
> Uproots it from its trench, and stretches it upon the ground;
> Such was Panthous' son, Euphorbus of the fine ash-spear,
> Whom Menelaus, son of Atreus, stripped of his armor after he had slain him.

> <note>The following is an English translation of the Latin poetic rendering of the same Homeric passage.</note>
> And his pleasing hair was wet, sprinkled with blood,
> And his ruddy curls, beautiful with gold and silver.
> Just as a sprout of a green olive tree in summer,
> Which a diligent farmer brings forth in a pleasant field,
> Which a stream of nearby water refreshes as it flows,
> And gentle spirits of the air soothe with mild breezes,
> And it clothes itself everywhere in joyful white flowers.
> But if by chance a wind should rush with a violent whirlwind;
> It lays it low on the ground and strikes it down, torn from its deepest root:
> Such was the son of Panthous, cast down by a bitter wound,
> Whom the son of Atreus, robbed of life, stripped of his arms.

# PORPHYRY

Ea enim quæ de Euphorbi Phrygis scuto, Mycenis in honorem Argiuæ Iunonis cũ alijs spolijs Troianis suspenso, referuntur, vt nimis peruulgata præterimus. Caucasus quoque fluuius transeuntem cum multis amicorum allocutus, & clara atque liquida voce cunctis audientibus dixisse fertur; *Salue Pythagora*. quin etiam vna eademque die & Metaponti in Italia, & Tauromenij in Sicilia, amicis quos vtrobi-que habebat adfuisse, & in publico cœtu adlocutum fuisse omnes propemodum affirmant; cum multorum stadiorum, quā terra quā mari, interuallum intercedat, non nisi complurium dierum itinere conficiēdum. quod enim Abaridi Hyperboreo, qui Apollinem Hyperboreum eum esse credebat, cuius sacerdos Abaris erat, aureum ostenderit femur, qui verum id esse confirmaret, nemo non nouit. quodque naui quadam appellente, & amicis precantibus sibi merces quas vehebat obtingere, Pythagoras dixerit: ergo mortuus vobis erit: nauisque accedens mortuum tulerit; & sexcenta alia multo admirabiliora,
->and<-

<column-break/>

As for the stories concerning the shield of Euphorbus the Phrygian <note>original: *Euphorbi Phrygis*; Pythagoras claimed that in a previous life he was Euphorbus, a Trojan warrior. To prove this, he reportedly identified Euphorbus's old shield in a temple.</note>—which was dedicated to Argive Juno <note>The Roman name for the Greek goddess Hera.</note> at Mycenae along with other Trojan spoils—we shall pass over them as being too well known. It is also said that the river Caucasus <note>original: *Caucasus fluuius*; while the Caucasus is known as a mountain range, ancient sources here refer to a river, often identified by scholars as the river Kosas or Alpheios.</note> addressed him while he was crossing with many of his friends, and spoke in a clear and distinct voice for all to hear: "*Greetings, Pythagoras.*" Furthermore, almost everyone affirms that on one and the same day he was present both at Metapontum in Italy and at Tauromenium in Sicily, where he spoke with the friends he had in both places; even though these locations are separated by a distance of many stades <note>A 'stade' was an ancient unit of length, approximately 600 feet.</note> across both land and sea, a journey that cannot be completed except over many days. Moreover, it is common knowledge that he showed his golden thigh <note>A legendary physical mark of Pythagoras used to prove his divine status, specifically suggesting he was the god Apollo in human form.</note> to Abaris the Hyperborean, who believed that Pythagoras was the Hyperborean Apollo (whose priest Abaris was), in order to confirm that this was true. And it is said that when a certain ship was coming to shore, and his friends were praying that they might obtain the goods it carried, Pythagoras said: "Then there will be a corpse for you." And as the ship arrived, it was indeed carrying a dead body. And six hundred other even more wonderful things...

# ON THE LIFE OF PYTHAGORAS.
->A 2<-

...that the ship was carrying a corpse. A thousand other things, even more wonderful and divine, are told of this man consistently and with one voice; to put it simply, of no one else have <margin>5</margin> so many or more divine things been believed. For his infallible predictions of earthquakes are remembered, and his swift averting of plagues, along with his calming of violent winds <margin>10</margin> and hail-storms, and the parting of waves in both rivers and the sea for the easy crossing of his companions. Having received <margin>15</margin> these powers from him, Empedocles, Epimenides, and Abaris performed such things in many places, as their own poems clearly show. Furthermore, "Warder-off of Winds" <note>original: *Alexanemos*</note> <margin>20</margin> was the surname given to Empedocles, "the Purifier" <note>original: *Kathartes*</note> to Epimenides, and "Air-walker" <note>original: *Aithrobates*</note> to Abaris; for <margin>25</margin> he, riding upon an arrow given to him by the Hyperborean Apollo <note>A mythical sun-god associated with a legendary land in the far north.</note>, crossed rivers, seas, and <margin>30</margin> inaccessible places, traveling through the air in a certain way. Some suspect that Pythagoras also experienced this when he was in Metapontum and Tauromenium with his friends <margin>35</margin> in each place...

<column-break/>

...and more divine things are constantly and with one voice affirmed concerning this man, so that, simply speaking, more and more excellent things are believed of no one else. For his most certain predictions of earthquakes are commemorated, and his most present warding off of plagues; also his suppressions of violent winds and most heavy hail, and his calming of storms, as well in rivers as on the sea, for the easy and favorable crossing of his friends. They say that Empedocles, Epimenides, and Abaris, having drawn these powers from him, performed such deeds everywhere, and that their poems clearly testify to this. Indeed, Empedocles had the surname of "Alexanemus," or "Warder-off of Winds"; Epimenides that of "the Purifier"; and Abaris "the Air-walker" <note>original: *Aerambulus*</note>. Since he, riding upon the arrow of the Hyperborean Apollo which he had received as a gift, passed over rivers and seas and other inaccessible places as if flying through the air. Some suspect that Pythagoras also did this, since on one and the same day he [visited] friends at Metapontum and Tauromenium...

### PORPHYRY
->Page 20<-

...on the same day in both places <note>original Latin: *die utrobiq; conueniret*</note>. He used to soothe the passions of the soul and the body with rhythms, songs, and incantations. And these indeed he applied to his friends; but he himself perceived the harmony of the universe <note>original Latin: *vniuerfi concentum*; refers to the "Music of the Spheres," a Pythagorean belief that the movements of celestial bodies produce a divine harmony</note> with his ears, for he understood that entire and universal harmony of the celestial spheres and the stars that move within those spheres—which we, because of the weakness of our nature, do not hear. Empedocles also testifies to these things concerning Pythagoras in these verses:

> *Among these there was a certain man of extraordinary knowledge,*
> *Who possessed a vast wealth of understanding within his breast,*
> *Investigating all the learned discoveries of the wise.*
> *For whenever he exerted all the powers of his mind,*
> *He easily saw each of all existing things,*
> *Even throughout ten or twenty human generations.*

For the phrases "extraordinary things," and "he saw each of existing things," and "wealth of understanding," and the like, indicate an exquisite and most accurate [perception] in Pythagoras above others...

<column-break/>

...met with his companions on the same day. He used to soothe <margin>5</margin> the passions of the soul and the body with rhythms, songs, and incantations. And while he applied these to his companions, he himself listened to the harmony of the universe, <margin>10</margin> understanding the universal harmony of the spheres <term>sphaerarum</term> and the stars that move within them, which we do not hear because of the smallness of our nature. To these things <margin>15</margin> Empedocles also testifies, saying of him:

> *Among these there was a certain man of extraordinary knowledge,*
> *Who possessed a vast wealth of understanding* <term>prapidon</term> <note>prapidon: a Greek term for the midriff or diaphragm, often used poetically to refer to the seat of the mind or heart</note> *within his breast,*
> *Investigating all the learned discoveries of the wise.*
> *For whenever he exerted all the powers of his mind,*
> *He easily saw each of all existing things,*
> *Even throughout ten or twenty human generations* <term>aionessi</term> <note>aionessi: lifetimes or ages</note>.

For the "extraordinary things," and "he saw each of existing things," and "wealth of understanding," and <margin>35</margin> similar expressions, are especially emphatic of that exquisite and more accurate [constitution] compared to others...

### ON THE LIFE OF PYTHAGORAS

->Page 21<-

B 3

...organization in seeing, hearing, and understanding of Pythagoras. He maintained that the sounds of the seven stars <note>original Greek: ἑπτὰ ἀστέρων; referring to the seven "wandering stars" or planets known in antiquity</note> and the sphere of the fixed stars—along with that which is above us, called by them the Antichthon <term>Antichthon</term> (a "Counter-Earth" positioned on the opposite side of a central fire in the Pythagorean cosmos)—constituted the Nine Muses. The collective combination, symphony, and, as it were, the mutual connection of all of them—of which each is a part and an emanation, being eternal and unbegotten—he named Mnemosyne <note>original Greek: Μνημοσύνην; the goddess of Memory and mother of the Muses</note>. Describing his daily way of life, Diogenes <note>likely Diogenes Laertius, a biographer of philosophers</note> says that he exhorted everyone to flee from ambition and the craving for glory, for these things especially produce envy; and to avoid the company of the masses. He himself conducted his morning studies at home, tuning his own soul to the lyre and singing certain ancient Paeans <term>Paeans</term> (solemn hymns of praise or healing) composed by Thales. He also used to sing the verses of Homer and Hesiod, whatever he thought might calm the soul. He also performed certain dances, as many as he thought might provide agility and health to the body. His walks he took...

<column-break/>

...of his body and mind, in seeing, hearing, and understanding. Therefore, those sounds which are produced by the <margin>5</margin> seven wandering stars, the sphere of the fixed stars, and the Earth opposite to ours, which they call the Antichthon <note>original Latin: *antichthona*; the Pythagorean "Counter-Earth"</note>, he affirmed to be the Nine Muses. But the <margin>10</margin> assembly and harmony of all together, and as it were their mutual bond—of which each one is a single part and portion, being eternal and unbegotten—he called Mnemosyne <margin>15</margin> <note>original Latin: *Mnemosynem*; Memory</note>. But Diogenes, in explaining his daily way of living, reports that he warned everyone to flee from ambition and the desire for <margin>20</margin> glory, because from these things, above all else, envy is kindled; and that they should avoid the conversations and commerce of the common people. Furthermore, <margin>25</margin> he held his morning exercises at his own home, composing his mind to the lyre, and singing certain ancient Paeans <note>original Latin: *Pæanas*; hymns</note> of Thales. <margin>30</margin> He also sang the poems of Homer and Hesiod, by which the mind seemed to be softened and rendered more tranquil. Moreover, he danced <margin>35</margin> certain dances, which he believed secured agility and health for the body. Nor were his walks...

# PORPHYRY

->Page 22<-

...he did not [take his walks] indiscriminately with many, but with only one or two companions in temples or groves; choosing for himself places furthest removed from all noise and most pleasant. He was a great lover of friends; for he was the first to declare: **All things are in common among friends**, and **a friend is a second self**. And while they were in good health, he always associated with them; if they suffered from any disease of the body, he cared for them; but if they were sick in mind, he consoled them, as we said, easing the pain partly with incantations and magical songs, and partly with music. For he had certain special songs with which he healed the diseases of bodies; when he sang these, he restored the sick to their former health. There were also those by which he brought about the forgetting of pain, mitigated anger, and removed unseasonable lust. Regarding his diet, his lunch consisted of honeycomb or honey; dinner was bread made of millet, or porridge, and cooked or raw vegetables; but very rarely meat from sacrificial victims; and not indiscriminately from any part. The inner shrines of the Gods

<margin>5</margin> ...he did not make [his walks] conspicuously with many, but with another or a third in temples or groves, selecting the most quiet and beautiful of places. He loved his friends <margin>10</margin> exceedingly, being the first to declare that **the things of friends are common** <note>original Greek: κοινὰ τὰ τῶν φίλων; a famous Pythagorean maxim</note>, and that **a friend is another self**. And while they were <margin>15</margin> healthy, he always spent time with them; but when they were sick in body, he cured them; and those sick in soul he consoled, as <margin>20</margin> we said, with incantations and magic, and some with music. For he had melodies that were healing <note>original Greek: παιώνια (paionia); referring to the Paeans or healing hymns of Apollo</note> for diseases of the body, which, by singing them, he raised up <margin>25</margin> the sick. There were also those which worked a forgetting of grief, and soothed anger, and removed improper desires. Regarding <margin>30</margin> his lifestyle, his lunch was honeycomb or honey; and for dinner bread of millet, or a barley-cake <term>maza</term> (a staple Greek food made of barley meal), and vegetables, both boiled and raw; and rarely meat from sacrificial <margin>35</margin> victims, and this not from every part. For the most part, whenever he was about to enter the inner sanctuaries <note>original Greek: ἀδύτοις (adytois); the "adytum" was the most sacred, restricted inner chamber of a temple</note> of the gods and spend some time there, he used foods that prevent hunger and thirst. <margin>35</margin> preserved the same condition; so that it was not now healthy, now sick; now fat and thick, now thin and gaunt. The habits of his mind likewise always 
<margin>35</margin> shone forth from his face. Nor was he [moved] by pleasure

# PORPHYRY

...row <note>This completes the word "sorrow" from the previous page.</note>, nor was he ever seen to be possessed by joy or pain. Indeed, no one ever saw him laughing or weeping. When he sacrificed to the Gods, it was not with a burdensome or envious lavishness, but he appeased the Gods with meal, cakes, incense, and myrrh, and by no means with animals—except perhaps with roosters and the tenderest of piglets. Furthermore, as the more accurate authors affirm, he even sacrificed an ox made of flour <note>A bloodless substitute for animal sacrifice, reflecting the Pythagorean tenet of reincarnation and respect for life.</note> when he discovered that the side subtending a right-angled triangle <note>The hypotenuse.</note> is equal in power to the sides that contain it <note>This refers to the Pythagorean Theorem ($a^2 + b^2 = c^2$). In ancient Greek geometry, "equal in power" refers to the area of the squares built on those sides.</note>. Whatever he discussed among his listeners, he taught either with clear and abundant speech, or obscurely through the veils of riddles; for his method of instruction was twofold. Among his disciples, some were called <term>Mathematicians</term> <note>From the Greek *mathematikoi*, meaning "those who study" or "the learned."</note>, and others were called <term>Listeners</term> <note>From the Latin *auscultatores* or Greek *akousmatikoi*, meaning "those who hear."</note>. The Mathematicians were those who grasped the fuller and more elaborate account of scientific reason; the Listeners, however, were those who heard only the summary headings and precepts of the writings without more detailed explanation. He gave instructions, moreover, concerning the race of Gods, Daemons, and heroes...

<column-break/>

...possessed by grief; indeed, no one ever saw him laughing or weeping. When he sacrificed to the Gods, he was not burdensome, but appeased the Gods with 
<margin>5</margin> barley meal, cakes, incense, and myrrh, and least of all with living things—unless perhaps with roosters and 
<margin>10</margin> the softest of piglets. It is said by those who are more precise that he once sacrificed an ox made of dough when he discovered that 
<margin>15</margin> the hypotenuse of a right-angled triangle is equal in power to the sides containing it. As for the things he discussed with 
<margin>20</margin> those who came to him, he advised them either in a detailed way or through symbols. For the form of his teaching was twofold. Of those who came to him, some were called 
<margin>25</margin> *Mathematici* and others *Akousmatikoi*. The *Mathematici* <note>original Greek: *μαθηματικοί*</note> were those who had thoroughly learned the 
<margin>30</margin> more advanced and precisely labored account of knowledge; the *Akousmatikoi* <note>original Greek: *ἀκουσματικοί*</note> were those who had heard only the summary 
<margin>35</margin> points of the instructions without more precise narration. He advised that regarding the race of the divine, of daemons, and of heroes, one should speak with reverence and have a good mind; and to be well-disposed toward parents and benefactors. To the...

### ON THE LIFE OF PYTHAGORAS.

<columns>
...laws <note>Completes the word "laws" from the previous page.</note> and to obey them. One must worship the Gods not as a side-task or in passing, but rather by setting out from home specifically for this purpose. 
<margin>5</margin> To the celestial Gods, one should sacrifice odd numbers <note>The Pythagoreans associated odd numbers with the limited, the masculine, and the divine.</note>; but to the terrestrial Gods, even numbers. For he called the better of the opposing powers the <term>Monad</term> <note>original: "monas" or "unitas." The principle of oneness and the source of all things.</note>, and light, and the right side, 
<margin>10</margin> and the equal, and the stable, and the straight. But the worse power he called the <term>Dyad</term> <note>original: "duas" or "binarum." The principle of "twoness" or "otherness," associated with division and the material world.</note>, and darkness, and the left side, and the unequal, and the unstable, and the moving. 
<margin>15</margin> He also gave advice of this kind: that a cultivated and fruit-bearing plant, and also any animal that is not by nature harmful to the human race, should neither be destroyed nor harmed. One must faithfully 
<margin>20</margin> preserve a deposit <note>a thing held in trust</note>, not only of money, but also of words, for the sake of the one who deposited it. He held that there are three distinct classes of things 
<margin>25</margin> worthy of zeal, which must be pursued and managed. First, those things which are glorious and honorable; next, those things which are useful for life; 
<margin>30</margin> and third and last, those which are pleasant. However, he did not accept common or bewitching pleasure, but rather that which is firm, most dignified, 
<margin>35</margin> and pure from the insult of slander. For there are two kinds of plea...

<column-break/>

...divine genus <note>Completes the thought from the previous page: "to speak about the divine genus with the highest reverence."</note> with the highest reverence, and to think of them in the best possible way. One must show benevolence toward parents and benefactors; one must obey the laws. The Gods should not be worshiped in passing, nor should you approach them unless you have set out from home with that specific intention. To the celestial Gods, sacrifice odd numbers; to the terrestrial Gods, however, sacrifice even numbers. For of the opposing powers, he called one better—the unit, light, the right side, the equal, the stable, and the straight; the worse, however, he called the pair, darkness, the left side, the unequal, the unstable, and the mobile. Furthermore, he also commanded these things: that a cultivated and fruit-bearing plant, and also animals that are harmless to the human race, should neither be destroyed nor harmed. A deposit not only of money but also of words must be kept in good faith for the depositor. Three classes of things are to be established as worthy of being pursued and acquired with all zeal: first, indeed, those which are glorious and honorable; second, those serving the use of life; third, truly, and last, those things which are delightful or associated with pleasure. However, he did not admit common and bewitching pleasure, but rather that which is solid, grave, and pure from all crime. For there are two kinds of plea...

### PORPHYRY.

<columns>
...[distinction] between pleasures; of which that one which indulges the belly and the lusts of Venus <note>original: "Veneris libidinibus." Referring to sexual desire, named after the goddess of love.</note> through the profusion of wealth, he compared to the murderous songs of the Sirens <note>In Greek mythology, Sirens were creatures who lured sailors to their deaths with enchanting music; here they symbolize destructive temptations.</note>. 
<margin>5</margin> But the other pleasure, which consists of honorable and just things and those necessary for life, he held to be equally pleasant in the present 
<margin>10</margin> and not followed by regret as a companion. This he said was similar to a certain harmony of the Muses <note>The nine goddesses of inspiration in the arts and sciences, representing divine and intellectual order.</note>. 
He advised that care and consideration should especially be given to two times: 
<margin>15</margin> namely, the time when one goes to bed, and when one rises from bed again. For at both times one must weigh in the mind both the things already done, 
<margin>20</margin> and those soon to be done. Accounts of one's actions must be demanded by each person from themselves, while future actions must be cautiously provided for. Therefore, he wished each person to sing these verses to themselves before 
<margin>25</margin> sleep.

<column-break/>

...of pleasures, that which is granted to the belly and to sexual desires through luxury, he compared to the man-slaying songs of the Sirens. But the pleasure found in beautiful and just things, those necessary for life, is likewise immediately sweet and without regret in the future; this he said resembled a certain harmony of the Muses. He also exhorted that care be placed in two particular times: the one when one turns toward sleep, and the other when one rises up from sleep. For it is proper in each of these to examine both the things already accomplished and the things to come; of the things that have happened, each person must take an account from himself, and for the things to come, he must exercise foresight. Concerning sleep, then, he would have each person chant these verses to himself:

# PORPHYRY

### Page 28

...[sword]: that is, one who is swollen and angry should not be provoked with harsh words. The crown must not be plucked: that is, the laws must not be violated, for these are the crowns of cities. Besides these and other things of this kind: do not eat your heart: that is, one should not consume oneself with sorrow. Do not sit upon a *choenix* <note>original: "choenici" / "χοίνικος". A choenix was a Greek unit of volume, roughly a daily ration of grain. To "sit" on it meant to remain idle while consuming one's resources rather than working.</note> or a bushel: that is, do not live idly. When you have set out, do not turn back: that is, at the point of death, do not long for this life. One must not walk on the public highway: by which he forbade following the opinions of the common crowd; instead, the opinions of the few and the learned should be pursued. Swallows are not to be received into the house: that is, talkative people and those with unrestrained tongues should not be admitted into one's companionship. Help men by adding to their burdens, not by taking them away: by which he advised that effort should be lent to others not for the sake of idleness, but for the sake of virtue. Images of the Gods are not to be worn in rings: that is, wisdom and discourse concerning the Gods should not be kept rashly or everywhere at hand, nor should it be spread among the common people. Libations should be made to the Gods at the ear <note>Referring to the "ear" or handle of the drinking cup.</note> of the cups: for by this he judged that the Gods should be honored and celebrated with music:

<column-break/>

...to stir [the fire] with a sword: that is, do not provoke a man who is swollen and angry with sharp words. And do not pluck the crown, that is: do not do violence to the laws, for these are the crowns of cities. Again, other things of this kind: such as, do not eat the heart. That is, do not grieve yourself with sorrows. Nor sit upon a *choenix*: that is, do not live idly. Nor turn back when departing: do not cling to this life when dying. And do not walk on the highways: through which he forbade following the opinions of the many, but to pursue those of the few and the educated. Nor receive swallows into the houses: that is, do not make talkative men or those with unrestrained tongues your housemates. Help to put a burden on those who are carrying it, but do not help to take it off: through which he advised: be of use to no one for the sake of ease, but for the sake of virtue and labors. And do not wear the images of the gods in rings. That is: do not have your opinion or discourse about the gods ready or visible, nor bring it to the many. And make libations to the gods at the [ear]...

# ON THE LIFE OF PYTHAGORAS

### Page 29

...from the poems; for from this he hinted that one should honor the gods and praise them with music, for it [music] enters through the ears. One must not eat those things which it is not lawful to eat: namely generation, growth, beginning, and end; nor those things from which the first hypothesis <note>original: "ὑπόθεσις" / "hypothesis". In this context, it refers to the underlying foundation or basic principle of a physical body.</note> of all things is formed. And he said to abstain from the loins, and testicles, and genitals, and marrow, and feet, and head [of sacrificial animals]. For he called the loins the "foundation," because upon this, as upon a base, living beings are constituted. He called the testicles and genitals "generation," for without their activity, no living thing is born. He called the marrow "growth," which is the cause of growth for all living things. The feet he called the "beginning," and the head the "end," because these hold the greatest leadership over the body. In the same way, he advised abstaining from beans, just as from human flesh. They record that he forbade this for such a reason: that when the first principle and origin of the world were in a state of chaos, and many things were brought together and coiled and rotted together in the earth, little by little generation and distinction took place, and animals were born together...

<column-break/>

...for it [music] penetrates the ears. Nor are those things to be eaten which it is forbidden to eat; namely, generation, <margin>5</margin> growth, beginning, and end: nor those things from which the first basis and foundation of all things consists. For he said one must abstain from the loins, testicles, <margin>10</margin> genitals, marrow, feet, and head of sacrificial victims; for he called the loins the "basis," because animals subsist by leaning upon them as on a foundation. But "origin" or <margin>15</margin> "generation," he called the testicles and genitals: for without their labor no animal is generated. He called the marrow "growth," which <margin>20</margin> provides the cause of growth to all animals. He called the feet the "beginning"; the head, however, the "end," because the body is most especially ruled by these. <margin>25</margin> He warned that one must abstain from beans just as from human flesh. They report that he forbade this for the following reason: when the first <margin>30</margin> principle and origin of things were mutually confused, and many things had merged together in the earth and rotted together, <margin>35</margin> little by little individuals arose and were separated. And when at the same time animals were being born...

# PORPHYRY

### Page 30

...and plants were produced; then from that same decay, both humans emerged and beans blossomed. He brought forward manifest proofs of this matter: for if someone chews a bean, and exposes it, crushed by the teeth, to the burning rays of the sun for a little while, and returns after a short interval of time, they will perceive in it the scent of human blood. <note>Pythagoras and his followers famously avoided beans, believing them to be closely linked to the essence of human life or containing the souls of the dead.</note> Furthermore, if someone—at the time when beans are putting forth their flowers—takes a small portion of a flower that is already turning black, and places it in an earthen vessel, covers it with a lid and buries it in the ground, and keeps it buried for ninety days, and then digs it up and brings it out again and removes the lid, they will see grown in place of the bean either the head of a young boy or the genitals of a woman. <note>This bizarre experiment reflects ancient theories of "spontaneous generation," where life was thought to arise directly from decaying organic matter.</note> But he also commanded that one must abstain from certain other things: namely the womb <note>original: "vulua"; likely referring to the sow's womb, which was a culinary delicacy in the ancient world</note>, the red mullet, the sea-nettle, and almost all other sea creatures. Moreover, he referred himself back to certain people who had already finished their lives: asserting that he had first been Euphorbus, then Aethalides, third Hermotimus, fourth Pyrrhus, and finally Pythagoras; through which he [showed] the soul [to be] immor— <note>original: "immortatem", the word for "immortal" is split by the page break.</note>

<column-break/>

...and plants were sent up; then indeed from the same decay, humans were constituted and beans sprouted. <margin>5</margin> And he brought forward clear proofs of this: for if someone, having chewed a bean and <margin>10</margin> ground it with their teeth, should place it in the warmth of the sun's rays for a short time, and then, having stepped away, should return after a little while, they would find it <margin>15</margin> smelling of human slaughter <note>original: "φόνου" / "phonou"; meaning blood or murder</note>. And if, while the bean is blossoming during its growth, someone taking a little of the flower as it turns dark should put it <margin>20</margin> into a ceramic vessel, and having put a lid on it, should bury it in the earth and keep it for ninety days after it has been buried; then after <margin>25</margin> these things, having dug it up and taken it, and having removed the lid, he would find instead of the bean either the head of a child joined together, or the genitals of a woman. And he advised <margin>30</margin> to abstain from other things: such as the womb, the red mullet, and the sea-nettle—and nearly all other sea creatures altogether. And he <margin>35</margin> referred himself back to those born previously: first saying that he was Euphorbus, second Aethalides, third Hermotimus, fourth Pyrrhus, and now Pythagoras; through... <note>The text ends here mid-sentence, transitioning to the explanation of why his soul is immortal.</note>

### ON THE LIFE OF PYTHAGORAS

<columns>
...by which he demonstrated that the soul is immortal. And for those who had been purified, he brought back into memory their former lives. He practiced Philosophy, the goal of which was to rescue and set free the <term>Mind</term> <note>original Greek: "νοῦς" (nous); the higher intellect or divine part of the human spirit</note> that is imprisoned within us from such restraints and bonds; without which one could not learn or perceive anything healthy or true at all, nor see clearly through any active sense whatsoever. For the Mind, by itself, sees all and hears all; but all other things are deaf and blind. To one who has then been purified, one must introduce some of the things that bring benefit. And he introduced these by devising certain methods: first, gently training himself toward the contemplation of eternal and kindred <term>incorporeal</term> <note>things existing without physical bodies, such as numbers, geometric truths, or divine essences</note> things—which always exist in the same way and in the same state—by leading [himself] forward little by little. He did this so that, not being thrown into confusion by a sudden and total change, he might not turn away and give up because of such a great and long-standing corruption of nourishment. Therefore, through mathematical sciences and those contemplations that lie in the borderlands between the corporeal and the incorporeal—which are divided in three ways as bodies, but...

<column-break/>

...showed that [the soul] was immortal. And for those whose minds were rightly purified, he recalled to memory the life already completed long ago. Moreover, he professed a Philosophy whose goal was to rescue the mind implanted in us from such impediments and chains, and to restore it to liberty: without which no one can learn or perceive anything at all sound and true through the help and benefit of any sense. For the mind, according to his opinion, *sees all things and hears all things: the rest are deaf and blind.* <note>original Latin: "cuncta cernit, cunctaq; audit: surda, cæca, cætera"; this is a famous maxim attributed to the poet Epicharmus, frequently cited by Pythagoreans to emphasize that the intellect, not the senses, is the source of true knowledge.</note> Furthermore, to the purified mind, certain useful and profitable things must then be presented. He presented these through certain devised methods and reasons. In the beginning, he gradually accustomed himself to the contemplation of eternal things of the same kind and of incorporeal things, which always maintain themselves in the same way and manner: progressing in order from smaller things, lest by a sudden and unexpected change he might be turned away and refuse, on account of such a great and long-lasting depravity of nourishment. Therefore, by mathematical sciences and those speculations which are intermediate between the corporeal and the incorporeal, and as if in both...

</columns>

<summary>This page describes the Pythagorean view of philosophy as a means of liberating the mind (nous) from the "chains" of the body and the senses. It explains that Pythagoras used mathematics as a bridge to help students transition from focusing on physical things to contemplating eternal, incorporeal truths.</summary>
