--- Page 34 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Pythagoras’s pedagogical methods for purifying the soul. While the previous page focused on the soul’s immortality and the liberation of the mind (nous) from physical restraints, this page explains the specific role of mathematics and number theory as intermediary steps between the physical world and the divine realm.</meta>

# PORPHYRY

<columns>
...borderland (for [mathematical objects] possess a triple dimension like bodies, but without resistance, as if they were incorporeal). He used these as a kind of preliminary training, little by little, for the contemplation of those things which truly exist. By a certain skillful method, he drew the eyes of the mind away from corporeal things—which never remain the same or in the same way, nor stay in one and the same state for even a moment—toward a desire for that nourishment by which, through introducing the contemplation of things that truly exist, he made men blessed. Therefore, the use of the mathematical arts was employed for this end.

Moreover, they zealously cultivated the doctrine of numbers, as Moderatus of Gades <note>Moderatus of Gades (1st century AD) was a Neopythagorean philosopher whose work is a primary source for Porphyry’s understanding of the school.</note> affirms, who most learnedly encompassed the Pythagorean teachings in eleven books. For this reason, he says: since they were unable to clearly explain the primary <term>Forms</term> <note>original Latin: "formas"; the eternal, unchanging patterns of reality</note> and primary principles in speech—because they are most difficult both to understand and to express—they resorted to numbers for the sake of clearer instruction. In this, they imitated geometers and grammarians; for just as these, when about to teach letters and their power, resorted to those marks...

<column-break/>

...without resistance, as if they were incorporeal. Being led forward little by little toward the things that truly exist, away from those corporeal things which <margin>5</margin> never remain the same or in the same way, nor stay in the same state for any length of time, he directed the eyes of the soul with technical guidance toward the longing for <margin>10</margin> [spiritual] nourishment. Through these things, by introducing the vision of things that truly exist, he made men blessed. Thus, the training in mathematics <margin>15</margin> was adopted for this purpose.

The study of numbers—as others say, and especially Moderatus of Gades, who very wisely collected the doctrines of these men in eleven <margin>20</margin> books—was pursued for this reason. Since they were not able, he says, to clearly transmit the primary forms and primary principles <margin>25</margin> through speech—on account of their being hard to grasp and difficult to express—they turned to numbers for the sake of clear instruction, <margin>30</margin> imitating geometers and grammarians. For just as these men, wishing to transmit the powers of the <term>elements</term> <note>original Greek: "στοιχείων" (stoicheion); here meaning the letters of the alphabet as the fundamental components of language</note> and the elements themselves, turned to <margin>35</margin> characters, saying that these are for the first...
</columns>

<summary>Porphyry explains how Pythagoras used mathematics as a bridge to lead the soul from unstable physical realities to eternal truths. He cites Moderatus of Gades, who argues that the Pythagoreans used numbers as symbolic placeholders for philosophical concepts that were otherwise too difficult to describe in words.</summary>

# ON THE LIFE OF PYTHAGORAS

<columns>
...teaching that these are the <term>Elements</term> <note>original Greek: "στοιχεῖα" (stoicheia). This word refers both to the letters of the alphabet and the fundamental physical or metaphysical components of the universe.</note>. 
Later, however, they teach that these characters are not the elements themselves, but through these <margin>5</margin> a mental concept is formed of the elements that exist in truth. Even geometers, being unable to represent incorporeal things in speech, resort to the drawing <margin>10</margin> of shapes, saying that "this Δ <note>The Greek letter Delta (Δ) is used here as a visual diagram of a triangle.</note> is a triangle"—not intending that this object which falls under our sight is the triangle, but rather something of such a kind; and through this <margin>15</margin> they represent the concept of the triangle. The Pythagoreans did the same regarding the primary ratios and <term>Forms</term> <note>original: "εἴδη" (eide). In Platonism and Pythagoreanism, these are the eternal, non-physical blueprints of all things.</note>. <margin>20</margin> Being unable to transmit the incorporeal forms and first principles in speech, they resorted to demonstration through numbers. And so <margin>25</margin> the principle of Unity, and of Identity and Equality, and the cause of harmony and sympathy, and of the preservation of the <margin>30</margin> whole (which remains the same and in the same state), they called "The One." For indeed, the One which exists in the individual parts is of such a nature, being united <margin>35</margin> with the parts and in harmony with them, through participation in the First Cause. But the principle of Diversity and...
<meta>catchword: it-</meta>

<column-break/>

...resort [to marks]; and they say that these are, as it were, the primary elements of teaching. Nevertheless, they afterwards teach that those marks are not the elements; but that by their aid the true elements are known. And Geometers, since they are unable to set forth incorporeal forms in words, resort to the descriptions of figures: saying that this (Δ) is a triangle; not that they wish this very thing, which falls under our sight, to be the triangle; but that which possesses a figure of that kind, and which, by the aid of this, represents the concept and knowledge of a triangle to the mind. Therefore, the Pythagoreans did the same in the primary ratios (or properties) and forms. For since they could not hand down incorporeal forms and primary principles in words, they resorted to demonstration through numbers; and thus they called the principle of Unity, Identity, and Equality—and the cause of the friendly agreement, mutual affection, and preservation of the universe (which persists according to the same and in the same manner)—"The One." For that One, which is observed in particular things, exists as such; it is united and in harmony with the parts, on account of its participation in the First Cause. [The principle] of Diversity and Inequality, however...

This is a characteristic Pythagorean etymological pun: the number ten "receives" or "contains" all other numerical ratios within it.</note>; for this reason they also say that the number ten is a "perfect" number. Or rather, it is the most perfect of all, for it encompasses within itself every difference of number, every type of ratio, and every proportion. For if the nature of the universe is defined according to the ratios and proportions of number, and if everything that comes to be, grows, and is perfected proceeds according to numerical ratios—and since the Decad contains all these ratios, all proportions, and every kind of number—how could this number ten not be called perfect? # PORPHYRY

<margin>5</margin> Such was the use of arithmetic among the Pythagoreans. Since this was a kind of primary philosophy <note>The Pythagoreans believed that numbers were the fundamental building blocks of reality, making "arithmetic" the foundation of all higher knowledge.</note>, it was for this very reason that the Pythagorean philosophy eventually became extinct. First, because it was shrouded in riddles; <margin>10</margin> second, because their own commentaries were written in the Doric dialect <note>Doric was an ancient Greek dialect associated with the Peloponnese and Southern Italy. By the time of the Roman Empire, it was considered archaic and difficult to read compared to the standard "Koine" Greek.</note>. This dialect itself possesses a certain inherent obscurity; and for this reason, the teachings recorded in that dialect could not be understood, <margin>15</margin> as if they were counterfeit and poorly understood—for those who brought these ideas before the public were not true Pythagoreans. Moreover, Plato, Aristotle, and Speusippus, <margin>20</margin> as well as Aristoxenus and Xenocrates—as the Pythagoreans claim—sold off the most useful parts [of the teachings] as their own, having changed only a few things. <margin>25</margin> However, the common and more trivial things, as well as whatever was later devised by the envious and by slanderers to subvert and ridicule the Pythagorean school, they gathered together <margin>30</margin> and left behind as the specific hallmarks of that sect. But these things happened later. Pythagoras himself, as well as his friends and associates, were for a long time held in such great admiration in Italy <margin>35</margin> that the cities even entrusted the governance of their Republics to his disciples. They...
<meta>catchword: ...entrusted</meta>

<column-break/>

...among the Pythagoreans. And because of this being the primary philosophy, it happened to be extinguished: first, because of its enigmatic nature; next, because the writings were written in the Doric dialect—the dialect having a certain lack of clarity. Because of this, the doctrines hinted at through it were not understood, appearing as if they were illegitimate and misunderstood, because those who published them were not truly Pythagoreans. In addition to these, Plato and Aristotle, Speusippus and Aristoxenus, and Xenocrates—as the Pythagoreans say—appropriated the "fruitful" <note>original Greek: "κάρπιμα" (karpima), meaning productive or profitable ideas.</note> parts for themselves after a brief study. But the superficial and light things, and whatever was later put forward by slanderers to mock and ridicule the teaching, they collected and recorded as the specific traits of the school. But these things occurred later. Pythagoras, for a long time throughout Italy, was so greatly admired—both he and the companions who were with him—that the cities even entrusted the management of their states to his followers. # ON THE LIFE OF PYTHAGORAS <note>original: "DE VITA PYTHAGORÆ"</note>

...a conspiracy was formed against them in this manner. Cylon, a man of Croton <note>Croton was a prominent Greek city in Southern Italy where Pythagoras established his school.</note> by birth, who surpassed all his fellow citizens in the glory of his ancestors and the abundance of his wealth,
<margin>5</margin> was otherwise a harsh and tyrannical man. He used the circle of his friends and the power of his wealth to gain strength for his wrongdoings. Since he deemed himself worthy of all other things that were considered noble,
<margin>10</margin> he also believed he was most deserving to share in the philosophy of Pythagoras. He approached Pythagoras, praising himself and wishing to associate with him.
<margin>15</margin> But Pythagoras, having immediately assessed the man’s character <note>original: "φυσιογνωμονήσας" (physiognomonēsas). <margin>25</margin> as if he had been insulted; for he was in other respects a difficult man and lacked control over his temper. Therefore, having gathered his friends together, he began to slander Pythagoras and
<margin>30</margin> prepared a plot against him and his associates. From that point, some say that while Pythagoras's companions were gathered in the house of Milo the athlete <note>Milo of Croton was a legendary six-time Olympic wrestling champion and a devoted follower of Pythagoras.</note>,

<margin>20</margin> explored the nature and character of the man, and had seen through the kind of disposition he had from the indicators he gathered from his physical presence,

<margin>30</margin> of an uncontrollable spirit. Therefore, having brought his friends together into one place, he began to accuse Pythagoras and prepare traps for him and his friends. And

### PORPHYRY <note>original: "PORPHYRIVS"</note>

...[Pherecydes] whom he had previously had as a teacher, he had departed for Delos, so that he might attend to him after he was seized by the "louse-disease"—which they call *Phthiriasis* <note>original: "φθειριάσεως" (phtheiriaseōs). A horrifying ancient medical condition where the body was said to be consumed by lice or similar parasites; it was often attributed to historical figures as a form of divine or poetic justice.</note>—and provide for his care.) All of them were burned, fire having been set from every side, or they were overwhelmed with stones; only two escaped the fire, Archippus and Lysis, as Neanthes reports. Of these, Lysis lived in Greece and joined himself to Epaminondas <note>A famous Theban general and statesman known for his integrity; Lysis is credited with bringing Pythagorean ethics to him.</note>, whose teacher he once was.

But Dicaearchus and more accurate authors say that Pythagoras himself was also present when those plots were laid. For Pherecydes had already passed away before Pythagoras departed from Samos. Of his close companions, forty were caught together in a certain house and surrounded by the conspirators; most of the others, however, were intercepted separately throughout the city as they attempted to reach the town center.

Pythagoras, meanwhile, after his friends were seized, was first saved at the port of Caulonia, and from there he traveled again to the Locrians <note>The inhabitants of Locri, a Greek city in southern Italy known for its strict and stable legal code.</note>. But the Locrians, having learned of his arrival, sent some of their elders to the borders of their territory to question him. --- Page 42 ---
<meta>This page continues the account of the aftermath of the "Sedition against the Pythagoreans." Following the death of Pythagoras and the destruction of his school in Metapontum and Croton, the text describes how his secret teachings were nearly lost to history.</meta>

->PORPHYRY<-

When these men were overwhelmed by this disaster, the knowledge that they had until then kept secret and hidden in their hearts perished along with them; except for certain difficult concepts which the <term>external auditors: students who were permitted to listen to the public lectures but were not yet initiated into the inner, secret circle of the school</term> recalled from memory. For indeed, no writing of Pythagoras himself existed. Furthermore, Lysis and Archippus, and whoever happened to be away from home or traveling at that time, preserved certain small sparks <note>original: "igniculos," literally "little fires"</note> of philosophy, though they were quite obscure and difficult to track down. For being now left alone, and disheartened by the grief of the crime committed against them, they were scattered in different directions, shunning human society. However, fearing that the name of philosophy might utterly perish among men, and that for this reason they might also face the anger of the gods themselves, they composed certain summary commentaries <note>original: "commentaria"</note>, collecting both the writings of the ancients and those things which they themselves remembered, which they left behind in whatever place each of them died. They instructed their sons and daughters, or their wives, not to make these available to anyone outside their own family. And so, announcing this same charge to their descendants in a private and continuous succession, they observed it for a very long time...

<meta>catchword: observed</meta>

<column-break/>

When such a disaster had overtaken these men, the science also vanished along with them, having been kept unspoken <note>original: "arrhetos," meaning "ineffable" or "not to be spoken"</note> in their breasts until that time, <margin>5</margin> with only the difficult parts being remembered by those on the outside. For there was no written work of Pythagoras. But those who escaped, Lysis <margin>10</margin> and Archippus, and as many as happened to be living abroad, saved a few sparks <note>original: "zopyra," a term for the small embers used to rekindle a fire</note> of philosophy, which were faint and hard to catch. For having been left solitary <margin>15</margin> and being despondent over what had happened, they were scattered here and there, turning away from fellowship with other men. But taking care <margin>20</margin> lest the name of philosophy should perish entirely from among men, and they should thus become hateful to the gods themselves, they composed summary notes, <margin>25</margin> collecting both the writings of the older members and the things they themselves recalled. Each left behind whatever he happened to possess when he died, <margin>30</margin> charging their sons or daughters or wives not to give them to any of those outside the household. And they preserved this for a long time, passing on the same <margin>35</margin> command to their descendants in succession. And we may take as evidence, says Nicomachus <note>Nicomachus of Gerasa was a later mathematician and Pythagorean biographer whose work Porphyry is citing here.</note>, concerning the...

But the other, Telauges, was his father's successor. His daughters were Sara and Myia. <margin>15</margin> Moreover, Theano is remembered not only as a disciple, but also as one of his daughters.

The Pythagoreans used to say that <term>unity</term> <note>original: "unitatem" / "μονάδα" (monas)</note> and <term>the one</term> <note>original: "unum" / "ἕν" (hen)</note> differ. <margin>20</margin> For unity was considered by them to be that which exists among <term>intelligible things</term> <note>original: "intellectualibus" / "νοητοῖς"; things perceived by the mind rather than the senses.</note>; but "the one" was that which exists in numbers. In the same way, "two" exists in numbers, but they called <margin>25</margin> <term>duality</term> <note>original: "dualitatem" / "δυάδα" (dyas)</note> the infinite.

Unity is taken according to equality and measure, while duality is taken according to excess and de- <margin>30</margin> fect. Therefore, the mean and the measure cannot admit of the "more" and the "less"; but excess and defect, since they proceed into infinity, admit of them. <margin>35</margin> And for this reason, they called duality itself the infinite. ### ON THE LIFE OF PYTHAGORAS.

...duality. And since they referred everything to numbers—both from the monad and the <term>dyad</term> <note>original: "dyas" / "dualitate"; the principle of two or "otherness," which the Pythagoreans associated with the material world.</note>—they called <margin>5</margin> all existing things "numbers." Number is completed by the ten. And the ten is the sum of the first four numbers, counted by us in order <note>The Pythagoreans noted that 1 + 2 + 3 + 4 = 10, creating a perfect triangle.</note>; <margin>10</margin> and because of this, they called all number the <term>Tetraktys</term> <note>original: "tetraktys"; literally "the four-fold," a sacred symbol and oath for the Pythagoreans.</note>. They said that a human being becomes better than <margin>15</margin> themselves in three ways. First, through communion with the gods. For it is necessary that one who approaches them must, at that time, separate oneself <margin>20</margin> from every vice, making oneself as like unto God as possible. Second, by doing good. For this is characteristic of God, and an imitation of the divine. Third, by <margin>25</margin> dying. For if the soul—separated to some extent from the body while the living thing lives—becomes better than itself, both in sleep through <margin>30</margin> dreams and in the trances of diseases it becomes prophetic. It is much more improved when it is completely separated from the body. ... That the Pythagoreans called the <term>Monad</term> <note>original: "monada"; the unit or principle of oneness.</note> <margin>35</margin> the principle of all things. Since they said the point was the principle of a line...

<column-break/>

...all things from unity and duality they refer back to numbers; they even called all beings "numbers." Number <margin>5</margin> is completed by the ten. Truly, the ten is composed when we count the four in order. Therefore, they called every number <margin>10</margin> the <term>Quaternary</term> <note>original: "Quaternarium"; the Latin equivalent of the Tetraktys.</note>. They affirmed that a person escapes to become better than oneself in three ways. First, indeed, by the <margin>15</margin> conversation with the Gods: for it is necessary for one approaching them to abstain from all wrongdoing at that time, likening oneself to God according <margin>20</margin> to one's strength. Second, by doing good. For this is God's own attribute, and in this one imitates God. Third and finally, by <margin>25</margin> dying. For if the soul, while this life is lived, being separated a little from the body, escapes better than itself: both in sleep through dreams, and <margin>30</margin> in the ecstasies of diseases, it begins to divine: then it surely becomes much more excellent when it is entirely separated from the body. <unclear>Thus</unclear> the Pythagoreans called <margin>35</margin> unity the principle of all things. For they said the point was the principle of a line, the line of a surface, the surface of that which is extended in three ways—that is, the body: before it...

They also abstained from beans for the same reason: because they cause flatulence and are very nourishing. But they also offer several other reasons why they abstain from eating beans.
They say that Pythagoras predicted many things, and all of them came to pass.

# ON THE LIFE OF PYTHAGORAS

...[black], and the intermediates: yellow, gray, pale, red, dark blue, <margin>5</margin> floury-white, radiant, and dusky-purple. Hearing is the judge of high-pitched and low-pitched sound. Smell is the judge of fragrant and foul odors, and the six intermediates: <margin>10</margin> those that are rotting, soaking, melting, or smoking. Taste is the judge of sweet and bitter juices, and the <margin>15</margin> five intermediates; for there are seven juices in total: sweet, bitter, sharp, pungent, <term>porous</term> <note>original: "somphos"; refers to a spongy or light texture in food.</note>, salty, and astringent. Touch is the judge of many more qualities: <margin>20</margin> such as heavy, light, and their intermediates; hot and cold, and their intermediates; rough and smooth, and their intermediates; dry and <margin>25</margin> moist, and their intermediates. And while these senses are in the head and are enclosed in individual organs, touch is diffused through the head and <margin>30</margin> through the whole body; it is common to every sense, but it provides a more evident judgment in the hands.
<margin>35</margin> He says that there are twelve orders in the heaven. The first and outermost is the un-

<column-break/>

[black], and concerning the others placed between: yellow, dark, pale, red, sky-blue, purple, glowing-red, and saffron-yellow. Hearing judges the voice high and low. Smell judges good and bad odors, and the six placed between: the rotten, the moist, the liquid, and the vaporous. Taste discerns sweet and bitter flavors, and the five intermediates. For there are seven in total: sweet, bitter, harsh, acidic, soft, salty, and austere. But touch truly judges many things: namely heavy and light, and those that come in between; hot and cold, and those lying between; hard and soft, and their intermediates; dry and moist, with their intermediates. Indeed, the remaining four senses have their station in the head alone, and are confined by their own organs. But touch is spread through the head and through the entire body; and it is common to all senses: but in the hands it exerts a more manifest power of discerning.

They say that in heaven there are twelve spheres distinguished by order. Of which the first and outermost is the <term>un-wandering</term> <note>original: "inerrabilis"; referring to the "fixed stars" which, unlike the planets, do not move relative to one another.</note> or the firmament...

# ANONYMOUS

...[of the spheres], in which the first God is, and the intellectual gods <note>original: "dij intellectuales"; these are divine beings understood through the intellect rather than the senses.</note> as Aristotle preferred, or the Ideas according to the mind of Plato. After the unerring order, the next is the star of Saturn, and then the other six planets: Jupiter, I mean, Mars, Venus, Mercury, the Sun, and the Moon. After these is the sphere of fire: then of air: then of water: and beneath them all lies the orb of the earth. In the highest of these twelve orbs, or the firmament <note>The "firmament" was believed to be the fixed, outer shell of the universe containing the stars.</note>, resides the first cause. And they affirm that whatever is near to it is firmly and most excellently composed; those things that are further away are less firm. They say a constant order is preserved all the way to the Moon: but below it, all things are moved confusedly. Hence evil also necessarily exists around the region of the earth: since the earth holds the position of the dregs <note>original: "fundi instar"; literally "like a bottom" or "base," suggesting the earth is where the heavier, less pure elements of the universe settle.</note> relative to the whole world: being the proper and most convenient receptacle for those things which sink to the bottom. And indeed they say that all other parts are governed by providence, a stable order, and fate, following the divine will. But those things which are below the moon are ruled by four causes: God, fate, our own counsel, and fortune. For example, the boarding of a ship itself...

<column-break/>

...[the un]moving sphere; in which is both the first God and the intelligible gods, as it seems to Aristotle; but according to Plato, the <margin>5</margin> Ideas. After the unmoving sphere, the star of Cronus <note>Greek name for Saturn.</note> is stationed, and the six planets in order: I mean that of Zeus <note>Jupiter</note>, that of Ares <note>Mars</note>, that of Aphrodite <note>Venus</note>, that of <margin>10</margin> Hermes <note>Mercury</note>, that of the Sun, and that of the Moon. Then the sphere of fire; next that of air; after it that of water; the earth is the last and remains over all. <margin>15</margin> Although there are twelve orders, the first cause is in the unmoving sphere. And they say that whatever is near it is ordered firmly and excellently; <margin>20</margin> but the things further away, less so. The order is preserved as far as the Moon. But the things under the Moon are no longer likewise. Out of necessity, <margin>25</margin> evil is in the place around the earth; since the earth holds the role of a base <term>pythmenos</term> <note>original: "πυθμένος"; the bottom or floor of a vessel where sediment collects.</note> to the whole world, and being the last, it is suitable for <margin>30</margin> receiving [the dregs]. And they say that all the other parts are managed according to providence, the firm order, and the fate of God which follows him; <margin>35</margin> but the things after the Moon [are managed] by four causes: by God, by fate, by the [our...]

<summary>This page describes a Greco-Roman cosmological hierarchy where the universe is organized into twelve spheres, with the earth at the bottom as a "receptacle" for imperfect things. It distinguishes between the perfect, providential government of the upper heavens and the influence of fate and fortune on the sublunary world.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the governing forces of the universe, specifically defining the roles of human choice, fortune, and providence. It completes a sentence from the previous page regarding the decision to board a ship.</meta>

# ON THE LIFE OF PYTHAGORAS.

...our own choice, and according to fortune. For example: to board a ship or not to board is within our power; however, for a storm and tempest to suddenly arise in clear weather is the result of fortune. Yet, for a sinking ship to be saved against all hope is by the providence of God. There are many modes and types of fate <note>original: "εἱμαρμένη" (heimarmene); the fixed, natural order of the universe.</note>. It differs from fortune <note>original: "τύχη" (tyche); random chance or luck.</note> in that fate possesses a sequence, order, and consequence; whereas fortune is characterized by spontaneity and whatever happens by chance. For example, to pass from childhood to youth, and to go through the successive ages of life in a natural way, is one mode of fate.

That the zodiac moves obliquely <note>original: "λοξῶς" (loxos); the sun's path (the ecliptic) is tilted relative to the earth's equator, which creates the seasons.</note>, as Aristotle seemed to discover upon investigation, is for the sake of the generation of life in the regions around the earth, to bring the whole of nature to fulfillment. For if it moved in a straight circle, there would always be only one season of the year—either summer, or winter, or some other. But now, because the sun and the other planets move from one zodiac sign to another, the four changes of the seasons occur; and from their mutual transition into one another, fruits grow and the diverse forms of living beings are established.

<meta>catchword: That</meta>

<column-break/>

...to board <note>The Latin text 'scendere' completes the word 'conscendere' from the previous page's catchword.</note>, or not, is in our power: but for gales and a tempest to suddenly arise in fair weather <margin>5</margin> depends on fortune: for a submerged ship to be preserved beyond hope, that would be by divine providence. Furthermore, there are many and diverse modes of fate. <margin>10</margin> It differs from fortune because the former has a series, order, and sequence: but fortune happens by reckless chance. <margin>15</margin> For example: to emerge from a boy into a young man, and then to pass through the other ages in order, happens by one mode of fate. The Zodiac is carried by an <margin>20</margin> oblique motion, which Aristotle, by diligent investigation, seems to have discovered, for the sake of the generation of the regions around the earth, and for the fulfillment of the <margin>25</margin> universe. For if it were moved in a straight line, there would always be one and the same season of the year, whether of summer, or winter, or some other. But now, <margin>30</margin> because the sun and other planets pass from one sign to another, four changes of the seasons occur: and from their mutual <margin>35</margin> transition, fruits come forth, and other generations of living creatures exist.

<meta>catchword: Sun</meta>

<summary>This page distinguishes between human will, random fortune, and the ordered sequence of fate, while explaining how the tilt of the Zodiac (obliquity of the ecliptic) is necessary for the four seasons and the existence of life.</summary>
<keywords>Pythagoras, Aristotle, Zodiac, Fate, Fortune, Providence, seasons, generation of life, free will, cosmology</keywords>

| ...[than] the [other] animals, even the most incomplete; but because he possesses all the powers of the cosmos. For in the cosmos there are gods, there are also the <margin>5</margin> four elements, there are irrational animals, and there are plants. Man possesses all these powers. For he has a divine power, the <margin>10</margin> rational faculty; he has the nature of the elements, the nutritive and augmentative <note>The "nutritive and augmentative" powers refer to the basic biological functions of eating and growing, which ancient philosophers believed we shared with plants.</note>, and the power to generate its like. An athlete who competes in five different events; here used as a metaphor for being a "jack of all trades, master of none."</note>, possessing all the powers, is inferior in each to those who practice only one of the sports; so also <margin>20</margin> man, possessing all the powers, falls short in each one. For we have a weaker rational power than the gods; and the [powers] of the elements are <margin>25</margin> likewise weaker than they; and our spirit <note>original: *θυμὸν* (thymon), often translated as "spiritedness" or "anger."</note> and desire are more deficient than those in irrational [creatures]; and the nutritive and augmentative <margin>30</margin> power is less than that in plants. Whence, being composed of diverse powers, we have a life difficult to manage; for each of the others is steered by <margin>35</margin> a single nature, but we are pulled in different directions by various powers. For example, sometimes we are led up by God toward... <meta>catchword: better things</meta> | ...which other animals also, however small, possess: but because he contains all the virtues <note>original: *virtutes*. In this context, it means "powers" or "capacities" rather than moral virtues.</note> of the world. For in the world there are Gods and the four elements, brute animals, and plants. All these powers man possesses: for he has a divine faculty, namely the rational one. He has the nature of the elements, surely the nutritive and growth-giving force, and the producer of its own likeness. But in each of these he is inferior: for as in the case of a **pentathlete** <note>original: *quinquertio*. The Latin term for an athlete in the five-fold contest.</note>, who possesses all virtues, yet in each is less than him who practices only one kind of contest: so also man, having all virtues, is surpassed in each one individually. For we have a lesser faculty of reason than the Gods: so also less of the elements than the elements themselves: likewise desire and anger are less in us than in the brutes. And in the quality of nourishing and growing, we are surpassed by plants. Therefore, since we are composed of various faculties, we also obtain a life difficult in its use. For all other things are ruled by one nature: but we are distracted by diverse qualities. For example, now we are drawn to better things by God... |

| ...now with the natural force prevailing toward worse things: and so too in the other powers. If anyone, therefore, cultivates that which is divine within us, and sets it over himself like a watchful and skilled **charioteer** <note>The charioteer is a famous Platonic metaphor; the rational mind must guide the unruly "horses" of emotion and desire to prevent the soul from crashing.</note>, he will be able to rightly use each of the remaining faculties—namely, the mixture of the elements, anger, desire, and appetite. And therefore that precept, ***Know thyself*** <note>original: *Nosce te ipsum*. This was one of the famous maxims inscribed on the Temple of Apollo at Delphi.</note>, although it seems very easy, is nonetheless the most difficult of all. They say it belongs to **Pythian Apollo** <note>The god of the sun, prophecy, and music at Delphi.</note>, though it is also attributed to **Chilo** <note>Chilo of Sparta (6th century BCE), a statesman and philosopher known for his brief, pithy wisdom.</note>, one of the **seven sages**. It warns us that each should know his own power and virtue. But *to know oneself* is nothing other than to know the nature of the whole world—which cannot happen unless we apply ourselves to **Philosophy** <note>original: *Philosophię*. | ...sense is false knowledge through the body; **imagination** is a movement in the soul; **art** is a productive habit accompanied by **reason**. The phrase "with reason" is added because the spider also produces, but not with reason <note>The spider's web is a common classical example used to distinguish between natural instinct and the rational, "teachable" art (τέχνη) unique to humans.</note>. **Prudence** is a habit of choice regarding the rightness of practical matters; **scientific knowledge** is a habit regarding things that are always the same and in the same state; **wisdom** is the science of first causes; **intellect** is the principle and source of all things beautiful. | ...[proper] opinion is one's own. **Sense** is a fallacious knowledge through the body; **imagination** is a movement in the soul; **art** is an active habit with <margin>5</margin> reason. It is added, however, "with reason," because even the spider works, but by no means with reason. **Prudence** is a habit choosing that which is <margin>10</margin> right in things to be done. **Science** is a habit of those things which always hold themselves in the same way. **Wisdom** is the <margin>15</margin> knowledge of first causes. **Mind** is the principle and source of all good things. |
| <column-break/> | <column-break/> |
| That there are three parts of **teachability**: **sagacity**, **memory**, and **sharpness**. Memory is the preservation of what one has learned; sharpness is the speed of the mind; sagacity is the ability to hunt for things one has not learned based on the things one has learned. | There are three parts of **teachability** <note>original: *docilitatis*. This refers to the natural aptitude or "teachableness" of a student.</note>: **sagacity**, **memory**, and **sharpness of wit**. Memory is the <margin>20</margin> guardian of those things which one has learned. Sharpness of wit is the velocity of the intellect. Sagacity is the ability to investigate, from those things which one has learned, those things which he has not learned. |
| That **heaven** is spoken of in three ways: first, the fixed sphere itself; second, the distance from the fixed sphere down to the moon; third, the entire world—that is, the heaven and the earth together. | There are three things which are called by the name of **heaven**: <margin>25</margin> first, the fixed sphere itself, or the firmament; second, that distance which is from the firmament down to the Moon; third, the entire <margin>30</margin> world—the heaven, I say, together with the earth. |
| He says that the most perfect and the worst things are disposed by nature to be always active: these are **God** and **plants**. For God is always active according to intellect and reason, as are those things closest to Him. | The most perfect and the worst things, he says, are disposed by nature toward always acting: <margin>35</margin> namely, **God** and **plants**. For God, and whatever things are nearest to Him, [are active] according to... |

<note>This final section presents a striking Pythagorean paradox: God (the most perfect) and plants (the "worst" or lowest form of life here) are both characterized by "always acting." God acts through eternal reason, while plants are in a constant state of growth and biological function, unlike animals or humans who rest or act intermittently.</note>

| ...[highest things] are, they always operate according to mind and reason. Plants also always operate: for they are nourished night and day. For they are nourished both by night and by day. But man does not always act, nor do the irrational animals. Rather, for nearly half the time, they sleep and are at rest. |
| The Greeks always excel the <margin>10</margin> barbarians <note>In this context, "barbarian" is a standard classical term for anyone who did not speak Greek or share Greek culture, rather than a purely derogatory term.</note> in their customs: because they inhabit a temperate region of the world. But the Scythians <note>An ancient people of central Eurasia, used here as the archetype of those living in extreme cold.</note> and Ethiopians, of whom the former are afflicted by cold and the latter by heat, <margin>15</margin> possess the worst temperament <note>The "temperament" (Latin: *temperamentum*; Greek: *krasis*) refers to the "mixture" of bodily humors and qualities like heat and moisture, which was believed to be dictated by the climate.</note> because the outer surface of the body is constricted and much heat and moisture is consumed within. Therefore, those who inhabit those <margin>20</margin> regions are violent and audacious. And so, in proportion to how much they are closer to the middle or the extremes, they participate in the temperament of that proximity. Therefore, as Plato testifies <margin>25</margin>, whatever discipline the Greeks receive from the barbarians, they bring it forth better; and especially the Athenians, more than all other Greeks <margin>30</margin>. Wherefore they were the first of all to apply themselves to military affairs: and they invented painting, and all mechanical and military arts, as well as the faculty of <margin>35</margin> speaking, and the mathematical sciences. Wherefore— | That the Greeks are always better in character than the barbarians, as they inhabit the temperate zone; but the Scythians and Ethiopians, some troubled by cold, others by heat, have been allotted a poor temperament because the surface of the body is thickened and much heat and moisture is trapped within. It follows, therefore, that those who inhabit those places are skilled in horsemanship <note>original: ἱππικοὺς. While the Latin translator chose "violent" (*violenti*), the Greek specifically notes their horsemanship, a trait the Greeks associated with the wilder, nomadic Scythians.</note> and are bold. Analogously, those who are nearer to the middle or the extremes participate in the mixture of those adjacent to them. Therefore, as Plato says, whatever lesson the Greeks might take even from the barbarians, they bring this out better; especially the Athenians among the other Greeks. For this reason, they also became the first generals, and the inventors of painting, and of every art, both manual and military. Furthermore, [they were masters] of words and of mathematical studies. Therefore, it is easily shaped, so to speak— |

### ON THE LIFE OF PYTHAGORAS ###

->D 4<-

...education in Athens is not something added from the outside, but exists by nature; because the air there is very strong and very pure; so as not only to thin the soil (the reason why Attica is called "thin-soiled" <note>original: λεπτόγεως (*leptogeos*), referring to the light, stony soil of the Athenian region</note>) but also to refine the souls of men. For it happens that thin air harms the earth but benefits the souls.
— Regarding the fact that the Etesian winds <note>Annual Mediterranean summer winds that blow from the north.</note> blow during the peak of summer for this reason: the sun, being higher in the sky and moving further north from the southern regions, dissolves the moisture that is in the north. When these northern waters are dissolved, they evaporate into the air; once evaporated, they become wind. From these vapors, the Etesian winds are born, arising from the melting of northern moisture. They are then carried toward the opposite southern regions. When they are carried there, they strike the highest mountains of Ethiopia; falling there in great and concentrated masses, they produce rains. From these rains, the Nile overflows in the summer, though it flows from southern and parched regions. Aristotle also investigated this matter. He himself...

<column-break/>

...[physi]cal <note>The Latin text begins with the suffix "-ca", likely completing "Physica" from the previous page.</note> nor is learning, so to speak, something adventitious <note>original: *adventitia* – meaning something imported or not inherent to the person.</note> at Athens, but it is native: since the air of that region is most thin and most pure; so that it not only <margin>5</margin> thins the earth (which is the reason why Attica is sterile) but also renders the intellects <note>original: *ingenia* – natural talent or capacity.</note> of men subtle. For thin air is accustomed <margin>10</margin> to harm the earth, but to benefit the intellects.
The Etesian winds blow at the hottest time of summer for this sort of reason. The sun, <margin>15</margin> now higher and turned more toward the north from the southern places, dissolves the moisture which is toward the north. This moisture, <margin>20</margin> once dissolved, is first changed into air, then into a breeze, from which the Etesian winds arise. They come, of course, from the vapor that originates from the dissolved <margin>25</margin> northern moisture. Therefore, they are carried toward the opposite southern regions; and having been brought there, they cling to the highest mountains of Ethiopia. When <margin>30</margin> they have been amassed and condensed there, they bring forth rains. And from these rains the Nile increases in the summer season, flowing forth from southern <margin>34</margin> and arid places. And Aristotle discovered this by investigation. For he himself...

<meta>The catchword "ex" at the bottom of the Latin column indicates the next page begins with "exposuit" or a similar word, continuing the citation of Aristotle's findings.</meta>

<summary>This page presents a classical geographical theory that links the "thin" air of Athens to the sharpness of its citizens' minds and explains the summer flooding of the Nile as a result of northern winds blowing moisture toward the Ethiopian mountains.</summary>
<keywords>Athens, Attica, intelligence and climate, Etesian winds, Nile flooding, Ethiopia, Aristotle, meteorology, Pythagoras</keywords>

--- Page 58 ---
<meta>This page concludes the argument regarding the Nile's flooding. It provides the empirical proof for Aristotle's theory—which was introduced on the previous page—by citing an expedition sent by Alexander the Great to Ethiopia to observe the summer rains firsthand.</meta>

# ANONYMOUS #

...[he] grasped this from the nature of the matter, having requested Alexander the Macedonian <note>Alexander the Great (r. 336–323 BCE). Aristotle was his childhood tutor and maintained a scientific correspondence with him during his conquests.</note> to send people to those locations, so they might witness the cause of the Nile’s rising with their own eyes. Therefore, he says, one should no longer inquire into this matter; for it is clearly evident that the river increases from rainfall. And it is most wonderful indeed that in the driest places of Ethiopia, where there is neither winter nor any water, it happens that in the summer time, the most abundant rains arise.

<column-break/>

For he observed this in fact from the nature of the thing, having requested that Alexander the Macedonian send [men] into those places, and there <margin>5</margin> observe the cause of the Nile’s rising. Therefore he says that this is no longer a problem <note>original: πρόβλημά (*problema*), a scientific question or puzzle.</note>. For it was seen clearly that it increases from rains. And the <margin>10</margin> paradox <note>original: παράδοξον (*paradoxon*), something contrary to expectation.</note> is that in the driest places of Ethiopia, in which there is neither winter nor water, it happens in the summer that the most rains occur.

PORPHYRY

<note>A rectangular woodcut banner decorates the top of the page, featuring symmetrical scrolls and floral designs.</note>

# PORPHYRY
## THE PHILOSOPHER
### STARTING POINTS
### toward the intelligible. <note>original Greek: Ἀφορμαὶ πρὸς τὰ νοητά (*Aphormai pros ta noēta*). These are concise propositions meant to guide the student toward understanding the "intelligible" world—the realm of pure thought and being above the physical world.</note>

1. Every body is in a place; but nothing that is incorporeal <note>A being that does not have a physical body, such as a soul or an intellect.</note> by nature, or anything of that sort, exists in a place.

2. Things that are incorporeal by nature, because they are superior to every body and place, are everywhere; not in a spread-out way, but indivisibly. <note>original Greek: ἀμερῶς (*amerōs*). This suggests that the soul or divine mind is not "stretched" across space, but is present in its entirety at every point.</note>

3. Things that are incorporeal by nature are not present to bodies in a local way <note>By occupying physical space.</note>; they are present to them whenever they wish, by inclining toward them as it is their nature to incline. Not being present to them locally, they are present by a relationship. <note>original Greek: σχέσει (*schesei*). This means the soul connects to the body through a specific state or "disposition" rather than by being "inside" it like a liquid in a jar.</note>

4. Things that are incorporeal by nature, in their subsistence and essence

### Sentences leading toward intelligible realities.

I. Every body is in a place: but no incorporeal being 
<margin>20</margin>
in itself, or anything of this kind, is in a place.

II. Incorporeal beings in themselves, by the very fact that they are more excellent than every body and place, 
<margin>25</margin>
are everywhere: not extensively, but indivisibly.

III. Incorporeal beings in themselves, which are not present to bodies by local presence, are present to them whenever they wish: by turning toward them,
<margin>30</margin>
specifically as they turn by a natural instinct. But since they are not present to them with a local presence, they are nonetheless present by a disposition or affection. <note>original Latin: *habitudine seu affectu*. This translates the Greek *schesei*, emphasizing a mental or spiritual connection rather than a physical one.</note>

The content focuses on the nature of incorporeal beings (like the soul), arguing they exist beyond physical space and relate to the body through "disposition" rather than location.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s "Sentences" (propositions on the nature of reality) from page 59. It concludes the fourth sentence and moves through the ninth, focusing on how the soul relates to the physical body.</meta>

<header>PORPHYRY</header>

...they are not present in bodies; for they are not mixed with them. Nevertheless, by the existence of their downward inclination <term>inclination: (Greek: *rhopē*) the tendency of a higher spiritual being to lean toward or focus on the lower material world</term>, they communicate a certain power that is close to the bodies; for this inclination establishes a different power neighboring the bodies. <note>original Latin: *propinquam corporibus*; original Greek: *προσεχοῖς τοῖς σώμασιν*. This describes a "bridge" or "link" between the spiritual and physical realms.</note>

**V.** The Soul is a certain middle ground between indivisible substance <note>something that cannot be broken into parts, like a thought or a mathematical point</note> and the substance that is divided among bodies; but the Intellect <term>Intellect: (Latin: *mens*, Greek: *nous*) in Neoplatonism, the divine or higher mind that perceives truth directly, above the level of logical reasoning</term> is an indivisible substance alone. However, qualities and material forms are divisible according to the bodies they inhabit.

**VI.** Whatever acts upon something else does not do so through physical proximity or touch. However, even those things that act through proximity and touch only use that proximity by accident. <note>original Latin: *per accidens*. In philosophy, this means that while touch might happen, it is not the actual cause or essential reason the action occurs.</note>

**VII.** The soul is bound to the body by turning itself toward the passions <term>passions: (Greek: *pathe*) not just emotions, but any "suffering" or impression received by the body, such as hunger or pain</term> of the body. Conversely, it is released again when it is no longer affected by any attachment to the body.

**VIII.** What nature has bound together, nature also loosens; and what the soul has bound, the soul itself loosens. Nature indeed bound the body to the soul <note>Note that the original text says nature bound the body *in* the soul, suggesting the soul is the larger container for the body, not the other way around.</note>; but the soul bound itself to the body. Therefore, nature releases the body from the soul; but the soul releases itself from the body.

**IX.** Death is of two kinds: one indeed...

<column-break/>

...surpasses in essence; for they are not mixed with bodies. But by the subsistence derived from their downward inclination, they share a power with things close to the bodies; for the inclination established another power neighboring the bodies.

**5.** The Soul is a middle point between the indivisible essence and the essence that is divided among bodies; but the Intellect is an indivisible essence only. However, qualities and the forms inherent in matter are divided among bodies.

**6.** That which acts upon another does not act by approaching or by touch; but even those things that act by approach and touch use that approach only as a side-effect.

**7.** The soul is tied to the body by the act of turning toward the experiences that come from it. And it is released again through the state of being unaffected by it.

**8.** What nature has bound, nature also looses; and what the soul has bound, the soul itself looses. Nature bound the body in the soul; but the soul bound itself in the body. Therefore, nature looses the body from the soul; but the soul looses itself from the body.

**9.** Death is of two kinds.

<meta>catchword: qui- (Latin: "one indeed...")</meta>

<summary>This page presents Porphyry's Neoplatonic arguments on the soul's independence from the body, defining the soul as a middle ground between the divine intellect and physical matter. It introduces the concept of two types of death: the natural separation of the body from the soul and the philosophical separation of the soul from physical desires.</summary>

# SENTENCES

->twofold: one is commonly known, where the body is released from the soul; the other is the way of philosophers, where the soul is released from the body; and it is not always the case that one follows from the other.<-

**X.** We do not perceive in the same way in all things, but rather according to the specific nature of each essence. In the intellect, we perceive intellectually; in the soul, rationally; in plants, through their generative seeds; in bodies, through images; and in that which is beyond all things, we perceive beyond the capacity for fertility and beyond essence itself. <note>original Greek: *ὑπερουσίως*; original Latin: *superessentialiter*. In Neoplatonic thought, "super-essential" refers to the "One" or the highest divinity, which exists on a level so high it cannot even be called "being" in the human sense.</note>

**XI.** Incorporeal <term>incorporeal: existing without a physical body, such as a soul, a mind, or a mathematical truth</term> substances, as they descend, are divided and multiplied into individual things as their power decreases; but as they ascend, they are unified and return once more to a state of togetherness through an abundance of power.

**XII.** Ambiguity <note>original Greek: *ὁμώνυμον*; original Latin: *eiusdem nominis*. This refers to words that have multiple meanings depending on the context.</note> exists not only in bodies, but life is also spoken of in many ways. For there is one life for a plant, another for a creature with a soul, another for an intellectual being, and another for the nature beyond; for that higher nature is also intellectual. For that [higher nature] also lives, even if nothing that exists because of it possesses a life similar to its own.

**XIII.** Everything that generates by its own essence generates something inferior to itself; and everything that is generated

<column-break/>

<margin>5</margin> ...twofold: one is indeed equally known to all, where the body is released from the soul; the other is truly that of the philosophers, when the soul is released from the body: nor does the one always follow the other.

**X.** We do not understand in the same way in all things, but according to the specific substance of each: for in the intellect,
<margin>10</margin> it is done intellectually; in the soul, rationally; but in plants, through their seeds; in bodies, through images; and in that which is above all these, it is above and beyond
<margin>15</margin> the intellect and super-essentially.

**XI.** Incorporeal substances, when descending, are divided and multiplied with a lessening of their powers among individual
<margin>20</margin> things. But when ascending, they are unified and come together into one, through an abundance of power or faculty.

**XII.** Not only in bodies is there
<margin>25</margin> an ambiguity of the same name: but life also is among those things which are accepted in various meanings. For one is the life of a plant, another of a living creature, another of an
<margin>30</margin> intellectual essence, and another of a higher nature: one is of the soul, another of the intellect. For those things also live, even if none of those things that exist from them
<margin>34</margin> can obtain a life similar to theirs.

# PORPHYRY'S WORKS

->ii. O<-

...then by its nature it turns back toward its progenitor. Of those things which generate, however, some do not turn at all toward the things they have produced; others turn in part, and in part do not; while others are turned only toward what they have produced, but do not turn back toward themselves.

**XIV.** Everything that is begotten <term>begotten: in this context, something that is brought into existence or "generated" by a higher cause</term> has the cause of its generation from another; for indeed, nothing is produced without a cause. Furthermore, whatever generated things possess their essence <term>essence: the fundamental nature or "being" of a thing</term> through composition <term>composition: being made up of different parts joined together</term> are, for that very reason, corruptible <note>original Latin: *corruptibilia*. In ancient philosophy, this means something that can break down, decay, or cease to exist because its parts can be separated.</note>. However, whatever is simple and non-composite, having its essence in a simple subsistence <term>subsistence: a state of independent existence</term>, are both indissoluble and incorruptible. These things are called "begotten" not because they are composite, but because they depend on some cause. Therefore, bodies are begotten in a twofold sense: both insofar as they depend on a producing cause, and insofar as they are composite. But the soul and the intellect are begotten only insofar as they depend on a cause, and not as composite things. For this reason, bodies are indeed begotten, dissolvable, and corruptible; but these [soul and intellect] are unbegotten <note>original Latin: *ingenita*. Here, it means they are not "made" or "assembled" from pre-existing parts, even if they have a divine source.</note> in the sense that they are non-composite, and therefore also indissoluble and in-

<column-break/>

...what is begotten <note>original Greek: *γεννηθὲν*</note> naturally turns back toward the begetter. But among those things that have begotten, some do not turn at all toward what is begotten; others both turn and do not turn; while others are only turned toward their offspring and do not turn back toward themselves.

**14.** Everything begotten has the cause of its generation from another. For nothing happens without a cause. But of those things that are begotten: those that possess their being through composition, these would be, for this reason, corruptible. But those that are simple and non-composite, possessing their being in the simplicity of their existence, being indissoluble, are alone incorruptible. They are called begotten, not by being composite, but by being dependent upon some cause. Therefore, while bodies are begotten in two ways—both as dependent on the cause that produces them and as being composite—the soul and the intellect are begotten only as being dependent on a cause. They are certainly not composite. Therefore, while bodies are begotten, dissolvable, and corruptible; these [soul and intellect] are unbegotten in the sense that they are non-composite, and in this way also indissoluble and incorruptible; yet they are begotten in the sense that they are dependent on a cause.

<summary>This page outlines the Neoplatonic theory of "conversion," where created things naturally orient themselves back toward their source, and distinguishes between physical bodies (which are composite and perishable) and the soul or intellect (which are simple and eternal).</summary>

# SENTENCES

<unclear>...</unclear>
<unclear>...</unclear>
The Intellect is not the first principle of all things; for the Intellect is a multiplicity. Before the many, it is necessary for there to be the One. It is clear that the Intellect is many; for its thoughts are always not one, but many, and they are not distinct from the Intellect itself. If, therefore, being identical to them, the thoughts are many, the Intellect itself must also be many. That it is identical to the objects of thought <note>original: *noētois*. In Neoplatonic philosophy, these are the "intelligibles"—the pure ideas or forms that the mind grasps directly.</note> should be considered in this way: If there is something that contemplates, it will contemplate this either as contained within itself or as situated within another. And that it does contemplate is clear; for it is itself the act of thinking, with which existence also co-exists. Indeed, if you were to take away the Intellect, you would take away its essence. If, then, we examine what happens regarding forms of knowledge, we must trace the nature of their contemplation. The cognitive powers within us are gathered together as: sensation, imagination, and intellect. In this way, whatever uses sensation to grasp external things contemplates them without being united with what it contemplates, but instead receiving only an impression <note>original: *typon*. Like a seal pressed into wax, the sense receives the shape or "type" of the object without the object itself entering the mind.</note> of them through contact with them. Thus, when the eye sees the visible object, it is impossible for it to become identical with the object seen. For if it were already...

<column-break/>

corruptible: but "begotten" <note>original: *genita*. Referring to things that have been brought into being by a higher cause.</note> insofar as they are dependent on a cause.

**XV.** The Intellect <term>Intellect: the higher Mind (*Mens* or *Nous*), which in this system is the second highest reality after the One</term> is not the principle of all things; for the Intellect is many; yet before the many, it is necessary that there be the One. But that the Intellect is many is evident. For it always understands the concepts of the mind, which are not one but many; nor are they other than the mind itself. Therefore, if the mind is one and the same with them, and they are many, then the Intellect itself will also be many. Furthermore, that the Intellect is the same as those things which it understands is shown thus: If there is anything that contemplates, it will contemplate it either in itself or placed in another. That it does contemplate is clear; for while it understands, it will certainly be the intellect. But if you take away the intellect, you take away also its essence. Therefore, having observed those things which are accustomed to happen concerning types of knowledge, it is necessary to investigate the contemplation of the intellect. Indeed, the powers of knowing in us are generally: sense, imagination, and intellect. Whatever uses the sense to reach external things contemplates them not as if united with the things it contemplates, but only receiving an image from contact with them. Therefore, when the eye sees a visible object...

<meta>vifi- (continuing to the Latin word 'visibile' on the next page)</meta>

<summary>Porphyry argues that the Intellect (Mind) cannot be the ultimate first principle because it contains a multiplicity of thoughts, whereas the true first principle must be the One. He also distinguishes between sensory knowledge, which only receives an "impression" of external objects, and intellectual knowledge, where the mind is identical to its objects of thought.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of perception and cognition from Proposition XIV. It maintains the bilingual format of the original manuscript, with the Latin translation and the original Greek text presented side-by-side. The text explores why physical senses must move outward while the mind must look inward.</meta>

# PORPHYRY

## SENTENCES

<column-break/>

[...it would not see the] visible, for the visible can in no way be one and the same as that which sees it. Indeed, it would not perceive it unless it stood at a certain distance. Furthermore, that which is touched, if it were to become identical with the thing touching it, would perish <note>Porphyry argues that physical perception requires a gap between the subject and the object; if they merged entirely, the act of "sensing" would vanish into "being."</note>. From this it is clear that sensation itself, and the faculty that employs sensation, are carried toward external things whenever they wish to perceive a sensible object.

In a similar way, the imagination is also always carried toward external things, and through its stretching out, an image <note>original: *simulacrum*. A mental "double" or representation of a physical thing.</note> comes into existence; or rather, the very extension toward the exterior prepares the image. The comprehension of these faculties is of such a kind that neither of them, if turned back into itself or gathered within itself, can perceive a sensible or even an immaterial <note>original: *insensibilem*. Referring here to forms that exist beyond the reach of physical organs.</note> form.

In the mind <note>original: *mente*. Here referring to the higher Intellect or *Nous*.</note>, however, comprehension does not happen in that way. Instead, it occurs when the mind is turned into itself and considers itself. For if it goes out beyond itself to consider its own functions—acting like an eye toward its own operations and other substances—it will understand nothing at all.

Therefore, as the senses are related to the sensible object, so the intellect is related to the intelligible. But while the sense contemplates by extending itself toward the exterior, finding the sensible object positioned in matter, the intellect is gathered into itself...

<column-break/>

[...it] would be necessary, if it were not in a state of distance. Likewise, that which is touched, if it came into a state of identity <note>original: *tautotēti*. Literally "sameness."</note>, would be destroyed. From these things it is clear that sensation, and that which makes use of sensation, is always carried toward the outside if it is to grasp the sensible object.

Similarly, the imagination is always carried toward the outside, and by its tension the image <note>original: *eikonisma*. A likeness or "icon" in the mind.</note> is established, or it even projects it; either the imagination itself, through its tension toward the outside, displays the image as if it were something existing outside.

And the comprehension of these is of this kind: none of them, by leaning toward itself or being gathered into itself, could attain either a sensible or an invisible <note>original: *anaidētou*. Literally "formless" or "unseen," referring to the intelligible realm.</note> form.

But in the case of the intellect, comprehension is of such a manner: it happens by the intellect leaning toward itself and contemplating itself. For having gone out from the contemplation of its own activities—becoming an eye, as it were, directed toward other substances—it would perceive nothing.

Therefore, just as sensation stands to the sensible, so the intellect <note>original: *nous*.</note> stands to the intelligible. Sensation contemplates by being extended toward the outside, finding the sensible situated in matter; but the intellect is gathered into itself. But those...

<meta>catchwords: nor, not</meta>

<summary>Porphyry distinguishes between sensory perception, which requires looking outward at a distance, and intellectual understanding, which requires the mind to look inward at itself.</summary>
<keywords>Porphyry, Neoplatonism, sensation, intellect, imagination, intelligible, sensible, self-contemplation, perception</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical investigation into the nature of the Intellect (the higher mind) versus the physical senses and imagination. Having established that sensation looks outward, Porphyry now argues that the Intellect must look inward to find Truth, eventually leading to the Neoplatonic concept of "The One."</meta>

# PORPHYRY

## SENTENCES

<column-break/>

[The Mind] does not stretch outward; indeed, this is what it appeared to do, even though a distinction of names was added between the subsistence of the intellect <note>original: "νοῦ" (*nous*). In Neoplatonism, the Intellect is the first emanation from the One, containing all the Forms.</note> and that of the imagination <note>original: "φαντασίας" (*phantasias*). The faculty that creates mental images of physical things.</note>. For among certain philosophers, imagination in a rational being was considered to be thought. 

But if they indeed derive all things from matter and bodily nature, it followed that they would also hang the mind from these physical things. Yet our intellect is a contemplator of bodies and other substances alike. Where, then, will the mind find those substances situated? If they exist outside of matter, they would be nowhere in a physical sense. It is clear, then, that intellectual things will be brought together with the mind and with the act of thinking. 

And therefore, if they are intellectual, the mind will contemplate the intelligible <note>original: "νοητόν" (*noeton*). Objects that can only be grasped by the mind, not the senses.</note> and itself, while thinking of these intelligibles; and withdrawing into itself, it thinks by the very act of entering into those things. But if the intelligibles are many—for the intellect thinks of many things, and not just one—then the intellect itself would necessarily be many. Yet the One <note>original: "τὸ ἕν" (*to hen*). The supreme, simple source of all existence in Porphyry’s philosophy.</note> lies before the many; thus, it is necessary that the One exists before the mind.

XVI. Memory is not a preservation of images, but is rather the ability to bring forth anew the matters that had previously been meditated upon.

<meta>Catchword: XVI.</meta>

<column-break/>

[The Mind] contemplates, not diffused toward external things. As it also seemed to some, the name of "imagination" was attributed to the subsistence of the intellect, differing only in name. 
<margin>5</margin> 
For they judged the imagination of a rational animal to be the intellect. But if these thinkers indeed wish 
<margin>10</margin> 
all things to depend on matter and corporeal nature, it follows that they refer the mind to the same; yet our intellect contemplates both bodies and 
<margin>15</margin> 
other substances. Therefore, it will grasp them as being placed in some location. But if they are outside of matter, this could in no way happen. It is manifest, therefore, that 
<margin>20</margin> 
intellectual things are to be joined with the intellect: because if intellectual things are in the intellect, it will contemplate both the intellectual thing and itself 
<margin>25</margin> 
while it understands the intellectual things; and receding into itself, it understands, because it enters into those things. But if there are many intellectual things—since 
<margin>30</margin> 
the intellect understands many, not one—necessarily it too will be many. But before the many is the one: so that necessarily the One exists before the mind.

XVI. Memory is not a 
<margin>35</margin> 
preserver of images: but is that which allows you to be able to bring forth again those things which you had meditated upon in your soul.

E
<meta>Catchword: XVII.</meta>

<summary>Porphyry argues that the Intellect must be internal and self-contemplating rather than sensory or material; he concludes that because the Intellect contains many truths, there must be a singular 'One' that precedes it. He also redefines memory as the recall of meditation rather than just a storage of mental pictures.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s "Sentences" (Starting Points for the Intelligibles). While the previous page distinguished the pure Intellect from the imagination, this page explores how the Soul acts as an intermediary, containing the rational blueprints of all things and functioning differently than physical matter.</meta>

# PORPHYRY

->**SENTENCES**<-

XVII. The soul indeed contains the principles <note>original: "rationes" (Latin) / "logoi" (Greek). In Neoplatonism, these are the formative rational powers or "seeds" that allow the soul to understand and shape the world.</note> of all things; it acts according to them, either being called into action by something else, or turning itself inward toward them. When provoked by an external object, it sends out the senses toward the outside. But when it enters into itself toward the mind, it is occupied with thoughts <note>original: "intelligentiis" / "noeses". Refers to the activity of the higher intellect.</note>. Therefore, one might say that neither are the senses outside of the imagination, nor are the thoughts. Just as in a living creature the senses do not function without an affection <note>original: "passione" / "pathous". In this context, it means being acted upon or receiving an impression from the outside.</note> of the sensory organs, so too thoughts do not occur without the imagination <note>original: "phantasia". The faculty that produces mental images.</note>. This is true unless a certain proportion is maintained; so that just as a physical impression or figure follows a sensing animal, so a mental image follows the intellect of the soul in a living being.

XVIII. The soul is an essence <note>original: "essentia" / "ousia". The fundamental "what-ness" or substance of a thing.</note> devoid of size <note>original: "magnitudinis" / "amegethes". Meaning it is non-spatial and takes up no room.</note> and matter, possessing its own existence in a life that is vital through itself.

XIX. Bodies are affected <note>original: "patiuntur" / "paschein". To undergo a change or be acted upon.</note> in one way, and incorporeal things in another. The affection of bodies occurs through change; but the affections and passions of the soul are certain acts or operations <note>original: "actus sive operationes" / "energeiai". Porphyry argues that when a soul "feels," it is actually performing an internal action, unlike a body which is merely altered by outside forces.</note>. They are in no way similar to the heating or cooling of physical bodies. For this reason, if the affection of bodies...
<meta>Latin catchword: passio (passion/suffering)</meta>

<column-break/>

<margin>1</margin> 17. The soul possesses the principles of all things, and it acts according to them, either being called out into activity <margin>5</margin> by something else, or turning itself back into these principles toward the interior. And when called out by another, it allows the senses to be led toward the external. <margin>10</margin> But when entering into itself, it comes to be among thoughts. Therefore, one might say that neither are the senses outside of the imagination, nor are the thoughts; <margin>15</margin> just as in the animal, the senses do not exist without the affection of the sensory organs, so too thoughts do not exist without the imagination. Unless there is a proportion: <margin>20</margin> as the impression is a consequence of the sensing animal, so the mental image of the soul of the animal follows the act of thinking.

18. The soul is an essence <margin>25</margin> without size, immaterial, and incorruptible, possessing life from itself in a life that has gained existence.

19. The "suffering" <note>original: "paschein". This refers to the philosophical concept of 'passion'—the state of being acted upon by an external force.</note> of bodies is one thing, <margin>30</margin> and that of incorporeal things is another. For in the case of bodies, suffering occurs with change. But for the soul, its adaptations and passions are activities <margin>35</margin>; they are not like the heating and cooling of bodies. Therefore, if the suffering of bodies...
<meta>Greek catchword: των (of the)</meta>

<summary>Porphyry defines the soul as an immaterial essence that contains the rational principles of all things, acting either outwardly through the senses or inwardly through thought. He distinguishes between the "passions" (changes) of physical bodies and the "passions" (internal activities) of the soul.</summary>
