<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s "Sentences," completing the discussion from the previous page regarding how the soul remains unaffected by physical sensations. It introduces a famous Neoplatonic analogy comparing the soul to a musician and the body to a musical instrument.</meta>

# PORPHYRY

->**SENTENCES**<-

<column-break/>

...ection occurs with change, it must be said that all incorporeal things are exempt from affection <note>original: "passio" (Latin) / "pathos" (Greek). In this context, it refers to the state of being acted upon or undergoing a physical change.</note>. For those things which are separated from matter and bodies are, in their activity, the same as their essence. But those things that draw near to matter and bodies are themselves impassible <note>Meaning they cannot be harmed or changed in their fundamental nature.</note>; yet those subjects in which they are perceived do suffer. 

<margin>10</margin> For when a living creature perceives, the soul seems to be like a separate harmony <note>original: "harmoniae separatae". An abstract mathematical or musical order that exists even if no instrument is playing.</note>, moving the strings—which have been appropriately tuned—by its own power. The body, however, is like the harmony that is inseparable from the strings themselves. 

<margin>15</margin> The cause of movement appears to be the living creature itself, because it is endowed with a soul. It is also somewhat similar to a musician, because the creature itself possesses a certain internal harmony. 

<margin>20</margin> But the physical bodies, moved by sensory affection, are like strings that have been correctly tuned. For even in that case, the separate harmony suffers nothing, but the string does. 

<margin>25</margin> And the musician indeed moves the instrument according to the harmony he possesses; yet the string would not be moved in a musical fashion, even if the musician willed it, unless the harmony of the instrument itself allowed for it.

<margin>30</margin> XX. The name "incorporeal" <note>original: "incorporeorum". Entities like the soul, the mind, or mathematical truths that do not have physical mass.</note> is not applied according to a shared commonality of one and the same genus <note>A "genus" is a general category. Porphyry is arguing that "incorporeal" isn't a type of "thing," but rather a description of what a thing is NOT.</note>, in the way that "bodies" are named. Instead, it is named according to a bare privation <note>original: "privationem" / "steresin". A definition defined by the absence of a quality—in this case, the absence of a body.</note> in respect to bodies.

<sig>E 2</sig>
<meta>catchword: cor-</meta>

<summary>Porphyry concludes that the soul remains "impassible" (unchanged) even when the body feels sensations, using the analogy of a musician (soul) playing a stringed instrument (body). He also notes that "incorporeal" is defined by what it is not—the absence of physical body—rather than being a category of its own.</summary>

<keywords>incorporeal, soul as musician, harmony, impassibility, affection, body vs soul, Neoplatonism, Porphyry</keywords>

<margin>5</margin>...of bodies. Wherefore there is nothing to prevent some of them from being beings, and others non-beings: likewise, some exist before bodies, and others after bodies: furthermore, some are separated from bodies, and others by no means separated: then, some are self-subsisting, while others are in need of external help <margin>10</margin> for their existence: likewise, some are the same as their own activities and lives, which possess movement from within themselves, while others exist alongside certain lives and specific activities. <margin>15</margin> For they are named according to the negation of those things which they are not, not according to the affirmation of those things which they are.

XXII. The properties of Matter <note>original: "materia" (Latin) / "ὕλη" (Greek). In Neoplatonic thought, Matter is the ultimate "receptacle," the bottom-most layer of reality that has no qualities of its own until form is impressed upon it.</note> <margin>20</margin> according to the ancients are these: that it is incorporeal <note>Because it is the substrate that underlies bodies, it cannot itself be a body.</note>, for it is different from bodies. That it is devoid of life; for it is neither intellect, nor soul, nor anything living through itself. <margin>25</margin> That it is formless, variable, infinite, and powerless. Therefore it is not even a "being," but "non-being"; yet not "non-being" in the same way that movement is <note>Movement is considered non-being because it is a lack of rest, but Matter is "true non-being" because it lacks all inherent reality.</note>, but it is true non-being. <margin>30</margin> A semblance and a phantom of bulk <note>original: "molis" (Latin) / "ὄγκου" (Greek), referring to physical volume or mass.</note>: because it is that which primarily underlies bulk: powerless, and a yearning for existence; consisting not in a state of rest, and always appearing as its own opposite within <margin>35</margin> itself: small and great, likewise less and more: deficient and exceeding:
which

<column-break/>

...as he says, whence some of them are beings, and others non-beings; it is not prohibited; and some exist before bodies, and others after bodies; and some are separate from bodies, and others inseparable; and some are self-subsisting, while others are in need of others for their existence; and some are the same as their activities and self-moving lives, while others are supported by specific lives and activities. For they are named by the negation of what they are not, and not by the assertion of what they are.

22. The properties of matter according to the ancients are these: incorporeal, for it is different from bodies. Life-less, for it is neither intellect, nor soul, nor anything living by itself; formless, variable, infinite, powerless. Therefore it is not even being, but non-being. Not non-being in the way that movement is, but a true non-being. An image and a phantom of mass. Because it is that which is primarily within mass, the powerless, and a longing for existence, and staying not in a state of rest; and it is that which is always appearing as a contradiction within itself; small and great; and less and more; falling short and exceeding; always becoming, and never staying, nor

<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s Sententiae, specifically the conclusion of Sentence 22 and the beginning of Sentence 23. It continues the metaphysical investigation into the nature of Matter (Hyle) and how "passions" (the state of being acted upon) relate to physical destruction. The text remains in a parallel Greek and Latin format, which I have translated into English while preserving the original layout.</meta>

# SENTENCES:

...always able to diminish <note>original: "ἀεὶ φθίνειν δυνάμενον"</note>; alien to all being; on which account everything it promises, it lies; and even if it be imagined as great, it is small; for it is like a plaything <note>original: "παίγνιόν". Porphyry describes matter as a deceptive "toy" because it mimics reality without possessing any substance of its own.</note>, fleeing into non-being; for the flight is not in place, but by the abandonment of being; wherefore even the images in it are in a worse image <note>A "worse image" because matter is already a phantom; the forms reflected in it are thus shadows of shadows.</note>; just as in a mirror that which is situated elsewhere, appears elsewhere; and being filled, as it seems, and having nothing, and appearing to be all things.

XXIII. The passions <note>original: "Τὰ πάθη" (Greek) / "passiones" (Latin). In this context, "passions" refers to "affections" or the capacity of a thing to be acted upon or changed by an external force, rather than human emotions.</note> all concern the same thing, around which destruction also exists; for the road to destruction is the reception of passion; and to be destroyed belongs to that to which it also belongs to suffer <note>To "suffer" here means to undergo a change or influence.</note>. But nothing incorporeal is destroyed; some of them either are or are not, so that they suffer nothing; for that which suffers must not be such a thing, but such as to be altered and destroyed by the qualities of things entering in and producing the suffering. For to that which is within, alteration comes from any chance thing. Neither, therefore, does Matter suffer; for it is without quality according to itself; nor do the forms entering into it and departing from it; but the whole <note>The "whole" refers to the composite of matter and form—the physical object itself.</note>.

<column-break/>

...which is always becoming, nor ever remains, nor yet can it escape or slip away: the defect of all being. Therefore whatever it <margin>5</margin> promises, it lies: and if it seems to be great, it is small. For like a playful thing it flees and vanishes into non-being. For its flight does not happen in space, but while it ceases to <margin>10</margin> be. For which reason the images which are in it are in a worse image: just as in a mirror a thing placed elsewhere appears elsewhere. And it is filled, as indeed it <margin>15</margin> seems, yet having nothing, though it seems to have all things.

XXIII. All passions <margin>20</margin> revolve around the same thing, around which also destruction turns. For the path to destruction is the taking on of passion. And it is for the same thing to perish as it is to suffer. But nothing incor- <margin>25</margin> poreal perishes. Some of those things either are or are not, so that they suffer nothing at all. For whatever suffers ought not to be of that kind; but such <margin>30</margin> that it can be altered and corrupted by the qualities of things entering and bringing about passion. For that which is in it is not altered by just anything. <margin>35</margin> Wherefore neither does matter suffer: for according to itself it is devoid of quality: nor do the forms...

<sig>E 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: re-</meta>

<summary>Porphyry concludes that Matter is a deceptive "plaything" that mimics reality without having any, and argues that only composite physical objects can "suffer" or be destroyed, while pure Matter and incorporeal forms remain impassive.</summary>

<meta>This page concludes Sentence 23 and begins Sentence 24 of Porphyry's "Sententiae" (Thoughts). It continues the investigation into why the soul is immortal and "impassible" (unable to be harmed or changed by external forces) compared to the physical body. The text is presented in parallel columns of Latin and Greek.</meta>

-># OF PORPHYRY<-

<column-break/>

...of the things entering into it and departing from it. Rather, "passion" <note>original: "passio" (Latin) / "πάθος" (Greek). In this philosophical context, "passion" refers to "undergoing" or being acted upon by an external force, rather than human emotion.</note> concerns that which is composed of both [matter and form], and whose existence consists in the union of the two. For it is in this composite <note>original: "συναμφότερον" (sunamphoteron). This refers to the "both-together" state of the body and soul combined.</note> that the contrary powers and qualities, which enter into matter and induce these passions, are seen to be sustained. 

Therefore, those things to which life comes from the outside, rather than from their own nature, are able to suffer both living and not living. But for those whose essence consists in a life free from being acted upon, it is necessary for them to remain consistently in life. Just as for those things lacking life, insofar as they lack life, a state of "impassibility" <note>The quality of being unable to suffer or be changed by external influence.</note> necessarily belongs to them. 

Therefore, to be changed and to suffer belongs to the composite of matter and form—that is, the body—but by no means to matter itself. Likewise, living and dying, and consequently being acted upon, is seen in the composite of body and soul. This does not happen to the soul itself, since it is not something forged out of non-life and life, but consists of life alone; and this is so because its essence is simple, and the nature of the soul is moved by its own motion <note>Porphyry follows the Platonic definition of the soul as "self-moving," meaning it is the source of its own activity and cannot "die" or be "moved" into non-existence by something else.</note>.

### XXIV.
Intellectual substance <note>The realm of pure Mind or Intellect (the "Nous").</note> is so constituted that all its parts are similar to one another, so that beings exist both in the particular intellect and in the universal intellect. But in...

<meta>catchword: in-</meta>

<column-break/>

...passion <note>original: "πάθος"</note> concerns the composite, and that for which existence is found in the union of the two; for in this, through the contrary powers and incoming qualities that produce suffering, the undergoing of passion is observed. Therefore, for those to whom life is from the outside, and not from their own nature, these are capable of undergoing living and not living. 

But for those whose being is in a life free from passion, it is necessary that they remain as life; just as for "non-life," the state of not suffering belongs to it insofar as it is non-life. Thus, just as being turned and suffering is in the composite of matter and form, which was the body—though this does not happen to the matter itself—so also living and dying, and suffering in this regard, is observed in the composite of soul and body. 

Yet this does not happen to the soul; because it is not a thing composed of non-life and life, but of life alone; and this is because it is a simple essence, and the principle of the soul is self-moving.

### 24.
Intellectual substance is composed of parts similar to one another, so that beings exist in the particular intellect as well as in the universal intellect; but in the universal, the...

<meta>catchword: particular</meta>

<summary>Porphyry argues that "suffering" or "change" only affects composites (like the body), while the soul, being a simple and self-moving essence of "life alone," remains unaffected by death or external passion. He also begins to define the "Intellectual substance" as a realm where the part and the whole share the same nature.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s "Sententiae" (Sentences or Thoughts on Intelligibles). It concludes Sentence 24, which discusses the relationship between universal and particular intellects, and moves into discussions on the nature of life in incorporeal beings, the process of emanation, and the limits of human thought when contemplating the divine.</meta>

-># SENTENCES<-

<column-break/>

...in the universal intellect, particulars also exist universally; but in the particular intellect, both universals and particulars exist particularly. <note>original: "μερικὰ καθολικῶς... ἐν δὲ τῷ μερικῷ, καὶ τὰ καθόλου καὶ μερικὰ μερικῶς." This explores the Neoplatonic idea that everything is in everything, but according to the nature of the container.</note>

**XXV.** For any essence whose being consists in life, and whose "passions" <note>original: "πάθη" (pathē) / "passiones." In this philosophical context, "passions" refers to "undergoings" or the ways an essence is acted upon, rather than human emotions.</note> are themselves forms of life, for such an essence even death consists in a certain kind of life. It does not consist in the absolute deprivation of life, because the "lack of life" <note>original: "ἀζωΐα" (azōia) / "vitæ vacuitas."</note> found within it is not a "passion" or a path that leads entirely toward non-living.

**XXVI.** In the case of incorporeal <note>Non-physical or spiritual.</note> lives, the progressions <note>original: "πρόοδοι" (proodoi) / "progressus." In Neoplatonism, this refers to "emanation," the way reality flows out from a higher source to a lower one.</note> or emanations occur while the prior beings remain firm and immovable. They do not lose any part of themselves or change in the production of those things beneath them. Thus, the things produced are not created through any corruption or change. Nor does anything among them exist as a "becoming" <note>original: "γινετὸν" / "generatio." This refers to physical birth or growth, which always involves change and eventual decay.</note> that participates in decay and change. Therefore, they are unbegotten and incorruptible, and have been produced in an unbegotten and incorruptible manner.

**XXVII.** Concerning that which is beyond the Mind <note>original: "ἐπέκεινα νοῦ" (epekeina nou) / "supra mentem." This refers to the "One," the highest principle in Porphyry's philosophy, which is beyond even divine thought.</note>, many things are indeed said through the intellect. However, it is perceived much more correctly through the stillness and vacation of the intellect <note>original: "ἀνοησίᾳ" (anoēsia) / "otio & vacatione intellectus." A state of mystical "un-thinking" or intellectual silence.</note> than by active thinking. Just as in the case of one who sleeps, through...

<column-break/>

<note>This column contains the Latin translation of the Greek text provided in the left column.</note>

...in the universal intellect, particulars also exist universally; in the particular, however, both universals and particulars exist particularly.

**XXV.** For any essence whose being consists in life, and whose passions are lives; for that same essence, death also consists in a certain kind of life: not in the total deprivation of life. For even the void of life which is in it is not a passion or a way by which it entirely ceases to live.

**XXVI.** In incorporeal lives, progressions or emanations occur while the prior causes remain firm and immovable, without losing anything of their own or changing for the production of lower things. Thus, the things produced are not produced with any corruption or change. Nor does any of them exist in the same way as generation joined with corruption and change. They are therefore unbegotten and incorruptible: and accordingly produced without generation or corruption.

**XXVII.** Concerning that which is above the mind, many things are indeed said through the intelligence. But it is much more rightly perceived by the stillness and vacation of the intellect than by understanding: just as...

<meta>catchword: ad- (leading into the Latin "admirationem" or similar on the next page)</meta>

<summary>Porphyry discusses how incorporeal essences (like the soul) undergo "death" only as a different mode of life and how higher realities emanate into lower ones without losing their own substance. He concludes by noting that the highest principle, being beyond the mind, is best understood through intellectual stillness rather than active thought.</summary>

<keywords>Porphyry, Neoplatonism, emanation, incorporeal life, essence, intellect, the One, stillness, immortality, corruption, generation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Porphyry's "Sentences on the Intelligibles," specifically concluding Sentence 27 and covering Sentences 28 and 29. It explores the Neoplatonic epistemology where "like knows like" and the ontological distinction between two types of "non-being": the divine source above Being and the privation of matter below it. It also discusses how incorporeal realities are unaffected by physical mass or location.</meta>

-># PORPHYRY<-

<column-break/>

...just as concerning a person who is sleeping, while one is awake, many things indeed are affirmed; however, the knowledge and experience of these things are perceived through sleep. For like is known by like: because all knowledge is an assimilation <term>assimilation: the process of a mind becoming similar in form or nature to the object it is studying</term> to that which is known.

**XXVIII.** Non-being is either something we produce ourselves, when we are separated from Being: or we conceive it beforehand in the mind, while we are joined with Being. Accordingly, if we are separated from Being, we do not conceive in the mind that "non-being" which is above Being <note>In Neoplatonism, the ultimate source (The One) is called "non-being" because it is so perfect it transcends the category of "existence" altogether.</note>; rather, we forge a non-being that is a certain false and deceptive passion <note>original: "passionem" / "πάθος" (pathos). In this philosophical context, a "passion" is an internal state or an impression one undergoes.</note>, which usually happens to a person departing from their true self. For just as each individual could, in truth and through themselves, be led back to that non-being which is above Being: so, by departing from Being and from oneself, one passes to the non-being which is the defect and lack of Being <note>This "lower" non-being refers to matter or the void, which is a total absence of form and reality.</note>.

**XXIX.** The substance of a body in no way hinders the incorporeal <note>The "incorporeal" refers to spiritual or intellectual realities, such as the soul or divine minds.</note>—which exists in itself—from being wherever and however it wishes. For just as that which lacks mass <term>mass: "mole" in Latin or "onkos" in Greek, referring to physical bulk or spatial extension</term> is incomprehensible to a physical body, and nothing about it pertains to the body: so to the incorporeal, that which is endowed with mass cannot be an obstacle, but lies before it as if it were non-being. Nor is the incorporeal carried here and there by local motion <note>movement through physical space from one point to another</note> wherever it wishes: for physical place encompasses the mass of a body...

<column-break/>

...through wakefulness many things are said; but through sleeping comes knowledge and comprehension. <margin>5</margin> For by like is the like known; because all knowledge is an assimilation of the thing known.

<margin>10</margin> **28.** Non-being is on one hand what we generate, having been separated from Being; and on the other hand, what we conceive beforehand, by clinging to Being. <margin>15</margin> So that if we should be separated from Being, we do not conceive beforehand the non-being that is above Being; but we generate a false passion, the non-being that happens to one who has stood outside <margin>20</margin> of themselves. For indeed, each person, just as they are truly and through themselves able to be led up to the non-being above Being, can also be led forward to the downfall <margin>25</margin> of the non-being of Being <note>The "downfall" refers to the descent into the lower non-being of the material world.</note>.

**29.** The subsistence of the body in no way hinders the incorporeal (which exists in itself), <margin>30</margin> so as not to be where it wishes and as it desires. For just as that which is without mass is ungraspable to the body, and nothing in relation to it; <margin>35</margin> so to the incorporeal, that which has mass is non-obstructing, and lies as if it were non-being. Nor <unclear>... ... ...</unclear>

<summary>Porphyry discusses the principle that knowledge requires the knower to become like the known. He distinguishes between the divine "Non-being" above existence and the "Non-being" of material lack, while arguing that spiritual entities are not restricted by physical bodies or space.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s "Sentences on the Intelligibles," specifically concluding the discussion from Sentence XXIX and moving into Sentence XXX. The text explores how the incorporeal (the soul or mind) relates to physical space and the body, emphasizing that it is not "inside" a body like a physical object but is connected through its own power.</meta>

-># SENTENCES<-

<column-break/>

The incorporeal moves locally wherever it wishes. For "place" <term>place: in Neoplatonic thought, "place" is a property of physical matter, not of the soul</term> exists alongside mass; nor is it <margin>5</margin> cramped by the presence of bodies. For only that which possesses some degree of mass can be cramped and can make a transition from place to place. But that which is entirely without mass and without magni- <margin>10</margin> tude <note>original: "ἀμέγεθες" (amegethes) - lacking physical dimensions</note> cannot be held by those things that have mass, and it has no share in local motion. Therefore, it is found in a certain place according to its disposition, <margin>15</margin> existing everywhere and nowhere in terms of physical location... for this reason, by a certain disposition, it is held either above the heaven or in some part of the world. But when it is <margin>20</margin> held in some part of the world, it is not seen with the eyes; rather, its presence becomes manifest through its works.

**XXX.** If the incorporeal should be held in a <margin>25</margin> body, it must not be shut in like wild beasts in a cage <note>original: "ζωγρείῳ" (zōgreiō) - a place for keeping animals alive, such as a pen or enclosure</note>; for nothing can shut it in this way, nor can a body encompass it; nor is it like a wineskin that <margin>30</margin> draws in some liquid or air. Instead, it must itself establish powers that incline outwards from its union with itself; and by these powers, the whole is interwoven with the <margin>35</margin> body. Thus, through an ineffable extension of itself, it joins the body to its own nature. Therefore, it is not another...

<column-break/>

...is permitted.) nor is it constrained by bodies. For whatever is endowed with mass in any way whatsoever, that can both be constrained and make a transition by local motion. But that which is entirely without mass and magnitude can neither be contained by that which is endowed with mass, nor is it a participant in local motion. Therefore, it is found there by a certain disposition, according to the state by which it is inclined, while existing meanwhile in a place, both everywhere and nowhere. And therefore, by a certain disposition, it is contained either above the heaven or in some part of the world; nevertheless, when it is contained in some part of the world, it is not perceived by the eyes: but its presence appears from its own works.

**XXX.** If the incorporeal be contained in a body, it is not necessary that it be enclosed in such a way as wild beasts in a cage <note>original: "roborario" - a place of confinement, often made of oak (robur)</note>: (for no body can enclose and embrace that thing in such a manner.) Nor as a wineskin holds some liquid or breath. But the incorporeal itself must provide certain faculties or powers verging outwards from its union with the body, by which, descending, it is connected to the body. And so, through an ineffable extension of itself, it happens that the body...

<meta>Catchword: body</meta>

<summary>This page argues that the soul, being incorporeal and without mass, is not physically "trapped" in the body or restricted by space, but relates to the material world through its own internal disposition and outgoing powers.</summary>

--- Page 76 ---
<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s "Sentences on the Intelligibles," specifically concluding the discussion from Sentence XXX regarding the soul’s connection to the body, and beginning Sentence XXXI, which contrasts universal and particular souls.</meta>

# PORPHYRY

...body be coerced. And therefore, nothing else binds the soul, but the soul binds itself. Wherefore, it is not the wounded and corrupted body that releases the soul, but the soul releases itself by turning away from its emotional attachment <note>original: "affectu corporeo" / "προσπαθείας" (prospatheias) — a strong, passionate inclination or clinging to physical sensation</note> to the body.

### XXXI. 
None of the universal and perfect substances <term>substances: here referring to "hypostases," the distinct levels of reality in Neoplatonic thought, such as Mind, Soul, and Matter</term> turns toward its own offspring; rather, all perfect substances are led back to their own authors <note>original: "auctores" / "γεννήσαντα" — the higher principles that generated them</note>, reaching even as far as the mundane body <note>the "body of the world" or the physical universe itself</note>. For since the world-body is perfect, it is subject to a soul endowed with intellect; and for this reason, it moves in a circle. Its soul, however, is directed toward the Mind; and the Mind is subject to the First Principle <note>original: "primum principium" / "πρῶτον" (proton) — The One, the ultimate source of all existence in Porphyry’s philosophy</note>. 

Therefore, all things reach toward this goal, starting from the very last thing and proceeding according to the capacity of each. That return to the First Principle happens either from a state of closeness or from a distance. These things can therefore be said not only to desire God, but also to enjoy Him as much as they are able. 

However, in the case of particular or divided substances—those which are capable of inclining toward the many—it is also innate for them to turn toward their own offspring <note>this refers to individual souls like ours, which have the tendency to become preoccupied with the lower, material things they govern</note>. This is why error exists in these substances, and in them is found that shameful treachery <note>original: "perfidia" / "ἀπιστία" (apistia) — here implying a loss of faith or a turning away from the divine source</note>. Matter infects these substances with evil because they are capable of being deflected toward it, even though they are also capable of turning toward God...

<column-break/>

...is bound, but it binds itself. Nor, then, does the body being broken and corrupted release it; but the soul releases itself by turning away from its attachment.

### 31. 
Of the whole and perfect substances, none is turned toward its own offspring. But all perfect substances are raised up toward those things that generated them, even as far as the cosmic body. For being perfect, it is raised up to the Soul, which is intellectual. And because of this, it is moved in a circle. But its Soul is raised to the Mind, and the Mind to the First. 

All things, therefore, extend toward this, beginning from the last, according to the power that each possesses. This raising up toward the First is either immediate or from afar; therefore these things might be said not only to yearn for God, but also to enjoy Him according to their power. 

But in the particular substances, which are also able to incline toward the many, there is the capacity to turn toward their offspring. Whence in these there is sin <note>original: "ἁμαρτία" (hamartia) — literally "missing the mark," used here to describe the soul losing its way by looking downward instead of upward</note>, and in these is the reviled unfaithfulness. Matter, therefore, brings evil to these, because they are able to turn toward it, while also being able to turn...

<summary>This page concludes Sentence XXX, explaining that the soul's release from the body is a mental act of detachment, and begins Sentence XXXI, describing the hierarchy of the universe where all things strive to return to the "First Principle" (God).</summary>
<keywords>soul, intellect, First Principle, Neoplatonism, matter, universal substances, particular substances, conversion, return to God</keywords>

# SENTENCES

...to be turned toward the divine. But perfection establishes the secondary things from those that come before them, keeping them turned toward the first principles; imperfection, however, turns even the prior things toward what comes after them, and makes those things which were previously turned toward what preceded them love these [lesser things].

### XXXII. God is everywhere because He is nowhere; 
and Mind is everywhere, because it is nowhere; and the Soul is everywhere, because it is nowhere. But God is everywhere and nowhere before all things that come after Him; and He is exactly as He is and as He wills. Mind is indeed in God, but everywhere and nowhere relative to those things after it. The Soul is in Mind and in God, everywhere [within them], and nowhere in the body; but the body is in the Soul, and in the Mind, and in God. And as all beings, and non-beings, are from God and in God, yet He Himself is neither the beings nor the non-beings, nor is He in them. For if He were only everywhere, He would Himself be all things, and in all things; but since He is also nowhere, all things come to be through Him, and in Him, because He is everywhere; yet they are distinct from Him, because He is nowhere. So also Mind, being everywhere and nowhere, is the cause of souls, and of those things after souls, and is not—

<summary>Porphyry concludes Sentence XXXI by contrasting how perfection maintains the hierarchy of being while imperfection causes a "downward" focus. Sentence XXXII introduces a core Neoplatonic paradox: the higher principles (God, Mind, Soul) are "everywhere" because they sustain all things, yet "nowhere" because they are not limited by space or the things they create.</summary>
<keywords>Porphyry, Neoplatonism, Omnipresence, Transcendence, God, Nous, Soul, Hierarchy of Being</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the bilingual Greek-Latin edition of Porphyry’s "Sentences on the Intelligibles," completing Sentence XXXII and beginning Sentence XXXIII. The text explores the Neoplatonic concept of how the soul "occupies" space without being physical.</meta>

# PORPHYRY

<column-break/>

...follows the souls: nor is he himself the soul, nor those things which are after the soul, nor in them. Because he is not only everywhere among those things that come after him, but also nowhere. And the soul is neither body, nor in the body; but the cause of the body: because while it is diffused everywhere through the body, it is nowhere. And this progression of the universe proceeds until that which is neither everywhere at once, nor can be nowhere; but is a partaker of each in part.

XXXIII. **Just as** the soul's being on earth is not to tread upon the earth like bodies, but to preside over a body treading upon the earth; so also to be in the underworld <note>original Latin: "in inferno," reflecting the Greek "Hades." In this context, it refers to a state of being rather than a physical location.</note> is for the soul, when it presides over an image <note>original Latin: "simulacrum," Greek: "eidolon." This is the "shade" or lower aspect of the soul that retains a human form.</note>, which indeed has such a nature that it is in a place, but obtains its existence in darkness. Therefore, if the underworld is a subterranean and dark place, the soul exists in the underworld, though not torn away or separated from true Being, by drawing the image to itself. For as it departs from the solid body, there also accompanies it the spirit <note>original Latin: "spiritus," Greek: "pneuma." This refers to the "astral body" or "pneumatic vehicle" of the soul.</note> which it had collected from the spheres <note>The celestial spheres of the planets, which the soul passes through during its descent into birth.</note>. But since, from its affection toward the body, it has a partial reason <note>original: "rationem partialem," referring to the specific formative principle of that individual life.</note> cast before it, according to which it obtained a certain relationship toward such a body...

<column-break/>

...the souls, nor those things after the soul, nor in these; because he was not only everywhere <margin>5</margin> among those things after him, but also nowhere. And the soul is not body, nor in body, but the cause of body; because while being everywhere in the body, <margin>10</margin> it is nowhere. And indeed the progression of the universe [goes] to that which is able neither to be everywhere at once, nor nowhere, but partaking in turn of each. 

<margin>15</margin> XXXIII. **Just as** being on earth is for the soul, not to step on the earth as bodies do, but to preside over a body which steps on the earth; so also to be in <margin>20</margin> Hades <note>original: "ᾅδου" (Hades). In Neoplatonism, Hades is often interpreted as the state of the soul being "invisible" (a-ides) or trapped in the darkness of the material image.</note> is for the soul, when it presided over an image <note>original: "εἰδώλῳ" (eidōlō), the phantom or shadow of the self.</note>, having a nature to be in a place, but possessing its subsistence in darkness. So that if Hades is an <margin>25</margin> underground dark place, the soul, even if not torn away from Being, becomes [present] in Hades, drawing the image with it. For as it <margin>30</margin> goes out from the solid body, the spirit <note>original: "πνεῦμα" (pneuma), the subtle "breath" or vehicle that carries the soul between worlds.</note> follows it, which it collected from the spheres; and from its prior passion toward the body, it holds <margin>35</margin> the partial reason <note>original: "λόγον" (logon), here meaning the individual formative principle or blueprint.</note> projected, according to which it had a relationship toward such a body...

<summary>Porphyry explains that the soul is "everywhere and nowhere" because it is a non-physical cause of the body. He further defines being in "Hades" not as a physical journey, but as the soul's attachment to a "shadow-image" and a subtle "spirit-body" gathered from the stars.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s "Sentences on the Intelligibles," specifically Sentence XXXIII. It details the Neoplatonic doctrine of the "soul-vehicles"—the idea that as a soul descends from the divine realm toward the physical world, it clothes itself in increasingly dense "bodies" (ethereal, solar, lunar, and finally vaporous/earthly) based on its moral and mental state.</meta>

-># APHORISMS<-

...while in life, from this predisposition, the form of the **imagination** <note>original Greek: "phantasia"</note> is imprinted upon the **spirit** <note>original Greek: "pneuma," referring here to the "astral" or "pneumatic" vehicle of the soul.</note>, and in this way the **image** <note>original Greek: "eidolon," the shade or ghostly double of the person.</note> is drawn along. But the soul is said to be in the underworld <note>original Greek: "Hades."</note> because the spirit has happened upon a formless and dark nature. And since this heavy and moist spirit extends even to the subterranean places, the soul is likewise said to go beneath the earth; not because the essence itself moves from place to place or exists within a location, but because it adopts the conditions of bodies that are naturally suited to move through space and occupy places. It receives these conditions according to the suitability of such bodies, based on the specific disposition of the soul toward them. For in whatever way the soul is disposed, it finds a body defined by its own rank and specific characteristics. Therefore, for a soul that is more purely disposed, the body naturally joined to it is that which is closest to the immaterial—namely, the **ethereal** body. But for a soul that has proceeded from pure reason to the projection of the imagination, the **solar** body is its natural companion. If the soul becomes softened and captured by a passion for forms, the **lunar** body is attached to it. And for a soul that has fallen into [earthly] bodies, when it exists according to its lack of form, composed of moist vapors...

<column-break/>

...while it lived, from that leaning affection, the figure of the **imagination** is imprinted upon the **spirit**, and so the **image** <note>original Latin: "simulacrum."</note> is attracted. It is said to be in the underworld, however, because the spirit has obtained a formless and dark nature. And since a heavy and moist spirit reaches as far as the subterranean places, for this reason the soul itself is said to go beneath the earth: not that the substance itself passes from place to place, or exists in physical locations, but because it takes on the habits of bodies which, by their own nature, pass through places and occupy them. It takes these on because such bodies receive the soul according to their own fitness, resulting from a certain disposition of the soul toward them. For just as the soul has been disposed, so it finds a body defined by its own order and properties. Therefore, for a soul more purely disposed, a body is joined that is as close as possible to the immaterial—that is, the **ethereal** body. But for one that has progressed from reason to the object of the imagination, the **solar** body is born alongside it. But for a soul that is effeminated and captured by the love of forms, the **lunar** body is joined. Truly, for one fallen into [earthly] bodies, when they exist composed of moist vapors according to its formless appearance, the perfect...

<summary>Porphyry explains how the soul's mental and emotional state determines which "spiritual body" (ethereal, solar, or lunar) it inhabits after death, and clarifies that the soul's presence in "Hades" is a state of being rather than a physical movement to a location.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s "Sentences on the Intelligibles," specifically the conclusion of Sentence XXXIII and the beginning of Sentence XXXVI. # PORPHYRY

...complete ignorance of true being, and darkness and infancy follow. And indeed, at the very moment of departure, still carrying a spirit clouded by moist evaporation, the soul draws a shadow <note>original Latin: "vmbra"; Greek: "σκιὰν". In Neoplatonism, this refers to the 'ghostly' image or 'shade' of the deceased.</note> behind it and is weighed down. Such a spirit, by its own nature, tends toward the depths of the earth, unless some other cause pulls it back in the opposite direction. Therefore, just as it is necessary for one enclosed in this **earthy shell** <note>original Latin: "terrea hac testa"; Greek: "γεῶδες ὄστρεον". This is a famous Platonic metaphor for the physical body as an oyster shell trapping the soul.</note> to dwell upon the earth, so too, when the soul draws a moist spirit, it necessarily has a **likeness** <note>original Latin: "simulacrum"; Greek: "εἴδωλον", an image or phantom.</note> joined to it. It draws this moisture when it continually strives to associate with "nature," whose work consists in moisture and which is primarily subterranean. But when the soul strives to withdraw from nature, a **dry splendor** <note>original Latin: "splendor siccus"; Greek: "αὐγὴ ξηρὰ". This refers to a fragment of Heraclitus: "A dry soul is wisest and best." To the Neoplatonists, dryness represents intellectual purity.</note> arises, obscured by no shadow or cloud. For humidity creates a mist in the air, but dryness produces a dry and clear splendor from the exhalation.

**XXXVI.** These things are truly affirmed regarding the **sensible** <note>Things perceptible by the senses.</note> and **material**: that it is entirely extended or diffused, that it is mutable, that it exists in a state of alteration, that it is composite, that it exists as a thing unto itself, that it is perceived in a specific location and in bodily mass, and other things like these. But concerning true be—

<column-break/>

<margin>5</margin> ...constituting essence, a complete ignorance of being follows, as well as darkness and infancy. And indeed, even in the exit [from the body], the soul still possessing the spirit clouded by moist exhalation, draws a shadow and is weighed down; <margin>10</margin> such a spirit naturally giving way toward the hollow of the earth, unless some other cause pulls it back. Just as, therefore, being surrounded by the **earthy oyster-shell**, it is necessary <margin>15</margin> to be held fast upon the earth; so also, drawing a moist spirit, it is necessary that an **image** be wrapped around it. It draws moisture whenever it meditates upon continually associating with <margin>20</margin> nature, whose work is in moisture, and is rather subterranean. But when it meditates upon departing from nature, a **dry light** arises, without shadow and without cloud. <margin>25</margin> For moisture constitutes a cloud in the air, but dryness from the vapor produces a dry light.

**36.** The things predicated <margin>30</margin> of the sensible and the material are truly these: to be everywhere dispersed, to be changeable, to subsist in "otherness" <note>original Greek: "heteroteti", meaning a state of constant flux and difference from itself.</note>, to be composite, <margin>35</margin> to exist as something different in itself, to be viewed in a place and in bulk, and whatever things are similar to these.

<summary>This page concludes a discussion on how the soul's attachment to "moisture" and physical nature creates a heavy, shadowy spirit that sinks toward the earth, whereas a "dry" soul ascends toward light. It then begins a formal definition of the material world as being characterized by change, location, and physical mass.</summary>

...likened; but regarding that which **truly exists** <note>original Greek: "τοῦ δὲ ὄντως ὄντος". This refers to the Platonic concept of the Intelligible realm, which is considered more real than the physical world because it is eternal.</note>, and subsists by itself, its being is always established within itself, and likewise <margin>5</margin> remains in the same state, having its essence in **identity** <note>original Greek: "ταὐτότητι"; Latin: "identitate". In Neoplatonism, this implies a state of perfect self-sameness that never changes or fluctuates.</note>, being immutable in its essence, uncompounded, neither <margin>10</margin> dissoluble, nor located in a place, nor spread out into **mass** <note>original Greek: "ὄγκον"; Latin: "mole". This means the soul or true being has no physical bulk, volume, or weight.</note>, neither coming into being nor perishing, and all such things. Following these [rules], <margin>15</margin> we must neither say anything different or alien regarding their distinct nature, nor listen to others saying such things.

<column-break/>

...and subsisting by itself, <note>This column provides the parallel Latin translation of the Greek text to the left. It continues the sentence from the previous page: "These things are truly affirmed regarding the sensible... [but of that which is through itself]..."</note> these things are predicated: to be always situated within itself, to be in the same way and according to the same thing, to consist of the same **substance** <note>original Latin: "substantia".</note> in identity, to be immutable according to substance, not composed, nor dissoluble, nor in a place, nor distended in mass: nor having arisen, nor to perish: and whatever things are similar to these. Following which rule, it is fitting that we neither speak ourselves, nor listen to others speaking, anything changed or alien concerning their diverse nature.

### Concerning the Virtues,

### 34.

There are some virtues belonging to the citizen <note>original Latin: "politici"; Greek: "πολιτικοῦ". In this context, "political" refers to "civil" or "social" life—the excellence of a person living in a community.</note>; others belong to the one ascending toward **contemplation** <note>original Latin: "contemplationem"; Greek: "θεωρίαν". This refers to the philosophical practice of looking inward or upward toward divine truths.</note>, who for that reason is called a contemplative, or even a beholder; and still others belong to the **Intellect** <note>original Latin: "mentis"; Greek: "νοῦ". In Neoplatonism, this is the highest part of the human spirit that can commune with the divine.</note>, insofar as it is Intellect and pure from its association with the soul. The virtues of the citizen, which consist in the moderation of the passions, are these: to follow the guidance of reason in the duties and actions of life, and to obey it. Therefore, since they aim at ensuring that no neighbor suffers harm from our social interactions, they are called **political** from the gathering of civil society. And indeed, **prudence** <term>prudence: (Latin: "prudentia"; Greek: "phronêsis") practical wisdom or the ability to judge the right course of action in daily life.</term> concerns the rational part; **fortitude** <term>fortitude: (Latin: "fortitudo"; Greek: "andreia") courage or spiritual strength, specifically regarding the "spirited" or emotional part of the soul.</term> concerns the spirited part; while **temperance** <term>temperance: (Latin: "temperantia"; Greek: "sôphrosunê") self-control or moderation, specifically the harmony between the desiring part of the soul and reason.</term> consists in the agreement and harmony...

### Concerning the Virtues.

### 34.

<margin>15</margin>
Some virtues are those of the citizen, and others are those of the one ascending toward contemplation, and because of this called contemplative, and already a beholder. And others are those of the Intellect, insofar as it is Intellect, and pure from the soul. The virtues of the citizen, lying in the moderation of passions, consist in following and obeying reason regarding duties in actions. Therefore, looking toward the harmless fellowship of neighbors, they are called **political** from the herding together of society. And prudence is about the reasoning part; courage is about the spirited part; temperance is in the agreement and harmony of the desiring...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Porphyry’s "Sentences" or "Points of Departure to the Intelligible." Specifically, it moves from the social or "political" virtues toward the "purgative" or "purifying" virtues. The text explores the distinction between the process of cleansing the soul and the ultimate Good that remains once the soul is purified.</meta>

# PORPHYRY
### SENTENCES.

<column-break/>

...according to the **contemplative virtues**, in a state of **apathy** <term>apathy: (Greek: "apatheia") in Neoplatonic philosophy, this is not a lack of emotion, but a state of being "without passions"—where the soul is no longer disturbed by the body’s desires or pains.</term> or emptiness of passions: the end of which is **likeness to God** <note>original: "similitudo cum Deo" / "πρὸς θεὸν ὁμοίωσις" (homoiosis theo). A fundamental Platonic goal where the human soul aims to mirror the divine nature through virtue and intellect.</note>. But because **purification** <term>purification: (Latin: "purgatio"; Greek: "katharsis") the secondary level of virtue where a philosopher begins to detach the soul from physical sensations.</term> is of two kinds—one which purifies, and the other belonging to those already purified—the purifying virtues are considered according to both meanings of purification. For they both cleanse the soul and are present in the soul once it has been cleansed. For the end of purifying is to emerge pure. And since both the act of purification and the state of purity are, as it were, a removal of everything alien, the **Good** itself will surely be something distinct from the purifying process. Indeed, if that which is purified had been the Good itself before it contracted impurity, then purification alone would suffice. But while purification is desirable, that which remains after the process is the Good, not the purification itself. Now, the nature of the soul is not the Good, but it is that which is capable of participating in the Good and is "good-formed" <note>original: "boniforme" / "ἀγαθοειδές" (agathoeides). This suggests the soul is not God/the Good itself, but is a "form" or "image" designed to receive and reflect that goodness.</note>. Otherwise, it would never have fallen into evil. Therefore, its Good consists in this: if it is joined to its **Author** <note>original: "auctori" / "γεννήσαντι" (gennésanti). Referring to the Divine Source or "Progenitor" from which the soul emanated.</note>; whereas its evil consists in being joined to inferior things. Furthermore, this evil is two-fold: first, that it is joined to inferior things at all; and second, that this joining happens with an excessive vehemence of passions. For this reason, all the **political virtues** <note>The basic level of virtue dealing with social life and the moderation of passions.</note>, which free the soul from at least this one evil, have earned the name and value of "virtues." The purifying...
<meta>catchword: however</meta>

<column-break/>

...all according to nature. But that according to the **contemplative virtues** consists in **apathy**; the end of which is **likeness to God**. Since purification is one thing as purifying, and another as of the purified; the purifying virtues are seen according to
<margin>5</margin> both meanings of purification. For they cleanse
the soul, and they are present with the soul that has been cleansed. For the end of purifying is to have been purified. But since the act of purifying and being pure was the removal of
<margin>10</margin> everything alien, the Good would be something different from the purifying agent. If indeed the thing being purified was the Good even before its impurity, the purification would have
<margin>15</margin> been sufficient. But while purification is pleasing, that which is left behind will be the Good, not the purification itself. For the nature of the soul was not the Good, but it was
<margin>20</margin> something able to participate in the Good, and "good-like." For it would not have been in evil otherwise. Therefore, its Good lies in its being present with its
<margin>25</margin> Progenitor. But its evil lies in its attachment to the lower things. And this evil is twofold: both the being with these things, and being with an excess
<margin>30</margin> of passions. Wherefore all the **political virtues**, freeing the soul from at least one of these evils, were called virtues and are held in honor. But the purifying...
<margin>35</margin>
<meta>catchword: one.</meta>

<summary>Porphyry defines the "purgative virtues" as a middle stage between social life and divine contemplation, explaining that the soul is not the "Good" itself but a vessel meant to be purified so it can rejoin its divine source.</summary>
<keywords>Porphyry, Neoplatonism, purification, katharsis, apathy, likeness to God, political virtues, contemplative virtues, nature of the soul</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s classification of virtues. The previous page discussed the "political" (social) and "purgative" (purifying) virtues. This page moves into the higher "intellectual" and "exemplary" virtues, which represent the soul's return to the Divine Mind.</meta>

# SENTENCES.
### F 2

...more excellent <note>The text continues from the previous page describing the "purgative" or purifying virtues.</note>. And they deliver the soul from vice. It is necessary, then, for the soul—once purified—to be present with its begetter <note>original: "γεννήσαντι" (gennēsanti). This refers to the Intellect or the Divine Source from which the soul originated.</note>. Therefore, its virtue after its **conversion** <term>conversion: (Greek: "epistrophe") in Neoplatonic thought, this is the "turning back" of the soul toward its divine source after it has been purified of material distractions.</term> exists in knowledge and the science of true Being; not as if those things prior to it were the same.

There is, then, another third genus of virtues after the purgative and political ones: these are the **intellectual virtues** of the soul acting as a mind. **Wisdom** <note>original: "σοφία" (sophia)</note> and **prudence** <note>original: "φρόνησις" (phronesis)</note> consist in the contemplation of what the mind possesses. **Justice** consists in "doing one's own work" <note>A reference to Plato’s definition of justice in the *Republic*: each part of the soul performing its proper function.</note> in following the mind and acting according to the mind. **Temperance** <note>original: "σωφροσύνη" (sophrosyne)</note> is the inward turning toward the mind. **Fortitude** <note>original: "ἀνδρεία" (andreia)</note> is **apathy** <term>apathy: (Greek: "apatheia") not a lack of care, but a state of "passionlessness" where the soul is no longer affected by the fluctuations of the body or emotions.</term> according to the likeness of that which it looks upon, which is by nature impassible. And these virtues follow one another, just as the others do.

The fourth kind of virtues is that of the **paradigms** <term>paradigms: (Greek: "paradeigmata") the "exemplary" or archetypal virtues. These are the perfect, original versions of virtues that exist within the Divine Mind itself, serving as the models for human virtue.</term>. These, which exist in the Mind, are superior to the virtues of the soul and are the models for them; the virtues of the soul are but likenesses of these. And the Mind is that in which all things exist together as paradigms. Here, knowledge is prudence. Wisdom is the Mind in the act of knowing. That which turns toward itself is temperance. Its proper work is justice. Fortitude is identity—or being "the same"—and the...

<column-break/>

...but more excellent: liberating the soul from evil insofar as it is the soul. For which reason, it is necessary for the purified soul to be joined with its author <note>original: "auctore suo". This mirrors the Greek "begetter" on the opposite column, referring to the Divine Mind.</note>; therefore its virtue after conversion consists in knowledge and the science of the true being; by no means, however, unless those prior stages have preceded it.

There remains, therefore, another certain third genus of virtues after the purgative and political, belonging to the soul operating intellectually. Here, **wisdom** and **prudence** consist in the contemplation of those things which the mind possesses. **Justice** is the performance of one's proper duty while the soul follows the mind and works according to the mind. **Temperance**, however, is the inward conversion toward the mind. **Fortitude** is the absence of passions, according to the likeness of that toward which the soul looks, which by its own nature is impassible. And these virtues indeed follow one upon the other, just as the others do.

The fourth genus of virtues is the **exemplary** <note>original: "exemplarium". These are the archetypal virtues mentioned in the left column.</note>, which exist in the mind; these stand far above the virtues of the soul. These are the models of those virtues, just as the virtues of the soul were likenesses of these. And the mind is indeed that in which all things exist at once, as models. Here, science is the equivalent of prudence. Wisdom is the intellect in the act of knowing. That which is converted toward itself is temperance. That which performs its proper duty is...

<meta>catchword: τὸ (Greek), tur (Latin - part of "fungitur")</meta>

<summary>Porphyry defines the higher levels of virtue: the "intellectual" virtues, where the soul mimics the Mind, and the "exemplary" virtues, which are the divine archetypes of Wisdom, Justice, Temperance, and Fortitude.</summary>
<keywords>Porphyry, Neoplatonism, intellectual virtues, exemplary virtues, purification, Wisdom, Justice, Temperance, Fortitude, conversion, Divine Mind</keywords>

### F 2

<margin>5</margin> ...be restrained <note>The text continues from the previous page: "in order that it may not be restrained by the body."</note> by the body. And therefore it does not bind anything else, but binds itself; wherefore it is not the wounded and corrupted body that it releases, but it releases itself by turning away from bodily affection <note>original Latin: "affectu corporeo." This refers to the emotional or physical attachment the soul feels toward the material world.</note>.

**XXXI.** Of universal and perfect substances <term>substances: (Latin: "substantiarum", Greek: "hypostaseon") in Neoplatonism, these are the fundamental levels of reality, such as the Soul, the Intellect, and the One.</term>, <margin>10</margin> none is turned toward its own offspring: but all perfect substances are led back to their own authors <note>original Latin: "auctores." In this context, it refers to the higher divine principle from which the substance originated.</note>, even as far as the mundane <note>meaning: the physical universe</note> body. For <margin>15</margin> since it is perfect, it is raised up toward the soul endowed with intellect, and for this reason it moves in a circle; its soul, however, is raised toward the mind; and the mind is raised toward the first <margin>20</margin> principle. To this, therefore, all things—having started from the last point—arrive, each according to its own powers. That return to the first happens either from near or from far. <margin>25</margin> These, therefore, can be said not only to desire God, but also to enjoy Him according to their strength. In particular or divided substances, however, <margin>30</margin> and those which can incline toward many things, it is also innate that they turn themselves toward their own offspring. Whence in these sin <note>original Latin: "delictum." In Neoplatonic thought, "sin" is a "falling away" or a "misdirection" of the soul's attention from the divine to the material.</note> exists; in <margin>35</margin> these, shameful faithlessness <note>original Latin: "perfidia."</note>. Matter therefore infects these with evil, for the reason that they are able to be deflected into it, even though they are also able to be turned toward God.

<column-break/>

...binds [itself], but it [binds] itself. It does not, then, release the wounded and corrupted body, but it [releases] itself by being turned away from the emotional attachment <note>original Greek: "prospatheias" (προσπαθείας). This specifically refers to a passionate or suffering attachment that "glues" the soul to the body.</note>.

**31.** Of the whole and perfect substances, none is turned toward its own offspring. But all perfect substances are led upward toward those things that begat them, even unto the body of the world. For being perfect, it is led up toward the soul, which is intellectual. And for this reason it moves in a circle. But its soul is [led up] toward the mind; and the mind toward the first. Therefore all things reach toward this, beginning from the last, according to what each is able. The leading up toward the first is either immediate or from afar; wherefore these would not only be said to long for God, but also to enjoy Him according to their power. But in the particular substances, and those capable of leaning toward many things, it is present [in them] to also turn toward their offspring. Whence in these is sin, in these is the reviled faithlessness <note>original Greek: "apistia" (ἀπιστία). In this philosophical context, it's the soul's failure to remain focused on its divine origin.</note>. These, then, matter harms, by their being able to turn toward it, though they are [also] able to be turned...

<summary>Porphyry explains that the soul binds and releases itself from the body. He describes a cosmic hierarchy where universal substances always look "upward" to God, while individual souls can "fall" by looking "downward" toward matter.</summary>

### F 2

...they are able. Indeed, perfection establishes the secondary things from the primary ones, keeping them turned toward the first <note>original Greek: "πρωτέρων". This refers to the higher levels of reality in the Neoplatonic hierarchy, such as the One or the Intellect.</note>; but imperfection nourishes even the first toward the later things, and makes them love these, having turned away from those that were before them.

36. God is everywhere because He is nowhere; and the Mind <term>Mind: (Greek: "nous", Latin: "mens") the divine Intellect, which contains the Platonic Forms and is the second principle in Neoplatonism.</term> is everywhere because it is nowhere; and the soul is everywhere because it is nowhere. But God indeed is everywhere and nowhere of all those things which are after Him; He Himself is only as He is and as He wills. Mind, however, is in God, but is everywhere and nowhere regarding those things which are after it. And the soul is in Mind and in God everywhere, and nowhere in the body; but the body is in the soul, and in the Mind, and in God. And while all beings and non-beings <note>original Greek: "τὰ ὄντα, καὶ μὴ ὄντα". This refers to the distinction between things that truly exist (intelligible realities) and things that are in a state of becoming (matter).</note> are from God and in God, yet He Himself is not the beings, nor the non-beings, nor is He in them. For if He were only everywhere, He would indeed be all things, and in all things; but since He is also nowhere, all things come to be through Him and in Him, because He is everywhere. But they are different from Him, because He Himself is also nowhere. So also Mind, being everywhere and nowhere, is the cause of souls and of those things after them; and it is not...

<column-break/>

<margin>5</margin> ...they are able. To such an extent that perfection indeed substitutes secondary things for the primary, preserving them turned toward the first: but imperfection deflects even the first toward the last, and brings it about that they love those things which have deflected <note>original Latin: "deflexerunt". This implies a straying or "turning away" from the divine source.</note> before them.

<margin>10</margin> **XXXII.** God is everywhere, because He is nowhere: and the mind is everywhere, because it is nowhere: and the soul is everywhere, because it is nowhere. But God indeed is everywhere and nowhere of all those things which are after Him: but He is there <margin>15</margin> only as He is and as He wills: Mind, however, is in God, but is everywhere and nowhere regarding those things which are after it: and the soul is in the Mind and in God everywhere, and nowhere in the body: the body, however, is in the soul, and in the Mind, and in God. And as all <margin>20</margin> beings and non-beings are from God, and in God; yet He Himself is not the beings or non-beings, nor is He in them. For if He were only everywhere, He would indeed be all things, and would be in all things: but because the same is also nowhere, <margin>25</margin> all things are made through Him, and in Him, because He Himself is everywhere. They are, however, different from Him, because He Himself is also nowhere. So also the Mind, existing everywhere and nowhere, is the cause of souls, and of those things after... <margin>30</margin>

<summary>Porphyry explains that perfection maintains the cosmic hierarchy by keeping lower things focused on their source, while also introducing the paradox that God, Mind, and Soul are everywhere (immanent) because they are not confined to any one place (transcendent).</summary>
<keywords>Porphyry, Neoplatonism, Omnipresence, Transcendence, Mind, Soul, Divine Will, Hierarchy of Being</keywords>

...while it was living, from that inclined affection, a figure of the imagination is imprinted upon the spirit <term>spirit: (Greek: "pneuma", Latin: "spiritus") in Neoplatonism, this is a "vehicle" or intermediate substance that carries the soul between the divine realm and the physical body.</term>, and <margin>5</margin> thus the phantom <note>original Greek: "eidolon"; Latin: "simulacrum"</note> is attracted to it. This is not because the substance itself passes from place to place or exists within locations, but because it takes on the conditions <margin>20</margin> of bodies that naturally move through and occupy places: such bodies receive the soul according to each one’s fitness, resulting from a <margin>25</margin> certain disposition within the soul itself. <meta>This page continues the discussion of the soul’s "spirit-vehicle" (pneuma). It explains how the soul’s moral state and its attachment to the material world affect its "weight" and clarity after death. A "moist" or "damp" spirit is drawn toward the earth and the underworld, whereas a "dry" and purified soul ascends to a state of clear light. It then transitions to a list of the characteristics of the material world.</meta>

# PORPHYRY
->### SENTENCES ###<-

<margin>5</margin> ...ignorance of [true] being, and <margin>5</margin> darkness and infancy follow. And indeed, in the very departure [of the soul], still carrying a spirit <term>spirit: "pneuma" (Greek) or "spiritus" (Latin), an intermediate substance that serves as the "vehicle" for the soul as it descends from the divine realm into a body.</term> disturbed by moist evaporation, it <margin>10</margin> attracts a shadow and is weighed <margin>10</margin> down—for a spirit of this kind, by its own nature, strives for the depths of the earth, unless some other cause pulls it back in the opposite direction. Therefore, <margin>15</margin> just as it is necessary for the soul <margin>15</margin> to dwell on earth when covered by this earthy shell <note>original Latin: "terrea testa"; Greek: "geodes ostreon" (earthy oyster-shell). A common Platonic metaphor for the physical body.</note>, so also when drawing a moist spirit, it necessarily has a phantom <note>original Greek: "eidolon"; Latin: "simulacrum"</note> <margin>20</margin> joined to it. Moreover, it attracts <margin>20</margin> moisture when it constantly strives to associate with nature, whose operation consists in moisture and which is rather subterranean. But when it has <margin>25</margin> made an effort to withdraw from <margin>25</margin> nature, a dry splendor arises, obscured by no shadow or cloud. For humidity forms a mist in the air, but dryness from evaporation produces a dry and clear <margin>30</margin> splendor. <margin>30</margin>

XXXVI. These things are truly affirmed regarding the sensible <note>The "sensible" world refers to everything we can perceive with our five senses.</note> and the material: that it is entirely <margin>35</margin> extended or diffused, that it is <margin>35</margin> changeable, that it exists in alteration, that it is composite, that it exists in its own right; that it is seen in place and in corporeal mass, and other things similar to these. But regarding true Being...

<column-break/>

<margin>5</margin> ...complete ignorance of Being <margin>5</margin> follows, along with darkening and infancy. And truly, even in the exit [from the body], while the soul still holds a spirit clouded by moist exhalation, it <margin>10</margin> draws a shadow to itself and is <margin>10</margin> burdened. Such a spirit tends by its nature to go into the heart of the earth, unless some other cause pulls it upward. Just as, therefore, when it <margin>15</margin> is surrounded by the earthy shell, it <margin>15</margin> is necessary for it to be held fast upon the earth; so also when it draws a moist spirit, it must be surrounded by a phantom. It draws moisture <margin>20</margin> whenever it chooses to associate <margin>20</margin> continually with nature, whose work is in moisture and is mostly underground. But when it chooses to stand apart from nature, it becomes a dry ray of light, without shadow and <margin>25</margin> without cloud. For moisture <margin>25</margin> composes a cloud in the air, but dryness from the vapor produces a dry ray of light.

36. The things predicated of the <margin>30</margin> sensible and the material are <margin>30</margin> truly these: to be spread out in every way, to be changeable, to subsist in "otherness" <note>Greek: "heterotes", the state of being different or changing from one thing to another.</note>, to be composite, to exist by itself, to be observed <margin>35</margin> in place and in bulk, and <margin>35</margin> whatever is similar to these.

<summary>Porphyry describes how the soul’s attachment to material "moisture" creates a heavy, shadowy spirit-vehicle that sinks after death, whereas purification leads to a "dry" state of clear light. He begins defining the characteristics of the material world, such as extension and changeability.</summary>

->### SENTENCES ###<-

...similar things. But regarding that which truly exists <term>Truly exists: "ontos on" (Greek), referring to the Intelligible realm or Platonic Forms which possess a higher reality than physical objects.</term> and subsists in its own right, [the following is said]: its being is always established in itself, staying the same and in the same way, substantialized in identity, and it is immutable in essence <term>essence: "ousia" (Greek) or "substantia" (Latin). In Neoplatonism, this refers to the true, unchanging nature of a divine or intellectual being, as opposed to the shifting qualities of physical matter.</term>, non-composite, and neither dissoluble nor situated in a place <note>Neoplatonists believed that divine beings are non-spatial; they are "everywhere" because they are not confined to a single "where."</note>, nor dispersed into physical mass, being neither generated nor perishing, and all other such qualities. Holding fast to these [principles], we should neither say anything else about their differing natures ourselves, nor listen to others when they speak of them differently.

<column-break/>

...and to subsist through itself, these things are declared: to be always situated within itself, to behave in the same manner and according to the same [principles], <margin>5</margin> to consist of its own substance in identity, to be immutable according to its substance, not composite, nor dissoluble, nor in a place, nor distended by mass <note>original Latin: "mole distensum"; Greek: "eis ogkon diaphoreisthai." This refers to the property of having physical volume or size, which Porphyry argues is absent in the spiritual realm.</note>: <margin>10</margin> neither having been born, nor to perish: and things which are similar to these. Following which rule, it is fitting that we neither speak ourselves, <margin>15</margin> nor listen to others speaking, anything changed or foreign concerning their different nature.

### On Virtues &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; On Virtues <note>original: "περὶ ἀρετῶν" (*peri aretōn*)</note>

### XXXIV. &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; 34.

Some virtues belong to the statesman <term>statesman: "politicus" (Latin) or "politikos" (Greek). In this context, it refers to a person living an active life within human society, governed by social and civic duties.</term>; others to those ascending toward contemplation <note>original Latin: "contemplationem"; Greek: "theōrian." In Neoplatonism, contemplation is the process of turning the soul inward and upward toward divine realities.</note>; whom for that reason they call a contemplative, or even a beholder: and still others belong to the Mind <term>Mind: "mens" (Latin) or "nous" (Greek). In Porphyry's hierarchy, the "Mind" or "Intellect" is the level of reality above the human soul, characterized by perfect, intuitive knowledge.</term>, insofar as it is Mind, pure and free from association with the soul.
<margin>15</margin>
The virtues of the statesman, situated in the moderation of the passions, are these: to follow the lead of reason in the duties and actions of life, and to obey it. And because they look toward this goal—that no neighbor suffers any harm from our social interactions—they are called "political" <note>Here "political" (from *polis*, city) refers to social or civic conduct rather than modern party politics.</note> from the gathering together of social life. And indeed, prudence <term>prudence: "phronēsis," the wisdom used to navigate practical life.</term> is concerned with the rational part; fortitude with the spirited <note>original Latin: "irascentem"; Greek: "thumoumenon." SOME virtues belong to the statesman, and others to those ascending toward contemplation and for that reason called contemplative, and even now a beholder. And others belong to the Mind, insofar as it is Mind, and pure from the soul. Those of the statesman consist in the moderation of the passions, namely following and obeying the reasoning of duty in one’s actions. Therefore, because they look toward a social life that is harmless to one's neighbor, they are called political from the gathering together of society. But the virtues of one progressing toward contemplation <note>original Greek: "θεωρίαν" (*theōrian*). In this tradition, contemplation is not just thinking, but a direct spiritual vision of reality.</note> consist in a withdrawal from things here; therefore these are called **purifications**, being viewed in abstinence from actions performed with the body and from the passions felt toward it. For these belong to the soul as it departs toward true being. The **civic virtues**, however, adorn the mortal man; and the civic virtues are precursors to the purifications. For it is necessary that one who has been adorned by them should resolve to live within the body and to endure it as a primary duty. Therefore, in the purifications, the state of not forming opinions based on the body, but acting alone, establishes **prudence**; which is perfected through pure thinking. Not sharing the same passions establishes **temperance**. Not fearing the departure from the body, as if into something strange and non-existent, establishes **courage**. ...most especially; and they release the soul from the evil [directed] toward the soul.
