# PORPHYRY

<columns>
...is called the proper duty of **justice**. **Fortitude**, however, consists in remaining always in the same state and being pure within itself because of the excellence of its powers. 

We have therefore shown four kinds of virtues. Some are of the **Mind** <term>Mind: In Greek, *Nous*, the level of divine reality containing the eternal forms or "blueprints" of all things.</term>, namely the **exemplary virtues**, which exist together with its very substance. Others are of the soul, specifically of the soul already looking toward the Mind and being filled by it. Others belong to the human soul occupied in purification, or already purified from the body and irrational passions. Still others belong to the soul of the man who regulates the human state, in that he sets limits and measure for the irrational faculty and brings about the **moderation of passions** <note>original Latin: *passionum moderationem*; Greek: *metriopatheia*. This refers to the classical ideal of controlling emotions rather than completely eradicating them.</note>. 

And he who possesses the greater virtues necessarily possesses the lesser ones as well; but the reverse is not true. Nor will one equipped with the greater virtues—even though he possesses the lesser—act primarily and chiefly according to those lesser ones, but only as the circumstances of the mortal condition require. 

The **aims** of these virtues, as has been said, also differ according to their kinds. For the aim of the **civic virtues** <note>original Latin: *politicarum*. These are the practical virtues used by a person living in society, like those described by Aristotle.</note> is to apply measure to the emotions, so that actions may be carried out according to the command of nature. The aim of the **purificatory virtues**, however, is to entirely erase the memory of the passions. As for the others, their aims are in proportion to what we have said.

->therefore<-

<column-break/>

...remaining pure in itself through the abundance of its power. Four kinds of virtues have thus appeared. Some were of the **Mind**, the **exemplary** ones, which run along with its essence. Others were of the soul already looking toward the Mind and being filled by it. Others were as of a human soul being purified and having been purified from the body and irrational passions. Others were of the human soul as it brings order to the human being, by defining the measures for irrationality and producing **moderation of the passions**. 

And he who has the greater necessarily has the lesser. But not the other way around. Furthermore, by having the lesser ones as well, the one who has the greater will not act according to the lesser ones as his primary purpose, but only according to the circumstances of **generation** <term>Generation: The process of being born into a physical, changing body, which Neoplatonists viewed as a descent from the stable divine realm into the world of "becoming."</term>. 

The aims, as has been said, also differ according to their kind. For [the aim] of the civic virtues is to impose a measure on the passions for the practical activities of natural energy. Of the purificatory ones, it is to arrive at a state where the passions are completely [erased] from the mind. Of the remaining virtues, the aims are analogous to those mentioned. Therefore, he who acts according to the practical energies is a virtuous **man**...

->man-<-

</columns>

<summary>Porphyry summarizes the four levels of Platonic virtue—Civic, Purificatory, Intellectual, and Exemplary—explaining that while a higher-level sage possesses the lower virtues, they act primarily from the higher ones.</summary>

...man. But he who acts according to the **purificatory virtues** <note>original Greek: *kathartikas*. These virtues cleanse the soul of bodily attachments.</note> is a **daemonic man** <note>original Greek: *daimonios anthropos*. In this context, "daemonic" refers to a person who has attained a semi-divine, spiritual status, not something evil.</note>, or even a **good spirit**. He who acts according to the **intellectual virtues** <note>original Greek: *noeras*. Virtues of the pure Mind or *Nous*.</note> alone is a **God**. But he who acts according to the **exemplary virtues** <note>Virtues that serve as the "blueprints" or divine models in the Mind of God.</note> is the **Father of the Gods**. We must, therefore, pay the greatest attention to our purification, considering that the attainment of these virtues is possible in this life; and through them, the way is opened for the ascent to more honorable things. For this reason, we must consider to what extent and in what manner purification can be achieved. For it is a separation from the body and from the irrational movement of passions; we must now say how and to what point this occurs.

First, as it were, the foundation and basis of purification is to **know oneself**—to know that one is a soul bound within a foreign thing of a completely different nature.

Second, arising from this foundation, is the resolution: to collect oneself from the body, and from various places, as it were, while remaining entirely unaffected by its passions. For if one acts continually according to the senses, even if he does so without a strong inclination toward the pleasure of the experience, his **Mind** <note>original Greek: *nous*.</note> <unclear>Mind</unclear>

<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s "Sentences" (Sententiae), specifically following the discussion on how the soul is joined to the body. <columns>
...while it is joined to the body through the senses. But we are also affected by the pleasures or pains of **sensible things** <note>sensible things: Objects or experiences perceived through the physical senses, as opposed to intellectual or spiritual concepts.</note> with a certain inclination of the soul and a propensity toward these feelings. It is from this kind of disposition that one must, above all, purge oneself.

This purification occurs if one admits even necessary pleasures and sensations only as a **remedy** <note>original Latin: *medela*; Greek: *iatreia*. Porphyry views physical needs like eating not as ends in themselves, but as "medicine" to keep the body functioning so it does not distract the soul.</note>, or for the sake of driving away pains, so that the soul is not hindered. Pains must also be removed; or if that is not possible, they should be gradually withdrawn or diminished by the fact that we do not "suffer with" them <note>This refers to the concept of *apatheia* or *protopatheia*: experiencing a physical sensation without the soul emotionally "subscribing" to it.</note>. 

Anger, as much as possible, must be eliminated, and it must never be stirred up by any **deliberate plan** <note>original Latin: *meditato consilio*. One should never intentionally cultivate rage or harbor a grudge.</note>. If this is not possible, at least the soul’s purpose must not be mixed with it, so that the sudden and unpremeditated commotion belongs to another part of our nature <note>i.e., the animal or irrational part of the soul, not the rational mind.</note>. For whatever happens without the **soul’s purpose** <note>original Latin: *animi proposito*; Greek: *proairesin*. This is a technical term for the faculty of "deliberate choice."</note> is weak and of little consequence. 

All fear must also be removed, for the purified man will fear nothing. But here too, the soul’s purpose must be absent. However, one should indeed use both anger and fear when **giving admonition** <note>original Latin: *in admonendo*; Greek: *noutheresei*. Porphyry allows for the "appearance" of anger or fear if it serves a pedagogical purpose, such as correcting a student or a child.</note>. 

Every desire for what is evil must be exterminated. Regarding food and drink, the man himself, in his true essence, will not indulge. As for natural sexual matters, let him not even feel sudden or involuntary movements; or at least let them be accompanied by only the lightest imagination, such as...

<column-break/>

...while it is joined to the body through the senses. But we are also affected by the pleasures or pains of **sensible things** through a certain inclination of the soul and a sympathetic movement. It is from this very state that it is most fitting to cleanse oneself. 

This will happen if one takes up even necessary pleasures and sensations only for the sake of **healing**, or to be released from pain, so that one is not hindered. One must also take away pains. But if that should be impossible, one must bear them gently and diminish them by not **suffering along with them** <note>original Greek: *sympaschein*. In Neoplatonism, the goal is for the higher soul to remain a detached observer of the body's sensations.</note>. 

Anger, as far as possible, must be taken away, and one must not practice it. If not, at least one must not join one’s **deliberate choice** <note>original Greek: *proairesin*. This is the core of moral character in Greek philosophy—the conscious "Yes" or "No" we give to our impulses.</note> to it. Let the involuntary impulse be something else. What is involuntary is weak and small. 

Every fear must be removed, for he will fear nothing. Let the involuntary be present here as well. However, one should use both anger and fear in **admonition**. 

Every desire for what is base must be banished. As for food and drink, the man himself will not approach them as himself <note>The "true self" is the intellect/soul, which does not eat; the "animal self" is what consumes nourishment.</note>. Concerning natural sexual acts, there should be not even an involuntary movement; or if there is, only so far as a fleeting and headlong imagination, such as...
</columns>

<summary>This page details the practical application of purificatory virtues, instructing the seeker to detach the rational will (proairesis) from physical sensations, anger, and fear. Porphyry argues that while physical impulses may occur involuntarily, the soul must refuse to consciously participate in or "choose" them.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Purificatory Virtues" from Page 86. Having discussed the distancing of the soul from bodily sensations, Porphyry now addresses the internal state of the soul as it moves toward complete detachment (apatheia). ### SENTENCES

->87<-

<columns>
...images presented in sleep. In general, however, the intellectual soul of the one being purified is entirely pure of these things within. Let it resolve that regarding the movements toward the irrational part of the bodily passions, it shall move without **sympathy** <note>original Greek: *asympathōs*. In this context, "sympathy" means the soul "feeling along with" the body's impulses.</note> and without attachment, so that these movements are immediately dissolved by their proximity to the reasoning part. This state will exist while purification is still progressing; but henceforth, for those whom reason rules, the inferior part will feel shame, so that even this worse part is annoyed if it moves at all, because it did not keep quiet in the presence of its **master** <note>original Greek: *despotou*. Porphyry uses the metaphor of a master and servant to describe the relationship between the rational mind and the animal instincts.</note>, and it reproaches itself for its own weakness. And these are still what we call **moderated passions** <note>original Greek: *metriopatheiai*; Latin: *moderatae passiones*. The Stoic and Platonic stage where emotions are not yet gone, but are strictly controlled by reason.</note>, which, as they are mastered, give birth to a complete **freedom from passion** <note>original Greek: *apatheia*. This is the ultimate goal of the philosopher: a state where the soul is no longer even stirred by irrational impulses.</note>. For when the "co-feeling" disposition is completely purged away, then freedom from passion is present; this is because the passion only received its movement because reason had previously loosened the reins by its own inclination.

---

**35.** <note>The Latin text labels this sentence XXXV, while the Greek uses the numeral 30 (λ̅). This reflects the varying numbering systems in early modern editions of Porphyry.</note> Everything is "somewhere" according to its own nature, if indeed it is anywhere at all, and not in a state contrary to its nature. For a body subsisting in matter and bulk, to be "somewhere" is to be in a **place** <note>original Greek: *topos*. Porphyry is beginning a classic Neoplatonic argument: physical things occupy space, but incorporeal things (like the soul) exist in a different "where."</note>. Therefore, for the body of the world...

<column-break/>

...images presented to the mind through sleep. But the intellectual soul of the man who has been purified from all these things is itself entirely pure.
<margin>5</margin> Let it also strive so that the movements of the bodily affections toward the irrational part are moved without the will either consenting or paying them attention; so that these movements, by the
<margin>10</margin> proximity of reason, may immediately grow faint. This will not happen while purification is still being pursued, but rather for those in whom reason is held in its proper honor.
<margin>15</margin> That inferior part will stand in awe of reason, so much so that the worse part is indignant if it is moved at all, because it did not remain quiet in the
<margin>20</margin> presence of its master, and it reproaches itself for its own powerlessness. And these are indeed still "moderated passions," which finally advance into a total **void of affects** <note>original Latin: *vacuitatem affectuum*. This translates the Greek *apatheia*.</note>.
<margin>25</margin> For when all **compassion** <note>original Latin: *compassio*. Used here in the literal sense of "suffering with" the body, rather than the modern sense of pity.</note> has finally been purged away, then the void of passions is present. This is because the affect drew its movement from the fact that reason, by its own inclination, had as it were loosened the reins.
<margin>30</margin> ---
**XXXV.** Each thing is "somewhere" according to its own nature, if only it is indeed somewhere, and not in a state against nature. For a

<meta>This page follows Porphyry's discussion of the purification of the soul (Page 87). # PORPHYRY

### SENTENCES

->88<-

Since for the material and those things endowed with **mass** <note>original Latin: *mole*. In this context, it refers to physical bulk or weight that occupies space.</note>, "to be everywhere" consists in **extension** <note>original Latin: *interuallo*. This refers to spatial dimensions—length, width, and depth.</note> and the location of that extension. But for the **intellectual world** <note>original Latin: *mundo intellectuali*. The realm of pure Being and divine thought in Platonist philosophy, which exists beyond the physical universe.</note>, and for every immaterial thing without exception—since it is endowed with no mass and is not expanded by any extension—being in a place is not present in any way. To the extent that "being everywhere" for an incorporeal thing is not local. Therefore, neither will a part of it be here, while another part is there. For it will neither be outside of place, nor expanded by an extension; rather, it is **whole**, wherever it is. Neither is it indeed here, while elsewhere it is not. For in that way it would be contained by this place, and separated from that place. Nor is it far from this, and near to that: just as "far" and "near" are said of those things which by their nature exist in a place, according to the spaces of their extensions. Whence the world indeed is present to the intellectual realm by way of extension; but the incorporeal is present to the world neither divided by parts nor by spaces. Moreover, the undivided exists as a whole in that which is separated or expanded by spaces, according to whatever part of that space: existing as **one and the same in number** <note>This means that it is not a "copy" in each place, but the exact same singular entity appearing everywhere at once.</note>. But to that which is manifold by nature and expanded by magnitude, that which is indivisible and incapable of being multiplied is expanded and multiplied by magnitude. And so it enjoys it for...
<meta>catchword: as</meta>

<column-break/>

To the material and bulky world, "being everywhere" belongs in extension and in the place of extension; but to the **intellectual world** <margin>5</margin> and generally to the immaterial and inherently incorporeal, which is without bulk and without extension, "being in a place" is not present at all. Thus, "being everywhere" <margin>10</margin> for the incorporeal is not local; therefore, it does not have one part here and another part there. For in that case, it would be outside of place, and not without extension; but <margin>15</margin> it is **whole**, wherever it is. Neither is it here while not being elsewhere; for it would then be occupied by a place here, and absent from there. Neither <margin>20</margin> is it far from here and near to there; for "far" and "near" are said of things naturally existing in a place, according to the measures of distances. Whence <margin>25</margin> the world is present to the intellectual realm through extension; but the incorporeal is present to the world without parts or extension. And the partless, in that which has extension, becomes **whole** <margin>30</margin> throughout every part, being the same and **numerically one** <note>original Greek: *en arithmo*. A technical term meaning that despite its appearance in many places, its identity remains a single, undivided unit.</note>. Therefore, to that which is naturally multiplied and increased in size, the partless and non-multiplied is increased in size <margin>35</margin> and multiplied; and so it may receive it as it is naturally suited, not as that [the incorporeal] is...

...to the **indivisible** <note>original Greek: ἀμερεῖ. Literally "without parts" or "unpartitioned."</note> and **un-multiplied** nature, the divisible and multiplied is itself indivisible and un-multiplied; and thus it is present to it. That is: it is present without parts, without multiplicity, and without being bound by place—according to its own nature—to that which is divisible and multiplied by nature and exists in a place. But the divisible, multiplied, and localized thing is present to the other—which exists outside of these conditions—in a divided, multiplied, and localized manner. Therefore, in our investigations, we must hold fast to the property of each and not confuse their natures. Rather, one should not imagine or form opinions about the incorporeal based on the attributes of bodies or anything like them. For no one would ever attribute the properties of the purely incorporeal to bodies. For everyone is accustomed to bodies; but as for those incorporeal things, one hardly achieves knowledge of them, remaining uncertain about them and not immediately grasping them so long as one is dominated by the **imagination** <note>original Greek: φαντασίας. ...seen, nor in itself, because it is not spread out by any **mass** <note>original Latin: mole. This refers to physical bulk or volume.</note>. If the one is an image, while the other is the primary **exemplar** <note>original Latin: exemplar. An archetype or original model.</note>: the image obtains its being according to that which is perceived by the mind; but the other exists in itself, for every image is an image of the mind. But it is necessary to remember the property of both, and not to wonder that in their mutual meeting a transformation occurs—if indeed we should even call it a "meeting" or "conjunction." For we are not looking at a meeting of physical bodies, but of realities that entirely transcend one another according to the property of their **subsistence** <note>original Latin: subsistentiae; Greek: ὑποστάσεως. The fundamental reality or "way of being" of a thing.</note>. Therefore, this joining exceeds all those things usually considered regarding things of the same substance. For this reason, it is neither a blending, nor a mixture, nor a conjunction, nor an attachment; rather, it is a different mode: appearing in some way in all the shared relationships of **consubstantial** <note>original Latin: consubstantialium; Greek: ὁμοουσίων. Things sharing the same essence or substance.</note> things with one another, yet transcending everything that falls under the senses. It is present as a whole in all parts of an extended space, even if they were infinite, while it itself is spread out by no space. It is not present to a part by being divided into parts, sharing only a piece of itself; nor does it offer itself in a manifold way by being multiplied to match a multitude. Instead, it is present as a whole both to all the parts separated by mass, and to each individual part of the...

<column-break/>

...toward the **intelligible** <note>original Greek: νοητὸν. The realm of reality accessible to the mind rather than the senses.</note>, it possesses being; but the other exists in itself. For every image is an image of the mind, and is like a copy. One must, considering the property of <margin>5</margin> both, not wonder at the transformation in the meeting—if one must call it a meeting at all. For if we were indeed considering a meeting of bodies, [it would be different]; but we are looking at things <margin>10</margin> completely surpassing one another, according to the property of their **hypostasis** <note>original Greek: ὑποστάσεως. The underlying state or individual reality.</note>. Wherefore the meeting also surpasses those things usually observed <margin>15</margin> in things of the same essence. It is therefore neither a blending, nor a mixture, nor a meeting, nor an apposition; but another mode, which manifests <margin>20</margin> the communions of some things with others of the same essence in whatever way they occur; yet it surpasses all things that fall under perception. Within infinite parts, if <margin>25</margin> it so happens, of the **extended** <note>original Greek: διαστατοῦ. Having dimensions or intervals in space.</note>, the **unextended** <note>original Greek: ἀδιάστατον. Existing without spatial dimensions or parts.</note> is present as a whole; nor is it present in a divided way, giving a part to a part; nor, being multiplied, <margin>30</margin> does it provide itself to the multitude in a manifold way. Instead, it is present as a whole to all the parts of the **mass** <note>original Greek: ὄγκου. Physical volume or bulk.</note>, and to each one of the multitude, and to the whole mass, <margin>35</margin> and to the whole multitude, it is present impartibly and without multiplication, and as one in number. **37.** True being is neither great nor small; for "great" and "small" are properties specifically belonging to **mass** <note>original Greek: ὄγκου (onkou). In Neoplatonic philosophy, this refers to the physical bulk or volume that occupies space.</note>. But **Being** <note>original Greek: τὸ ὄν (to on). The fundamental reality that exists beyond the physical world.</note> exceeds both the great and the small; it is above both, and indeed above the maximum and the minimum, being one and the same in number; although it is shown to be perceived simultaneously through participation by everything greatest and everything smallest. Therefore, you should not conceive of it as the "maximum"; otherwise, you will wonder how that which is the maximum is present to the smallest masses without being diminished or contracted. Nor should you think of it as the "minimum"; for again you will wonder how that which is the minimum is present to the greatest masses without being multiplied, or increased, or extended. ...and of its multitude; and to the entire mass and the entire multitude, it is present undivided and not multiplied, and as if one in number.

<margin>10</margin> they measure the nature of the other; indeed, they even doubt the substance, which from its own [perception] seems to have passed into that [of the body].
**XXXVII.** True being is neither
<margin>15</margin> great nor small. For great and small are proper to **corporeal mass** <note>original Latin: moli corporeæ. The physical "stuff" or dimensions of a body.</note>. But [Being] exceeds both great and small, above both, and indeed even above
<margin>20</margin> the maximum and the minimum, being one and the same in number; although it is perceived to be grasped simultaneously through participation by every maximum and minimum. Therefore,

**38.** That which is greater in physical bulk <note>original Latin: *mole corporea*; Greek: *ὄγκῳ* (onkō). Therefore, that which most excels in strength or power is foreign to all corporeal magnitude. For its power, returning into itself, [is] full...

# SENTENCES.

<header>SENTENCES.</header>

...[concentrated], it becomes more perfect, and strengthening itself, it possesses its own power. Therefore, as the body has advanced into <term>bulk</term> <note>original Greek: *onkos*; a technical term in Neoplatonism for the "mass" or "density" of the physical world, which is seen as a dilution of spiritual power.</note>, it has departed into a decrease of power compared to the power of the incorporeal, <term>True Being</term> <note>original Greek: *ontōs on*; refers to the ultimate reality or spiritual substance in the Platonic tradition, which is unchanging and non-spatial.</note>, just as True Being was not emptied out into bulk, remaining in the magnitude of that same power because it is without bulk. Therefore, just as True Being is without magnitude or bulk compared to physical bulk, so the corporeal is weak and powerless compared to True Being. For that which is greatest in magnitude of power is lacking in bulk. Thus, although the world exists everywhere, and meets with the True Being that is (as they say) everywhere, it is not able to grasp the magnitude of its power. It meets it, not as if Being were present in a divided way, but as something present without magnitude and without division. Therefore, its presence is not local, but **assimilative** <note>original Greek: *homoiōtikē*; meaning the relationship is based on "becoming like" the divine through likeness, rather than through physical proximity or location.</note>; insofar as it is possible for a body to be made like the incorporeal, and for the incorporeal to be seen within a body that has become like it. And yet, the incorporeal is not present, insofar as it is impossible for the material to be made like that which is purely immaterial; and it is present insofar as the corporeal is able to be made like the incorporeal; in one way still and through

<column-break/>

...becomes stronger and more perfect; and strengthening itself, it obtains its own powers. For this reason, the body, being extended in **bulk** <note>original Latin: *mole*; parallel to the Greek *onkos*.</note>, recedes by a diminution of strength from the power of the incorporeal and True Being, just as True Being is not consumed or emptied by corporeal bulk, remaining in the magnitude of the same power, because it lacks all corporeal bulk. Just as, therefore, True Being in respect to corporeal bulk lacks all magnitude and bulk, so the corporeal, in respect to True Being, is weak and destitute of strength and power. For that which is greatest in magnitude of power lacks all corporeal bulk. Thus, although the world exists everywhere and is present everywhere to the True Being which truly and in reality exists everywhere, it is unable to embrace the magnitude of its power. It is present, however, not dividedly or in parts, but to that which is present without magnitude and indefinitely. Therefore, the presence is not local <note>original Latin: *localis*; referring to physical location in space.</note>, but **assimilative**: insofar as a body can be assimilated to the incorporeal, and the incorporeal can be perceived by contemplation in a body assimilated to itself. The incorporeal is therefore not present, insofar as the material cannot be assimilated to the purely immaterial; and it is present insofar as the corporeal can be assimilated to the incorporeal; nor indeed truly through

--- Page 105 ---
<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s metaphysical exploration of how the spiritual realm (True Being) interacts with the physical world. The previous section discussed why the incorporeal remains undivided even when it is present throughout the material world. Here, Porphyry concludes that if the material and immaterial were to truly "merge" or "grasp" one another physically, both would be destroyed by the change in their fundamental natures.</meta>

# PORPHYRY

->PORPHYRY<-

through grasping <note>Latin: *comprehensionem*; Greek: *καταδοχῆς*. In this context, it refers to a physical containment or a total merging of substances.</note>: for both would be destroyed: since the material, in receiving the immaterial, would be changed into it, while the immaterial would become material. Therefore, through such a great diversity of substance, the likenesses and participations of powers and weaknesses pass between one another, being poured from one into the other. For this reason, the world is most distantly removed from the power of **Being** <note>Latin: *ens*; Greek: *on*. This refers to the ultimate, stable reality of the spiritual world.</note>, and Being is far removed from the weakness of the material. But that which is in the middle—the thing that makes like and is made like, joining the extremes together—has provided the cause of error regarding those extremes, by joining one to the other through this likeness.

**XXXIX.** But **True Being** is said to be "many"; not by diverse locations, nor by the measurements of corporeal **bulk** <note>Latin: *moles*; Greek: *onkos*. A technical term for the density or "massiness" of physical matter which limits spiritual power.</note>; not by accumulation, nor by the outlines or grasps of divisible parts. Instead, it is divided into a multitude through an immaterial, non-physical, and non-multiplying **diversity** <note>Greek: *heterotēti*. Porphyry is explaining how spiritual things can be "many" (like different ideas) without being separated by distance or physical parts.</note>. And for this reason, it is also "one": not as a single body is one, nor as if it were in one place, nor as a single mass, nor as a "one" that many parts constitute. For insofar as it is one, it is diverse; and its diversity is both divided and united. For its diversity is neither an external addition, nor is it accidental to it, nor

<column-break/>

through reception. For each would be destroyed: the material, in receiving the immaterial, through a change into it; and the immaterial, by having become material. Therefore, likenesses and participations of powers and weaknesses pass between these things of such different substances <note>Greek: *heterousia*; things possessing a different essence or nature.</note>, from one into the other. Consequently, there is a great distance: for the world, from the power of Being; and for Being, from the weakness of the material. But that which is in the middle—the thing that makes like and is made like, joining the extremes—has become the cause of the deception regarding the extremes: by looking at one through the likeness of the other.

**39.** That which **Truly Is** <note>Greek: *To ontōs on*. This is the core Platonic term for ultimate reality.</note> is said to be "many," not by different places, nor by the measures of bulk, nor by a heap, nor by the sketches or boundaries of divisible parts; but it is divided into a multitude by an immaterial, non-bulky, and non-multiplying diversity. Therefore, it is also "one": not as one body, nor as if in a place, nor as one bulk, nor as one composed of many. For insofar as it is one, it is different; and its difference is divided and united. For its diversity is not acquired from the outside, nor is it episodic <note>Greek: *epeisodiōdēs*; occurring like an added scene in a play, rather than being essential to the plot.</note> to it, nor [from another...]

neither; <note>Latin catchword: *neq;*</note>
from— <note>Greek catchword: *με-* (the beginning of *metokhē*, meaning "participation")</note>

<summary>Porphyry explains that the spiritual and material realms remain distinct to prevent their mutual destruction, though they interact through "likeness." He defines "True Being" as a unity that contains diversity without being divided by physical space or mass.</summary>
<keywords>Porphyry, Neoplatonism, True Being, bulk, immateriality, diversity, unity, participation, metaphysics</keywords>

--- Page 106 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Sentence XXXIX (39) from Porphyry’s *Sententiae* regarding the nature of "True Being" (the intelligible or spiritual realm) versus the nature of physical bodies. The previous page established that the immaterial does not merge physically with the material. This page explores how the spiritual can be "many" while remaining "one," contrasted with how physical objects are "one" only by being held together externally.</meta>

# SENTENCES

->I<-

[is not] divided <note>The text continues from the previous page: Being is not divided by outside forces.</note>, but is many within itself. For it acts through all its activities while remaining stable;
<margin>5</margin> whence it established all **diversity** <note>Greek: *heterotēta*; Latin: *diuersitatem*. This refers to the "otherness" or variety of ideas within the divine mind.</note> through **identity** <note>Greek: *tautotētos*; Latin: *identitatem*. The state of being exactly the same; the spiritual realm is diverse yet remains a single, identical essence.</note>. This is not because of a difference of one thing seen in relation to another, as is the case with
<margin>10</margin> bodies. For in bodies, the opposite is true: **unity** exists within diversity. It is as if diversity holds the primary place within them, while unity
<margin>15</margin> happens to them from the outside and as an addition. For in the case of **True Being** <note>Greek: *ontos*; Latin: *vero ente*. The ultimate reality that exists beyond the physical world.</note>, unity and identity precede; but diversity arises from the activities which act toward unity.
<margin>20</margin> Therefore, that [Being] is multiplied within the **indivisible**; but this [body] is united within its multitude and **bulk** <note>Greek: *ongkō*; Latin: *corpus*. This refers to physical mass or volume that takes up space.</note>. And while that [Being] is established within itself, existing as one within itself,
<margin>25</margin> this [body] is never within itself, but has received its existence as if in **ecstasy** <note>Greek: *ekstasei*; Latin: *progressu*. Literally "standing outside oneself." Porphyry means that physical things are spread out in space and thus are never "centered" or "at home" in the way a thought or soul is.</note>. Thus, the One is all-acting; but the multitude is "made one."
<margin>30</margin> One must therefore examine closely how that [Being] is "one and another" <note>Meaning it contains variety within its oneness.</note>; and how this [body] is "multitude and one"; and not exchange the unique properties of the one for the properties
<margin>35</margin> belonging to the other.

->XL. It must not be thought<-
<margin>the</margin>

<column-break/>

nor by participation in another, but many within itself. For it operates with all its powers while existing as one:
<margin>5</margin> because it brings forth all diversity through identity; not considered as a difference of one thing to another, in the manner of
<margin>10</margin> bodies. For in these, the matter stands in a contrary way, and unity consists within diversity. Indeed, the first and principal thing in
<margin>15</margin> them is diversity: while unity comes from the outside and as an accessory. For in True Being, unity and identity precede: but diversity is added, because
<margin>20</margin> unity brings forth various operations. Therefore, that [Being] is multiplied in an indivisible unity: but the body is united in a multitude. And while that [Being] is situated within
<margin>25</margin> itself, existing in itself according to the One: this [body] by no means subsists within itself, as it has obtained its existence by a **progression** <note>Latin: *progressu*. The Latin translator uses "progression" to render the Greek "ecstasy," suggesting that material things have "moved out" or "flowed away" from the center of reality.</note>.
<margin>30</margin> That One is therefore operating in all things: but this multitude is made one. Wherefore, it must be more closely scrutinized how that [Being] is "one and diverse"; and again
<margin>35</margin> how this [body] is "multitude and one": nor should the properties of the one be swapped with the properties of the other.

->XL. It must not be thought,<-
<margin>because</margin>

<summary>Porphyry distinguishes between spiritual "Being," which is inherently one but contains diverse powers, and physical "Bodies," which are naturally diverse and only unified by external force. He warns against confusing these two types of existence, noting that physical things exist "outside themselves" in space.</summary>
<keywords>Porphyry, Neoplatonism, Unity, Diversity, Identity, Being, Corporeal, Ecstasy, Multiplicity, Lucas Holstenius</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the exploration of Porphyry's Sentence XXXIX (39). Having previously established that unity precedes diversity in the spiritual realm (True Being), the author now explains how individual souls relate to the Universal Soul. He argues that souls are not "cut up" by physical bodies, but exist in a state of "many-in-one" similar to how various subjects of knowledge exist within a single mind.</meta>

### PORPHYRII
**ξ**
<meta>catchword: differ—</meta>

[It is not] that the multitude of souls
is produced because of the multitude
of bodies; rather, before bodies exist,
the souls exist as both many and as one.

soul prevent the many from existing

one among themselves. For they are 
divided from one another, not by a 
<margin>10</margin>severing <note>Latin: *defectu*; Greek: *apokopeisai*. Porphyry means they aren't "cut off" like pieces of a physical object, which lose their connection to the whole when separated.</note>; nor do they tear apart that

tiny particles. They are joined to 
one another, yet not confused among 
themselves, nor do they create a whole 
<margin>15</margin>by way of a heap or a pile <note>A "heap" (Latin: *acerui*) implies a collection of separate parts that only touch on the outside. Porphyry argues the soul's unity is deeper and more internal.</note>; for they

physical boundaries. Nor again are they 
jumbled together—just as many different

# PORPHYRY

possesses the universal power
of the parts: so also in the soul
free from matter, whatever is 
conceived of as its part
possesses the power of the 
whole soul: whatever, however, 
inclines toward matter, 
possesses indeed that <term>Form</term> <note>original Latin: "formam"; Greek: "eidos". In this context, it refers to the specific shaping principle or "blueprint" of a physical thing.</note> 
into which it inclines, and it
can enjoy the fellowship of the
immaterial species (even if it 
has matter joined to itself) 
whenever, withdrawing itself
from the material, it turns
back toward its own self. 
Since indeed, by sinking into 
matter, it suffers a lack 
of all things, and also a loss 
or an emptying of its 
own powers: but conversely, 
having been raised up to the 
<term>Mind</term> <note>original Latin: "mentem"; Greek: "noun" (Intellect). In Neoplatonism, the "Mind" or "Intellect" is the divine realm of pure thought above the soul.</note>, 
it experiences the fullness 
of the powers of the whole soul: 
the former state they rightly 
called Penury <note>original Latin: "inopiam"; Greek: "penia". This is an allusion to Plato's "Symposium," where Penury (Poverty) is the mother of Love.</note>, 
but this latter state they 
called Satiety <note>original Latin: "satietatem"; Greek: "koros" (Abundance/Fullness). Porphyry is explaining that the soul's "hunger" or "wealth" depends on its focus.</note>, 
those who first of all 
hinted at this fullness 
of the soul.

[The soul] has the whole 
power in each of the 
parts; in the case of the 
immaterial soul, that which 
<margin>5</margin> is conceived as a part 
has the power of the entire 
soul. That which inclines 
toward matter, might indeed 
rule in accordance with the

<margin>10</margin> immaterial form, even if

from the immaterial, it

however, inclining toward 
matter, it would have a 
<margin>15</margin> poverty <note>original Greek: "aporian", meaning a state of being at a loss or without resources.</note> 
of all things, and an 
emptying of its own power; 
but being led back to the

have its fullness according 
to the power of the whole; 
<margin>20</margin> the former [they called] 
Penury and the latter Satiety, 
those who first knowing 
this hinted at the 
fullness of the soul.

> At the head of the harbor is a slender-leaved olive tree.
> Nearby is a pleasant, shadowy cave,
> Sacred to the nymphs who are called Naiads <note>water nymphs</note>.

> One toward the North, for men to descend,
> But the other toward the South is more divine;
> No men enter by that way, for it is the path of the immortals.

--- Page 113 ---
<meta>This page continues Porphyry's "On the Cave of the Nymphs" in the 1630 Holstenius edition. On the previous page, the author began posing the question of what Homer intended by his specific description of a cave in Ithaca. This page provides the Latin verse translation of the Homeric passage followed by the beginning of the philosophical commentary, presented in parallel Latin and Greek columns. Porphyry argues that the cave is not a literal, physical location but a symbolic or allegorical one.</meta>

# PORPHYRY
<page-num>102</page-num>

*An olive tree with spreading branches stands at the head of the harbor:*
*Near it, a pleasant and dark cave recedes,*
*A sacred house for the Nymphs, who are called Naiads.*
<note>original Latin: Najades. In Greek mythology, Naiads are nymphs associated with fresh water, such as springs and streams.</note>
*Inside are mixing bowls, and wide jars made of living marble;*
<note>original Latin: marmore vivo. "Living" stone refers to natural rock formations or stone still attached to the earth, rather than quarried stone.</note>
*There, bees deposit their honey with a sweet murmur.*
*Inside there are also long stone looms, where the Nymphs*
*Weave purple webs, a wonder to behold.*
*Inside are perpetual waters; but the door is twofold:*
*One, facing the north wind, admits men; but the other,*
*Looking toward the south, is more divine; it is entirely impassable*
*For men, and offers a path for the immortals alone.*

---

Indeed, it is evident that the poet did not make mention of this cave based on historical truth. This appears from the fact that those who have described the geography of that island make no mention of any such cave, as Cronius <note>Cronius was a 2nd-century Pythagorean philosopher whose works are mostly lost, except for fragments preserved by Porphyry.</note> affirms. This is also manifest: it would be absurd if, having fashioned a cave through poetic license—a figment of a story thought up recklessly and at whim—a mortal man hoped he would find credit for his story, opening paths for men and gods by some new art in the region of Ithaca. Or, if it were not a man, but nature itself that had there shown a descent for all men and another way for all gods. For the whole world is full of men and gods. But it is far from being persuasive that in an Ithacan cave there is a descent for men and an ascent for gods.

<meta>catchword: By these (original: Hisce)</meta>

<column-break/>

<margin>15</margin> That he did not record this memory based on history, those who have written accounts of the island make clear, <margin>20</margin> mentioning no such cave upon the island, as Cronius says. And that he would be <margin>25</margin> unbelievable if he fashioned it by poetic license—fashioning something just by chance and as it happened—hoping to persuade anyone that in the land of Ithaca a man <margin>30</margin> had engineered paths for men and gods. Or if it were not a man, but nature itself that had there designated a descent for all men, and again another path for all gods, it is clear [it would be absurd]. For the <margin>35</margin> whole world is full of men and gods. But the Ithacan cave is far from being able to persuade anyone that within it there is a descent for men and an ascent for gods.

<meta>catchword: gods (original: θεῶν)</meta>

<summary>This page presents Homer’s poetic description of the Cave of the Nymphs and begins Porphyry’s argument that the cave is an allegory rather than a real geographical feature of Ithaca.</summary>
<keywords>Homer, Odyssey, Ithaca, Naiads, Cronius, Allegory, Neoplatonism, Gates of the Sun, Immortality</keywords>

--- Page 114 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical analysis of Homer’s "Cave of the Nymphs" from the Odyssey. On the previous page, Porphyry (citing Cronius) argued that Homer’s description of the cave in Ithaca is not geographically literal but a symbolic allegory. ->...and the olive plant nearby.<- 
All of these details to track down and explain was considered a difficult task even for the ancients; and we too, following them, now attempt to discover these things through our own efforts. Regarding the local history of the place, those who believed the cave and everything told about it were entirely fictions of the poet <note>The "poet" here refers to Homer, whom the Neoplatonists viewed as a divinely inspired sage rather than a mere storyteller.</note> seem to have written rather carelessly. It is narrow and high, possessing a harbor called Phorcys. There is a shore within it, and there a cave sacred to the nymphs; it is where Odysseus is said to have been set ashore by the Phaeacians." Therefore, it cannot be an entirely Homeric fiction; but whether he described the site exactly as it was, or whether he himself added certain elements, the questions nonetheless remain: to investigate the intention of either those who established the sanctuary, or of the poet who handed down...

# PORPHYRY
<page-num>105</page-num>

...that the ancients consecrated temples with mythical symbols; nor was **Homer** accustomed to narrating anything about them at random. But indeed, the more one attempts to show that the narration concerning the cave is not a fiction of **Homer’s**, but that it was already consecrated to the Gods before the time of **Homer**, the more it will be discovered that this sacred cave is filled with ancient wisdom. 

For that reason, it is worthy of investigation; and its symbolic consecration must necessarily be explained more clearly and, as it were, brought into the light. The ancients rightly and deservedly consecrated caves and caverns to the **World** <note>original Latin: *Mundo*; Greek: *κόσμῳ* (Kosmos). In this context, it refers to the ordered universe or the physical realm of existence.</note>, whether taken as a whole or in its individual parts. They did this in such a way that they attributed "earth" as a symbol for **matter** <note>original Latin: *materiæ*; Greek: *ὕλης* (hyle). To a Neoplatonist, matter is the passive, underlying substance that receives the form of the divine ideas.</note> from which the world consists. 

Hence, in this passage, some were of the opinion that "earth" should be understood as matter, signifying the world created from matter by the use of caves as a symbol. This is because caves are, for the most part, naturally formed and are akin and related to the earth itself, enclosed by a uniform rock; the interior of which is hollow, while the exterior extends into the immense space of the earth. Furthermore, the world—generated by its own nature and mutually related to itself—is closely linked to matter, which the secret symbols signify by "stone" and "rock"...

<column-break/>

...the poet’s tracks, since the ancients did not establish their sacred places without mythical symbols, nor did **Homer** recount these things by mere chance. For the more one strives to show that the details of the cave are not a fabrication of **Homer**, but were dedicated to the gods by those before **Homer's** time, the more the offering will be found to be full of ancient wisdom. 

Because of this, it is worthy of research, and the symbolic consecration within it requires an explanation. Indeed, the ancients quite properly consecrated caves and caverns to the **World** [the Cosmos], whether taking it as a whole or according to its parts. They handed down the "earth" as a symbol of the **matter** from which the world is made. 

Wherefore some, following this line of thought, posited that matter is the earth; they introduced the world that arises from matter through the symbol of caves. This is because caves are generally self-formed and share the same nature as the earth, being surrounded by a single kind of rock. Of this rock, the inner parts are hollow, while the outer parts are raised into the infinite expanse of the earth. The world is self-generated and self-harmonizing, being naturally attached to matter—which the ancients called "stone" and "rock" because of its inert and resist- 

<note>original Greek: ἀντί- (anti-). This is the start of the word *antitypia*, referring to the "resistance" or "solidity" of matter.</note>

# ON THE CAVE OF THE NYMPHS

...they hinted that it is a counter-image to **form** <note>original Greek: *εἶδος* (eidos). In Platonic thought, Form is the eternal, perfect blueprint of a thing, while matter is the imperfect substance that receives that shape.</note>; establishing it as infinite according to its own formlessness. But because it is fluid, and by its own nature lacks the **form** by which it is shaped and made visible, they appropriately adopted the watery, moist, and dark nature of caves—which the Poet <note>Referring to Homer.</note> called 
<margin>5</margin> "misty"—as a symbol of the qualities that belong to the world because of **matter** <note>original Latin: *materia*; Greek: *ὕλη* (hyle). To the Neoplatonist, matter is the "lowest" level of reality, associated with darkness and non-being.</note>. Because of matter, then, the world is misty and dark; but through the intertwining and arrangement of form—from which it also 
<margin>10</margin> derived the name **World** <note>original Greek: *κόσμος* (Kosmos), which means both "the universe" and "order" or "ornament." Porphyry is making a pun: the world is called *Kosmos* because it is "ordered" and "beautiful."</note>—it is beautiful and lovely. 

Hence it might rightly be called a cave: lovely indeed to one first 
<margin>15</margin> encountering it, because of its participation in the forms; but misty to one who contemplates its foundation and enters into it with the mind. Thus, the exterior and superficial parts are lovely, but the interior and deep parts are misty. 

In the same way, the **Persians**, 
<margin>20</margin> mystically signifying the descent of souls into the lower regions and their return, initiate the mystic in a place they call a "cavern." For as **Eubulus** <note>A lost author who wrote a history of Mithras.</note> says, **Zoroaster** was the first to consecrate a natural cave in the 
<margin>25</margin> nearby mountains of Persia—a flowery cave...
<meta>catchword: and</meta>

<column-break/>

...they call it by this designation: because it is inert and idle, and receives the impression of form. They established it as infinite on account of its formlessness; but since it is fluid, and by itself lacks the **species** <note>The Latin translation of *eidos* (form).</note> by which it is shaped and perceived, they 
<margin>5</margin> aptly used the well-watered humidity and shadowy obscurity—to use the words of the poet—as a symbol of those things which are present in the world because of matter. 

Therefore, on account of matter, the world is obscure and gloomy; but because of the presence and 
<margin>10</margin> adornment of the added form (from which it also derived the name "world"), it is beautiful and pleasant. Whence it can rightly and deservedly be called a cave: pleasant indeed to one first entering, because of the participation in forms; but obscure to 
<margin>15</margin> him who considers its lowest depth and enters it with the mind. To such an extent that the exterior parts, and those which first occur on the surface, are pleasant; but its interior and profound parts are obscure.

In the same way, the **Persians**, 
<margin>20</margin> mystically signifying the descent of the soul into the lower regions and its return, initiate the priest, the name of "cavern" having been given to the place itself. Since, according to the author **Eubulus**, **Zoroaster**...

# PORPHYRY

...was the first of all to consecrate a natural cave in the nearby mountains of Persia—blooming and watered by springs—in honor of Mithras <note>original Latin: *Mithræ*; Greek: *Μίθρου*. Mithras was the central deity of a "mystery religion" popular in the Roman Empire, often associated with the sun and cosmic order.</note>, the creator and father of all. He did this so that the cave might provide him with a figure of the **world** <note>original Greek: *κόσμου* (kosmos). To a Neoplatonist, the world is a structured, ordered whole.</note> established by Mithras; those things indeed which were inside the cave, arranged at certain intervals from one another, bore the symbols or figures of the mundane elements and climates. Then, after Zoroaster <note>The ancient Persian prophet. Porphyry credits him with initiating the practice of using caves for sacred rites.</note>, the custom also prevailed among others to perform initiations and sacred rites in caves and caverns, whether natural or handmade. For just as they established temples, shrines, and altars for the **celestial Gods**; but for **earthly gods** and heroes, altars; and for the **subterranean gods**, pits and small shrines: so they dedicated caves and caverns to the **world**. Likewise they dedicated them to the **nymphs**, because of the waters dripping and gushing in caves; over which the **Naiad** nymphs <note>Water nymphs who presided over fountains, wells, and brooks.</note>, as we shall soon discuss, preside. Nor did they make the cave a symbol only of the generated—that is, the perceptible—world, as we have said; but they also adopted the same as a symbol for all **invisible powers** <note>original Greek: *ἀοράτων δυνάμεων* (aoratōn dynamōn). These are the spiritual or intellectual forces that exist beyond the reach of the five senses.</note>. This is because caves are dark, and the essence of those powers is unknown. Hence also Saturn <note>The Roman name for the Greek titan Kronos, often associated with time and the primordial state of the universe.</note>...
<meta>catchword: urnus</meta>

<column-break/>

...and having springs, he dedicated it in honor of the creator and father of all, Mithras, the cave bearing for him an image of the **world** which Mithras had fashioned. Within the cave, at proportionate distances, were symbols representing the cosmic elements and regions. After this Zoroaster, the practice also took hold among others to offer up sacred rites through caves and caverns, whether naturally formed or made by hand. For as they established temples, sanctuaries, and altars for the **Olympian gods**; hearths for **earthly gods** and heroes; and pits and chambers for **underworld gods**; so they dedicated caves and caverns to the **world**. Likewise, they did so for the **nymphs** because of the waters that flow down or rise up within caves, over which the **Naiads**—as we shall shortly explain—preside as nymphs. And it was not only as a symbol of the world—that is, the generated and sensible world—that they used the cave, as we said; but they also took the cave as a symbol of all **invisible powers**, because caves are dark, and the essential nature of these powers is unseen. Thus also Kronos <note>Original Greek: *Κρόνος*. The father of Zeus, whose reign was considered a Golden Age; here he is linked to the primordial "ocean" or depths.</note> in the Oce-

<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s Neoplatonic interpretation of the cave as a religious and philosophical symbol. It transitions from mythological examples (Saturn and Ceres) to the formal philosophical allegory found in Plato’s Republic, establishing the cave as a metaphor for the physical, sensory world.</meta>

# PORPHYRY

->*At last we have reached this cave lying before us.*<-
<note>original Latin: *Hoc te sub obiectum tandem peruenimus antrum.* A poetic line, likely adapted from Virgil's *Aeneid*, used here to introduce the specific discussion of the cave's nature.</note>

And in Plato, in the seventh book of the **Republic**, it is said: *Behold, for there are men as if in a subterranean cave, and in a dwelling similar to a cavern, whose entrance lies open toward the light widely throughout the whole cave.* Then, where the other speaker says: *You describe an absurd likeness,* he <note>Socrates, the narrator of the Republic.</note> adds: *Therefore, O friend Glaucon, this likeness must be applied to all that has been said before. This dwelling, indeed, which we perceive with our eyes, is to be likened to the dwelling of a prison; but the light of the fire shining within it, to the power of the sun.* 

**That** the theologians <note>Porphyry uses "theologians" to refer to ancient divinely-inspired poets and philosophers like Orpheus, Homer, and Plato.</note> therefore established caves as a symbol of the world and the worldly powers is clearly seen from these things. But it was already said before that they established the same as a symbol of the **intelligible substance** <note>original Latin: *intelligibilis substantiae*; Greek: *νοητῆς οὐσίας*. This refers to the realm of pure thought, Ideas, or Forms that exist beyond the physical senses.</note> as well; though they were moved by different, and not the same, reasons. For they placed them as symbols of the **sensible world** <note>The physical world perceived through the five senses.</note> because caves are dark, stony, and moist. They determined the world to be of this kind because of the matter from which it consists, being both resistant <note>original Latin: *obnoxium*; Greek: *ἀντίτυπον* (antitypon). Suggesting matter is a hard, resistant reflection of higher realities.</note> and in a state of flux. But of the intellectual [world]...

<column-break/>

And in Plato, in <margin>5</margin> the seventh book of the Republic, it is said: *Behold, for there are men as if in a subterranean cave and in a cave-like dwelling, having its entrance open to the light, extending along the whole cave.* Then, when the one <margin>10</margin> talking with him says: *You speak of a strange image,* he adds: *This image, then, O dear Glaucon, must be applied to all the things said before. The <margin>15</margin> seat appearing through sight is to be likened to the dwelling of the prison; and the light of the fire in it to the power of the sun.* That <margin>20</margin> the theologians established caves as a symbol of the world and the cosmic powers has been made clear through this. But it has already been said that they are also a symbol <margin>25</margin> of the **intelligible substance**; however, they did so starting from different and not the same conceptions. For [they made it a symbol] of the **sensible world** because caves <margin>30</margin> are dark, and stony, and very moist. And they established that the world is such because of the matter from which the world is composed, being both resistant and flowing. But of the <margin>35</margin> **intelligible** [world], because it is invisible to them...

<meta>catchword: by that</meta>

<summary>Porphyry connects the "Cave of the Nymphs" to Plato's famous Allegory of the Cave, explaining that a cave serves as a dual symbol for both the dark, shifting physical world of matter and the hidden, invisible realm of the intellect.</summary>
<keywords>Plato's Republic, Allegory of the Cave, Glaucon, symbolism, sensible world, intelligible substance, matter, flux, theologians</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Porphyry's explanation of cave symbolism. Previously, he established that caves can represent both the physical (sensible) world and the spiritual (intelligible) realm. He now moves into the specific qualities of caves that make them suitable symbols for material existence.</meta>

# ON THE CAVE OF THE NYMPHS. 111

...because it is not open to the sense of sight: then on account of the solidity and firmness of substance <note>original Latin: *substantiæ*; Greek: *οὐσίας*. In Neoplatonism, this refers to the fundamental reality of a thing.</note>, since even particular powers are inconspicuous in the same way, especially those joined with matter. For, looking at the natural constitution of caves, their nocturnal and dark gloom, and their stony hardness, they established them as symbols—not at all with respect to their shape, as some suspected. For not all caves are round, like this double-opening <note>original Greek: *δίθυρον* (dithyron); Latin: *bipatens*. Refers to the cave in Homer's Odyssey having two entrances, one for gods and one for mortals.</note> cave of Homer. Furthermore, since the cave is twofold, they took it to be a symbol not only of the sensible, but also of the intelligible substance. 

Just as this present cave, because it has perennial <note>Ever-flowing or eternal.</note> waters, will bear the symbol not of an intellectual substance, but of one joined with matter. And for that reason, it is sacred not to the mountain-dwellers, nor those of the heights or the peaks, or others of this kind, but to the **Naiads**, who are so named from springs (which the Greeks call *namata* <note>original Greek: νάματα, meaning flowing streams or spring water.</note>). 

Indeed, we properly call the powers presiding over waters "Naiad Nymphs"; but they also commonly gave this name to all souls descending into generation <note>The process of being born into a physical body.</note>. They believed that souls settled near the water, which is fostered by a divine spirit, as **Numenius** <note>A 2nd-century philosopher who bridged Pythagoreanism and Platonism; he is a major influence on Porphyry.</note> says: and for this reason he says the prophet <note>Referring to Moses, the presumed author of Genesis.</note> spoke: *The Spirit of God was carried over the water.*

<column-break/>

<note>This column contains the Latin translation of the Greek text provided in the first column, following the same philosophical argument.</note>

...because it is not open to the sense of the eyes: then because of the solidity and firmness of substance, since even particular virtues <note>In this context, 'virtues' (Latin: *virtutes*) refers to inherent powers or qualities of a thing.</note> are inconspicuous in the same way: especially those which are joined with matter. For, regarding the natural constitution of caves, and the nocturnal and dark gloom, and the stony hardness, they established symbols: but by no means with respect to the figure, as some suspected. Since not all caves are round, like this double-opening one of Homer. 

Moreover, since the cave is twofold, they received it not only for the sensible, but also for the intelligible substance. Just as this present cave, because it has perennial waters, will carry the symbol not of the intellectual, but of the substance joined with matter. And for that reason it is sacred not to the mountain-dwelling [nymphs], nor the airy ones, or those dwelling on the ridges, or others of this sort; but to the **Naiads**, who are so named from springs (which the Greeks call *namata*). 

Indeed, we properly call the virtues presiding over the waters "Naiad Nymphs": but they also commonly called all souls undergoing generation by this name. They thought these souls sat by the water, which is fostered by the divine spirit, as **Numenius** says: and therefore that the prophet said: *The Spirit of God was carried over...*

<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s allegorical interpretation of Homer’s Cave of the Nymphs. ...through waters. The Egyptians also, for this reason, represent all Daemons <note>original: *Dæmones*; Greek: *δαίμονας*. In this context, these are not "demons" in the modern sense, but intermediary spirits or guiding powers.</note> not standing on solid ground, but all upon a ship. This includes the sun itself and, in short, all those whom one must know as souls descending into generation <note>The process of being born into the physical world of matter.</note>, who fly over the moisture. Hence Heraclitus also says: *It is a delight, not death, for souls to become moist.* Delight for them is the fall into generation. And elsewhere he says: *We live their death, and they die our life.* <note>Heraclitus Fragment 62; a paradoxical statement suggesting that the "life" of the soul in a body is actually a form of spiritual death, and vice versa.</note> Therefore, the poet <note>Homer.</note> calls men engaged in generation *dieres* <note>original Greek: διερὲς. A Homeric term usually meaning "fluid" or "living."</note>, that is, "moist," because they have souls drenched in moisture. For this reason, these souls rejoice in blood and moist seed; but for the souls of plants, water provides nourishment. Some even assert that the aerial and celestial bodies are nourished by the vapors of springs and rivers and other exhalations. But it was the view of the Stoics <note>A school of Hellenistic philosophy.</note> that the sun is nourished by the exhalation of the sea; the moon by that which is emitted from the waters of springs and rivers; and the stars from the exhalation of the earth. Therefore, the Sun is a certain intellectual knot <note>original Latin: *compagem intellectualem*; Greek: *ἄμμα νοερὸν*. A "knot" or "bond" of fire/intelligence.</note> from the sea; the moon from river waters; and the stars from the ear-

Hence <margin>10</margin> Heraclitus also says: *It is a delight, not death, for souls to become moist.* Delight for them is the fall into generation. And elsewhere he <margin>15</margin> says: *We live their death, and they die our life.* Therefore, the poet calls those in generation *dieres* <note>original: διερὲς, meaning "moist" or "fluid."</note>, those having <margin>20</margin> moist souls. Thus, blood and moist seed are dear to these souls. But for the souls of plants, water is the nourishment. <meta>This page continues Porphyry’s allegorical analysis of Homer’s Cave of the Nymphs. Having previously discussed how celestial bodies are nourished by moisture, he now explains the Neoplatonic theory of how souls become "wet" or "thickened" as they descend from the spiritual realm into physical, "moist" bodies.</meta>

->### ON THE CAVE OF THE NYMPHS.<-

**Left Column**

...from waters; but the stars arise from the exhalations of the earth. It is necessary, therefore, that souls—whether they are bodily in nature, or incorporeal but attracted to a body—
<margin>5</margin> and especially those about to sink into blood and moist bodies, should incline toward the moist and become "embodied" by being moistened. For this reason, the souls of the dead are said to approach the outpouring of bile and blood <note>In ancient Greek ritual, the dead were believed to be attracted to the blood of sacrifices to regain a temporary vitality.</note>.
<margin>10</margin> And indeed, those souls that love the body, by attracting a moist spirit <note>original: *pneuma*; the "breath" or ethereal vehicle that carries the soul into the body.</note>, cause it to thicken like a cloud. For moisture thickened by air
<margin>15</margin> constitutes a cloud. When the spirit within them is thickened by an abundance of moisture, they become visible. And from such a state, those souls becoming embodied
<margin>20</margin> color the spirit according to their imagination, creating the appearances of phantoms <note>original: *eidolon*; a ghostly double or mental image.</note>. Pure souls, however, turn away from generation <note>The cycle of physical birth and material existence.</note>.
<margin>25</margin> As Heraclitus himself says: *The dry soul is the wisest.* <note>Heraclitus Fragment 118; suggesting that spiritual clarity and intellect are "dry," while the passions of the body are "moist."</note> For this reason, here too, according to the desires of union, the spirit becomes drenched and
<margin>30</margin> more damp, as the soul draws in a moist vapor from its inclination toward birth. Therefore, Naiad nymphs are the souls
<margin>35</margin> proceeding into birth. From which fact it is also the custom to call those being married "Nymphs," as if they were joining for the sake of birth—
join-

<column-break/>

**Right Column**

...from the exhalations of the earth: it is necessary, therefore, that souls—whether they are bodily, or incorporeal but attracted to a body—and especially those which must be joined and bound together with blood and moist bodies, should incline toward the moist, and by being moistened, become embodied. For this reason, the souls of the dead are summoned by the outpouring of bile and blood. And souls intoxicated by the love of bodies, attracting a moist spirit, condense it like a cloud. For moisture condensed in the air constitutes a cloud: and when the spirit in them is condensed by an excessive abundance of moisture, they become visible. And from their number are those souls which, with a polluted spirit, encounter us through the appearance of spectral shows. But pure souls turn away from generation. Whence the same Heraclitus says: *the dry soul is the wisest*. And for this reason, here also by the lust of cohabitation, the spirit is rendered wet and more moist, while the soul attracts a moist vapor from its propensity toward generation. Therefore, Naiad Nymphs are souls undergoing generation. From which also comes the custom of calling those marrying "Nymphs," as if joined for the sake of generation—

-># PORPHYRY <-

<column-break/>

**Left Column (Latin)**

...to call them [Nymphs], and to drench them with washings from springs or streams or perpetual fountains. Moreover, to souls that have already progressed into nature and to generative spirits <note>original: *genitalibus genijs*; spirits that preside over birth and the physical manifestation of life.</note>, this world appears sacred and pleasant, even though by its own nature it is gloomy and dark. For this reason, the souls themselves were believed to be airy, and to draw their substance from the air. Therefore, it is fitting and appropriate for them to have a cave consecrated on earth, which is both pleasant and dark after the likeness of the world, in which souls dwell as if in a most great temple. A cave is also rightly suitable for the Nymphs who preside over the waters, in which perennial waters flow. Let this cave, then, be attributed to souls and to those Nymphs who are among the more specific powers; and because they preside over springs and fountains, they are for that reason called Pegaeae <note>original: *Pegaeae*; from the Greek *pēgē*, meaning a spring or fountain.</note> or fountain-nymphs, and Naiads. What different symbols, then, do we have—some belonging to souls and others to the powers of the waters—so that we may consider the cave to be consecrated to both? Let the symbols of the Hydriads <note>A class of Nymphs associated specifically with water.</note> or aquatic Nymphs be the stone mixing-bowls and amphorae. For these are also the symbols of Bacchus...

...naked ones, and to call them Nymphs and pour over them washings taken from springs or streams or ever-flowing fountains.
<margin>5</margin> But to souls coming into nature and to birth-giving spirits, the world is both sacred and lovely, even though it is dark by nature and misty;

<margin>20</margin> a cave is fitting, where ever-flowing waters are within. Let the cave in question, then, be dedicated to souls and to the Nymphs who are among the more particular powers, who, presiding over streams

<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s allegorical analysis of Homer’s Cave of the Nymphs. Having previously discussed the stone looms as metaphors for the "weaving" of the body, Porphyry now shifts to the meaning of the heavens as a garment and the specific symbolism of honey and bees within the cave.</meta>

...since the ancients also called heaven itself a "peplum" <note>original: *peplum* / πέπλον; a large, ornate robe or shawl, specifically the one dedicated to Athena.</note>; as if it were a veil or covering for the celestial Gods. But what is the reason why the amphorae <note>original: *amphorae* / ἀμφορεῖς; tall jars used for storage.</note> are filled not with water, but with honeycombs? In these, he says, the bees store their honey. Indeed, the very word for "storing honey" <note>original: *tithaibōsein* / τιθαιβώσειν</note> is said to be derived from "placing nourishment" <note>original: *tithenai tēn bosin* / τιθέναι τὴν βόσιν</note>, because food and nourishment for the little bees is honey. Furthermore, the Theologians <note>A term Porphyry uses for ancient poets and philosophers who wrote on divine matters.</note> have used honey for many and diverse symbols, because it consists of various powers. For it possesses both a cleansing and a preserving power; indeed, through the benefit of honey, things remain incorrupt <note>original: *incorruptae* / ἄσηπτα; not subject to decay.</note>, and old ulcers are cleansed by honey. It is also sweet to the taste, and gathered from flowers by the little bees, which are usually born from oxen <note>original: *bougeneis* / βουγενεῖς; referring to the ancient belief in "bugonia," where bees were thought to spontaneously generate from the carcasses of bulls.</note>. For this reason, when they pour honey instead of water for the washing of hands for those being initiated into the Leontic mysteries <note>original: *Leonticis sacris* / λεοντικὰ; the "Lion" grade of initiation in Mithraism, associated with the element of fire.</note>, they command them to keep their hands pure from every harmful and wicked crime; just as the same people purify the initiate with fire, which is endowed with a peculiar power for cleansing, shunning water as being contrary to fire. Moreover, they cleanse the tongue from all sin with honey. But when they offer honey to the Persian <note>Refers to the "Persian" grade in the Mithraic hierarchy.</note> guardian of the fruits...

<column-break/>

...the guardian of the infinite <margin>5</margin> is handed over. Since the ancients also called heaven a robe, as it were a covering of the celestial gods. Why then are the <margin>10</margin> amphorae not full of water, but of honeycombs? For in these, he says, the bees store honey. The term "storing honey" signifies "placing nourishment." <margin>15</margin> And honey is the nourishment and food for the bees. Indeed, the theologians have used honey for many and various <margin>20</margin> symbols, because it is composed of many powers. Since it is of a cleansing power, and of a preservative one; for by honey things remain uncorrupted. And <margin>25</margin> chronic wounds are cleansed by honey. It is also sweet to the taste, and collected from flowers by bees, which happen to be ox-born. <margin>30</margin> Therefore, when they pour honey into the hands of those being initiated into the Leontic rites instead of water for washing, they command them to have hands pure from all <margin>35</margin> that is painful, harmful, and foul. And as they bring the proper wash-water to the initiate, as if to one who is of the purifying fire, they reject water as being most hostile to fire. And they also cleanse the tongue with honey from every sin. But when they bring [honey] to the Persian...

### ON THE CAVE OF THE NYMPHS

...they offer honey, they establish its preservative faculty as a symbol. For this reason, some have chosen to understand honey as the "nectar and ambrosia" which the Poet <note>Homer, in the *Iliad* (19.38-39), where Thetis drops nectar and ambrosia into Patroclus’s nostrils to preserve his body.</note> <margin>5</margin> drops into the nostrils of the dead to ward off putrefaction; for honey is the food of the Gods. Therefore, in a certain passage, he calls it *tawny nectar* <note>original: *nectar fuluum* / νέκταρ ἐρυθρόν; literally "reddish-gold" or "tawny."</note>, for the color <margin>10</margin> of honey is of this kind. But whether honey is to be understood in place of nectar, we shall examine more accurately elsewhere. However, according to Orpheus <note>Refers to the Orphic cosmogonies, a set of ancient Greek poems and hymns attributed to the mythical singer Orpheus.</note>, <margin>15</margin> a trap is set for Saturn <note>original: *Saturnus* / Κρόνος (Kronos)</note> by Jupiter <note>original: *Iuppiter* / Ζεὺς (Zeus)</note> by means of honey. For, filled with honey, he becomes intoxicated and his vision is clouded as if by wine, and he <margin>20</margin> falls asleep; just as in Plato, Porus <note>original: *Porus* / Πόρος; the personification of "Plenty" or "Resource" in the *Symposium* (203b).</note> is distended with nectar, for wine did not yet exist. For in Orpheus, Night suggests the trick of ensnaring him with honey to <margin>25</margin> Jupiter in these words:
