<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s interpretation of Orphic and Homeric myth, moving from the intoxication of Saturn (Kronos) to the broader metaphysical symbolism of honey as a catalyst for the soul's descent into the physical world (generation) and its role in the rites of the water Nymphs and Mithras.</meta>

# PORPHYRY

...the Heavens <note>original: *Cœlus* / *Ouranos*. In Orphic myth, the castration of the Sky-god by his son Saturn represents a division in the divine order.</note>. By this, the poet implies that divine essences are lured by sweetness and led down into generation <note>The process of being born into a physical body.</note>; and that certain powers, dissolved by pleasure, sow their seed. Hence, Saturn castrates the Heavens as he descends toward the earth with a desire for union. <margin>5</margin> Indeed, for these writers, the sweetness of honey signifies the pleasure of union, by which Saturn is lured and castrated. For Saturn and his sphere <margin>10</margin> are the first of those who move in a direction contrary to the Heavens. Furthermore, certain powers descend both from the Heavens and from the wandering stars <note>The planets.</note>; but Saturn receives the powers of the Heavens, <margin>15</margin> and then Jupiter <note>original: *Iupiter* / *Zeus*</note> receives those of Saturn. Since, therefore, honey is taken both for purification and as a natural protection against decay, <margin>20</margin> and for the pleasure which leads toward generation, a fitting symbol is also established for the water Nymphs <note>original: *Nymphis aquaticis* / *Hydriades*</note>, because of the <margin>25</margin> nature of the waters they oversee—which are immune to decay—and because of the purifying power of those waters and their cooperation in generation. For water cooperates in generation. <margin>30</margin> And for this reason, bees make honey in mixing bowls <note>original: *crateribus* / *kratērsi*</note> and amphorae <note>Large ceramic storage jars.</note>; since the mixing bowls represent the symbol of springs—just as <margin>35</margin> in the rites of Mithras <note>A mystery religion centered on the god Mithras, often practiced in caves.</note>, a mixing bowl is placed in place of a spring; while the amphorae...

<column-break/>

...the Heavens. By this, the theologian <note>A term Porphyry often uses for Orpheus or divinely inspired poets.</note> suggests that divine beings are bound and led down into generation by pleasure; and that powers, released into pleasure, emit seed. Hence, Saturn castrates the Heavens as he descends to earth through a desire for sexual union. For them, the sweetness of honey is taken to mean the same thing as the pleasure of union, by which Saturn is enchanted and castrated. For Saturn is the first of those moving against the Heavens, as is his sphere; and powers descend from the Heavens and from the wandering stars; but Saturn receives those from the Heavens, and Zeus <note>Jupiter</note> receives those from Saturn. Since, therefore, honey is used both for purification and against natural decay, and for the pleasure of the descent into generation, a fitting symbol is also placed for the water nymphs, regarding the rot-resistant nature of the waters over which they preside, and their purification, and their cooperation toward generation. For water cooperates in generation. Therefore, the bees are said to store honey in mixing bowls and amphorae, the mixing bowls bearing the symbol of springs; just as with Mithras, the mixing bowl is stationed instead of a spring, while the amphorae...

# ON THE CAVE OF THE NYMPHS

<columns>2</columns>

...in which we again draw from the springs. Springs and streams are proper to the water nymphs <note>original: *Hydriades*</note>, and even more so to those Nymphs who are souls; these the ancients specifically called "bees" <note>original: *melissas*</note>, as they are the workers of pleasure. For this reason, Sophocles spoke not inappropriately regarding souls:

->A swarm of the dead hums, and another follows.<-

---

And the priestesses of Demeter <note>original: *Ceres*</note>, as the initiates of the earth goddess, were called "bees" by the ancients. Persephone <note>original: *Kore* / *Proserpina*</note> herself they called "honey-like." They also called the Moon—who is the guardian of generation—a "bee," especially since the Moon is the Bull, and the "exaltation" <note>A term in ancient astrology referring to a zodiac sign where a planet’s influence is most harmonious and powerful.</note> of the Moon is in the sign of the Bull. Bees are "bull-born" <note>original: *bougenēs*. This refers to the ancient belief in "bugonia," the idea that bees were spontaneously generated from the carcasses of oxen.</note>, and souls proceeding into generation are also "bull-born." Even the "bull-stealing" god <note>original: *bouklopon*. Likely a reference to Mithras, who is often depicted stealing or slaying a bull to bring life to the world.</note> is he who secretly oversees generation. Honey has also been used as a symbol of death; for this reason, they offered libations of honey to the gods of the underworld. Yet gall was a symbol of life—perhaps hinting that the soul's life dies through the sweetness of pleasure, but is restored to life through bitterness. Therefore, they also sacrificed gall to the gods, or perhaps because death is a release

->from labor.<-

<column-break/>

...and indeed, the amphorae are the vessels from which we draw from the springs. Springs and rivulets are proper to the water Nymphs, and especially <margin>5</margin> to the Nymphs who are souls, whom the ancients specifically called bees, as they are the workers of sweetness. From this, Sophocles also not incongruously said of souls:

->*A wandering swarm of the dead hums:*<-

---

<margin>15</margin> Furthermore, the priestesses of Ceres, as the overseers of the sacred rites of the earth goddess, were called "bees" <note>original: *melissas*</note> by the ancients. They called <margin>20</margin> Proserpina herself "honeyed." They also called the Moon the guardian of generation, the Bee. By another name they called her the Bull, and the <margin>25</margin> exaltation of the Moon is the Bull. Bees are "bull-born," and souls progressing into generation are also called by this name. And the thief of cattle is that god <margin>30</margin> who secretly oversees generation. They also made honey a symbol of death; for that reason, they sacrificed a libation of honey to the terrestrial gods. But gall was a symbol of life; <margin>35</margin> whether they meant that the life of the soul dies through the sweetness of pleasure, but through bitterness it revives. Whence they also sacrificed gall to the gods; or because death

### PORPHYRY

<columns>2</columns>

...the end of miseries; but this mortal life is troublesome and bitter. Yet they did not indiscriminately call all souls that had entered into generation "bees" <note>original: *melissas*</note>; but only those who were destined to live according to the rule of justice, and who, after performing works pleasing to the Gods, would return again to God. For this animal loves to return, and is most observant of justice, and is sober. For this reason, libations made with honey are called "sober" <note>original: *nephalioi*; these were wineless offerings intended to maintain clarity and purity.</note>. Nor do they settle upon beans <note>original: *fabae*</note>; which the ancients established as a symbol of continuous generation progressing in a straight line, because the bean is almost alone among all seeded plants in having a stalk that is hollow and perforated throughout, not cut or interrupted by any closures at the joints. Thus, honeycombs and bees were symbols both specific and common to the Water Nymphs <note>original: *Hydriades*</note> and to those souls who proceed toward generation like brides <note>The "bride" metaphor refers to the soul's union with the physical body.</note>. Since, therefore, the most ancient peoples—even before temples were devised—consecrated grottos and caves to the Gods: in Crete, the Curetes <note>The Curetes were semi-divine guardians who protected the infant Zeus in a cave.</note> dedicated them to Jupiter; in Arcadia to the Moon and Pan Lyceus; and in Naxos to Bacchus <note>Dionysus</note>; and wherever they recognized Mithras <note>A Persian deity whose mystery cults were famously conducted in underground chambers called Mithraea.</note>, they performed sacred rites to the god in caves: it was not enough for Homer to say the cave of Ithaca had twin doors...
at-
tri- <note>Catchword for the following page: likely *tribuit* (attributed).</note>

<column-break/>

...labor; but life here is full of toil and bitter. However, they did not simply call all souls going into generation "bees" <note>original: *melissas*</note>; but only those destined to live with justice, and to return again having performed what is dear to the gods. For the animal is fond of returning, and most just, and very sober; whence also the "sober" <note>original: *nephalioi*</note> libations are those made through honey. And they do not sit on beans <note>original: *kyamois*</note>; which they took as a symbol of generation in a straight and unbending line; because it is almost the only one of the seeds to be perforated throughout, not blocked by closures between the joints. Therefore, they bring the honeycombs and the bees as proper and common symbols of the Water Nymphs <note>original: *hydriadōn nymphōn*</note>, and of souls being "brought as brides" into generation. Caves and grottos, then, the most ancient people dedicated to the gods before they even thought of temples; in Crete, of the Curetes to Zeus; in Arcadia, to the Moon and Pan Lyceus; and in Naxos, to Dionysus; and everywhere they knew Mithras, they propitiated the god through a cave. Homer was not satisfied with calling the Ithacan cave double-doored; but also that some [doors] were toward the North...

# ON THE CAVE OF THE NYMPHS

<page-num>121</page-num>

<columns>2</columns>

...[that the doors] were turned, the others toward the South, and the Northern ones are indeed for descent, but those toward the South [are for the gods], as for the descent [by the south gate], he did not signify, except only that:

->By no means through that way<-
->Do men enter; but it is the path of the immortals.<-
<note>original: "Οὐδέ τι κείνῃ / Ἄνδρες εἰσέρχονται· ἀλλ’ ἀθανάτων ὁδός ἐστιν." This is a quote from Homer's *Odyssey*, Book 13, lines 110-112.</note>

It follows then to seek the intention, whether of those who established the cave, as the poet relates in his report, or whether he fashioned the enigma himself as a fiction of the narrative, while the image and symbol he presents is that of the world. Numenius <note>Numenius of Apamea was a 2nd-century philosopher who influenced Neoplatonism by blending Pythagorean and Platonic thought.</note>, and his companion Cronius <note>Cronius was a Neopythagorean philosopher and a close associate of Numenius.</note>, say there are two extremities in heaven; of which nothing is more southern than the winter tropic, nor more northern than the summer one. The summer tropic is at the sign of Cancer; the winter at Capricorn. And since Cancer is closest to us, it is reasonably assigned to the Moon, which is closest to the earth; but since the South pole is still unseen, being still far removed and unknown—

<column-break/>

...had assigned: but he adds besides, that one gate was turned toward the North <note>original: *Boream*</note>, but the other indeed more divinely toward the South: likewise that the Northern one was open for descent. <margin>5</margin> Whether indeed one could also descend by the southern gate, he does not indicate: he only says, that

->*it is altogether impassable*<-
->*To man: and provides a way for the immortals alone.*<-
<note>original: "*inuia prorsus / Est homini: præbetque viam immortalibus vnis.*" This is the Latin translation of the Homeric lines in the left column.</note>

Next, therefore, it is necessary to <margin>20</margin> inquire what this narrative means; whether the poet describes a cave truly consecrated and dedicated by others with true faith; or if he signifies something hidden by it; or if it is merely his own fiction. But since this cave <margin>25</margin> bears the image and symbol of the world, Numenius and his friend Cronius say there are two extremities in heaven: of which one has nothing further to the south than the winter <margin>30</margin> tropic <note>original: *tropico hyberno*; the Winter Solstice point.</note>; nor anything more northern than the summer one. And the summer tropic is indeed at the sign of Cancer; the winter at Capricorn. And since Cancer <margin>35</margin> is most near to us, it is with the best reason attributed to the Moon, which is closest to the earth. And since the South pole <meta>Catchword: [Australis] south</meta>

# PORPHYRY

<page-num>122</page-num>

<columns>2</columns>

...the Southern [pole] is inconspicuous to us, and therefore Capricorn is especially assigned to the highest and most supreme of the planets, namely Saturn. And the signs from Cancer to Capricorn obtain their position in this order: first indeed Leo, the house of the Sun; then Virgo, of Mercury; Libra of Venus; Scorpio of Mars; Sagittarius of Jupiter; and Capricorn of Saturn. In reverse order starting from Capricorn: Aquarius of Saturn; Pisces of Jupiter; Aries of Mars; Taurus of Venus; Gemini of Mercury; and finally Cancer of the Moon. These two gates, therefore, the Theologians <note>original: *Theologi*; Porphyry refers here to ancient authorities on sacred mysteries, likely Orphic or Pythagorean sources.</note> established: Cancer and Capricorn, which Plato calls "two mouths." Of these, they say Cancer is the gate through which souls descend; Capricorn is the gate through which they ascend again. Indeed, Cancer is northern <note>original: *borealis*</note> and suited for descent, while Capricorn is southern <note>original: *australis*</note> and convenient for ascent. The northern parts are proper to souls descending into generation <note>the process of being born into a physical body.</note>. And it is rightly said that the gates of the cave looking toward the North are open for the descent of men. The southern parts, however, are not for the Gods, but for those ascending to the Gods. For that reason, [Homer] did not call it the way of the gods, but of the immortals; a term common also to souls, which are immortal of themselves or by their own essence. Of these...
<meta>catchword: Of these (original: "Harum")</meta>

<column-break/>

To the highest indeed of all the wandering stars <note>original: *πλανωμένων* (planōmenōn); the planets.</note>, Capricorn was assigned, that is, to Saturn. And the positions of the signs have their order from Cancer to Capricorn. First, Leo, the house of the Sun; then Virgo, of Mercury; Libra, of Venus; Scorpio, of Mars; Sagittarius, of Jupiter; [then] of Mars, Aries; Taurus, of Venus; Gemini, of Mercury; and finally Cancer of the Moon. These two gates, then, the theologians established: Cancer and Capricorn. Plato called them "two mouths" <note>original: *στόμια* (stomia); used by Plato in the *Republic* to describe the openings in the heavens and earth through which souls travel.</note>. Of these, they say that Cancer is that through which souls go down; and Capricorn that through which they go up. But Cancer is northern and for descending; Capricorn is southern and for ascending. The northern parts belong to souls descending into birth; and rightly the gates of the cave toward the north are for the descent of men; the southern parts are not for gods, but for those ascending to the gods. For the same reason, he said it was not the path of gods, but of the immortals; which is common to souls as well, whether they are immortal of themselves or in their essence.
<meta>signature: H</meta>

<summary>Porphyry identifies the two gates of the Homeric cave with the zodiac signs Cancer and Capricorn, detailing the planetary "houses" and the path of the soul's descent into and ascent from the physical world.</summary>

# ON THE CAVE OF THE NYMPHS

<page-num>123</page-num>

<columns>2</columns>

They say that Parmenides also mentions these two gates in his work *On Nature* <note>original: *ἐν τῷ φυσικῷ*; refers to Parmenides' philosophical poem where he describes the Gates of Night and Day.</note>. He notes that these traditions are preserved among both the Romans and the Egyptians. For the Romans celebrate the Saturnalia when the sun is in Capricorn. During this festival, it is the custom for slaves to wear the shoes of the free and for all people to share things in common with one another. By this, the lawgiver subtly hinted that through this gate of heaven, those who are now slaves by virtue of their birth <note>original: *γένεσιν* (genesis); in Neoplatonic thought, "generation" refers to the state of being born into a physical body, which was seen as a form of imprisonment or slavery for the soul.</note> are set free through the festival of Saturn and the house assigned to Saturn <note>Capricorn is traditionally ruled by the planet Saturn.</note>. Here they are restored to life and return to the source of their own existence. However, the path descending from Capricorn is a retrograde one for them. For this reason, they call the door a *janua* and have named the "door-keeping" month January, in which the sun returns from Capricorn toward the east, turning its course back toward the northern regions. For the Egyptians, however, the beginning of the year is not Aquarius, as it is for the Romans, but Cancer. For the star Sothis is near Cancer, which the Greeks call the Dog Star <note>original: *κυνὸς ἀστέρα*; referring to Sirius. Its "heliacal rising" marked the beginning of the Egyptian new year and the flooding of the Nile.</note>. To them, the new moon is the rising of Sothis, which governs the beginning of generation into the world. Therefore, they did not dedicate the gates to the east and the west, nor to the equinoxes,

<meta>catchword: such as</meta>

<column-break/>

They say that Parmenides too makes mention of these two gates in his book *On the Nature of Things*. Among the Romans and Egyptians as well, the memory of these things remains. For the Romans are accustomed to celebrate the Saturnalia as the sun approaches Capricorn. They celebrate it in such a way that they dress slaves in the boots of free men <note>original: *caligis*; heavy military or outdoor boots, contrasted here with the barefoot status or simple sandals of slaves.</note> and hold all things in common among themselves. By this very act, the legislator intended to signify that near this gate of heaven, those whom the condition of being born made slaves are set free through the feast of the Saturnalia and the house attributed to Saturn; there they come back to life and return to the fountain of generation. However, the path which begins from Capricorn is for them a backward one. Therefore, since they call a door a *janua*, they also named the month of January as a sort of "doorkeeper," in which the sun returns from Capricorn toward the region of the east, directing its course toward the northern parts. To the Egyptians, truly, the beginning of the year is not Aquarius, as among the Romans, but Cancer. For near Cancer is the star Sothis, which the Greeks call the Dog. Their new moon is the rising of Sothis, which is the principle of generation in the world. For this reason, they did not assign the gates to the east and the west, nor to the equinoxes,

<meta>catchword: to Aries</meta>

...namely to Aries and Libra; but rather to the south and the north, with the gates toward the south being the most southern, and those toward the north the most northern. <margin>10</margin> 
Therefore, he carries the sword of Aries (the sign of Mars) and is carried by the Bull (the sign of Venus). For Mithras, like the Bull, is the creator and producer of things, and the lord of **generation** <note>original: *generationis* / *γενέσεως*; "generation" refers to the process of souls entering physical bodies and the cycle of rebirth.</note>. 
<margin>15</margin> 
He is placed near the equinoctial circle, having the northern parts on his right and the southern parts on his left. The southern hemisphere is assigned to them from the south because it is warm; while toward the north, the northern hemisphere is assigned because of the coldness of its wind. 
<margin>20</margin> 
Furthermore, they quite reasonably associated the winds with souls proceeding into generation and returning from it. This is because souls themselves, in the opinion of some, draw in spirit <note>original: *spiritum* / *πνεῦμα* (pneuma); can mean both physical breath and divine spirit/essence.</note> and possess a spiritual substance. 
<margin>25-30</margin>

<margin>35</margin>

...such as Aries and Libra, but rather to the south and the north, and by the gates toward the south being the most southern, and those toward the north the most northern. Because the cave was consecrated to souls and to water-nymphs. These places are proper for the descent and the rebirth of souls. To **Mithras**, therefore, they assigned a proper seat at the equinoxes. For this reason, he bears the sword of Aries, a sign of Mars, and is mounted upon the Bull, which belongs to Venus. For Mithras is a demiurge <note>A "demiurge" is a craftsman-deity responsible for fashioning and maintaining the physical universe.</note> as the Bull is, and he is the master of generation. He is stationed at the equinoctial circle, having the northern regions on his right and the southern on his left. The hemisphere to the south was assigned to them because of its heat, and the one to the north because of the coldness of the wind. They reasonably assigned winds to the souls entering into generation and those departing from generation, because, as some have thought, the souls themselves attract breath and have such an essence. But **Boreas** is appropriate for those entering into generation. Therefore, the blast of Boreas restores to life those who are about to die, breathing upon them when they are gasping out their life-breath with difficulty. But the South wind...

<margin>5</margin> At noon in the temples of the gods they draw back the curtains. They do this by following that Homeric precept <note>Porphyry suggests that Homeric tradition dictates when the sacred is accessible to mortals.</note>, that when the sun is inclining toward the south, it is a sacrilege for humans to enter the temples of the
<margin>10</margin> gods; *but the path lies open for the immortals alone.* <note>original: *sed iter patet immortalibus vnis* / *ἀθανάτων ὁδός ἐστιν*</note>. Therefore, they establish the South <note>original: *Austrum* / *νότου*; the South Wind.</note> as the symbol of noon, because the God <note>Likely Helios, the Sun God.</note> stands over the threshold at the midday
<margin>15</margin> hour. For this reason, it was not permitted to speak at any other doors at any time, as if the doors themselves were sacred. Because of this, the Pythagoreans and the wise men of the
<margin>20</margin> Egyptians forbade anyone to speak while passing through doors or gates, venerating the God who is the Beginning of All <note>original: *vniuersi principium* / *ἀρχὴν τῶν ὅλων*</note> with silence. Moreover, even Homer was
<margin>25</margin> aware that doors are sacred, which Oeneus shows in a passage of supplication by beating upon the door:

---

->Shaking the well-fitted doors, he entreated his son.<-

->*And he strikes the doors joined with beams, praying to his son.*<-

<note>original: *Iliad* 9.583. These lines describe Oeneus, the father of Meleager, pleading with his son to defend the city. Porphyry uses the physical act of striking the door to illustrate its religious and symbolic importance.</note>

---

Quin & cæli portas Horis
commissas nouit , locis nebu-
losis principia sua habentes ;
quæ & aperiuntur & occlu-
duntur per nubes :
<meta>translator's note: catchword "Siue" (Whether)</meta>

<column-break/>

Furthermore, he <note>Homer</note> knew of the gates of heaven entrusted to the Hours <note>original: *Horis* / *Ὧραι*; the goddesses who oversaw the seasons and the gates of Olympus.</note>, which have their origins in misty
<margin>25</margin> places; these gates are both opened and closed by the clouds.
<meta>translator's note: catchword "Ἡ μὲν" (On the one hand)</meta>

<note>Vocabulary from the bottom of the page: Porphyry, Homer, Pythagoras, Oeneus, temple, doors, noon.</note>

<summary>Porphyry explains why the South and midday are sacred to the gods, requiring silence at thresholds and preventing human entry into temples at noon. He cites the Pythagorean and Egyptian practice of silent transition through gates and references Homer to show that doors are borders between the human and divine.</summary>

Whether to draw back the thick cloud, or to set it back in place.

Priests or mystics who guide or call forth the spirits of the dead.</note> offer them honey mixed with milk in libations; for they desire to come into generation through the lure of pleasure, and milk is naturally produced alongside them. <column-break/>
He also mentions the gates of the sun elsewhere, signifying Cancer and Capricorn. For the sun’s journeying is included within these spaces, descending from the North to the South, and from there ascending again to the Northern parts. But Capricorn and Cancer are near the Milky Way, whose extremities they occupy: Cancer the Northern part, and Capricorn the Southern. Furthermore, according to Pythagoras, souls are that crowd of dreams which he asserts are gathered into the Milky Way. It is given this name because of the souls who, having fallen into generation, are nourished with milk. And for this reason, those who call forth souls employ milked milk in their libations; since souls desire to come into generation by the enticement of sweetness, with which milk is usually produced. Furthermore...
</columns>

<meta>catchwords: τόπα (the beginning of "the places"), terea (the end of "Praeterea")</meta>

<summary>Porphyry identifies the Homeric gates of heaven with the zodiac signs of Cancer and Capricorn, explaining how souls descend from the Milky Way into physical birth (generation). He notes the Pythagorean belief that souls are nourished by milk during this descent, which explains the use of milk in rituals to summon spirits.</summary>

# PORPHYRY

<page-num>128</page-num>

<header>PORPHYRY</header>

<columns>2</columns>
Furthermore, southern regions produce small bodies. For heat usually thins them out significantly, and by this very process, tends to diminish and dry them. In contrast, in northern regions, all bodies grow large. Examples include the **Celts**, **Thracians**, and **Scythians**; their region is moist and abounds in pastures. Since the very name of **Boreas** <note>The North Wind.</note> comes from "devouring" <note>original: "vorando." Porphyry is using a Greek etymological wordplay linking the North Wind (*Boreas*) to the word for food or nourishment (*bora*).</note>, which refers to nutrition. Hence, the wind blowing from a land flowing with nutrients, being itself nourishing, was called *Boreas*, as if "devouring." For these reasons, therefore, the northern parts are properly suited to the race of men subject to mortality and the laws of birth; while the southern parts belong to the immortal. Just as the eastern parts are assigned to the **Gods**, and the western to the **Daemons** <note>In Greek philosophy, *daemons* are intermediary spirits between gods and humans, not necessarily "demons" in the modern sense.</note>. For since nature began from diversity, they established the <term>two-gated entrance</term> <note>original: "bipatente aditu" / "δίθυρον" (*dithyron*). This refers to the cave having two openings.</note> as its symbol everywhere. For progress occurs either through the <term>intelligible</term> <note>The realm of pure thought and eternal truths.</note> or the <term>sensible</term> <note>The physical world perceived by the senses.</note>. And if through the sensible, it is either through the fixed sphere or through the spheres of the planets. And again, either through the immortal or the mortal path. One pole of the world is above the earth, the other is subterranean; likewise one is eastern, the other western. And some parts of the world are on the left, others on the right; so too are night and day.
<meta>catchword: id-</meta>

<column-break/>

Southern regions make bodies small; for heat is accustomed to thin them out greatly; and in this very way it diminishes and <margin>5</margin> dries them. Yet in northern parts, all bodies are large; the **Celts**, **Thracians**, and **Scythians** demonstrate this; and their land is <margin>10</margin> very moist, and bears many pastures. Since even the name itself comes from nourishment <note>original: "βορᾶς" (*boras*).</note>; and *bora* is a name for food. And thus the wind blowing from the land <margin>15</margin> of a nourisher, since it is nourishing, is called **Boreas**. According to these things, therefore, the northern parts are familiar to the mortal race and that which has fallen under <margin>20</margin> generation; while the southern parts belong to the immortal. Just as the eastern parts belong to the gods, and the western to the daemons. For nature, having begun <margin>25</margin> from diversity, has everywhere made the "double-gate" its symbol. For the journey is either through the <term>intelligible</term> or through the <term>sensible</term>; and the sensible is either through the <margin>30</margin> fixed [stars] or through that of the wandering [planets]. And again, it is either through the immortal or through the mortal journey. And of the centers, <margin>35</margin> one is above the earth, and the other is beneath the earth; one is eastern, and the other western; and some are on the left, and others on the right; and there is night and day.
<meta>catchword: καὶ</meta>
</columns>

<summary>Porphyry provides a physical and etymological justification for why the North is associated with mortal "becoming" (due to its moisture and growth) and the South with the immortal "being." He establishes a system of cosmic dualities—East/West, North/South, Day/Night—as symbols of the soul's journey.</summary>
<keywords>Boreas, etymology, Celts, Scythians, cosmology, dualism, intelligible vs sensible, fixed stars, planets, Porphyry, Cave of the Nymphs</keywords>

# ON THE CAVE OF THE NYMPHS

<page-num>129</page-num>

<header>ON THE CAVE OF THE NYMPHS</header>

<columns>2</columns>
And for this reason, harmony is <term>back-stretched</term> <note>original: "παλίντονος" (*palintonos*). A concept attributed to the philosopher Heraclitus, suggesting that the universe’s order arises from the tension between opposing forces, like a tightened bow or lyre string.</note>, and hits its mark through opposites. Plato says there are **two mouths** <note>A reference to the "Myth of Er" in Plato's *Republic*, where souls arrive at a meadow with two openings leading to the heavens and two to the earth.</note>; through one, they ascend into heaven, and through the other, they descend to earth. And the theologians establish the **Sun** and **Moon** as the gates of souls: and they hold that souls ascend through the Sun, but descend through the Moon. And there are **two jars** <note>original: "πίθοι" (*pithoi*). Large storage jars often used for wine or grain.</note> in Homer:

->Of the gifts he gives, one of evils, the other of blessings.<-

The soul is also understood as a **jar** in Plato's *Gorgias* <note>In the *Gorgias*, Socrates uses the image of a leaky jar to represent the soul of an undisciplined person who is never satisfied.</note>. One is beneficent, the other malignant; one is rational, the other irrational. They are called "jars" because souls were containers of specific activities and habits. And in **Hesiod**, one jar is understood as bound [closed]; the other is what **Pleasure** <note>Hesiod originally identifies Pandora as opening the jar; Porphyry here interprets the cause as Pleasure or desire.</note> conceives and scatters into all things, with only **Hope** remaining. For in all those things where the corrupt soul, scattered about matter, fails in its order, it is accustomed to comfort itself with good hopes. Everywhere, therefore, of the two-gat-
<meta>catchword: ed</meta>

<column-break/>

Therefore, the harmony of the universe consists of a concord of opposites, and is set against itself through contraries. Plato also <margin>5</margin> mentions **two openings** or mouths, one of which is open to those ascending into heaven; the other to those descending to earth. Thus the <margin>10</margin> theologians also establish the **Sun** and **Moon** as the gates of souls: they maintain that they ascend through the sun, and descend through the moon. In Homer also there are **two jars**:

->Of which this one bestows evils, but that one contains blessings.<-

But also in Plato’s *Gorgias*, the soul is understood through the image of a <margin>20</margin> **jar**. One of these is beneficent, the other malignant; one is rational, the other indeed is devoid of reason. Moreover, souls are <margin>25</margin> called jars because they are capable of certain faculties and habits, and are, as it were, vessels. In Hesiod also we see one jar closed; the other which <margin>30</margin> **Pleasure** unseals and pours out through all things, with only Hope remaining. For in whatever matters the evil and <margin>35</margin> corrupt soul, poured out around matter, abandons its own order, in all those it is accustomed to feed itself on good hope. Since therefore every double-opening or bi-par-
<sig>I</sig>
<meta>catchword: tite</meta>

<summary>Porphyry explores the concept of the soul's journey through "gates" or "mouths," using the sun and moon as cosmic symbols and Homer's "two jars" to represent the dual nature of human existence and morality.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Porphyry's commentary on the *Cave of the Nymphs* in Homer's *Odyssey*. Having previously discussed the dual nature of the soul's path, Porphyry now examines the specific gates of the cave as symbols for the descent and ascent of the soul, referencing the cosmology of Plato and Pherecydes of Syros before turning to the symbolism of the olive tree.</meta>

# PORPHYRY

<page-num>130</page-num>

<header>PORPHYRY</header>

<columns>2</columns>

...bears a symbol of nature, it is also right that this cave has not one but two entrances, which are in reality different from one another: one of which is suited to the gods and the good, while the other is suited to mortals and the lesser sort. These things also gave **Plato** an opportunity to establish **Mixing Bowls** <note>original: "Crateres". In Platonic thought, particularly the *Timaeus*, these represent the vessels where the World Soul or individual souls are mixed or prepared for incarnation.</note>, and to put **large jars** <note>original: "dolia", corresponding to the Greek *pithoi*. These represent the storage of various qualities or fates of souls.</note> in the place of amphorae. And there are two entrances, as we have said, in the place of two gates. For **Pherecydes of Syros** <note>A 6th-century BCE pre-Socratic philosopher and mythographer, often considered a teacher of Pythagoras. He used allegorical language to describe the origins of the universe.</note> also mentions recesses, and pits, as well as caves, doorways, and gates; by which he signifies the births and departures of souls. But lest we extend this discourse too far by bringing in the opinions of the ancient Philosophers and Theologians, we believe the general meaning of this narrative has been sufficiently explained by us thus far. It remains for us to further explain what the symbol of the **growing olive tree** means; for it seems to signify something remarkable, since it is not said to simply grow nearby, but to grow at the **head** or summit.

<column-break/>

<margin>5</margin> ...nature being a symbol, it is reasonable that the cave is not single-doored, but has two doors, likewise differing according to the nature of things. One set of doors belongs to the gods and the good; <margin>10</margin> the other to mortals and the baser sort. From these things **Plato**, taking his start, also knows of **Mixing Bowls** <note>original: "κρατῆρας" (*kratēras*)</note>, and in place of amphorae he takes **large jars** <note>original: "πίθους" (*pithous*)</note>; <margin>15</margin> and he speaks of two mouths, as we said, for the two gates. And **Pherecydes the Syrian** speaks of recesses and pits and caves and doors and gates; and through these <margin>20</margin> he hints at the **births** <note>original: "γενέσεις" (*geneseis*)</note> and **deaths** <note>original: "ἀπογενέσεις" (*apogeneseis*), literally "departures from birth" or deaths.</note> of souls. But so that we do not lengthen the discourse by introducing the ideas of the ancient philosophers and theologians, <margin>25</margin> we consider the entire purpose of the narrative to have been handed down through these points. It remains then to go through the symbol of the **planted olive tree**, and what it might signify; <margin>30</margin> for indeed this tree offers something even more divine, being said not simply to be planted nearby, but "at the head."

---

->But at the head of the harbor is a long-leaved olive tree.<-
<note>Homer, *Odyssey* 13.102. Porphyry interprets the "head" (*kratois*) as a symbol of the intellectual or ruling part of the world or the soul.</note>

->And nearby it is a cave.<-
<note>Homer, *Odyssey* 13.103. The proximity of the olive tree to the cave symbolizes the relationship between divine wisdom and the material world of generation.</note>

<summary>Porphyry concludes his discussion of the cave's dual gates as symbols of the soul's journey, referencing Plato and Pherecydes, and begins to interpret the symbolic meaning of the olive tree at the harbor's head.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s allegorical interpretation of Homer’s "Cave of the Nymphs." Having established the cave as a symbol of the physical world and its two gates as paths for souls, Porphyry now turns to the specific detail of the olive tree mentioned in the Odyssey (13.102), interpreting it as a symbol of Divine Wisdom (Athena/Minerva) that presides over the cosmos.</meta>

# ON THE CAVE OF THE NYMPHS

<page-num>131</page-num>

<header>ON THE CAVE OF THE NYMPHS</header>

->*An olive tree stands with spreading branches at the head of the harbor:*<-
->*Near which is a pleasant cave.*<-

---

<columns>2</columns>

It is not, as one might suppose, that it sprouted there by some chance; rather, it contains the riddle of the cave. For since the world did not come to be aimlessly, nor just as it happened, but is the result of the wisdom of God and an intellectual nature, the olive is planted next to the image of the world—the cave—as a symbol of the wisdom of God. For the plant belongs to **Athena** <note>The Greek goddess of wisdom, craft, and war. Porphyry identifies her here with the Providential intelligence that governs the universe.</note>, and **Athena** is wisdom. Since the goddess is born from the head <note>original: "kratogenous". This refers to the myth of Athena springing fully formed from the head of Zeus, signifying that wisdom originates in the highest part of the Divine Mind.</note>, the **Theologian** <note>original: "ho theologos". Porphyry frequently refers to Homer as "the Theologian," viewing his epics as sacred allegories containing hidden philosophical truths.</note> <margin>5</margin> found a suitable place by consecrating her at the head of the harbor, signifying through this that this whole universe did not come to be from spontaneity or the work of irrational chance, but is the result of an intellectual nature and wisdom. While this wisdom is separate from the world, it is established near the "head" of the entire harbor. Being evergreen, the olive carries a certain very specific quality suited to the turnings of souls in the world, to whom the cave is dedicated. For during the summer, the white parts of the leaves bloom <note>This likely refers to the way olive leaves appear to change color or orientation depending on the light and season.</note> <margin>10</margin> <margin>15</margin> <margin>20</margin> <margin>25</margin> <margin>30</margin> <margin>35</margin>

<column-break/>

However, it does not sprout by chance, as someone might perhaps have thought <insert>6</insert>, but it encompasses the hidden meaning of the cave. For since the world did not emerge recklessly and by accident, but is the handiwork of the wisdom of God and of an intellectual nature, the olive is placed by the cave, which bears the image of the world; it is a symbol of divine wisdom. For it is the plant of **Minerva** <note>The Roman name for Athena.</note>, and Minerva is wisdom. Since she was born from the head of God, the **Theologian** <note>Homer.</note> found a proper place for her, while he consecrates her at the very head of the harbor. By this very thing, he signifies that this whole universe is not the work of a reckless movement, nor of a random accident, but the most perfect craftsmanship of an intellectual nature and of wisdom. This wisdom is indeed separate from the work itself, yet it is situated near the "head" of the whole harbor. But since the olive is always green, it maintains a certain property most fitting to the turnings <note>original: "conversionibus". Referring to the cycles of reincarnation or the "tropes" of the soul's movement between the spiritual and physical realms.</note> of souls in this world, for which souls we have said this cave is sacred. For in summer the whitening leaves project upward, but in winter indeed they are more white...

---

<note>The Latin text ends mid-sentence, mirroring the Greek. The term "whiteness" of the leaves was a common ancient observation regarding the olive tree's seasonal appearance.</note>

<summary>Porphyry argues that the olive tree at the head of the harbor in the Odyssey is a symbol of Divine Wisdom (Athena), demonstrating that the universe is governed by intelligence rather than chance. He connects the evergreen nature of the tree to the eternal cycles and "turnings" of human souls.</summary>
<keywords>Porphyry, Homer, Athena, Minerva, olive tree, Divine Wisdom, providence, allegory, Cave of the Nymphs, cosmology, souls</keywords>

--- Page 143 ---
<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s allegorical analysis of the olive tree situated at the head of the Cave of the Nymphs. Having previously established the tree as a symbol of Divine Wisdom, Porphyry now explains the physical characteristics of the olive—such as its evergreen leaves and their tendency to turn white—as metaphors for the soul's transition from the darkness of worldly suffering to the light of spiritual victory.</meta>

# PORPHYRY

<page-num>132</page-num>

<header>PORPHYRY</header>

<columns>2</columns>

...the whiter [sides of the leaves] are turned outward. From this, in prayers and supplications, people hold out olive branches: signaling that the darkness of their dangers will be changed into bright light for them. For this reason, the olive consists of a nature that is always green, bringing forth fruit as the reward of labor and effort. The same tree is attributed to **Minerva** <note>The Roman goddess of wisdom, identified with the Greek Athena. Porphyry views her as the personification of the Divine Mind that orders the universe.</note>: and a crown from it is given to victorious athletes. Also, a branch of supplication from the same tree is offered to those who are about to pray: thus the world also is ruled by an intellectual nature <note>original: "intellectuali natura". In Neoplatonism, this refers to the realm of pure being and intelligence (Nous) that exists above the physical world.</note>, and is driven by an eternal and ever-green wisdom: which gives the prize of victory and the consolation of many labors to those who struggle in the stadium of this life: and the Creator <note>original: "opifex". This refers to the Demiurge, the divine craftsman who shapes the material world according to ideal patterns.</note> of things themselves, who contains this world, restores the wretched and the suppliants. In this cave, therefore, **Homer** says, all foreign baggage must be set down. Here, one must sit at the root of the olive, stripped bare and clothed in the habit of a suppliant, with the body afflicted and all superfluous things cast aside, turning away from even the senses themselves, and take counsel with **Minerva**: how he may cut away and prune all things that lie in wait for the soul. For not without reason, in my [opinion]...

<column-break/>

...and during the winter, it turns its whiter [parts] outward. From which, both in litanies and supplications, they hold out the flourishing [branches] of the olive: <margin>5</margin> so that it might suggest to them the changing of the dark [nature] of dangers into the white [light]. By nature, therefore, the olive is held together as ever-green, <margin>10</margin> bearing fruit that aids in labors. It is dedicated to **Athena**: and to athletes, after they have conquered, a crown from it is given; and from it, a branch of supplication <margin>15</margin> to those in need. The world also is governed by an intellectual nature: led by an eternal and ever-green wisdom. From which the prizes of victory are given to the <margin>20</margin> athletes of life, and the cure for many labors; and the one who recovers the pitiful and the suppliants is the Creator who holds the world together. <margin>25</margin> Into this [cave], therefore, says **Homer**, it is necessary to put away every external possession. And having been stripped, and having put on the appearance of a beggar <note>A reference to Odysseus’s disguise upon his return to Ithaca, which Porphyry interprets as the soul shedding its material attachments.</note>, and <margin>30</margin> having beaten the body, and having cast away every superfluity, and having turned away from the senses, to take counsel with **Athena**, sitting with her under <margin>35</margin> the trunk of the olive: so that he might cut and prune away all the things plotting against the soul. For not...
<meta>catchword: For</meta>

</columns>

<summary>Porphyry interprets the olive tree in the Odyssey as a symbol of Divine Wisdom (Athena/Minerva), explaining that its evergreen nature and the ritual use of its branches represent the soul's victory over worldly trials and the transition from danger to spiritual enlightenment.</summary>

# ON THE CAVE OF THE NYMPHS. 133

<columns>2</columns>

And I believe it was not without reason that **Numenius** <note>Numenius of Apamea was a 2nd-century philosopher whose work bridged Pythagoreanism and Platonism; he often looked for deeper spiritual meanings in ancient myths.</note> and his followers held that **Odysseus** serves as an image for **Homer** of one who passes through the successive stages of generation <note>original: "γενέσεως" (geneseos). In Neoplatonism, this refers to the cycle of physical birth and material existence, which the soul must transcend to return to its divine source.</note>; and who is thus restored to those who are beyond every surging wave and are ignorant of the sea.

->Until you arrive among men who do not know the sea,<-
->Nor eat food mixed with salt.<- <note>Homer, Odyssey 11.122-123.</note>

For the deep, the sea, and the surging waves are also, in **Plato’s** philosophy, symbols of the material constitution of things <note>original: "ἡ ὑλικὴ σύστασις" (he hylike systasis). This refers to the unstable, ever-changing nature of the physical world as opposed to the eternal realm of Ideas.</note>. For this reason, I believe, the poet named the harbor after **Phorcys** <note>An ancient sea deity. In this allegory, the "Harbor of Phorcys" represents the threshold where the soul enters or leaves the "sea" of material life.</note>.

->There is a certain harbor of Phorcys, the old man of the sea.<- <note>Homer, Odyssey 13.96.</note>

Nor was it by chance that at the beginning of the *Odyssey*, the poet traced the lineage of **Thoosa** <note>A sea-nymph and daughter of Phorcys.</note>; from whom was born the **Cyclops**, whom Odysseus blinded. This was done so that, even until he reached his homeland, he might carry a memorial of his errors. Hence, his sitting beneath the olive tree is fitting for him as a suppliant of the goddess <note>Athena, the goddess of wisdom, who is associated with the olive tree.</note>, and under...

<column-break/>

In the same sense, **Numenius** thought that **Ulysses** in Homer’s *Odyssey* carries the image of one who traverses the cycle of generation <margin>5</margin> in order; and is finally brought to those who are ignorant of every storm and the swelling of the sea;

->*Until he comes to those who know not the sea’s plains*<-
->*Distant, nor to mix their food with marine salt.*<-

Moreover, in Plato, the mass of matter is called the deep, the sea, and the surge of storms. <margin>20</margin> Therefore, I judge the Poet called it the harbor of Phorcys.

->*The harbor of the sea-deity, by the name of Phorcys.*<-

Indeed, he also mentioned his daughter **Thoosa** near the beginning of the *Odyssey*: <margin>30</margin> from whom the **Cyclops** was born, whom Ulysses deprived of an eye. Truly, this was so that something might be present to refresh the memory of his sins all the way back to his fatherland. <margin>35</margin> Whence also his sitting under the olive tree rightly suits him: since he [was a suppliant] of the Goddess...

<meta>Catchwords: Greek 'ὑπὸ' (under), Latin 'fup-' (archaic 'sup-', likely for 'supplex', meaning suppliant).</meta>

<summary>Porphyry provides a Neoplatonic reading of the Odyssey, casting Odysseus as a soul navigating the "sea" of material existence. He explains that the hero's journey is an allegory for the soul's escape from the world of matter to a state of spiritual wisdom, represented by his rest under the olive tree.</summary>

# PORPHYRY 134

<columns>2</columns>

...he supplicates, and with a suppliant branch he appeases his natal <term>daemon</term> <note>original: "genialem daemonem" / "γενέθλιον δαίμονα." In Neoplatonism, the "natal daemon" is a guardian spirit or guiding principle assigned to the soul at its entry into the physical world.</note>. For it was not permitted for him to depart directly from this life of the senses, after having blinded it and having sought to abolish it in a single stroke; but the wrath of the sea gods and material gods <note>In this allegory, the sea and its gods represent the turbulent, changing nature of the physical/material world which "drowns" the soul.</note> pursued one who had dared such things. These gods had to be appeased first through sacrifices, labors, and endurance; sometimes by resisting the passions, and sometimes by employing deceptions and tricks, through which he might transform himself into various forms; so that, stripped of his ragged garments <note>Odysseus's rags are interpreted as the "garments" of the material body or the lower soul's attachments.</note>, he might cast everything aside. And yet, not even in this way will he be freed from his troubles. But only then, when he has completely escaped the sea, and while still living and seeing, has become so unacquainted with the works of the sea and matter through experience, that due to his total ignorance of maritime tools and works, he considers an oar to be a winnowing fan <note>original: "ventilabrum" / "πτύον." This refers to Tiresias's prophecy in *Odyssey* Book 11. The winnowing fan, used to separate grain from chaff, symbolizes the soul's ability to distinguish the spiritual from the material.</note>.

Nor should it be thought that interpretations of this kind are forced, or the mere clever conjectures of ingenious men. Rather, when we contemplate the ancient wisdom, and how great the prudence of **Homer** was, as well as his excellence in every kind of virtue, we must not deny that he secretly signaled images of divine things under the guise of a fabled story. For it is

<column-break/>

<margin>5</margin>...under the suppliant branch, soothing the natal daemon. For it was not possible to be simply released from this life of the senses by blinding it and quickly driving it away to abolish it. <margin>10</margin>But the wrath of the sea gods and material gods followed the one who dared such things. These gods one must first appease with sacrifices, and labors, and <margin>15</margin>endurance. Sometimes by fighting against the passions, and sometimes by using enchantments and deceptions, and changing himself in every way against them; so that, being stripped <margin>20</margin>of his rags, he might take everything off. And not even then is he released from his labors. But when he has become completely out of the sea <note>original: "ἔξαλος" (exalos), meaning "out of the sea," but also suggesting "beyond the salt."</note>, and while still alive is <margin>25</margin>unacquainted with sea-like and material works—to the point that he thinks an oar is a winnowing fan because of his complete ignorance of maritime tools and works. <margin>30</margin>One must not consider such interpretations to be forced, or the probabilities of those who invent clever arguments. But considering the ancient <margin>35</margin>wisdom, and how great a prudence belonged to **Homer**, and his accuracy in every virtue, one should not despair that he was hinting at images of more divine things in the fiction of a myth. For it is not...

<summary>Porphyry argues that Odysseus’s struggle against Poseidon is an allegory for the soul’s struggle to escape the material world, culminating in a state of "ignorance" of material things. He defends Homer as a divinely inspired philosopher whose myths contain hidden truths.</summary>

# ANOTHER APPENDIX
## Of the Sentences or Steps toward the Intelligibles.

*Recovered from a manuscript codex which the Distinguished Man Alvise Lollino <note>original: "Aloysius Lolinus." Lollino (1552–1625) was the Bishop of Belluno and a noted humanist scholar whose manuscript collection became part of the Vatican Library.</note>, Bishop of Belluno, bequeathed to the Vatican Library.*

---

->41<-

When the ancients <note>original: "Veteres" / "παλαιοί." This refers to the tradition of Pythagorean and Platonic philosophers.</note> attempted to explain the property of incorporeal Being through speech as far as is possible, when they say that it is "the One," they immediately add that it is also "everything"—just like a single thing among those known by the senses. But when we consider this "One" to be something different, because we do not see in the world exposed to the senses that this whole "One," insofar as it is "One," is "everything" (since everything is that "One" itself), they joined the phrase: "the One insofar as it is the One." They did this so that we might understand "everything" in Being to be something non-composite <note>original: "incompositum" / "ἀσύνθετόν." In Neoplatonism, this refers to a unity that is not made of separate parts, but is a single, indivisible essence.</note>, and so that we would not imagine a mere heap <note>original: "coaceruationem" / "σωρείας." A "heap" implies a collection of distinct, physical parts, which the ancients sought to distinguish from the spiritual unity of "the One."</note>. And while they say that it is everywhere, they add that it is nowhere,

<column-break/>

<margin>41</margin>
<margin>15</margin>
Wishing to represent, as far as is possible through speech, the unique quality of incorporeal
<margin>20</margin> Being, the ancients, when they call it "One," immediately add "all," as though it were one of the
<margin>25</margin> things known by the senses. But when we suspect this "One" to be of a different sort—not seeing in the sensible world this "whole One" which is "all things insofar as it is One" (by
<margin>30</margin> all things being that "One" itself)—they joined the phrase: "the One insofar as it is One." They did this so we might understand that "all things" exist in Being
<margin>35</margin> as something non-composite, and to keep us from the idea of a heap. And whenever they say it is necessary for it to be "all things," they add that it is "nowhere." But when it is "in all things," and "in every..."

<summary>This page introduces Porphyry's "Sentences on the Intelligibles," beginning with a metaphysical paradox: how "the One" can be "everything" without being a mere collection of parts. It explains that incorporeal Being is both everywhere and nowhere, existing as a non-composite unity.</summary>
<keywords>Porphyry, Neoplatonism, The One, Incorporeal Being, Alvise Lollino, Vatican Library, metaphysics, Intelligibles, non-composite unity</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the "Sentences on the Intelligibles" by Porphyry. Having established on the previous page that "the One" is not a physical collection or a "heap," the text now proceeds to explain why the incorporeal Divine must be described using contradictory terms and why physical categories like "place" cannot apply to it.</meta>

# SENTENCES.

...in everything and nowhere. But when they say it is in all things, they also say it is in every part capable of receiving it appropriately; they declare that it is the whole in the whole. They have demonstrated it through the most opposite terms, taking them together so that we might banish from our minds those reconstructions based on physical bodies <note>original: "ἀπὸ σωμάτων ἐπινοίας." The philosopher warns against "re-imagining" the divine as if it had a body or physical parts.</note>, which obscure the knowing properties of true Being.

42. When you grasp an eternal substance <note>original: "ἀένναον οὐσίαν." This refers to a reality that is "ever-flowing" or inexhaustible, a core concept in Neoplatonism regarding the source of all life.</note> that is an infinite power within itself, and you begin to perceive an untiring, unwearied existence, lacking nothing, but surpassing all things in its purest life, full of itself; established in itself and satisfied with itself, not even seeking itself; if you then apply to it the concept of "where" <note>original: "τὸ ποῦ." One of Aristotle's categories, referring to physical location.</note> or a "relation to another"—at the same moment you diminish it by assuming a need for place or relation, you have not diminished it, but have turned yourself away, taking as a veil the phantom of absurdity that has crept in. For you will not pass beyond such a nature, nor will you fix it in place, nor make it complete; nor will you limit it to something small, as if it no longer had...

<column-break/>

...[everywhere]. And when they say it is in all things, they add that it is nowhere in all; and when they say it is in all things, and in every particular <margin>5</margin> that is fit to receive it, they add that it is the whole in the whole. They express it through properties that are entirely contrary, which they join for this purpose: so that we might <margin>10</margin> remove fictitious thoughts of bodies from it—thoughts that overshadow those properties by which Being is known.

XLII. When you have conceived in your mind a substance <margin>15</margin> that is inexhaustible in itself with infinite power; and you begin to think of a subsistence that is indefatigable and invincible, never failing, <margin>20</margin> but set before all others with the purest life, full of itself, placed in itself, and sufficiently content with itself, seeking nothing outside <margin>25</margin> itself: if you add to it place and relation to another, immediately by that very fact—because something is lost by the requirement of place and relation to another—you <margin>30</margin> do not diminish it, but you turn yourself away from true knowledge; while you draw over yourself, like a veil, the image of a creeping thought <note>original: "subeuntem cogitationis imaginationem." A mental image that "sneaks in" from our experience of the physical world and covers the truth like a mask or veil.</note>. For you will neither be able to transcend <margin>35</margin> and exceed it, nor to stop it, nor to make it more perfect, nor to change it in the least...

<summary>Porphyry argues that the Divine or "true Being" exists outside of physical categories like space and relation, and can only be understood through paradoxes that strip away material biases. He warns that trying to locate the Divine in a specific "place" only serves to hide the truth from the seeker.</summary>
<keywords>Neoplatonism, Porphyry, metaphysics, incorporeal, divine substance, infinite power, categories of being, paradox, spiritual knowledge</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s "Sentences on the Intelligibles," specifically section 42. The previous page discussed how the divine or "True Being" cannot be limited by physical space. Here, Porphyry explores how the human soul relates to this universal Being and how adding specific, limited identities actually diminishes our spiritual stature.</meta>

<header>PORPHYRY</header>

reality: for it indeed cannot fail even the slightest degree. For it is unfailing, much more than any fountain can be thought of as ever and unfailingly gushing forth. If you are unable to reach it, nor be made like the universal, you will seek nothing that belongs to Being itself. Or if you do seek it, you will turn aside to contemplate something else. But if you seek nothing besides it, remaining in yourself and your own substance <note>original: "substantia." In this context, it refers to the soul's essential, spiritual nature rather than physical matter.</note>, you will be made like the universal: nor will you be stuck in any thing that comes after it. Therefore, do not say of yourself, "I am so great." By omitting that "so great," you become the universal. For even before, you were the universal. But something else was present with you alongside that universal: and you became smaller by that addition: since that addition did not come from Being <note>original: "ex ente." For Neoplatonists, "Being" is the ultimate reality; anything added to it from the material world is considered "non-being" because it lacks true permanence.</note>. For you can add nothing to it. Where, therefore, something is added from non-being, poverty and the lack of all things follows. Leave behind non-being, and then you will be most sufficient for yourself. * One recovers themselves by letting go of those things that make one more abject and vile. Especially when someone thinks of themselves as one of those things which are small by their nature, and not that which they truly are in reality. For at the same time, they have withdrawn both from themselves and from Being. And where

<column-break/>

[it is capable] of giving without falling short even by a little. For it is more unceasing than any of the fountains <margin>5</margin> that one might think of as always pouring out and never failing. Or, if you were able to run alongside it and be made like the whole <note>original: "τῷ παντὶ" (to panti). This refers to the "All" or the "Universal Soul."</note>, you will seek nothing that belongs to Being. Or if you seek, you will turn aside, having looked <margin>10</margin> at something else. But if you seek nothing else, standing upon yourself and your own essence <note>original: "οὐσίας" (ousias). The underlying reality of a person's soul.</note>, you were made like the whole; and you were not caught in any of the things that come after it. Nor did you <margin>15</margin> say, "I am so great." By not letting that "so great" remain, you have become the whole. And yet, even before, you were the whole. But something else was added to you along with the "whole": and <margin>20</margin> you were becoming smaller by the addition. Because that addition was not from Being; for you will add nothing to that. When, therefore, a place is produced even from non-being <margin>25</margin> as a companion to poverty, it is also in need of all things. Leave behind, then, the non-being, and then you are the fullness of yourself * <margin>30</margin> He takes it back, having let go of the things that humbled and made him small; and especially when those things are small by nature, and <margin>35</margin> he does not perceive what he is in truth. For he has departed from himself and, at the same time, departed from Being.

<summary>Porphyry argues that the human soul is naturally "universal" and "the whole," but it becomes diminished and "impoverished" when it identifies with specific, limited, or material things. By stripping away these additions (non-being), the soul returns to its true, self-sufficient essence.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Porphyry's "Sentences on the Intelligibles," specifically concluding the discussion on how the soul's focus dictates its proximity to the Divine. The text continues section 42 and moves into section 43, exploring the paradox of "presence" and "absence" in spiritual awareness.</meta>

### SENTENCES.

...truly, and there is someone who is present within himself to that which is present, then he is also present to Being <note>original: "τῷ ὄντι" (tō onti). In Neoplatonism, "Being" is the ultimate, unchanging reality of the divine intellect.</note>, which is present everywhere. But when you leave yourself behind, you depart from that Being as well. For this is the nature of the matter: it is present to the one who is present to himself, and absent to the one who is absent from himself. If indeed Being is present to us, but non-being <note>original: "τὸ μὴ ὂν" (to mē on). This refers to the material or illusory world that lacks true, permanent existence.</note> is absent; and yet when we are with other things, Being is not present to us. It did not "arrive" in order to be present, but rather we have departed from it because it is not present [to our awareness]. And what wonder is this? For you yourself, though present, do not depart from yourself; and yet you are not present to yourself, even though you are there. You are simultaneously both present and absent whenever you look toward other things, neglecting to look at yourself. If, therefore, being thus present to yourself, you are yet not present, and for this reason you are ignorant of yourself—perceiving all other things rather than yourself, things to which you are present even though they are far from you—why do you wonder if that which is not present [to your senses] is far from you? You have become far from it by becoming far from your own self. For as much as you are present to yourself—to that which is present and inseparable (since you are wherever you are present)—so much are you also present to that Being. For it is as inseparable from your essence <note>original: "καθ' οὐσίαν" (kath' ousian). Essence or substance refers to the soul's true, spiritual identity.</note> as you are from yourself. Thus, it is possible for you to know entirely both what is present to Being, and what...

<column-break/>

...whoever is present to that which is present within himself, then he is also present to Being <note>original: "enti." The Latin translation by Holstenius mirrors the Greek precisely here.</note>, which is everywhere. But where you neglect yourself, you also withdraw <margin>5</margin> from it. For it is of such great importance for a person to be present to that which is present to them, and to be absent from that which is outside of them. If indeed Being is present to us, <margin>10</margin> while non-being is absent; yet Being is not present to us while we are present to other things. It does not approach us in order to be present; rather, we withdraw from it when it is not present. And what <margin>15</margin> wonder? For you yourself, being present, do not depart from yourself, and yet you are not present to yourself. You are at once both present and absent when, looking at other things, you neglect to inspect yourself. If, therefore, <margin>20</margin> though so present to yourself, you are nevertheless not present to yourself, and for that reason you are ignorant of yourself—and you find all things (whether near or far from you) rather than yourself, who by nature <margin>25</margin> are present to yourself—why do you wonder if that which is not present is far away from you? For you are further from it because you are further from yourself. For as much <margin>30</margin> as you are present to yourself—though it be present and inseparably joined—so much also will you be present to that Being. For that Being can be no more separated or divided from you by its substance <margin>35</margin> than you can be from your own self. Thus, you are altogether able to know both what is present to Being, and what...

<summary>Porphyry explains that the Divine (Being) is always present everywhere, but humans become "absent" from it by neglecting self-knowledge and focusing on external, material things. Spiritual proximity is determined by how much one is present to one's own true essence.</summary>
<keywords>Porphyry, Sententiae, Neoplatonism, Being, self-knowledge, presence, absence, essence, divinity, Lucas Holstenius</keywords>

--- Page 151 ---
<meta>This page continues Porphyry's "Sentences on the Intelligibles," specifically concluding section 40. The text explores the Neoplatonic concept of spiritual presence, arguing that God (Being) is always present, but we become "absent" from the Divine by turning our attention toward material things and away from our own true essence.</meta>

# PORPHYRY

...is absent from the same: even though it is present everywhere, and likewise it is nowhere. For those who are able to penetrate into their own substance <margin>5</margin> with the intellect, and to know it, are able to recover themselves by that very cognition and the science of knowledge: namely, by that union <note>original: "unione." In Neoplatonism, the goal of philosophy is the mystical union (henosis) where the distinction between the observer and the object of thought vanishes.</note> in which the knower and the known <margin>10</margin> are united. And to these, who are present to themselves, Being <note>original: "ens." This refers to the higher reality of the intelligible world.</note> itself is also present. But those who wander away from their own essence <margin>15</margin> to other things, since they are absent from themselves, Being itself is also absent from them. Truly, if it were permitted to us to remain by nature in that same essence, and to be rich in our own resources <margin>20</margin>, and not to descend to that which we are not, and to be in want of ourselves, and therefore to suffer a lack of Being itself—even though it is present—and [if] we are separated from Being (from <margin>25</margin> which we are separated neither by place nor essence, nor joined apart by any other thing) by a turning toward non-being <note>original: "non-ens." This refers to the transient, material world which has no permanent existence of its own.</note>, we pay this just and sound penalty: <margin>30</margin> that by turning away from Being, we turn away from ourselves and are ignorant. Just as, conversely, through love of ourselves we recover ourselves and are joined to God. <margin>35</margin> Wherefore it was rightly said that [the soul is enclosed in the body] as if in a certain prison <note>original: "carcere." A reference to the Platonic idea (found in the Phaedo and Cratylus) that the physical body is a "prison" or "tomb" for the immortal soul.</note>.
<meta>catchword: &</meta>

<column-break/>

...is present to Being, and why it is absent from Being; [Being] being present everywhere, and yet again being nowhere. For to those who are <margin>5</margin> able to proceed into their own essence <note>original: "οὐσίαν" (ousian). The essential nature or true being of a person.</note> intellectually, and to know their own essence, they recover themselves in that very knowledge and perception of knowledge, according to <margin>10</margin> the unity of the knower and the known. And to these, who are present to themselves, Being is also present. But as many as might depart from <unclear>their</unclear> <margin>15</margin> own selves toward other things, Being is absent from those who are absent from themselves. If indeed we are naturally disposed to be established in that same essence and to be rich from our own resources, <margin>20</margin> and not to depart toward what we were not, and to be poor of ourselves, and through this again to be in company with poverty, even though [Being] is present; <margin>25</margin> and from Being (from which we are separated neither by place nor essence, nor cut off by any other thing) we are separated by the turning toward non-being. <margin>30</margin> Therefore, we pay this penalty: by the turning away from Being, we turn ourselves away and are ignorant. And again, in the love of ourselves <margin>35</margin> we recover ourselves and are joined to God. And rightly, therefore, it has been said, "as if in a certain prison." \* \*
<meta>catchword: ἔστι</meta>

<summary>This page concludes a discussion on the paradox of divine presence, explaining that while Being (God) is everywhere, humans only experience this presence by turning inward to their own essence. It ends with the famous Platonic metaphor of the body as a prison from which the soul must be liberated through self-knowledge.</summary>

--- Page 152 ---
<meta>This page continues Porphyry's "Sentences on the Intelligibles," specifically concluding section 42 and beginning section 43. The text utilizes the metaphor of the "runaway slave" to describe the soul's descent into the material world and explores the nature of justice and ontological independence.</meta>

# SENTENCES

->* * * * *<-

[...as a] runaway; <note>The text continues from the previous page, comparing the soul that has turned away from the Divine to a fugitive slave.</note> and one must try to loosen it from its bonds. For the soul, having turned toward the things of this world, has abandoned its own divine nature; as the poet says, *a fugitive from the divinity and a wanderer.* <note>original: "φυγὰς θεόθεν καὶ ἀλήτης" (Greek); "a numine profugus erras" (Latin). This is a famous line from the Pre-Socratic philosopher Empedocles, describing the soul’s fall into the cycle of reincarnation.</note> Thus, every wicked life is full of slavery, and for this reason, it is both godless and unjust; for in such a life, there exists a spirit full of impiety and, consequently, of injustice. And so, on the other hand, it has been rightly said that justice <term>idopragia; literally "doing one's own business." In Platonic philosophy, justice is defined as each part of the soul or state performing its own proper function without interfering with others.</term> is found in "doing one's own duty." Furthermore, in the distribution of what is fitting to each of those who live together, there lies an image and a shadow of true justice.

**43.** That which has its being in another, and does not subsist separately by itself apart from that other, if it should turn toward itself in order to know itself—doing so without that in which it is substantialized—and should attempt to grasp itself apart from that other, it will perceive that it is being destroyed. For it is separating itself from its own being. <note>Porphyry is discussing "accidents" or dependent properties. If a color (which exists only in an object) tried to exist or "know itself" apart from the object, it would cease to be.</note> But that which is able to separate itself from that in which it exists, and can grasp itself apart from it, and is able to do this without its own destruction, cannot possibly have its essence in that from which it turns itself toward itself...

<column-break/>

->* * * * *<-

...and is held bound in chains here, as runaway slaves are wont to be. Truly, he must attempt with all his zeal to <margin>5</margin> release himself from these bonds. For he who has turned toward these lower things has deserted himself, though born of a divine race; as that man says, *A fugitive from the divinity, you wander.* So it is that every wicked life <margin>10</margin> is full of slavery and impiety: and therefore profane and unjust. For in such a life, the spirit is full of impiety; and therefore also of injustice. Hence it was also rightly <margin>15</margin> said that right and equity are found in this: when each does what is his duty. And an image and pattern of true justice consists in this: that to those with whom we <margin>20</margin> live, that which is appropriate to each is granted.

**XLIII.** That which obtains its essence in another, and does not subsist in itself apart from another, if it <margin>25</margin> should turn into itself to know itself, doing so without that in which it subsists, and drawing itself away from it, it also perishes and is corrupted; for it is <margin>30</margin> separating itself from its own being. But that which can know itself without that in which it exists, by drawing itself away from it; and which is able to do this without its <margin>35</margin> own destruction, can in no way have its substance in that from which it turns itself...

<sig>L II</sig>
<meta>catchword: into</meta>

<summary>Porphyry concludes a discussion on justice as a spiritual state of "doing one's own duty" and begins an ontological proof that the soul/intellect is a self-subsisting substance because it can contemplate itself without perishing.</summary>

# PORPHYRY

...into itself without its own destruction, and was able to know itself without that [body]. If, therefore, sight and every sensory faculty neither perceives itself nor understands itself, nor remains preserved when separating itself from the body; but the mind <term>mens; the higher intellect or *nous*</term> when it separates itself from the body, then understands most of all, and turns toward itself, and is not destroyed; it is manifest that sensory powers obtain their operations by the benefit of the body; but the mind obtains both its operations and its essence not in the body, but in itself.

**44.** Incorporeals <term>incorporea; things without physical bodies</term> are primarily spoken of and understood according to the "privation" <note>In philosophy, "privation" refers to defining something by what it lacks—in this case, a body.</note> of body; just as matter was described by the ancients, and the form which is in matter when it is conceived as abstracted from matter; likewise natures and faculties; as well as place, time, and boundaries. For all such things are called incorporeal according to the privation of body. But there are also other things called incorporeal in an extended sense <note>original: "abusiue" (Latin); "καταχρηστικῶς" (Greek). This refers to using a term outside its strict definition.</note>—not because they lack body, but because they are by nature entirely unable to generate a body. For this reason, those of the first meaning exist around bodies; but those of the second
