**44.** Incorporeals are spoken <margin>20</margin> of and understood primarily according to the privation of body; such as matter according to the ancients, and the form in matter when it is <margin>25</margin> conceived as being separated from matter, as well as natures and powers; and in this way also place, time, and boundaries. <meta>This page continues the discussion from Section 44, detailing the distinction between two types of "incorporeal" things: those that depend on physical bodies (like space or boundaries) and those that are entirely independent (like the Mind). It then transitions into Section 49 (Greek) / 45 (Latin) regarding the relationship between the intellect and its objects of thought.</meta>

# SENTENCES

->* * * * *<-

<column-break/>

...the second kind, which are entirely separate from bodies, and from those incorporeal things that exist around bodies. For bodies indeed exist in a place, and boundaries exist in a body. But the mind <term>νοῦς; the higher intellect</term> and intellectual reason exist neither in a place nor in a body; neither do they give rise to bodies through any immediate affinity, nor do they coexist with bodies, or with those things called incorporeal by way of a "reflection" <note>original: "κατ’ ἔμφασιν". This refers to things that are non-physical only in relation to a body, such as a mirror image or a geometric limit.</note> of bodies. Nor, if one were to imagine a void <term>κενόν; empty space</term> as something incorporeal, could the mind possibly exist in that void. For the void would be capable of receiving a body; but it is impossible to separate the activity of the mind from its essence and grant a "place" to that activity. Since this genus of the incorporeal is seen to be twofold, those who follow Zeno <note>The Stoics, led by Zeno of Citium. Stoics were materialists who believed that only bodies were truly "real," and struggled to categorize non-physical things like time or the void.</note> did not understand the first type at all; and while they accepted the other type, seeing that the first was not of the same nature, they did away with it entirely. They ought rather to have suspected it was a different genus, and not assumed that because it was not the same as the other, it therefore did not exist.

<margin>μθ (49)</margin> Mind and the intelligible <term>νοητòν; that which is known by the mind</term> are one thing, and sense and the sensible <term>αἰσθητòν; that which is known by the senses</term> are another. And for the mind, the intelligible is its paired companion; for the sense, it is the sensible. But neither does the sense perceive itself by itself, nor does the sensi-

<column-break/>

...the second kind are entirely separate from bodies, and from those incorporeals which exist around bodies. For bodies indeed exist in a place, and boundaries exist in a body. But the mind <term>mens; the intellect</term> and intellectual reason subsist neither in a place nor in a body; they do not constitute bodies by their own affinity; nor do they exist with bodies or with incorporeals that are so named according to their lack of a body <note>original: "carentiam"; the Latin translator renders the Greek "reflection" as a "lack" or "deprivation" of physical substance.</note>. And if some incorporeal void were to be imagined, the mind could by no means exist in that void. For a void would be able to receive a body, but it can neither contain the operation of the mind within its scope, nor provide a place for its action. But since there is a twofold genus of the incorporeal, the followers of Zeno in no way accept the first kind; they do accept the other, but because they see that the other is not of the same genus, they do away with it entirely. They ought rather to have judged that it belonged to another genus, and not to have thought that because it is not the same as the other, it therefore does not exist at all.

<margin>XLV (45)</margin> The intellect <term>intellectus</term> and the intelligible are one thing, and the sense and the sensible are another. And to the intellect, the intelligible is related and joined; but to the sense, the sensible. But neither does the sense perceive itself through itself...

<summary>Porphyry distinguishes between "incorporeal" things that are properties of bodies (like space) and the "Mind," which is entirely independent of physical constraints. <meta>This page continues the philosophical argument from page 143 regarding the nature of the Mind (Intellect) and its relationship to the things it perceives. It specifically addresses the identity between the subject that thinks and the object being thought.</meta>

# PORPHYRY

...can [be perceived]; nor is it sensible. But since the intelligible <note>The Latin *intelligibile* and Greek *νοητόν* refer to things that can only be understood by the mind, not perceived by the physical senses—such as mathematical truths or divine forms.</note> is akin to the intellect, and is understood by the intellect, it in no way falls under the senses. Instead, the intellect is understood by the intellect. If, in truth, the intellect is known by the intellect, the intellect will certainly understand itself. If, therefore, the intellect is known by the intellect and not by sense, it will certainly be a kind of intelligible thing.
<margin>5</margin> But if it is intelligible to the intellect and not to the sense, it will also be the thinking subject. Therefore, the thinking subject and the object of thought are the same, the whole to the whole: not like the one who rubs and the one who is rubbed <note>original: "non vt is qui terit, & is qui teritur." Porphyry uses this analogy to distinguish physical interactions (where the person rubbing is different from the thing being rubbed) from the spiritual act of thinking, where the mind is both the actor and the object.</note>.
<margin>10</margin> It is not, therefore, understood by one part and thinking with another. For it is indivisible, and the whole is intelligible to the whole. Nothing that does not think is to be imagined in it.
<margin>15</margin> Therefore, it does not understand one part of itself while not understanding another. For insofar as it does not understand, it would be destitute of intellect. Nor does it move from one thing to another by departing [from the first].
<margin>20</margin> For from whatever it departs without understanding, it would be destitute of intellect in that regard. But if it does not understand things successively, one after another, it understands all things simultaneously and at once.
<margin>25</margin> If, therefore, all things are simultaneous, it does not understand this thing now and that thing later; all things are present to it at once, both the instant and the eternal.
<margin>30</margin>
<margin>35</margin>

<column-break/>

->❦ ❦ ❦<-
...sensible. But since the object of thought <term>νοητόν; the intelligible</term> is yoked to the mind <term>νοῦς; the higher intellect</term>, and the object of thought is in the mind, it is in no way subject to sensation; rather, it thinks with the mind insofar as it is an object of thought. If the mind is an object of thought to the mind, the mind would be an object of thought to itself. If, on the one hand, the mind is an object of thought and not sensible, it would be something thought. If, on the other hand, it is thought by the mind and not by sensation, it would be the thinking subject. Therefore, the thinking subject and the object being thought are the same, the whole to the whole. And not like the one who rubs and the one who is rubbed. It is not, then, known by one part and thinking with another. For it is partless, and the whole is an object of thought to the whole. And the mind belongs to the whole, having in itself no conception of non-thinking. Wherefore it does not think this part of itself while not thinking that part. For insofar as it does not think, it will be without thought. Nor does it change from this to that by withdrawing. For that from which it withdraws, not thinking it, it becomes without thought in that respect. But if it does not come upon one thing after another, it thinks all things together. Since, therefore, all things are together, and not this thing now and that thing later, all things are together now and forever.

<summary>Porphyry argues that the divine Mind (Intellect) is identical to its objects of thought, existing as an indivisible whole that perceives all truths simultaneously and eternally rather than through a succession of thoughts.</summary>

--- Page 156 ---
<meta>This page continues Porphyry's discourse on the nature of the Intellect (Mind). On page 143, he established that the Mind and its objects are identical. Here, he explores how this identity results in a state of eternal "Now," distinguishing the timelessness of the Mind from the sequential time experienced by the Soul.</meta>

### SENTENCES

If, therefore, the "Now" <note>original: "to nun" (Greek) / "praesens" (Latin). In Neoplatonism, this is the "Eternal Now," a state of being where all moments exist simultaneously.</note> is present to it, then both the past and the future are removed from it within an indivisible, timeless moment. Consequently, the "all at once" <note>original: "homou" (Greek) / "simul" (Latin). This signifies the simultaneous presence of all things within the divine Intellect, rather than a sequence.</note> applies to it, both in terms of the plurality of things and the interval of time. Therefore, all things exist according to the one, and in the one, within a moment that is divided by neither space nor time. But if this is so, the Mind does not move or wander from one point to another in its thinking; thus, there is no motion. Instead, there is an activity <term>activity: *energeia* in Greek, meaning the perfect, fully realized state of being or "at-work-ness," rather than a process of change.</term> that exists according to the one and within the one, which is free from all external addition, change, and transition. If this plurality exists according to the one, and the activity is both simultaneous and timeless, it is necessary that for such a substance there must exist as its foundation that which is "always in the one."
<margin>20</margin> This is Eternity. Therefore, Eternity <term>Eternity: *aion* (Greek) / *aeternitas* (Latin). For Porphyry, eternity is not "infinite time," but a state of being outside of time entirely.</term> is present to the Mind. On the other hand, for those things that do not think according to the one in the one, but rather through transition and with motion—such that in the act of thinking they leave one thing behind and take up another, dividing and moving through a sequence—Time becomes their foundation.
<margin>30</margin> For "future" and "past" are associated with such motion. The Soul <note>original: "psyche" (Greek) / "anima" (Latin). The Soul is the level of reality below the Mind; it must think in sequences, moving from one thought to the next, which creates the experience of Time.</note> passes from one thing to another, changing its concepts one after another; not as if the previous ones vanish, nor as if the second ones arrive from elsewhere, but rather as if the first ones were departing...

<column-break/>

If, therefore, the present is with it, then both the past and the future are taken away within the indivisible moment of this instant. Thus, that "all at once" is present to it, both according to the multitude of things and according to the interval of time. Therefore, all things are according to the one and in the one, in that instant which is divided by no interval of place or time. Because if this is so, the thinking Intellect does not run to and fro from here to there; wherefore its state is not one of motion. Rather, it is an act which exists according to the one in the one, and it removes all increase, change, and transition. But if a multitude is present according to the one, and at the same time an operation that is subject to no interval of time, it is necessary to attribute this also to a substance of this kind: that it exists always in the one. This indeed is Eternity; therefore, Eternity is present to the Intellect. Furthermore, that which does not understand according to the one in the one, but by transition and with motion—so that in understanding it leaves one thing behind, while it grasps another, and divides, and passes from one to the other—introduces Time along with itself. For the "future" and the "past" are suited to such motion. The Soul, however, passes from one to another, with its concepts being changed one after another; not as if...

<sig>K</sig>
<meta>Catchwords: λυ (left), prio- (right). These are the first syllables of the words starting the next page.</meta>

<summary>Porphyry defines Eternity as the timeless, simultaneous state of the divine Intellect, where everything exists in a single "Now." He contrasts this with the Soul, which moves through thoughts sequentially, thereby creating the experience of Time.</summary>

<keywords>Eternity, Time, Intellect, Soul, Neoplatonism, Porphyry, simultaneous existence, motion of thought</keywords>

--- Page 157 ---
<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s exploration of the relationship between the Soul and the Intellect. On the previous page, he discussed how the Intellect exists in an eternal "Now." Here, he transitions to explaining how the Soul’s movement creates Time, using the famous Neoplatonic analogy of a fountain that flows back into itself.</meta>

### PORPHYRY

<column-break/>

...the former thoughts fall away, and the second ones enter from elsewhere. But those prior thoughts have, as it were, passed on, although she <note>Refers to the Soul (*Anima* in Latin, *Psyche* in Greek), which is traditionally treated as feminine in this philosophical context.</note> remains in herself; these latter ones follow as if from another place. Yet they do not follow from elsewhere; rather, it occurs while she moves from herself into herself, and directs her eye to those things which are present to her part by part. <margin>5</margin> For she is like a fountain, not one that flows outward, but one that pours its own waters back into itself in a circle. <note>original: "fonti, non foras emananti; sed aquas suas per orbem refundenti in seipsum." This image describes the Soul's self-contained activity—it generates time through its internal movement without losing its essence.</note>

<margin>10</margin> Therefore, time corresponds to her movement, just as eternity <term>eternity: *aeon* (Greek) or *aevum* (Latin). In Neoplatonism, this is a state of being where all things are present simultaneously, distinct from "infinite time."</term> corresponds to the stability of the Mind within itself. And this eternity is not divided from the Mind, as time is from the Soul. <margin>15</margin> For in that higher realm, those two things subsist together as one. 

But that which is in perpetual motion <note>This refers to the Soul's movement, which we experience as the flow of time.</note> counterfeits the appearance of eternity, producing the perception of eternity because of the immeasurable length of its motion. <margin>20</margin> And eternity, which remains fixed, counterfeits the appearance of moving time, as if its own "now" were running through and multiplying itself according to the course of time. 

<margin>25</margin> Therefore, some people believed that time was considered no less in a state of rest or quiet than in motion; and that eternity—as I have said—is infinite time. <note>Porphyry is likely referencing earlier Stoic or Aristotelian views here to contrast them with his own Neoplatonic view that eternity and time are fundamentally different in nature.</note> It is as if each attributes its own qualities to the other. <margin>30</margin> Since time is always in motion, it reflects eternity through its perpetual nature, because it is stable and the same; and eternity <margin>35</margin> [reflects time through its] activity...

<column-break/>

<note>The following column contains the original Greek text of the passage translated above. In the 17th century, scholarly books often printed Latin and Greek side-by-side so readers could compare the terminology.</note>

<margin>5</margin> ...released, even though she remains in herself. But others enter as if from elsewhere; yet they did not arrive from elsewhere, but rather from her moving from herself and by herself into herself, and carrying her eye to those things which she possesses part by part. <margin>10</margin> For she is like a fountain, not one that flows away, but one that bubbles up what she possesses back into herself in a circle.

To this movement of hers, time <term>time: *chronos* (Greek). Sequential duration.</term> is attached; but to the permanence of the Mind <term>Mind: *Nous* (Greek). The divine Intellect.</term> within itself, eternity <margin>15</margin> is attached. Eternity is not divided from the Mind, as time is from the soul, because there the foundations are united. 

But that which moves <margin>20</margin> is modeled after eternity, and through the very immeasurability of its motion, it receives the concept of eternity. Likewise, that which remains stable is modeled after moving time, as if its "now" were expanding and <margin>25</margin> multiplying itself throughout the passage of time. Therefore, some supposed that time is observed no less in rest than in motion; and they considered eternity <margin>30</margin> to be, as we said, infinite time. This happened as if each added its own properties to the other: the moving thing [time] portraying eternity by its "always being the same" <margin>35</margin> through its identity with that which stands still; and that which stands still [eternity] in identity of activity...

<meta>catchword: eternity / activity (Latin: nitas / Greek: εργεί-)</meta>

<summary>Porphyry explains that Time is the byproduct of the Soul's internal movement, while Eternity is the stillness of the Mind. He argues that Time "counterfeits" Eternity by being endless, while Eternity "imitates" Time by being present in every passing moment.</summary>
<keywords>Porphyry, Neoplatonism, Eternity, Aeon, Time, Soul, Intellect, Fountain Analogy, Ontology, Philosophy of Time</keywords>

--- Page 158 ---
<meta>This page concludes Porphyry’s "Sentences on the Intelligibles." It continues the discussion from page 146 regarding how the Soul acts as a bridge between the eternal realm of the Intellect and the temporal realm of the physical world. The text specifically addresses how different celestial bodies have their own "times" or cycles, all of which are governed by the higher movement of the Soul.</meta>

# SENTENCES.

...of activity, <note>original: "ἐνεργείας" (energeias). This completes the sentence from the previous page regarding the continuous activity of the Soul.</note> attaching time by its own permanence to that activity. Furthermore, in the realm of sensible things <note>sensible things: things that can be perceived by the five senses, as opposed to "intelligible" things which are grasped by the mind.</note>, time is divided and differs from one thing to another. For example, there is one time for the Sun, another for the Moon, another for the Morning Star <note>original: "ἑωσφόρου" (heōsphorou) / "Luciferi". In this astronomical context, this refers to the planet Venus when it appears before sunrise, not the figure from later theological tradition.</note>, and so it is for each of the others. Therefore, the year of one is different from the year of another. There is also a year that encompasses all these others, which is summarized in the movement of the Soul, in imitation of which these celestial bodies are moved. However, because the Soul's movement is of one kind and the movement of these bodies is of another, the time of the Soul is different from the time of the bodies. The latter is extended by years and by movements and transitions through space.

<column-break/>

...[eter]nity, <note>The Latin text begins with "nitas," the conclusion of "Aeternitas."</note> persisting in one and the same act, seems to approach time by its own state and activity. But in the realm of sensible things, <margin>5</margin> time is distinct and varies among different things. As such, one is the time of the Sun, another of the Moon, another of the Morning Star, and another in each other thing. And therefore the year of different things is different. <margin>10</margin> There is also a year embracing the others, finished and perfected <term>perfected: This refers to the "Great Year" or "Platonic Year," the period of time it takes for all the planets to return to the same relative positions.</term> according to the movement of the soul, in imitation of which the rest are moved. <margin>15</margin> But since the soul's movement is different from the movement of the others, its time is also different from the time of the others. This time is distinguished by moments and by the intervals of local and transitional movement.

<meta>This page serves as an editorial introduction by Lucas Holstenius to a newly recovered fragment of Porphyry. It transitions from the philosophical "Sentences on the Intelligibles" found on the previous page to a specific passage regarding the Brahmans and the "Cave of the Nymphs."</meta>

<note>A decorative headpiece featuring a repeating woodcut pattern of symmetrical foliate scrolls and floral motifs, common in 17th-century book design.</note>

The following small fragment <note>original: "ἀποσπασμάτιον" (apospasmatidion), meaning an excerpt or piece torn from a larger work.</note> of Porphyry had been inserted by Joannes Stobaeus <note>A 5th-century anthologist who preserved many fragments of Greek literature that would otherwise be lost.</note> in his *Physical Eclogues*; but due to the haste of the transcribers, or rather their negligence in the Canter edition <note>Refers to the 1575 edition of Stobaeus edited by Willem Canter.</note>, it was omitted—as were many other fragments by excellent authors. Therefore, that most illustrious man *Andreas Schott* <note>A Jesuit scholar and noted humanist of the era.</note>, who strives in every way to assist the study of literature, wished to adorn his observations with this inclusion, since he had obtained a copy of it from the manuscript of *Antonio Agustín* <note>An influential Spanish jurist and historian.</note>. I have chosen to restore the same piece here, though somewhat more correctly, from an excellent Vatican manuscript, along with a Latin translation. This was done because this legendary cave of the *Brahmans* <note>Refers to the ancient Indian priestly class; Porphyry often wrote about foreign religions to find universal truths.</note> and the *Tantalic* water <note>Likely referring to water that recedes or is elusive, similar to the myth of Tantalus.</note> seemed to correspond quite well with the *Cave of the Nymphs* <note>A reference to Porphyry’s famous allegorical work, "On the Cave of the Nymphs."</note> and the waters gushing within it. Furthermore, it was included so that it might be clear from the evidence of this passage from which springs the errors of the ancient heretics concerning the *Demiurge* <note>term: Demiurge (from Greek 'demiourgos', meaning craftsman). In Platonic and Gnostic thought, this is the figure responsible for creating the physical world, often viewed by early Christians as a misguided or inferior being.</note> flowed—errors which the Blessed *Irenaeus*, *Epiphanius*, and others mention.

<note>A large ornamental tailpiece in the center of the page. It features a central face of a cherub or putto, surrounded by elaborate baroque scrolls, leaf patterns, and architectural motifs. A small, bunch-like ornament or tassel hangs from the bottom center.</note>

<summary>Editor Lucas Holstenius introduces a restored fragment of Porphyry concerning the Brahmans, noting its omission from previous editions and its relevance to understanding early "heretical" views on the creator of the world.</summary>

# PORPHYRY
### from the
### CONCERNING THE STYX.

<margin>5</margin> Behold, those who, during the reign of Antoninus <note>Refers to Emperor Elagabalus, who originated from the city of Emesa in Syria.</note> when he arrived in Syria from Emesa, met with Bardisanes <note>A 2nd-century Syrian philosopher and scholar who wrote extensively on the customs of India.</note> of Mesopotamia, recounted—as Bardisanes himself recorded—that there is a certain lake even now among the Indians called "The Trial."
<margin>14</margin> If any of the Indians, being charged with some sin, denies it, the Brahmans <note>The priestly and learned class of ancient India.</note> prepare him, inquiring of the man in this manner: whether he is willing for the examination to take place through the water.
<margin>20</margin> If he is not willing, they send him away as one liable to the charge, to suffer the penalty. But if he accepts the ordeal <note>original: "βάσανον" (basanon), a term for a touchstone used to test the purity of gold, here meaning a trial or test of truth.</note>, they lead him in along with his accusers.
<margin>25</margin> For these [the accusers] also descend into the water to be tested, lest they be acting as false accusers. Therefore, those who step into the water pass through to the other side of the lake.
<margin>30</margin> The depth is as high as the knees for everyone who enters it. Whenever the accused man steps in, if he is without sin...

<column-break/>

# PORPHYRY
### *A Passage from the Book*
### Concerning the Styx.

Those who, at the time when Emperor Antoninus <note>Marcus Aurelius Antoninus, known as Elagabalus.</note> traveled from Emesa into Syria, met with Bardisanes, a man of Mesopotamian origin, recounted that he had produced a written account stating that there is still among the Indians a lake which they call "of probation."
To this lake, if any of the Indians is accused of some crime and denies the act, he is brought; there, some of the Brahmans examine him in this manner.
First, they ask the man whether he is willing to undergo the test of that water. If he refuses, they send him back to judgment as one guilty; if he accepts the test, they lead him in together with the accusers.
For they also descend into the water so that they may be explored <note>original: "explorentur," meaning to be investigated or put to the proof.</note>, lest they have perhaps made a false accusation. Therefore, as many as have descended into the water cross to the opposite side of the lake; the depth of which does not rise above the knees of those entering.
If, therefore, he who enters the lake is free from fault...

<summary>A fragment from Porphyry describing an Indian trial by ordeal where the accused and the accuser must walk through a "lake of probation." The account was originally recorded by the Mesopotamian scholar Bardisanes during the 3rd century AD.</summary>
<keywords>Porphyry, India, Brahmans, Bardisanes, trial by ordeal, justice, Lake of Probation, Styx, Antoninus</keywords>

--- Page 161 ---
<meta>This page continues the fragment of Porphyry's "Concerning the Styx," specifically a passage sourced from Bardisanes regarding Indian customs of divine judgment and the description of a sacred cave.</meta>

-># PORPHYRY<-

<column-break/>

...is guiltless, he crosses fearlessly, the water not reaching beyond his knees. If he should be in fault, soon after he has progressed a little, he is submerged in the water up to his head. Then the Brahmans <note>The priestly and intellectual caste of India.</note>, having pulled him from the water alive, hand him over to those who had brought him; and they order him to be punished, though no sentence of death is passed against him. This is said to happen rarely, because no one dares to deny a deed due to the trial by water set before them. And by this water indeed, voluntary crimes <note>original: "spontanea delicta," referring to intentional sins or crimes one is conscious of committing.</note> are explored among the Indians. But there is another test which explores both voluntary and involuntary acts—and indeed, whether the entire life has been lived rightly. Concerning this, Bardisanes <note>A 2nd-century scholar who served as a bridge between Near Eastern and Greek thought; he is the source for this account of India.</note>—whose words I shall set down—writes thus: "They said also that there is a natural cave of vast size in a very high mountain, almost near the center of the earth. In that cave is a statue of ten, it seems, or twelve cubits <note>A cubit is an ancient unit of length, roughly 18 inches or 45 centimeters, based on the length of the forearm.</note>, in an upright posture, with hands extended in the form of a cross <note>original: "in formam crucis." In this context, it describes a geometric posture (arms outstretched) rather than a specifically Christian symbol.</note>: of which the right part of the face is male, and the left is female. And by the same logic, the arm, and foot, and the whole right side is male, while the left is truly female. This joining of parts strikes those watching with amazement, because in one and the same body they see the dissimilarity of both sides joined together. Of the right..."

<meta>mam-</meta>

<column-break/>

...therefore, he passes through without fear, having the water up to his knees. But if he has sinned, after proceeding a short way, he is submerged up to <margin>5</margin> his head. Then the Brahmans, pulling him up out of the water, hand him over alive to the accusers, and they require him to be disciplined without <margin>10</margin> a death sentence. They say this happens rarely, because no one dares to deny the sin because of the evidence of the water. Thus, the Indians <margin>15</margin> have this water as a trial for voluntary sins. But for both involuntary and voluntary sins, and for the rightness of life as a whole, there is another test, about which Bardisanes writes these things: <margin>20</margin> "They said also that there is a large natural cave in a very high mountain, nearly at the center of the earth. In which cave there is a <margin>25</margin> statue, which they estimate to be ten or twelve cubits, standing upright, having its hands spread out in the form of a cross. And the right <margin>30</margin> part of its face is male, and the left is female. Likewise, the right arm, and the right foot, and the whole side is male; and the left is female. So that <margin>35</margin> anyone seeing it is struck with amazement at the combination: how one can see the indivisible dissimilarity of the..."

<summary>This page describes two Indian "ordeals" or trials used to determine guilt: a lake where the guilty sink and the innocent walk, and a sacred cave containing a massive, androgynous statue representing the union of male and female principles.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the description of the cosmic androgynous statue found in the Indian cave, as recounted by Bardisanes via Sandales. The previous page ended while describing how the statue's body was split into male and female halves.</meta>

-># ON THE STYX<-

...of the two sides in one body. In this statue <note>original: "andrias," a term for a life-like statue or image</note>, they say there is carved upon the right breast the sun, and <margin>5</margin> upon the left, the moon. And along the two arms, a multitude of angels <note>original: "angelon." In this context, it likely refers to "messengers" or celestial beings in the Indian cosmology described by Bardisanes, though translated here with the familiar term for divine intermediaries.</note> is artistically carved, as well as mountains, the sea, a river, the ocean, plants, <margin>10</margin> animals, and simply all things that exist. They say that God gave this statue to his son when he was creating the world, so that he might have a model <note>original: "typon paradeigma," meaning a prototypical example or blueprint for creation.</note> to follow. "I inquired," <margin>15</margin> he says, "of what material it is made," and Sandales <note>The Indian envoy who served as the primary source for this account.</note> confirmed—and the others testified with him—that no one knows what material that statue is. For it is neither <margin>20</margin> gold, nor silver, nor bronze, nor stone, nor any other material; but rather it is most like the hardest and most rot-resistant <margin>25</margin> wood—yet it is not wood. They added, saying that one of the kings wanted to pluck a hair from those around its neck, and blood <margin>30</margin> flowed; and that king became so terrified that he only with difficulty regained his senses after the arms [of the Brahmans] received him. Upon the head of this [statue], they say there <margin>35</margin> is an image of a god, sitting as if upon a throne. They say also that in the heat of the sun, the whole [statue] breaks into a sweat...

<column-break/>

...breast of that statue, they say the Sun is carved, and on the left, the Moon. And on the two arms, a great number of angels are expressed by the sculptor’s art, and the other things which are contained in the world: namely the sky, mountains, the sea, a river, the ocean, plants, and animals, and whatever else exists in the nature of things. This statue they say God gave to his son, who would build the world, so that he might have a model set before him. "I inquired also," he says, "of what material it was." But Sandales affirmed to me, and the rest testified, that nothing is certain to anyone concerning the material of that statue: since it is neither of gold, nor silver, nor bronze, nor stone, nor of any other material. But it is indeed most similar to the hardest wood, not subject to any rot; yet it is not wood. They added, moreover, that a certain king attempted to pluck out a hair of those which hang down upon the neck of the statue, and blood flowed from there. Hence that king was so drained by fear that he barely returned to himself after the long prayers of the Brahmans <note>The priestly and intellectual caste of India.</note>. On its head, they say an image of God sits as if on a throne. Indeed, even in the summer heat, the whole statue is wet with sweat...

<meta>This page continues the account of the miraculous Indian cave and its cosmic statue, originally related by the philosopher Bardisanes through the testimony of the Indian envoy Sandales. The previous page described the physical characteristics of the androgynous statue; here, the text focuses on its supernatural properties and a ritual trial involving a narrow doorway within the cave.</meta>

->### PORPHYRY<-

->152<-

...[they say the statue] flows with moisture, and is cooled by the fanning of the Brahmans <note>original: "Brachmanis." The text refers to the Indian gymnosophists or priestly caste, whose ascetic practices were of great interest to Neoplatonists like Porphyry.</note>; and they say the sweat is thereby stayed. And unless the heat is tempered by these fans, so much sweat flows out that the ground all around becomes wet. Beyond this statue, in the inner recess of the cave, after a long distance, there is darkness. There, those who have the courage to enter go in with lit torches: and they come upon a certain door, from which water flows, which pools like a lake at the far end of the cave. Through this door enter those who wish to prove <note>original: "probare," meaning to test or verify one's spiritual state.</note> themselves. And whoever is pure from the defilements of life enters without any hindrance, as the door opens wider for them. And they find a very large spring of the clearest water and most pleasant to drink: from which that water I mentioned flows forth. But those who are guilty of some crime struggle greatly at the entrance of that door: nor can they enter, for the door narrows itself. These, being compelled, confess in the presence of others if they have committed any sin: and they ask that the rest pray to God for them: and they spend a long period of time in fasting. Here they say Sandales stated that on a certain appointed day the Brahmans gather: and some indeed stay there perpetually; but others from elsewhere...

<meta>Catchword: con-</meta>

<column-break/>

...this statue, [they say] is fanned by the Brahmans, and the sweats are stopped; and if they did not fan it, much sweat would be produced, <margin>5</margin> so that the ground around it is soaked. Further inside the cave, after a great distance, there is darkness; <margin>10</margin> there, those who wish to do so enter with torches, and they find a certain door; from which water comes forth, and forms a lake near the end of the cave. And as <margin>15</margin> if testing themselves, they enter through that door. And as many as are pure from the baseness <note>original: "phaylotētos," referring to moral worthlessness or wickedness.</note> of life enter without hindrance, the door <margin>20</margin> widening; and they find a very great spring of water, quite transparent and most drinkable, from which that water comes forth. But those <margin>25</margin> who are under self-condemnation are much distressed to enter through that door, and they are not able, as it contracts <note>This "intelligent" door acts as a gatekeeper, physically responding to the spiritual weight or "purity" of the person attempting to pass.</note>. These, being forced, <margin>30</margin> confess whatever they have sinned against others. And they make a petition, so that the rest might pray for them, and they will fast for a sufficient time. There they say <margin>35</margin> Sandales said that the Brahmans gather on a certain appointed day. But there are some who have their residence there...

<meta>This page concludes the account of the miraculous Indian cave and its androgynous statue, which served as a site for spiritual testing and oracular inquiry. The text finishes the description of the annual pilgrimage and the interpretation of the statue's iconography before transitioning to a reference by Apollonius of Tyana.</meta>

### ON THE STYX

153

...others gather from elsewhere during the summer season and around autumn, when the outflow <note>original: "ἀποῤῥοῆς" / "copia." The Greek implies a flowing out or discharge from the spring mentioned on the previous page.</note> of the water is more abundant, both to view the statue and to meet with one another, and so that each may make a trial of himself to see if he might be able to enter through the aforementioned door. They say that inquiries are conducted there concerning the figures carved upon that body <note>Referring to the androgynous statue mentioned previously, which was said to have the entire world—gods, mountains, animals—carved upon its surface.</note>. For it is not easy to understand every representation in the carving, both because they are so numerous and because not all animals and plants exist in every region. These, then, are the things the Indians relate regarding the water of testing <note>original: "δοκιμαστηρίου ὕδατος" / "aqua probationis." A ritual water used to determine guilt, innocence, or spiritual purity.</note> that exists among them. Moreover, I believe that Apollonius of Tyana <note>Apollonius was a famous Neopythagorean philosopher and wonder-worker of the 1st century AD, often compared to Jesus or Pythagoras.</note> also mentions this water—I mean the water in the cave. For in the letters he wrote to the Brahmans, he uses a certain oath: "No, by the Tantalian water by which you initiated me." For I believe he calls it "Tantalian" <note>A reference to Tantalus, the mythological figure punished in the underworld by having water and fruit stay just out of his reach. Porphyry suggests the water is "Tantalian" because it remains elusive to the unworthy.</note> because it always holds in suspenseful expectation those who have been eager to go beneath it and to draw a drink from it.

<column-break/>

...others convene in the summer and autumn, when the abundance of fruits is greatest: both to see that statue and for their mutual gathering: and so that each may make a trial in himself, whether he may be able to enter through the said door. They say that in the same place, questions are treated concerning the figures carved on that body. For indeed, not all figures can easily be known: both because there are so many, and because all animals and plants do not exist in one single place. And these are the things which the Indians relate concerning the water of testing among them. Furthermore, I believe that Apollonius of Tyana also remembered that water: namely, the one which is in the cave. For in the letters which he writes to the Brahmans, he uses a certain oath: *Not by the Tantalian water, with which you initiated me.* For I think he calls this water "Tantalian" because it holds those who attempt to go under it and draw a drink from it suspended in perpetual expectation.

Pythagoras, 12, 33. 30, 37:
**What the Soul is. 66, 24.** It is an intermediate substance between that which is divisible and that which is indivisible. 60, 9. <note>In Neoplatonic philosophy, the soul acts as a bridge between the eternal, unchanging world of forms and the changing, physical world.</note> How it is bound to the body, and how it is released from it. ibid. 23. 141, 4. How it operates. 66, 31. How it is said to suffer <note>original: *pati*. In a philosophical sense, this refers to being acted upon or experiencing emotions/sensations.</note>. 66, 32. It is properly incapable of suffering. 70, 27. How it is everywhere, and nowhere. 75, 20. <note>This refers to the soul's non-spatial nature; it influences the body but is not "located" in a physical spot like an object.</note> How it exists in the underworld or on earth. 76, 15. 77, 5. Its descent into the body. 77, 22. 113, 2. It is not "the good" itself, but is capable of receiving the good. 82, 23. It is not multiplied because of bodies. 96, 1. It is both one and many. ibid. What the soul's poverty and fullness are. 98, 22. It delights in blood and seed. 112, 20. <note>A reference to ancient theories of vitality and the soul's attraction to the materials of life and generation.</note> The dry soul is the wisest. 113, 27. <note>A famous aphorism from the philosopher Heraclitus, suggesting that a soul free from the "moisture" of worldly passions is more intellectual.</note> Souls called **bees**. 120, 3. Their seat is in the **Milky Way**. 127, 28. Their gates, according to the Theologians, are the Sun and the Moon. 129, 9. How it returns to God. 132, 25. ### I

**January** named after the **door** (*Janua*). 123, 22. <note>original: *Ianuarius dictus a Ianua*. In Roman mythology, Janus was the god of beginnings and transitions, often depicted with two faces looking toward the past and the future.</note>
**Plato** places **Ideas** in the firmament of heaven. 48, 3.
The **Idaean cave** in **Crete**. 11, 14. <note>A sacred site where the god Zeus was said to have been hidden and raised as an infant.</note>
Do not poke the **fire** with a **sword**. 27, 37. <note>original: *Ignem gladio ne fodias*. This is one of the famous Pythagorean "Symbols"—short, cryptic maxims that carried hidden moral or philosophical meanings. This one was often interpreted as a warning not to provoke someone who is already angry.</note>
The method of **purification by fire**. 116, 31.
What **imagination** is. 53, 3. How it occurs. 64, 10.
**Imperfection** pulls toward lower things. 75, 4.
The properties of the **Incorporeal**. 59, 20. 72, 25. In how many ways it is defined. 67, 32. In what way it may be affected. 66, 30. How its production occurs. 71, 15. In what way it exists within the body. 73, 22. Knowledge of it is difficult. 89, 28. It is more powerful than the corporeal. 92, 34.
How the **Intellect** understands. 64, 20. The understander and the understood thing are the same. 144, 15. <note>A fundamental Neoplatonic doctrine stating that in the realm of pure thought, the mind and the object of its contemplation are a single, unified reality.</note>
**Intellectual things** within the intellect. 65, 20. Both particular and universal, and in what manner. 70, 36.
Which are the **intellectual virtues**. 83, 10. They make a man into a **God**. 85, 6.

### K

**Epimenides** was called the **Purifier**. 19, 26. <note>original: *Καθαρτής* (Kathartēs). Epimenides was a semi-mythical seer and philosopher from Crete known for his ritual purifications.</note>
Why the heaven is called the **Cosmos**. 50, 52. <note>original: *Κόσμος* (Kosmos). In Greek, this word signifies both "order" and "ornament" or "beauty," reflecting the Pythagorean belief in a structured, beautiful universe.</note>

### L

**Souls** are called forth with **milk** and **honeyed wine**. 127, 33. <note>original: *Læ mulso*—likely *Lacte mulso*. These were traditional offerings in ancient Greek rituals intended to appease or summon the spirits of the dead.</note>
The **Milky Way** as the seat of souls. 127, 28.
**Pythagoras** called the sea a **tear**. 27, 25. <note>original: *Lacrimam vocabat mare*. This poetic or symbolic name likely referred to the saltiness of the sea or symbolized the world of material suffering.</note>
The **lake of testing** among the **Brahmans**. 149, 16. <note>original: *Brachmanos*. This refers to the priestly caste of India, whom ancient Neoplatonists viewed as fellow seekers of wisdom.</note>
**Laws** are the crowns of cities. 28, 5. They must be obeyed. 25, 5.
**Pythagoras** brought laws to cities. 13, 29.
The **Leontic rites**. 116, 25. <note>original: *Leontica sacra*. These were the "Lion" grade initiations in the mysteries of Mithras.</note>
**Libations** to the Gods should be made at the handle of the cup. 28, 34.
Thirsty **Libya**. 23, 28.
The **tongue** was purged with **honey**. 116, 35.
The **triple scripts** of the **Egyptians**. 8, 13. <note>Refers to the ancient Egyptian writing systems: Hieroglyphic, Hieratic, and Demotic.</note>

The **Lyre of the Muses** are the **Pleiades**. 27, 27. <note>A Pythagorean astronomical metaphor connecting the seven stars of the Pleiades to the seven strings of a lyre, representing the "harmony of the spheres."</note>

**Lysis** the Pythagorean, the teacher of **Epaminondas**. 38, 11; 40, 19. <note>Lysis was one of the few Pythagoreans to survive the persecution of the sect; he later moved to Thebes and mentored the famous general Epaminondas.</note>

# M

The **Magi** of the **Persians**, devoted to **initiations**. 4, 35. Their opinion concerning **God**. 27, 13. <note>original: *Magi Perfarum*. The Magi were the priestly caste of Zoroastrianism; ancient Greeks believed Pythagoras studied with them to learn secret wisdom.</note>
**Magna Graecia** [Greater Greece]. 13, 28. <note>The name given to the coastal areas of Southern Italy which were extensively populated by Greek settlers.</note>
**Evil** [resides] around the earth. 48, 22.
**Evil** is the turning away of the **soul** from **God** toward lower things. 82, 28. <note>A core Neoplatonic concept: evil is not a positive force, but a "privation" or a turning away from the source of good.</note>
Why the **hands** are washed with **honey**. 116, 26.
The course of the **planet Mars**. 50, 14.
**Mathematical objects** are intermediate between **bodies** and **incorporeal** things. 32, 35. Their use among the **Pythagoreans**. 32, 19.
The **Mathematicians**, disciples of **Pythagoras**. 24, 28. 44, 1. <note>The Pythagoreans were divided into two groups: the *Mathematici*, who studied the inner technical doctrines, and the *Acousmatici*, who followed the ethical precepts.</note>
Properties of **prime matter**. 68, 19. It is **impassible**. 69, 35. <note>original: *impassibilis*. This means matter in its fundamental state cannot be "acted upon" or changed in its essence, serving only as a mirror for forms.</note> Why it is called a **rock** and a **stone**. 106, 37.

**The Mind sees, the Mind hears**. 31, 15. <note>original: *Mens videt mens audit*. A famous line by the poet Epicharmus, often cited by philosophers to show that the physical senses are useless without the intellect.</note> The **principle of all good things**. 53, 15. It is **not the first principle**. 63, 3. It is the **same as those things which it understands**. 64, 20. <note>A Neoplatonic doctrine: in the realm of pure Intellect, the thinker and the thought are identical.</note> How it is **everywhere and nowhere**. 75, 18. How it **knows itself**. 142, 8.
It is forbidden to enter **temples at midday**. 126, 5. <note>original: *Meridie templa ingredi nefas*. Midday was considered a "liminal" or sacred time when spirits were active or the gods were resting.</note>

Properties of **True Being** <note>original: "Vere-entis"</note>. 79, 1. Neither large nor small. 91, 15. How it is said to be many and one. 136, 20. How it is known. 137, 15. <note>The concept of "True Being" is central to Neoplatonism, referring to the eternal, unchanging reality of the intelligible world.</note>
The **way of men and of Gods**. 109, 26.
**Do not walk on the public road**. Symbol. 28, 15. <note>A Pythagorean "Akousma" or symbolic precept advising followers to avoid common, unreflective opinions and lifestyles.</note>
**Vision**, and its objects. 46, 35. How it occurs. 63, 37.
**Four kinds of virtues**: civil, contemplative, intellectual, and exemplary. 80, 15. 84, 5. <note>This hierarchical classification of virtues was developed by later Neoplatonists like Porphyry to describe the soul's ascent.</note>
**Civil virtues**, what and of what kind they are. 80, 25.
Who is called a **good man**. 85, 2.
Why **human life** is difficult. 51, 30.

# DISSERTATION

## On the life and writings

# OF PORPHYRY THE PHILOSOPHER.

--- Page 183 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page follows the internal title page of Lucas Holstenius’s dissertation. The previous page established the author and subject; this page begins Chapter I of the scholarly essay.</meta>

# BY LUCAS HOLSTENIUS
## On the life and writings
# OF PORPHYRY THE PHILOSOPHER
## A DISSERTATION.
### CHAPTER I.

->*The branch of history known as biography <note>Greek: biographia</note>. In how many ways and by whom the Lives of the Philosophers were written. Suidas <note>The Suda, a 10th-century Byzantine Greek encyclopedia</note> explained. Heresies arising from Philosophy. Testimonies of the Holy Fathers <note>Early Christian theologians</note> concerning the erudition of Porphyry.*<-

<note>A large decorative initial 'Q' is set within a square frame, embellished with intricate floral and vine patterns, marking the beginning of the text.</note>

Those who have called history the "teacher of life" and the "mirror of human actions" seem to have understood its nature and dignity best of all. For since there are two things which govern human life—wisdom <term>Sapientia: theoretical or divine knowledge</term> and prudence <term>Prudentia: practical wisdom or foresight</term>—the former raises the mind above itself toward God and heavenly things through the knowledge of divine and human affairs; the latter, however, shows itself as the moderator of counsels and actions in this race-course of life. Both are surely acquired through the benefit of history. 

For since personal investigation is difficult, and firsthand experience <note>original: αὐτοψία (autopsia)</note> is full of danger, leading to the intended goal only by a long and arduous journey, history presents under a single view whatever has been subtly investigated and discovered with much labor by distinguished geniuses. It proposes excellent examples of virtues, the various outcomes of plans, and the uncertain turns of fate. Thus, as the Comic poet <note>A reference to the Roman playwright Terence in his play *Adelphoe*</note> says, one may look into the lives of outstanding men as if into a mirror, and take an example for oneself from others, learning what to pursue and what to avoid without any personal risk. Nor is this only a safer path, but also a shorter and easier one, which leads by way of examples. Indeed, nothing is more effective than the illustrious example of a great man; at the very memory of whom, the mind [forms its] counsels...

<summary>In this opening chapter, Holstenius argues that history and biography are essential for human life because they provide a safe, efficient way to gain wisdom and prudence through the examples of others. He sets the stage for his study of Porphyry by framing biography as a "mirror" for human behavior.</summary>

<keywords>biography, history, Lucas Holstenius, Porphyry, wisdom, prudence, Suda, Terence, examples, education</keywords>

--- Page 184 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page continues the introduction to Chapter I. The previous page discussed history as a mirror of human action; this page transitions into the specific value of biography (the life of an individual) as a subset of history, using Homer and Moses as primary examples.</meta>

and measure all deeds, as if by a rule; by whose virtue one may be spurred to the pursuit of praise and glory. And just as a large painting, displaying a great variety of men and actions wonderfully blended by the artist's genius, delights the eyes and minds of the viewers with that very variety; yet if one brilliant figure stands out among the rest, it calls the spectator back and holds them with a peculiar pleasure. So too, a complete history, written in a universal manner <note>original: καθολικῶς (katholikōs)</note>, instructs the mind of the reader with a great abundance of precepts and examples; but if the reader encounters the virtue and noble actions of one particular outstanding man, expressed with more careful study than the rest of the work, the mind lingers in admiration of his counsels and deeds, and is stirred by a more powerful emotion toward the imitation of such a beautiful example.

For this reason, we see that nearly a twofold method of writing has been observed by those who have transmitted the deeds of others to the memory of posterity through these monuments of genius: either they weave the entire history of a nation with a continuous thread, or they narrate the life of some outstanding individual in a specific work, painting his virtues or vices in "living colors," as the saying goes, so that each detail might be more clearly perceived when set apart.

And of this practice, if I am not mistaken, we might rightly call Homer the primary author. He proposed a model for both types of writing in his two works: in one, he describes the Trojan expedition undertaken by the shared counsel and arms of all the Greeks, and the clashing forces of Europe and Asia; in the other, however, he presents the wisdom <term>prudentia: practical wisdom or resourcefulness</term> of a single man, Ulysses <note>The hero of the Odyssey</note>, tested through various hazards and depicted with all the colors of art and genius. 

Unless, perhaps, one prefers to trace this origin even higher to Moses—the most ancient of all historians—and thus to the Spirit of God Himself, by whose inspiration we believe sacred history was committed to writing. For the divine Moses, having set forth the origins of the human race, the lineage of the Patriarchs, and the history, rites, and laws of the Jewish people in five books <note>The Pentateuch, the first five books of the Bible</note>, also produced in a specific work the life of Job. There, he recorded Job’s extraordinary constancy of mind amidst extreme calamities and his piety toward God, so that the spirits of the Jews—afflicted by long servitude and long-lasting wanderings—might be [encouraged by] such a... <note>The sentence continues on the following page.</note>

<summary>Holstenius distinguishes between general history and biography, arguing that the life of a single great man provides a more powerful moral example. He cites Homer’s two epics and Moses’s authorship of both the Pentateuch and the Book of Job as the foundational models for these two styles of writing.</summary>
<keywords>biography, historiography, Homer, Ulysses, Moses, Job, moral examples, Pentateuch, Porphyry</keywords>

--- Page 185 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page concludes the discussion of Homer's influence on biographical forms and transitions into a distinction between the "Active" and "Contemplative" modes of life. It uses a geographical metaphor (continents vs. islands) to explain why the lives of philosophers are easier to record than those of political figures.</meta>

# The Philosopher Porphyry

...confirm by a signal example of patience. For indeed, the most learned men believe Moses to be the author—or perhaps the translator—of this book <note>The author refers here to the Book of Job, often attributed to Moses in early modern scholarship, cited as the ultimate example of patience.</note>, and an infinite number of both sacred and secular authors thereafter imitated that same method of writing.

But since there are two kinds of life: one that is occupied with the administration of public affairs and the cultivation of the arts of war and peace, which they call the **active** <note>original: *actiuum*</note> life; the other, however, which sets aside the care of public business to be spent on the study of wisdom and the investigation of things divine and human, which is called the **contemplative** <note>original: *contemplatiuum*</note> life. 

The first kind belongs entirely to civil society, which is governed by the joined counsels and efforts of many. Therefore, those who follow that way of living seem not unlike actors, each of whom plays his own role in such a way that the actions of all, joined together, present a single **drama**. 

The other kind of life, however, lies hidden, enclosed within the shaded porticos of schools or the walls of a Museum <note>In this period, a *Musaeum* referred to a place dedicated to the Muses, meaning a library or a place of private study rather than a modern exhibition hall.</note>, and is spent in private leisure far from the noise of the marketplace. One might rightly compare its followers to *individual* <note>original: μερικοῖς (*merikois*), meaning solo or partial actors</note> tragic actors, who perform their parts each by themselves.

And therefore, I believe that authors who have written about famous men in either category can be rightly distinguished into two classes: one encompasses the lives of men illustrious in the arts of war or peace; the other encompasses those who shone with the praise of wisdom and learning. 

But just as a region of a great continent, surrounded on all sides by the borders of other provinces, is distinguished with difficulty because of the mixed and confused boundaries of its neighbors; whereas an island, which the open sea flows around, or a region defined by a continuous range of mountains or a deserted wasteland of sand, is described with no trouble; so the lives and deeds of political men are more difficult to explain in isolation. This is because many external matters are mixed in with them, which, since they can neither be fully narrated nor entirely omitted, make the history less easy to follow. 

On the other hand, because the pursuit of wisdom is followed in private leisure, the lives of philosophers are more correctly narrated in a dedicated work: which consists almost entirely in an enumeration of teachers and students, as well as a review of their doctrines and the titles of their works. And there exist excellent writers in both categories, whom if I were to review in this place after the most learned men who have written about the Greeks...

<meta>catchword: Greeks,</meta>

<summary>This page distinguishes between the "Active" (political) and "Contemplative" (philosophical) lives, arguing that the private, self-contained nature of a philosopher's life makes it a more suitable subject for a dedicated biography compared to the messy, interconnected lives of politicians.</summary>
<keywords>Active life, Contemplative life, Moses, Job, biography, political history, philosophical history, geography metaphor</keywords>

<meta>Continuity: The previous page concluded a distinction between the active life of political figures and the contemplative life of scholars. This page begins a bibliographic survey of those who have recorded the lives and teachings of philosophers.</meta>

### On the Life and Writings

...historians, both Greek and Latin, have recently treated, I would be doing work already done. Instead, I will explain only a few things concerning those who wrote the lives of men distinguished by their pursuit of wisdom or letters—whom the Greeks encompass under the common name of **philosophers** <note>original: φιλοσόφων (*philosophōn*)</note> or **sophists** <note>In this period, "sophist" did not necessarily have a negative connotation, but referred to professional teachers of philosophy, rhetoric, and culture.</note>—because this treatment pertains to our present purpose. 

<margin>Sermon 11.</margin>
Theodoret <note>Theodoret of Cyrus (c. 393–458 AD), a Christian bishop and influential theologian who often compared pagan philosophy with Christian truth.</note> distinguishes these writers into two kinds in his eleventh sermon: some who merely review the **dogmas** and maxims of the philosophers, and others who also join the lives of each individual to their particular teachings. To these, I think a third kind should be added: those who wrote only the lives, or eulogies. Of this sort, there still exist the *Lives of the Sophists* by Philostratus, contained in two books, and the *Lives of the Philosophers and Sophists* written by Eunapius. To these I also add the book by Marinus of Neapolis on the life of Proclus, which he titled by another name, *On Happiness* <note>original: περὶ εὐδαιμονίας (*peri eudaimonias*)</note>, and the funeral oration of Choricius praised by Photius, in which he most elegantly narrates the life of Procopius of Gaza. I shall soon make this oration public, along with the complete life of Proclus, from a Vatican manuscript.

To the first kind <note>Those who focus only on teachings or "dogmas."</note> belong Plutarch’s five books *On the Opinions of the Philosophers* <note>original: περὶ ἀρεσκόντων τοῖς φιλοσόφοις (*peri areksontōn tois philosophois*); literally "On things pleasing to the philosophers."</note> and their summary, which survives among the works of Galen under the title *Philosophical History*, though it is more correctly attributed to Aëtius by the testimony of Theodoret. To this same class should be referred the five books of Xenophon’s *Memorabilia* <note>original: ὑπομνημονευμάτων (*hypomnēmatōn*)</note>, or *Socratic Dissertations*, and the eight books of the *Discourses* <note>original: διατριβῶν (*diatribōn*)</note> of Epictetus, written by Arrian in imitation of Xenophon. Also included are his twelve books of *Sermons* or *Dialogues*, which Photius praises in his *Library*, as do Aulus Gellius (Book 1, Chapter 2 and Book 19, Chapter 1) and Simplicius at the beginning of his commentary on the *Enchiridion* of Epictetus; only four of these books survive today.

Of this same genre was Aristotle’s *Collection of Pythagorean Doctrines* <note>original: Συλλογή τῶν τοῖς Πυθαγορείοις ἀρεσκόντων (*Syllogē tōn tois Pythagoreiois areksontōn*)</note>, which Simplicius uses twice or thrice in his second book of *On the Heavens*. There is also Alexander Polyhistor's book *On the Symbols of the Pythagoreans*, cited by Cyril in his fifth book *Against Julian*, and the *Pythagorean Matters* of Xenocrates, which Laertius mentions. Likewise, the *History of Astrology* <note>original: ἀστρολογικὴ ἱστορία (*astrologikē historia*)</note> of Eudemus, in which he set out what each person had discovered in that science; Simplicius, among others, cites this several times in his second book of *On the Heavens*. There are similar works by others, for which there is now no time to mention. But as for those who wrote concerning the life and do—

<meta>catchword: and dogmas</meta>

<summary>Holstenius provides a bibliographic overview of ancient biographical literature, categorizing authors into those who record philosophical doctrines, those who record lives, and those who combine both.</summary>
<keywords>Theodoret, biography, doxography, Proclus, Epictetus, Pythagoras, Arrian, Plutarch, bibliography, history of philosophy</keywords>

<meta>Continuity: Following the classification of biographical styles on the previous page, Holstenius now provides a comprehensive bibliography of ancient and medieval authors who wrote "Lives" of famous scholars and philosophers to establish the context for Porphyry’s work.</meta>

### Of Porphyry the Philosopher

...and treat the doctrines of famous men and their works, a great many are recounted by the ancients. Even now, several outstanding writers in this genre survive, among whom **Herodotus** <note>The famous Greek historian, though the "Life of Homer" attributed to him is now considered pseudo-epigraphal (not written by him).</note> deserves the primary place for his published book on the lineage and life of Homer. There also exists a book by **Plutarch** in which he encompassed the lives of the ten Greek orators. By far the most famous, however, are the ten books of **Diogenes Laertius** *On the lives, doctrines, and sayings of those who excelled in philosophy* <note>original: περὶ βίων, καὶ δογμάτων, καὶ ἀποφθεγμάτων τῶν ἐν φιλοσοφίᾳ εὐδοκιμησάντων (*peri biōn, kai dogmatōn, kai apophthegmatōn tōn en philosophia eudokimēsantōn*)</note>, and the book of **Hesychius of Miletus** *On the wise men who shone in the field of education* <note>original: περὶ τῶν ἐν παιδείᾳ διαλαμψάντων σοφῶν (*peri tōn en paideia dialampsantōn sophōn*)</note>. The encyclopedist Suidas calls this latter work a "nomenclature or register of those famous in education" <note>original: ὀνοματολόγον ἢ πίνακα τῶν ἐν παιδείᾳ ὀνομαστῶν (*onomatologon ē pinaka tōn en paideia onomastōn*)</note>, and he inserted an abridgment of it into his own work. Indeed, this is what the following words mean: *this book is the epitome* <note>original: ἡ ἐπιτομή ἐστι τοῦτο τὸ βιβλίον (*hē epitomē esti touto to biblion*)</note>. 

In this passage, which is perfectly sound and intact, the distinguished **Johannes Meursius** <note>A Dutch classical scholar (1579–1639) known for his extensive commentaries on Greek antiquities.</note> attempts in vain to apply a "remedy" <note>A philological term for "correcting" a text one believes is corrupted.</note>. For one cannot rightly call the book of Hesychius an abridgment of Diogenes Laertius, since Hesychius records not only philosophers, but also grammarians, rhetoricians, poets, and historians. However, anyone who compares the two will not doubt that **Suidas** transcribed a great part of the lives of illustrious men into his own work from Hesychius. **Jacques Sirmond** <note>1559–1651; a French Jesuit scholar and royal confessor known for his immense knowledge of ecclesiastical history.</note>—a man of the greatest genius and deservedly praised for his total erudition—showed me old manuscript registers of this kind collected from Suidas, or rather transcribed by Suidas.

There also exist in this genre the eight books of **Philostratus** *On the Life of Apollonius*, and the first two books of **Iamblichus** *On the Life and Sect of Pythagoras*. I shall shortly publish these along with two others; for only four out of the original seven books have reached us. To this genre also belong the four books of **Porphyry** *On the Lives of the Philosophers*, the first of which I am now bringing to light for a second time. We must discuss this work a little more accurately shortly hereafter. Also of this type are the two books of **Suetonius** *On Illustrious Grammarians and the Lives of Rhetoricians*. Then there are the *Catalogs of Illustrious Writers* by **Saint Jerome** and those who followed him: **Gennadius**, **Isidore**, **Ildefonsus**, and **Sigebert**. Also relevant here are the *Lives of the Twelve Prophets* written by **Epiphanius**, as well as the small book by **Euthalius the Deacon** *On the Life and Travels of the Blessed Paul*, and others like them, which I intentionally omit, as my purpose is to treat only the lives of philosophers. **Photius** and Suidas read the *Life of the Philosopher Isidore* written by **Damascius** <note>Damascius (c. 458–538 AD) was the last head of the School of Athens; his biography of his teacher Isidore is a vital source for late Neoplatonism.</note>, from which they excerpted many things. And an unknown au—

Holstenius situates his edition of Porphyry within a long tradition of "Lives of the Philosophers" and "Illustrious Men."</summary>

--- Page 188 ---
<meta>Continuity: Following the previous discussion on the classification of biographical writings, Holstenius continues to list significant ancient authors and explains the theological importance of studying these lives to prove that Greek philosophy originated from older Hebrew and Chaldean wisdom.</meta>

### On the Life and Writings

I have appended fragments by the author concerning the life of Pythagoras from the same small book by Porphyry via **Photius** <note>A 9th-century scholar and Patriarch of Constantinople whose "Bibliotheca" preserved summaries of many lost ancient works.</note>. I also published the life of **Aristotle** by an unknown author, with a commentary by **Juan Nuñez** <note>Ioannes Nunnesius (1522–1602), a Spanish humanist scholar.</note>, a few years ago in Belgium. It would be tedious to list here all those authors of "Illustrious Lives" whom **Athenaeus**, **Diogenes**, **Suidas**, and others praise, but whom the ravages of time have denied us. Among those most celebrated are **Aristoxenus**, **Callimachus**, **Hermippus**, **Aristocles of Messene**, **Sotion**, and his abridger **Heraclides**; as well as **Antiphon**, **Hippobotus**, **Neanthes of Cyzicus**, and others like them cited by Porphyry in this book. 

Furthermore, if one compares those three types of writers with each other, it will undoubtedly appear that the first type—which contains only lives or eulogies—is prepared not so much for narrative or historical reliability as for "sophisticated display" <note>original Greek: ἐπίδειξιν (*epideixin*), a rhetorical term for a showy speech intended to demonstrate the speaker's skill.</note>. The second type, which reviews only dogmas and tenets, since it contains almost nothing "historical" <note>original Greek: ἱστορικὸν (*historikon*)</note>, should be called philosophical rather than historical. But the third type properly pertains to specific history, which brings much more utility to the solid knowledge of things. This is because it not only mentions dogmas, but also especially shows from where and from whom the Greek sages drew their doctrine, by narrating their travels to foreign nations and their long-term acquaintance with them. 

For from there, "the Greek theft from foreign philosophy" <note>original Greek: τῆς βαρβάρου φιλοσοφίας ἑλληνικὴ κλοπὴ (*tēs barbarou philosophias hellēnikē klopē*)</note> clearly appears; and it is transparently seen that the entire wisdom of the Gentiles was derived from Hebrew and **Chaldean** <note>A term often used in this period to refer to the ancient Babylonians or Near Eastern astrologers and sages.</note> sources. **Clement of Alexandria** in the *Stromata* (Books 5 and 6) and **Eusebius** in the *Preparation for the Gospel* (Book 10) have abundantly proven this by bringing forward the testimonies of the Greeks themselves. By this very means, the "haughty-looking and sophistical trifles" <note>original Greek: σεμνοφανεῖς κ. σοφιστικαὶ τερθρείαι (*semnophaneis kai sophistikai terthreiai*)</note> of the Greeks, as Eusebius calls them, and their blasphemous slanders against our religion are utterly torn down, and the faith and authority of the Sacred Scriptures are established. 

Then indeed, the sources and principles of the many errors that heretics have sought to bring into God's Church are uncovered. For it can be proven by more than one example from this little book of Porphyry that the Philosophers were not unjustly called "the patriarchs of almost all heretics" by **Tertullian** <note>An early Christian author (c. 155–220 AD) who famously argued that Greek philosophy was the source of all Christian heresies.</note>. For example, the dual "coupling" <note>original Greek: συζυγία (*syzygia*), a term used to describe pairs of opposing principles, often found in Pythagorean and Gnostic thought.</note> of the Pythagoreans, which [deals with] the opposites... <meta>catchword: res</meta>

<summary>Holstenius lists various lost and extant biographers of philosophers and argues that biographical history is valuable because it proves Greek philosophy was borrowed from older Hebrew and Eastern sources, thereby defending Christianity against pagan claims.</summary>

# *Of Porphyry the Philosopher*

...embraces **the union of unifying and separating powers** <note>original Greek: τῆς ἑνοποιῶν καὶ διακριτικῶν δυνάμεων συζυγίας (*tēs henopoiōn kai diakritikōn dymameōn syzygias*)</note>, it undoubtedly brought forth for us those two contrary principles of the Manichaeans <note>The Manichaeans were a dualistic religious group who believed the universe was a struggle between the kingdoms of Light and Darkness.</note>. It is more than clear from the reading of **Epiphanius** <note>A 4th-century Church Father known for his "Panarion," a massive refutation of various heresies.</note> alone that they borrowed many other things from Pythagoras. For as he writes in *Heresy 66, Chapter 9*, that **Mani** <note>The 3rd-century Persian prophet and founder of Manichaeism.</note> taught that one who eats flesh also devours a soul, and is transformed into that animal which he ate: is this not derived from the <term>metempsychosis</term> <note>The Greek term for the transmigration of souls, or reincarnation.</note> of Pythagoras? He commanded abstinence from eating living things **on account of the honor due to what is kindred** <note>original Greek: διὰ τὴν τοῦ συγγενοῦς τιμὴν (*dia tēn tou syggenous timēn*)</note>, and because he believed all souls to be **of the same kind** <note>original Greek: ὁμοιοειδεῖς (*homoioeideis*)</note>. 

And since the same Pythagoras had taught that the soul—a particle of divine breath fallen to lower realms—is shut within the body as if in a prison, and held captive by the chains of bodily affection, from which, unless it departs purified, it falls back into other bodies; but once purified, it finally returns to its kindred stars and the celestial choir of souls: Mani made these same dogmas his own, as can be seen from the cited passage of Epiphanius. For he asserted that the soul is a particle of God, torn from God by the princes of the contrary principle, and thrust captive into the body; and after transmigration or "transfusion" through five bodies, it is finally sent through the moon and the sun to the region of the blessed. This they called the **decanting and ferrying across** <note>original Greek: μεταγγισμὸν καὶ διαπορθμευσιν (*metaggismon kai diaporthmeusin*)</note> of the soul. 

These delusions Epiphanius abundantly refutes in chapters 28 and 55. To these are related the errors of **Origen** <note>An early Christian theologian whose views on the pre-existence of souls were later declared heretical.</note> **concerning the pre-existence of the soul** <note>original Greek: περὶ προϋπάρξεως τῆς ψυχῆς (*peri proyparkseōs tēs psychēs*)</note>, for which no one skilled in philosophical history doubts that he took the **starting points** <note>original Greek: ἀφορμὰς (*aphormas*)</note> and opportunity from the doctrine of Pythagoras and Plato. 

Furthermore, as to Porphyry’s report that Pythagoras called the sea a "tear," this entirely agrees with the fables of the Jews, who teach that whenever God remembers the calamities of the Jewish people, He pours out two tears into the ocean sea. And since **Proclus** (in his first book on the *Timaeus*) and **Aristotle** (*Meteorology*, book 2, chapter 3) testify that the Egyptians called the Nile—and **Empedocles** called the sea—the "sweat of the earth": who does not see from which workshop the dogma of those same Manichaeans and Valentinians emerged? Of whom **Irenaeus**, **Titus of Bostra** (book 1), and Epiphanius (*Heresy 66*) affirm that they called rain the "sweats of the princes of matter"? These profane delusions differ little from the opinion of that **rustic Comic poet** <note>A reference to Aristophanes, who in his play "The Clouds" mocks traditional religion by suggesting rain is just Zeus urinating through a sieve.</note>, who believed that when it rained, Jupiter was urinating through a sieve; or from the poet...

<sig>M 2</sig>
<meta>catchword: s...</meta>

<summary>Holstenius argues that Manichaean and Origenist doctrines, such as reincarnation and the pre-existence of the soul, are not original but are corrupted versions of Pythagorean and Platonic philosophy. He further links "heretical" views on nature—like rain being the sweat of demons—to ancient Greek and Egyptian metaphors.</summary>
