### Of the Life and Writings

...of the ancient fables <note>original Latin: *veterum fabulis*, completing the word from the previous page.</note>, which imagined that the hot springs of Himera <note>A Greek colony in Sicily famous for its thermal baths.</note> arose because **Hercules**, having defeated the giants, washed his sweat away there. I pass over in silence the incantations and other magical tricks which the followers of **Basilides** <note>A 2nd-century Gnostic teacher who claimed to follow secret traditions.</note> and **Mani** <note>The founder of Manichaeism.</note> held in common with **Pythagoras**; as well as those things regarding the symbolic and mystical method of teaching, the silence and the testing of disciples, and also what is handed down in this little work concerning the "man-slayers" <note>original Greek: ἀνθρωποκτινοῖς (*anthrōpoktinois*); likely a reference to controversial interpretations of ancient Hebrew laws or sacrifices.</note> of the Hebrews. I have touched upon some of these matters in these notes; I am saving others for more extensive observations on **Iamblichus** <note>A later Neoplatonist philosopher who wrote a more extensive, though more legendary, biography of Pythagoras.</note>.

Furthermore, since it was not permitted to bring forth the complete work of **Porphyry**—which the injury of time has begrudged us—I have decided to collect into one place those things I have observed concerning Porphyry's own life and writings among various authors. I do this so that I might in some part make up for the loss which the world of letters suffered through the destruction of such an excellent history. I also intend to re-examine more accurately certain points that seem to have been less correctly reported by the most famous writers of our century. In this matter, since my sole purpose has been that some benefit might come from our study toward the understanding of sacred and profane history, as well as the illustration of the philosophy of Pythagoras and **Plato**, I hope for easy pardon from fair judges if I should depart in any way from the accepted opinions of great men. 

Nor should anyone think that out of the vice of **self-importance** <note>original Greek: αὐθολκῆς (*autholkēs*), suggesting an stubborn or self-centered bias.</note> or a desire for contradiction, I have undertaken to defend an enemy of Christ and a slanderer of truth against the public prejudice of the whole world. I myself detest his blasphemies with all my soul, and I would not wish for such errors to have fallen from a man who was otherwise so great. Indeed, I confess that, along with the most holy writers of the Church, I greatly admire his excellence of genius and his subtlety in philosophizing. And if perhaps I might avoid the resentment directed at this writing by their authority, I will produce in this place some of their testimonies. In these, they praised Porphyry's genius and the magnitude of his learning, as well as the excellence of his works; thus, while I speak with them, I can only be rebuked and condemned alongside them. 

And indeed **Eusebius** <note>The Bishop of Caesarea (c. 260–339 AD), known as the "Father of Church History."</note>, who refuted Porphyry's blasphemies in thirty books, places him in the number of the most famous philosophers in Books 3 and 9 of his *Preparation for the Gospel* <note>original Latin: *De Praeparatione Evangelica*.</note>, or as he himself says: **among those most illustrious and known to all, whose fame in philosophy is not small...** <note>original Greek: τῶν μάλιστα διαφανῶν καὶ πᾶσι γνωρίμων, κλέος τέ οὐ μικρὸν φιλοσοφίας (*tōn malista diaphanōn kai pasi gnōrimōn, kleos te ou mikron philosophias*).</note>
among... <note>original catchword: παρ (*par*)</note>

<meta>Continuity: On the previous page, Holstenius explained his intent to reconstruct Porphyry’s biography from various sources to compensate for lost historical records. This page provides a series of testimonials from major Christian and Late Antique authors to establish Porphyry’s reputation as a peerless scholar, despite his opposition to Christianity.</meta>

## Of Porphyry the Philosopher

...renowned among the Greeks <note>original Greek: παρ’ ἕλλησιν ἀπενηνεγμένων (*par’ hellēsin apenēnegmenōn*).</note>. In the same manner, **Cyril** <note>Cyril of Alexandria, a 5th-century bishop known for his polemics against paganism.</note>, in the first book of his work *Against Julian*, says that on account of his secular learning, he obtained a **singular renown** <note>original Greek: δόξαν οὐκ ἀζυγῆ (*doxan ouk azyge*), meaning a fame that is unsurpassed or "not unyoked" from excellence.</note>—that is, he held an extraordinary reputation among his own people. Wherefore **Saint Augustine**, in the seventh book of *The City of God*, chapter 25, *calls him a noble philosopher.* In book 10, chapter 10, he calls him a *more learned Platonist*; and in chapter 32, *a man endowed with no mediocre genius.* Elsewhere, he calls him *a great philosopher of the gentiles*, and in book 19, chapter 22, he calls him the *most learned of philosophers, although (he says) he was the fiercest enemy of the Christians.* Indeed, the judgment of that most holy man could be hindered by no personal feeling from granting the praise of exceptional learning even to a most sworn enemy.

Furthermore, **Boethius** <note>A 6th-century philosopher and statesman who bridge the gap between classical antiquity and the Middle Ages.</note>—who is no less celebrated for the glory of his holiness and martyrdom than for his knowledge of all sacred and secular philosophy—showed how highly he valued **Porphyry**. This is apparent from the fact that he not only read his books, but translated them into Latin and illustrated them with repeated commentaries so that they might also be read by others. Moreover, in his explanation of **Aristotle** and his entire method of philosophizing, Boethius testifies that he prefers and follows Porphyry alone above all others. Thus, I believe he most closely joined **Platonic** doctrine with **Aristotelian** thought by following Porphyry’s example. This is very clearly shown by the honorable testimonies regarding his learning and writings which exist throughout the works of Boethius.

In his book on the <term>Categories</term> <note>Aristotle's work on the fundamental ways in which things can be described.</note>, Boethius says that in his exposition, he followed the opinion of Porphyry, *because it seemed more direct and clearer.* Likewise, in the first book of his larger commentaries on the book <term>On Interpretation</term> <note>Aristotle's *De Interpretatione*, a foundational text on logic and language.</note>, he testifies that he mostly translated his exposition from Porphyry. *For here,* he says, *he seems to us to excel as an interpreter both in the sharpness of his intellect and in the arrangement of his thoughts.*

And at the second text, he writes: *But Porphyry judges both matters acutely and subtly, and he approves of the opinion of **Alexander** more.* <note>Alexander of Aphrodisias, a famous 2nd-century commentator on Aristotle.</note> And soon he adds: *Wherefore Alexander must be approved either by his own opinion or by the authority of Porphyry.* 

In book 2, text 14, he says: *This passage, when best understood and truly perceived, has removed many confusions and many errors in speech; and this is the exposition which no interpreter before Porphyry saw.* 

And at text 21, he says that the opinion of Alexander is indeed not inconsistent with reason, but that *Porphyry’s is better*; and he adds that this passage was *diligently explained by Porphyry.* 

In book IV, at text 46, having rejected the views of **Herminus**... <note>Herminus was a 2nd-century Peripatetic philosopher and teacher of Alexander of Aphrodisias.</note>

exposition as being too obscure, he adds: *we, however, following Porphyry and agreeing with that most learned man, say, etc.* And in book five, text 58—which concerns the unity of propositions—when he had said that many are confused by the darkness of that passage, so that they cannot worthily follow or explain what was said by **Aristotle**, having brought forward the explanations of various men, he finally adds: *But all these understand nothing at all; rather, there is a better exposition which Porphyry gave.* And I have brought forward these testimonies of Holy men <note>original Latin: "Sanctorum hominum." Holstenius is pointing out that even canonized Christian saints like Boethius and Augustine respected Porphyry’s intellect.</note> lest the labor I have spent in restoring and illustrating the writings of **Porphyry** should be held against me by those who—contrary to the authority of the ancients—wish all his books to be banished from the schools and shaken from the hands of students; especially those books which **Boethius** long ago spent labor in translating and explaining. They wish this so that, by suppressing and deleting the memory of the ancients, they might sell their own "new wares" at a higher price. For such people, those disasters which the floods of **Goths**, **Saracens**, and other barbarians brought upon the world of letters <note>original Latin: "rei literariæ."</note> ought at least to have been sufficient. But now I shall proceed to tell of the life and writings of **Porphyry**.

### CHAPTER II.

->*Porphyry’s homeland and lineage: his native name Malchus: his age arranged by years: mention of Sicily. Saint Augustine’s opinion of Porphyry the Sicilian.*<-

**Porphyry**, as he himself left written in his *Life of Plotinus*, had **Tyre** <note>A major Phoenician port city, now in modern-day Lebanon, famous in antiquity for its purple dye.</note> as his homeland—the most noble and leading city of **Phoenicia**. **Eunapius** and **Suidas** <note>The *Suda* is a massive 10th-century Byzantine encyclopedia; Holstenius refers to it by the name of its supposed author, Suidas.</note> have related the same, following either his own word or the public consensus of all. **Eunapius** affirms that his parents were of no mean rank. And he himself mentions his father in the *Life of Plotinus*, saying that the name **Malchus** was common to him and his father in their native Syro-Phoenician tongue. This name signifies *king* in Greek <note>original Greek: βασιλέα (*basilea*).</note> and *rex* in Latin. From this, on account of the purple <note>The Greek word for purple is *Porphyra*. Since kings wore Tyrian purple, his teacher Longinus gave him the name "Porphyrius" as a Greek play on his Semitic name, "King."</note>, the insignia of royal dignity, **Longinus** bestowed the name **Porphyrius**...

<meta>Continuity: Following the introduction to Porphyry’s life in Chapter II, Holstenius begins to explain the origin of the philosopher's name, reconciling his native Syrian name with his famous Greek name.</meta>

his teacher gave him: because to Greek ears the name **Malchus** <note>original: "Malchus"</note> seemed unusual and foreign. The words of **Porphyry** himself in his *Life of Plotinus* are as follows: <note>original Greek: "γέγραφεν ὁ Ἀμέλιος..."</note> *Amelius wrote a book which he titled* (for so it must be read in that passage) *On the Difference between the Doctrines of Plotinus and Numenius; he dedicated it to me, Basileus. Now, Basileus <note>A Greek name meaning "King."</note> was my name—mine, Porphyry’s—being called Malchus in my native dialect, which was the same name my father had been called. But the name Malchus carries the interpretation of "King," if one should wish to translate it into the Greek language,* etc. *Amelius composed a book, which he titled "On the Difference between the Doctrine of Plotinus and Numenius": this book he dedicated to me, Basileus, that is, to the King. For that was the name for me, Porphyry: who in my native dialect was called Malchus. This name also belonged to my father. But the name Malchus, if anyone should wish to transfer it into the Greek tongue, signifies <term>Basilea</term> or King.* And he adds: **Longinus** addressed a book to him under his vernacular name of **Malchus**; but **Amelius** preferred to use the Greek word **Basileus**. **Eunapius** reports the same thing in his *Life of Porphyry* with these words: <note>original Greek: "Μάλχος δ' κατὰ τὴν Σύρων..."</note> *In the city of the Syrians, Porphyry was first called Malchus. This name can be said to mean "king." But Longinus named him Porphyry, bestowing the title as a reference to the royal <term>insignia</term> of the purple robe.* <note>The name Porphyry (Porphyrius) comes from the Greek word for purple, the color of royalty.</note> *His name at the beginning was Malchus, which in the language of the Syrians sounds like "king," but by a twisted significance referring to purple—the royal mark upon a garment—Longinus named him Porphyrius, as if to say "the Purple One."* And from this **Suidas** <note>A 10th-century Greek encyclopedia/lexicon.</note> is to be understood, who, omitting the name **Malchus**, mentions only that Greek one: <note>original Greek: "Πορφύριος , ὃς κυρίως ἐκαλεῖτο βασιλεύς..."</note> *Porphyry, who was properly called King, a Tyrian philosopher.* Likewise **Hesychius**: in whose works today one reads, *Makkos, king.* When, without a doubt, it was formerly written **Malchos**. For that **Malchus** signifies "king" in the dialect of the **Hebrews** and **Syrians**, besides being very well known, is also attested by **Saint Jerome** in his *Life of Malchus the Hermit*: *There was there a certain old man named Malchus, whom we in Latin can call "King," a Syrian by nation and language.* And **Ammianus Marcellinus** in book 24 mentions the river of the **Assyrians** <term>Naar-malcha</term>, which is the "Royal River" according to **Strabo** and **Ptolemy**, between the **Tigris** and **Euphrates**: *Another part of the river,* he says, *by the name Naar-malcha, which is interpreted as the River of Kings, flows past Ctesiphon.*

And **Pliny** <note>Pliny the Elder, a Roman natural historian.</note> in Book 6, Chapter 26, notes: *By the Assyrians, it was called Armilchar by everyone* (or rather **Narmalcha**), which signifies a "royal river." Furthermore, that the name **Malchus** was not uncommon among the Syrians is testified even by the **Sacred Scriptures**, which record the servant of the high priest by this name <note>Referring to the Malchus in the Gospel of John, whose ear was cut off by Peter.</note>. **Flavius Josephus**, in his *Jewish Antiquities* (Book 1, Chapter 16), also praises a certain prophet **Cleodemus**, who also bore the name **Malchus**, and who—following the example of **Moses**—had written a history of the Jews, citing **Alexander Polyhistor**. Mention of a General **Malchus** also appears in the same author's *The Jewish War* (Book 1, Chapters 6 and 9). Furthermore, **Eusebius** (Book 7, Chapter 2) records that **Malchus**, a martyr, suffered at Caesarea in Palestine. **Photius**, in codex 78, read of **Malchus of Philadelphia**, a <term>sophist</term> <note>A professional teacher or philosopher.</note> and a writer of Byzantine history, whom **Suidas** <note>A massive 10th-century Byzantine encyclopedia.</note> mistakenly believed to be from Byzantium for that reason. Finally, **Saint Jerome** wrote the *Life of Malchus the Hermit*, whom I mentioned just now—not to mention the infinite Hebrew names from the histories of the **Old Testament** which were derived from that same root <note>The Semitic root *MLK*, meaning king.</note>.

Now, as for the fact that **Longinus** exchanged the name **Malchus** for that other name, **Porphyry**, it is a perfect twin to the story told by **Julius Capitolinus** regarding **Clodius Albinus**: that when he happened to be wrapped in his mother’s purple bandage, his nurse jokingly gave him the name **Porphyry** <note>From the Greek *porphyra*, meaning purple.</note>. Nor is this unlike the title **Porphyrogenitus** <note>meaning "Born in the Purple"</note> which was granted to the children of the **Greek Emperors**: because, having just been brought into the light as heirs to their father's empire, they were wrapped in the royal purple. Although others write that this name was imposed upon them because of the royal palace, which the common people called the **Porphyra** <note>The Purple Chamber in the Great Palace of Constantinople.</note>: a matter which will be a more suitable place for fuller discussion in the second part of our *Geographical Compendium*. Concerning the age of **Porphyry**, **Suidas** briefly reports that he lived under **Aurelian** and reached the times of **Diocletian**. This he drew from **Eunapius**, who testifies that Porphyry’s life coincided with the reigns of the Emperors **Gallienus**, **Claudius**, **Tacitus**, **Aurelian**, and **Probus**. However, since Porphyry himself carefully notes the years of his age in his *Life of Plotinus* and maintains an accurate account of the times, it will be worth the effort to follow the details more closely, as they will be useful for what we shall say later. First, then, regarding his arrival in the city <note>Rome</note> and at the school of **Plotinus**, he writes thus: *In the tenth year of the reign of Gallienus...* <note>original Greek: "Τῷ δεκάτῳ δ' ἔτει τῆς Γαλιήνου..."</note>

<meta>Continuity: Following the discussion of Porphyry’s original name, Malchus, and its Semitic roots on page 14, Holstenius now turns to the chronological details of Porphyry’s arrival in Rome and his relationship with his teacher, Plotinus.</meta>

<page-num>15</page-num>
<header>Porphyry the Philosopher</header>

<note>original Greek: βασιλείας ἐγὼ Πορφύριος...</note> During the reign [of Gallienus], I, Porphyry, having come from Greece with Antonius the Rhodian, found Amelius in the eighteenth year of his association with Plotinus. Now Plotinus was, in the tenth year of the reign of Gallienus, around fifty-nine years old. I, Porphyry, first met him when I myself was thirty years old.

In the tenth year of the Emperor **Gallienus**, I, **Porphyry**, coming from Greece to Rome together with **Antonius the Rhodian**, found **Amelius** already having spent eighteen years in close friendship with **Plotinus**. Plotinus, moreover, in that tenth year of Emperor Gallienus, was approximately in the fifty-ninth year of his life. I, Porphyry, when I first joined him, had then completed thirty years. Since, therefore, the tenth year of Gallienus falls in the year 264 of **Christ**: by calculating the timeline, it is established that Porphyry was born in the tenth year of **Alexander** <note>Emperor Alexander Severus.</note>, which is the year of Christ 233. 

Furthermore, although he had come to Rome for the first time ten years earlier—that is, around the twentieth year of his age—he stayed there for only a short space of time, as Plotinus was then taking a break from his teaching duties. When he joined him during this second arrival, he devoted himself to his work for five full years. Finally, in the sixth year, he set out for **Lilybaeum** in Sicily <note>Modern Marsala.</note> to visit **Probus**, a distinguished man living there. That was the fifteenth, or last, year of Gallienus, which is the year 269 from the birth of Christ. 

He himself testifies to both facts in these words: *Plotinus was in his fifty-ninth year. I, however, spent this very year and five others besides with him; indeed, I had even come to Rome ten years before, etc.* Regarding the Sicilian journey, he adds shortly after: *At the time I was living in Sicily, where I had arrived around the fifteenth year of Emperor Gallienus, Plotinus composed five books.* 

I thought these things should be confirmed all the more carefully using Porphyry’s own words because the Illustrious **Cardinal Baronius** <note>Cesare Baronio (1538–1607), a prominent Catholic historian.</note> reports that he heard Plotinus in Rome for eighteen years—ascribing to Porphyry what should be attributed to Amelius, as is clear from the passage cited above. 

He set out for Sicily, moreover, on the advice of Plotinus, who sent him away from the City <note>Rome.</note> because he was suffering from <term>black bile</term> <note>Melancholia; in ancient medicine, an imbalance of humors believed to cause deep depression.</note>. For he writes that he was so disturbed by this disease that he once attempted to lay hands on himself <note>A reference to Porphyry's contemplated suicide.</note>, had not Plotinus, with his wisdom, hindered the insane plan. Regarding this matter, it is better to hear the man himself speaking: *I,* he says,

<page-num>16</page-num>

-> # On the Life and Writings <-

...at one time I even contemplated taking my own life. Plotinus, however, sensed this in a wondrous way, and as I was walking about my home, he immediately stood by me and said: "This pursuit and plan of yours is by no means the mark of a sound mind, but rather of a soul raging with black bile <note>original Latin: "atra bile"; a reference to the ancient medical theory of humors, where an excess of 'black bile' was thought to cause deep melancholy or clinical depression.</note>." 

Therefore, he ordered me to depart from Rome. Obeying him, I set out for Sicily, because I had heard that a certain **Probus**, an excellent man, was then living near **Lilybaeum** <note>Modern-day Marsala, Sicily.</note>. The result was that I did indeed desist from my plan of suicide; however, in the meantime, I was prevented from being at **Plotinus’s** side during his death. For Plotinus died in **Campania** three years after Porphyry’s departure, in the sixty-sixth year of his age, after the second year of the Emperor **Claudius** <note>Claudius II, known as Claudius Gothicus.</note>; this is the year of Christ 261 <note>The text reads "cclxi" (261), though historically Plotinus's death in the second year of Claudius II corresponds to 270 or 271 AD. This may be a chronological error in the 1630 edition.</note>, at which time Porphyry was living at Lilybaeum, as he himself testifies near the beginning of his *Life of Plotinus*. 

These are the facts which it has been possible to gather from Porphyry's own writings according to a precise calculation of the years. Concerning the remainder of his life, we can determine almost nothing with certainty: how long he stayed in Sicily, what places he lived in afterward, nor when he ceased to live. I shall, however, bring forward those things which I have gathered here and there, and present them under a single view, by which the life of Porphyry may be illustrated. 

In this regard, it must first be said that **Vincent of Lérins** and **Eusebius** have reported, on the testimony of Porphyry himself, that when he was almost a boy, he traveled to **Alexandria** to hear **Origen** <note>A prominent early Christian theologian (c. 184–253 AD). Porphyry, though a critic of Christianity, respected Origen's intellectual stature.</note>. Vincent’s words concerning Origen in chapter 23 are as follows: 

*“If anyone does not accept our Christian testimony concerning his [Origen's] incredible knowledge, let him at least receive the confession of a pagan, as the philosophers testify. For that impious Porphyry says that, moved by Origen's fame, he traveled to Alexandria when he was almost a boy, and there he saw him, already an old man; but clearly such and so great a man, who had built the very citadel of all knowledge.”*

Eusebius indeed cites a similar passage from the third book [of Porphyry] *Against the Christians*, in the sixth book of his *Ecclesiastical History*, chapter 19:

<note>original Greek: ὁ δὲ τρόπος τῆς ἀτοπίας...</note> *“Let the example of this absurdity be taken from Origen, a man with whom I had an acquaintance when I was very young, who was highly celebrated and is still celebrated through the writings he left behind...”* 

From this, I wonder why **Eunapius** <note>A 4th-century sophist and historian.</note> calls Origen a "fellow-student" <note>original Greek: συμφοιτητὼ (sumphoitētēn).</note> of Porphyry...

# Porphyry the Philosopher

...or that he had called him a fellow student; and since it is established by Porphyry's own words that Origen died at sixty-nine years of age in the year of Christ 256—which was the third year of the emperors Gallus and Volusianus—Porphyry was then in his 22nd or 23rd year. Moreover, since the ecclesiastical annals testify that Origen came to Rome during the reign of Philip and the papacy of Fabian, I believe it can be reasonably concluded that Porphyry set out for Alexandria either toward the end of the Emperor Gordian’s reign, or in the first years of Philip (who stripped Gordian of his power), around his 14th year. For those words, "being still quite young" <note>original Greek: *κομιδῆ νέος ὢν ἔτι*</note>, seem to indicate this. 

He also testifies in Book 3 of *On Abstinence* that he lived in Carthage, where he mentions a partridge he had raised himself, which was so tamed that it understood the human voice and tried to respond to it. It is likely that he crossed over there from Lilybaeum <note>Modern-day Marsala, Sicily.</note>. It appears from another of his passages—which Eusebius inserted in Book 10 of the *Preparation for the Gospel* from the first book of *Philological Lectures*—that he also spent some time in Athens, where all the studies of wisdom, but especially the doctrine of the divine Plato, were then flourishing. There, he recounts that the birthday of Plotinus was celebrated with feasts at the home of Longinus, and he commemorates the conversations held among the guests regarding the "thefts" <note>A term for literary plagiarism or uncredited borrowing of ideas, a frequent topic of debate among ancient scholars.</note> of authors. "Longinus," he says, "invited us to celebrate the festival of Plotinus in Athens, along with many others and Nicagoras the sophist" <note>original Greek: *τὰ Πλωτίνεια ἑστιᾶν ἡμᾶς Λογγῖνος Ἀθήνησι κέκληκεν ἄλλους τὲ πολλοὺς, καὶ Νικαγόραν τὸν σοφιστὴν*</note>, etc. From this, one can see that Porphyry moved there from Sicily after the death of Plotinus. 

Eunapius also testifies that after his stay in Sicily, he returned to Rome and practiced the art of oratory with the highest praise, so that the Roman Senate and People held him in great esteem and honor. It is said that he finally passed away there in deep old age. To these things I also add that his *Life of Plotinus* was published around the seventieth year of his age, since he mentions in that same work that in his sixty-eighth year he was seized by a divine ecstasy or "departure of the mind." However, according to Saint Augustine in Book 10 of *The City of God*, chapter 10, this should perhaps rather be judged as a disturbance of a melancholic brain, or a deceptive phantom of a demon transformed into an angel of light. That was the year of Christ 301, the 18th year of the Emperor Diocletian, who, abdicating his power three years later, had Constantius as his successor. But since Porphyry, in the mentioned passage of that 68th year, as previously...

<page-num>18</page-num>

### On his Life and Writings

...having slipped his mind; it seems likely that the *Life of Plotinus* was written by him in extreme old age, and toward the end of the reign of **Diocletian**. For **Suidas** <note>The *Suda*, a massive 10th-century Byzantine encyclopedia of the Mediterranean world.</note>, drawing correctly from **Eunapius**, asserts that **Porphyry** did not outlive the times of that emperor. Neglecting his authority, the author of the most *Illustrious Ecclesiastical Annals* <note>Referring to Cardinal Caesar Baronius (1538–1607), whose *Annales Ecclesiastici* was a massive history of the Church.</note> extends Porphyry's life much further—namely, to the 23rd year of **Constantine** and beyond—as we shall see shortly.

**Eusebius** testifies in Book 6, Chapter 19 of his *Ecclesiastical History* that while in **Sicily**, Porphyry wrote many other books, including those wicked ones against the Christian religion <note>Porphyry’s *Against the Christians* was so controversial that it was later banned and burned by imperial decree, leaving only fragments preserved in the writings of his critics.</note>. After him, **Saint Jerome** says the same in his *Catalogue of Illustrious Writers* where he reviews the works of Eusebius. It can certainly be clearly inferred that he lived for several years in **Sicily**, and indeed with the greatest reputation. For **Longinus**, after the death of **Plotinus**, summoned him from there back to his homeland and requested that the books of Plotinus be sent to him.

Then **Saint Augustine**, in Book II, Chapter 31 of his *Retractions*, believed because of his long stay on the island that "that Porphyry of Sicily," as he says, whose fame is most celebrated, was a different person from our philosopher here. In this matter, there is a similar error—whether by **Nicephorus Callistus** or his translator—in Book V, Chapter 13, where the aforementioned passage from Eusebius is transcribed. For while **Eusebius** says, "Porphyry who settled in Sicily in our time" <note>original Greek: *ὁ καθ' ἡμᾶς ἐν Σικελίᾳ καταστὰς Πορφύριος*</note>, in Nicephorus it is now read as "Porphyry the Sicilian." From this, one can see that he prepared a great and illustrious name for himself by writing during his Sicilian retirement; this could hardly have happened in the space of only one or two years.

Furthermore, **Ammonius** affirms that the *Isagoge* <term>Introduction</term> regarding the five terms <note>The *Isagoge* was an introductory text to Aristotle's logic that became a standard textbook in the Middle Ages.</note> dedicated to **Chrysaorius** was written by him in **Sicily**. His words will be cited by me in their proper place below, although he [Ammonius] offers another reason for the departure to Sicily, which in my judgment is quite foolish.

### CHAPTER III.

->*That Porphyry wrote against the Christians in Sicily.*<-
->*The opinion of Baronius is weighed according to Lactantius.*<-
->*When Saint Methodius suffered.*<-

**B**ut the most illustrious author of the *Annals* <note>Referring to Cardinal Caesar Baronius (1538–1607), the great historian of the Catholic Church.</note> contends, contrary to the accounts of **Eusebius** and **Jerome**, that **Porphyry** attacked the Christian religion with his impious books not in **Sicily**, but in **Bithynia** <note>An ancient Roman province in what is now northwestern Turkey.</note>. The foundation of his opinion rests on the words of **Lactantius** <note>Lactantius (c. 250 – c. 325 AD), a prominent early Christian author and advisor to Constantine the Great.</note> in Book V *On Justice*, Chapter 2, which are worth considering more closely. He relates that at the time when **Diocletian** raged with the most cruel persecution against the Christians, and was destroying the churches of the true God from their foundations, two individuals emerged who lent their blasphemous tongues to the fury of the Emperors, attempting to attack the Christian religion in writing: one was a philosopher, the other a judge. 

However, Lactantius names neither of them. I believe he did this so that he would not transmit to the memory of posterity the names of those who had sought to attack and extinguish the saving name of **Jesus Christ** with their wicked books. *“I,”* says **Lactantius**, *“when I was summoned to teach rhetoric in Bithynia, and it happened at the same time that the temple of God was overthrown, two men appeared there who insulted the lying and cast-down truth, I know not whether with more arrogance or insolence. One of whom professed himself a high priest of philosophy; but he was so corrupt that, as a master of self-restraint, he burned no less with greed than with lusts. In his lifestyle he was so lavish that, though in his school he was a defender of virtue and a praiser of frugality and poverty, he dined more sumptuously at the palace than he did at home. Nevertheless, he covered his vices with his long hair and his philosopher’s cloak, and—what is the greatest veil of all—with his riches. To increase these riches, he sought friendships with judges with wonderful ambition, and suddenly bound them to himself by the authority of a false name; not only so that he might sell their judicial decisions, but also to prevent his neighbors, whom he drove from their houses and fields, from reclaiming their property through this influence. This man, who indeed destroyed his own arguments by his conduct, or rather exposed his conduct by his arguments—”*

<meta>argue-</meta>

<summary>This page debates whether Porphyry wrote his famous polemic "Against the Christians" in Sicily or Bithynia, citing a detailed (and highly critical) description by Lactantius of an unnamed, hypocritical philosopher in Bithynia.</summary>

*...he accused himself, acting as a severe censor and a most bitter accuser against his own self. At that very same time, while the righteous people* <note>The Christians suffering under Roman persecution.</note> *were being wickedly torn apart, he vomited out* <note>original: *euomuit*—a strong, polemical term used by Lactantius to describe the production of these writings as something foul.</note> *three books against the religion and the name of Christians. He professed, above all else, that it was the duty of a Philosopher to aid men in their errors and to recall them to the true path—that is, to the worship of the Gods, by whose divinity and majesty (as he said) the world is governed. He claimed he could not allow ignorant men to be enticed by the deceits of certain people, lest their simplicity become prey and fodder for cunning men. To these things he adds many points concerning the design and argument of the entire work, which I would prefer be read in his own words.* As for who that man was, says the most illustrious author of the *Annals* <note>Referring to Cardinal Caesar Baronius, mentioned on the previous page.</note>, whom **Lactantius** says claimed the first place for himself among the philosophers of that time, no one else occurs to us whom we might suspect, except **Porphyry**. He excelled among the Platonists <note>The Neoplatonists, the school of thought founded by Plotinus which sought to synthesize Plato's philosophy with a mystical worldview.</note> of those times; and it is well established that he published commentaries against the Christian religion. Therefore, as he [Baronius] says, there is no reason why we should attribute what **Lactantius** reports to anyone other than **Porphyry**. Indeed, when I consider with what monstrous hatred those men were regarded by Christians—those who wished to become famous by undertaking a war against Christ—and how the detestable name of such men emerged from the writings and curses of Christians, I think it hardly possible that the memory and name of this philosopher, whoever he finally was, could have been lost; especially since he entered into this ill-fated contest with a naked and open head <note>original: *nudo & aperto capite*—a Latin idiom meaning to act openly, boldly, or without disguise.</note>, as they say. Furthermore, **Lactantius** testifies that the Christians took it most heavily of all that he had attacked them at that specific time when hateful cruelty was raging; when no one, for fear of dire persecution, dared to undertake the defense of the afflicted truth. These things undoubtedly increased the hatred, so that as soon as the Church could breathe again, they rose up against the enemy of Christ with even sharper spirits; since no protection remained for him in the favor of princes, and no escape was left in what was now an open and fair contest. The name of **Celsus** <note>A 2nd-century philosopher and early critic of Christianity.</note> was loathed, because he was that first "pig from the herd of **Epicurus**" <note>original: *Epicuri de grege porcus*—a famous quote from the poet Horace, used here as a common insult against philosophers who supposedly favored worldly pleasures over spiritual truth.</note> who dared to grunt with a filthy mouth against the rising light of truth. Nor did less envy follow that other enemy of Christ, **Hierocles** <note>Sossianus Hierocles, a Roman official and philosopher who was a primary instigator of the Diocletianic Persecution.</note>, who inscribed the delusions of his ignorance with the title *The Friend of Truth* <note>original Greek: Φιλαλήθης (*Philalethes*).</note>: so that with a deceptive...

It describes the deep resentment Christians felt toward these philosophers who used their pens to attack the Church while the state used the sword.</summary>

<header>Of Porphyry the Philosopher.</header>

...to ensnare the unwary with his enticements. Julian the Emperor <note>Julian the Apostate (reigned 361–363 AD), who attempted to revive Roman paganism.</note> also, having deserted the sacred rites of our religion <note>Christianity</note>, and swelling with the errors of the pagans, foolishly and arrogantly raised "the Hellenic brow" <note>original: τὴν ἑλληνικὴν ὀφρὺν — an idiom for Greek pride or intellectual haughtiness</note> against Christ. Lest he should seem to have defected from the truth without cause, he took up his pen to attack the faith, having learned from the experience of past centuries that it could not be destroyed by the sword. Thus, he became eternally infamous under the name of the "impious" and "enemy of God" <note>original: θεομάχου (theomachou)</note>. But the hatred against Porphyry was by far the most severe, against whom the universal Church of Christ took up arms with united strength. For about thirty Catholic writers refuted his blasphemies, if any trust is to be placed in the *Chronicles* of Flavius Lucius Dexter <note>A late 4th-century official and historian; his "Chronicle" is now largely considered a later forgery, though Holstenius treats it as a source here.</note>. Even the Emperors themselves, by their edicts, desired his name to be forever infamous.

While I consider all these things, I am inclined to follow the opinion of the most illustrious Baronius <note>Cardinal Caesar Baronius (1538–1607), the great historian of the Catholic Church.</note>; yet there are things that hold me back in no small way. First, indeed, is the authority of **Eusebius** and **Saint Jerome**, who clearly testify that he wrote against the Christians during his retreat in Sicily. For Eusebius says in the aforementioned passage: "Porphyry, who was among us and staying in Sicily, prepared writings against us" <note>original: ὁ καθ’ ἡμᾶς ἐν Σικελίᾳ καταστὰς Πορφύριος συγγράμματα καθ’ ἡμῶν ἐσκευάσατο</note>. And Saint Jerome reports that Eusebius published *thirty books against Porphyry, who was writing in Sicily at that same time.* Furthermore, the number of books does not match: for there were fifteen, not only as he published but as **Jerome** and **Suidas** <note>The *Suda*, a massive 10th-century Byzantine Greek encyclopedia.</note> testify. Then, especially, is the consideration of time and life. For neither his age nor his commitment to the Pythagorean way of life <note>An ascetic lifestyle involving vegetarianism, silence, and strict self-discipline.</note> seem to agree with those things which **Lactantius** recorded concerning that "Asian Philosopher." For since the "height" <note>original: ἀκμὴ (akmē)</note> of Diocletian's persecution and the destruction of the churches (which Lactantius mentions in the said passage) falls in the year of Christ 302—which was the nineteenth year of Diocletian's reign and the sixty-ninth year of Porphyry's life—I leave it to the judgment of others whether the things Lactantius relates concerning the lusts, luxury, flattery, and courtly parasitism of that "Asian Philosopher" are suitable for a decrepit old man. This is especially true for one who had always followed the dry and sober discipline of the Pythagorean life. That he observed this most diligently is abundantly proven by his books *On Abstinence* <note>original: περὶ ἀποχῆς (Peri Apoches). Porphyry's treatise arguing for vegetarianism and the avoidance of animal sacrifice.</note> written to Firmus Castricius. Nor do I think he would have urged that way of life and philosophy in such a laborious volume to an intimate friend, bound to him by the link of fraternal necessity, if he himself

<summary>Holstenius argues against the idea that Porphyry was the corrupt philosopher described by Lactantius, noting that Porphyry would have been nearly seventy years old and committed to a strict ascetic, Pythagorean lifestyle at the time.</summary>

...he himself would have neglected it. Nor did any of the ancients, at least as far as I have observed, object to anything of this kind against Porphyry—the same man whom Eusebius, Jerome, Augustine, and others perpetually call rabid, mad, impious, blasphemous, and an enemy of truth and God. These authors never missed an opportunity or handle to attack him. And although I am not unaware that many philosophers spoke one way in the school and lived another way at home, I have this reason above all for thinking otherwise of Porphyry: at the very same time that Lactantius says that "Asiatic philosopher" <note>Lactantius, in his *Divine Institutes*, describes an anonymous philosopher in Bithynia who wrote against Christians while living a life of extreme luxury and vice.</note>—overwhelmed by luxury and lust—wrote against the Christians, Porphyry, through a sparse and sober diet, studied how to raise his soul (kept pure from the contagion of the body and withdrawn from the fellowship of the senses) toward a life according to the mind <note>original: *vitam secundum mentem* — a core Neoplatonic ideal where the intellect rules the physical impulses.</note>. This is clearly testified by what he reported happened to him in his sixty-eighth year of age in his *Life of Plotinus*. Added to this is that huge number of books and commentaries to which he devoted his entire life to producing, so much so that even in extreme old age he published the writings of Plotinus, edited and arranged in a fitting order. I pass over the fact that his very age could have provided no small material for a rebuttal, and he might have been mocked under the suspicion of senility.

But that argument seems to me the weightiest of all: that Methodius, Bishop of Patara and Tyre—who composed books of elegant and refined prose against Porphyry—was crowned with martyrdom at the end of the persecution of Diocletian, or as others affirm, under Decius and Valerian. These are the words of Saint Jerome regarding that most holy man. And just as that latter opinion (about his martyrdom being completed under Decius) can be proved clearly false by the fact that when Decius was Emperor, Porphyry (whom Methodius refuted) had scarcely reached his twentieth year of age, and only after sixteen full years did he finally set out for Sicily; so the former opinion clearly shows that before the aforementioned nineteenth year of Diocletian <note>Approximately 303 AD, the start of the Great Persecution.</note>, Porphyry had already published his blasphemies and Methodius had responded to them. This is significant because Lactantius explicitly affirms that the Christians remained silent for the sake of the time <note>i.e., they kept quiet to avoid provoking further persecution.</note>. No one would think he would have said that if Methodius alone had then undertaken to defend the common cause of the Church. Nor would Eusebius and...

<summary>Holstenius argues that Porphyry's ascetic lifestyle and the historical timeline of his critics, such as Methodius of Olympus, prove he was not the corrupt philosopher described by Lactantius. He uses the dates of Roman Emperors and Porphyry's own writings to establish a more accurate chronology of his anti-Christian works.</summary>

<meta>Continuity: Holstenius continues his rebuttal of Cardinal Baronius. Having argued that Porphyry’s lifestyle was ascetic rather than indulgent, he now uses the testimony of Saint Augustine to show that Porphyry’s attitude during the Diocletianic persecutions was one of intellectual skepticism rather than the active, violent instigation described by Lactantius.</meta>

<page-num>23</page-num>
<header>Of Porphyry the Philosopher</header>

Jerome <note>Saint Jerome (c. 347–420 AD), a Church Father known for his Latin translation of the Bible (the Vulgate) and his historical writings.</note>, the most diligent observers of these matters.
Indeed, who would suppose that Saint Augustine was so unskilled or unmindful of Church history that—when he stated in Book 10, Chapter 32 of *The City of God* that Porphyry lived at the time when Diocletian was attacking the Church of Christ with the cruelest persecution—he would grieve that Porphyry was not moved by the constancy of so many martyrs to recognize the way of truth, yet omit in such a fitting place that Porphyry, at that very same time, was inciting the emperor's raging mind with his impious books against innocent Christians? And that he insulted the truth, which was then being crushed by arms, with such persistent insolence? For this is the very reason Lactantius so heavily accuses that "Asiatic Philosopher." I will set down Saint Augustine’s words here, so that anyone may judge the matter more easily. For when Augustine had written near the beginning of the chapter that Porphyry said in the first book of *On the Return of the Soul* <note>original: *de regressu animæ* — a lost work by Porphyry concerning the soul's journey back to the divine.</note>: *that a certain single sect which contains the universal way of liberating the soul has not yet been received. He then adds: What is this universal way, if not that which is not specific to any one nation, but is common to all nations and was divinely granted? This man [Porphyry], being endowed with no small talent, certainly does not doubt that this way exists. For he does not believe that divine providence could have left the human race without this universal way of liberating the soul. He does not say it does not exist; but only that so great a good, and so great a help, has not yet been received, nor yet been brought to his knowledge. Nor is it a wonder, for Porphyry was then still among the living, when that universal way of liberating the soul—which is none other than the Christian religion—was permitted to be attacked by the worshipers of idols and demons, and by earthly kings, for the sake of establishing and consecrating the number of martyrs, that is, witnesses of the truth: through whom it might be shown that all bodily evils are to be endured for the sake of the faith of piety and the commendation of truth. Porphyry therefore saw these things, and he thought that through such persecutions this way would quickly perish, and for that reason he did not think it was the universal way for liberating the soul; he did not understand that the very thing which moved him, and which he feared to suffer if he chose it, belonged instead to the confirmation of that way and its more robust recommendation.* Is it not gathered from this passage by the most certain reasons, that Porphyry either [witnessed] that last perse— <note>The text cuts off mid-word "persecution."</note>

<sig>N</sig>
<meta>catchword: persecution—</meta>

Augustine suggests Porphyry failed to recognize Christianity as the "universal way" because he mistakenly believed the persecutions would destroy the faith.</summary>

## CHAPTER IV.

->*That the life of Porphyry is not correctly extended to the time of*<-
->*Constantine the Great. Publilius Optatianus Porphy-*<-
->*rius the poet confused with Porphyry the Philosopher.*<-
->*Britain, a province fertile in tyrants.*<-
->*The edict of Constantine explained.*<-

But regarding these matters, I grant everyone the freedom to hold their own opinion, until the truth, more carefully polished by the efforts of others, may shine forth more clearly from the shadows of antiquity. But I do not think we should rashly agree with that eminent man <note>Holstenius is likely referring to Cardinal Caesar Baronius (1538–1607), whose *Annales Ecclesiastici* was the standard Catholic history of the time, though Holstenius often found its chronology of Porphyry to be flawed.</note> in this: that, contrary to the authority of Eunapius and the *Suda* <note>The *Suda* is a massive 10th-century Byzantine encyclopedia of the Mediterranean world, formerly attributed to an author named Suidas.</note>, he extends the life of Porphyry as far as the twenty-third year of Constantine the Great; and also in those things which he related concerning Porphyry's exile and his <term>palinodia</term> <note>original: *palinodia* — a formal recantation or retraction of one’s previous statements.</note>. These claims have not escaped the observations and censures of the most learned men. 

But it is better to set down here what the leader of ecclesiastical history writes regarding the year of Christ 325. Regarding Porphyry—who had poured out so many and such great blasphemies against the Christian religion, and had assailed the Emperor himself with insults, when among other things (as Gildas <note>Gildas (c. 500–570 AD) was a British monk known for his history of Britain; he cites Porphyry’s remark about British usurpers.</note> testifies) he said out of hatred for the Emperor that "Britain is a province fertile in tyrants"—Constantine records that he received a fitting reward for his labors. For Constantine appears to have affirmed this not only because Porphyry's books were condemned to the flames, but also because he was punished with exile. This clearly suggests that the same Porphyry was still surviving at the time when these things were being written against Arius <note>Arius (c. 256–336 AD) was a priest in Alexandria whose teachings led to the Arian controversy and the Council of Nicaea.</note>. 

Based on the testimony of Saint Jerome, it appears that three years later—namely in the twenty-third year of the same Emperor Constantine—he was freed from exile after sending a distinguished volume to the Emperor. In these words, learned men have long since warned that Porphyry the philosopher is being confused with Publilius <note>Publilius Optatianus Porphyrius was a 4th-century Latin poet who wrote complex "pattern poems." He was indeed exiled and later recalled by Constantine after dedicating a book of poetry to him.</note>...

<summary>Holstenius argues that historians have mistakenly extended Porphyry the Philosopher's life into the reign of Constantine the Great by confusing him with a poet of the same name, Publilius Optatianus Porphyrius.</summary>

<margin>
St. Augustine
...
</margin>

...to Optatianus Porphyrius, who was indeed a Christian poet, but in other respects most trifling; for Saint Jerome testifies that he spoke of him in the panegyric to Constantine Augustus, which still survives. <note>Publilius Optatianus Porphyrius was a 4th-century Latin poet who wrote elaborate "pattern poems" to gain the favor of Emperor Constantine while in exile.</note> And so that no room for doubt remains, I shall add the verses of the exiled Poet, in which he addresses his Thalia <note>Thalia was the Muse of comedy and lyric poetry; here the poet addresses his own poetic inspiration.</note> in the preface.

> *From here with trembling foot you seek the roofs of the venerable court,*
> *Where your elegance might be far too rough;*
> *The present fates of the poet deserve these habits;*
> *Scarcely has the place from which you come provided even this much.*
> *Yet you may go as a suppliant, and pray to the lord:*
> *Filth and these squalors are fitting for the wretched.*
> *When the merciful one has granted pardon, and restored son and home,*
> *You yourself shall go with your hair combed once more.*

Nor does he seem to hallucinate <note>In this context, *hallucinari* means to wander in judgment or be mistaken.</note> any less in this: that he concludes from Gildas the Briton <note>Gildas (c. 500–570 AD) was a British monk and author of *On the Ruin and Conquest of Britain*.</note> that Porphyry spoke insultingly of the Emperor Constantine and was for that reason condemned to exile; whereas the contrary is instead proven from Gildas’s own words. *“Therefore now omitting,”* he says, *“the ancient errors common to all nations, nor enumerating the diabolical portents of my own country—which almost surpass the Egyptian ones in number—and remaining silent about the ancient years of the savage tyrants, who were made known in other far-off regions: so that Porphyry, that rabid oriental dog against the Church, even added this with his pen of madness and vanity: ‘Britain,’ he says, ‘is a province fertile in tyrants.’”* <note>original: *Britannia, inquit, fertilis prouincia tyrannorum.* This famous phrase referred to the many usurpers who rose to power in Roman Britain.</note> What, I ask, is said here about Constantine, or the age of Constantine? Since he speaks explicitly of those times in which the Britons acknowledged neither the power of the Roman Empire, nor the truth of the Christian religion. And without a doubt, that man of the greatest judgment would have felt otherwise if he had observed the passage in Saint Jerome from which Gildas transcribed Porphyry's words; which I have thought should be added from the book to Ctesiphon against the Pelagians. <note>The Pelagians were followers of Pelagius, who emphasized human free will over divine grace; Jerome wrote extensively against them.</note> *“And finally, what your companion Porphyry is accustomed to object to us: for what reason did a merciful and compassionate God, from Adam until Moses, and from Moses until the coming of Christ, suffer all nations to perish in ignorance of the law and the commandments of God? For neither Britain, a province fertile in tyrants, nor the Scottish tribes, and all those as far as...”*

--- Page 206 ---
<meta>Continuity: Lucas Holstenius continues his refutation of the idea that Porphyry the Philosopher lived during the time of Constantine the Great. He is currently addressing a specific quote attributed to Porphyry regarding Britain being "fertile in tyrants," which some historians had used to link him to the era of Constantine’s rise. Holstenius argues this description actually refers to the ancient tribal state of Britain prior to the full establishment of Roman order.</meta>

# On his Life and Writings

<meta>catchword: That</meta>

The barbarian nations surrounding the Ocean had known the Prophet Moses and the other Prophets. Is it not clearer than the midday sun that nothing is said here against Constantine? Rather, those times are being described which preceded the advent of Christ and the expansion of the Roman Empire to the Britons. Nor does it allow us to doubt Porphyry’s intent, because this same question, taken from Porphyry’s books *Against the Christians*, was proposed among others to Saint Augustine, to which he responds at length in Letter 49 to Deogratias. <note>original: "Epistola XLIX. ad Deo gratias." St. Augustine’s Letter 102 (often numbered 49 in older editions) addresses six questions posed by pagans, many drawing from Porphyry's criticisms of Christian doctrine.</note>

But as for why Porphyry called Britain a "province fertile in tyrants," the words of that most grave author, Gaius Tacitus, concerning the Britons in his *Life of Julius Agricola*, teach us: *Formerly,* he says, *they obeyed kings; now they are drawn by princes into factions and rivalries. Nor is anything more useful for us against these most powerful nations than that they do not deliberate in common.* <note>In the classical world, a "tyrant" often referred to any ruler who seized power without legal right or a local chieftain, rather than just a cruel dictator.</note> And Strabo, in his fourth book, is the authority that among the Britons there were <term>lordships</term> <note>original: "δυναστείας" (dynasteias); refers to the rule of powerful local lords or petty kings.</note> and that most of these dynasts cultivated Caesar through embassies and services. These matters can be understood from the fourth book of the *Commentaries on the Gallic War*, where Caesar himself narrates his British expedition. Even in this present century, the tyrannical factions of families have not died out, especially among the Scots and the Irish; rather, as each nobleman is either more illustrious by birth or more prompt with his hand, so much more easily does he draw many subjects and clients into his own interests and parties—often dangerous even to the kings themselves. From these points, it is now sufficiently apparent what should be thought of the explanation of Constantine's edict. Anyone who inspects the edict itself will easily recognize that the opinion of that most illustrious writer <note>Likely referring to Baronius or another historian Holstenius is correcting.</note> is by no means supported by it. 

For when the Emperor had written at the beginning: *Porphyry, an enemy of religion, having published certain wicked commentaries against piety, has found a worthy reward;*—lest anyone should interpret this as referring to exile or some other kind of physical punishment, he himself adds: *and such a reward, that he himself indeed should be filled with the greatest reproach and disgrace, infamous among all posterity; but his impious writings should be utterly destroyed.* Is it not manifest that the Emperor had no other purpose than to brand Arius <note>Arius (c. 256–336 AD) was a priest in Alexandria whose teachings led to the Arian controversy; Constantine's edict sought to equate Arius's infamy with that of the pagan critic Porphyry.</note> and his followers with that same infamy of a shameful name with which he saw Porphyry burning? And that, by a similar example by which he had extinguished the books of Porphyry, he might suppress their impious writings?

<summary>Holstenius argues that Porphyry’s description of Britain as "fertile in tyrants" refers to its ancient tribal factions, not Constantine's era, and clarifies that Constantine's edict mentioned Porphyry only as a historical example of infamy to be applied to the heretic Arius.</summary>

<header>Of Porphyry the Philosopher.</header>

This excellent deed of the most pious Emperor [Constantine] was later followed by Theodosius, who, by a similar law, wished for the followers of Nestorius to be called "Simonians" <note>Followers of Simon Magus, often considered the first heretic by early church fathers.</note>—an invidious name taken from Simon Magus. He intended that those who had imitated Simon's crime in deserting God should rightly be seen to have inherited his name (Book 6 of the Codex, *On Heretics and Manicheans*). I wished to explain these matters more accurately because I see some who rashly and *without examination* <note>original: *ἀβασανίστως* (*abasanistōs*), meaning without critical testing or scrutiny.</note> embrace the opinion of Baronius <note>Cardinal Caesar Baronius (1538–1607), a major Catholic historian whose *Annales Ecclesiastici* Holstenius is critiquing.</note>, asserting that Porphyry, breathed upon by the spirit of God and finally converted to the faith, recognized Jesus as the true God just as Saul of Tarsus did. *Would that he had known Him (for I will use the prayer of Saint Augustine) and had more safely committed himself to be healed by Him rather than to his own virtue, which is human, fragile, and weak, or to his most destructive curiosity.* These are the words of the most holy Father concerning this philosopher in book 10 of *The City of God*, chapter 27.

### CHAPTER V.

->*That Porphyry was not a Jew, but a Phoenician; why Saint Jerome called him a "Batanaeote." Various conjectures on that passage.*<-

Nor perhaps would it be out of place to weigh here another opinion of a most great writer concerning Porphyry's homeland and lineage—that new opinion, derived from an obscure passage of Saint Jerome, which goes against the constant testimony of all other authors. In this matter, however, I can more easily prove what is false than affirm what is true. Saint Jerome's words in the preface to his commentaries on the *Epistle to the Galatians* are: *Which that Batanaeote and wicked Porphyry, in no way understanding it, objected in the first book of his work against us, saying that Peter was rebuked by Paul.* <note>Jerome refers to the incident in Galatians 2 where Paul confronts Peter; Porphyry used this to argue that the Apostles were divided and the religion inconsistent.</note> From these words, the Most Illustrious Cardinal [Baronius] contends that Porphyry was not a Phoenician, but a Jew; not a Tyrian, but a Batanaeote <note>A native of Batanea, the biblical Bashan, located in modern-day Syria/Jordan.</note>; born in Batanea, which is a city of Judea, fifteen miles from Caesarea in Palestine, celebrated for its most healthy springs of warm water. He argues that because Porphyry was ashamed of his country and race, he falsely and impudently claimed to be Phoenician, by which name he was judged both then and now by the common crowd.

<meta>catchword: Nor</meta>

<page-num>28</page-num>
# On the Life and Writings

Nor perhaps will this opinion seem to shrink from the truth to one who considers that Saint Jerome lived for many years in the heart of Judea in the closest familiarity with learned Jews. He searched out many of their works and became acquainted with many things that could in no way be known by others, because of the "unsociable and inhospitable" <note>original: *διὰ τὸ ἀκοινώνητον καὶ ἄξενον* (*dia to akoinōnēton kai axenon*). Holstenius refers here to the historical reputation for isolationism among the Jewish community in antiquity.</note> nature of that nation. I add also that which Socrates <note>Socrates Scholasticus (c. 380–439 AD), a Christian church historian.</note>, following Eusebius, has reported: that Porphyry was originally a Christian, but out of hatred for Christians—by whom he had been more severely rebuked at Caesarea in Palestine than his uncontrolled spirit could bear—he deserted our religion. For this argues for a light and fickle character, for whom "recklessness and deviation" <note>original: *ἀπονοία & παράβασις* (*aponoia & parabasis*). These terms suggest a psychological state of desperate madness and a moral wandering from the path.</note> were not unusual. To these a third reason is added: that he cites the Prophet Moses in his little book *On the Cave of the Nymphs*, and in books II and IV of *On Abstinence*, he relates many things about the institutions and sects of the Jews, drawing from the works of Theophrastus <note>A student of Aristotle who wrote on various religions.</note> and Flavius Josephus <note>The famous 1st-century Jewish historian.</note>.

But there are many more and stronger reasons by which the contrary seems to be proven. For I say nothing of how shameless that impudence would be for a man to lie about his lineage and homeland. I would easily concede that this could be done by one who had hardened his soul and his brow against God and truth itself; yet at least the fear of infamy would have deterred him, since such a lie could by no means remain hidden. This is because many people from the greatest city of Phoenicia <note>Tyre, Porphyry's undisputed birthplace in most traditions.</note> were migrating daily to both Alexandria and Rome, and by their evidence, any fraud would easily be uncovered. This is even more likely because Porphyry himself names a Tyrian father, and Eunapius <note>Eunapius (c. 347–414 AD), author of *Lives of the Philosophers and Sophists*, who provides much of our biographical data on Porphyry.</note> testifies that he was born of a noble family among his own people. 

And how could he have remained hidden, having become so famous for his learning and wisdom? And even if he were hidden from others, how could he be from Longinus, Iamblichus, and other Syrians who associated with him? Porphyry himself inserted a letter from Longinus into the *Life of Plotinus*, written to him from Phoenicia, by which he was summoned from Sicily; in this letter, Longinus mentions Tyre as Porphyry's homeland. Amelius, a fellow student of Porphyry, lived in Phoenicia at the same time. Could the lie regarding the homeland and lineage of their friend have been hidden from them? They nevertheless constantly called him a Tyrian in their writings. And when Libanius <note>A famous Greek teacher of rhetoric (c. 314–392 AD).</note>, the Syrian Sophist, in his funeral oration for the Emperor Julian, called Porphyry "the Tyrian elder" as a mark of honor; Socrates the historian, however, who in book III of his *Ecclesiastical History*, chapter 23, [attacked] those words of the Sophist with searched-out arguments and words...

and laboriously refutes him, yet he raises no dispute regarding his homeland. And who would believe that, in such a zeal for contradiction, he would have omitted that which could have cast a grave infamy upon the name of the **Tyrian** old man <note>Porphyry is often called the "Tyrian" because he was born in Tyre, Lebanon.</note>: if he had received any such report either in writing or by word of mouth? Nor would **Suidas** <note>The *Suda*, a 10th-century Byzantine encyclopedia formerly attributed to an author named Suidas.</note> have neglected it, who diligently transcribed from all quarters those things which pertain to the mocking of pagan philosophers; for I pass over in silence the fact that **Eusebius**, in more than one place in his books ***On the Preparation*** and ***Demonstration of the Gospel***, calls him a pagan and a pagan teacher and theologian. And the other **Fathers**, although they often compare the calumnies of **Porphyry** with the blasphemies of the **Jews**, yet no one writes that he shared a common lineage with them. Such a fact would have been most useful for increasing ill-will against him and for refuting his impious objections against **Christ**. That he had either seriously embraced the **Christian** religion, or at least had not been averse to it, I could easily believe, since as a boy he had heard **Origen of Alexandria**, the brightest light of the Christian religion; especially since, besides the authority of **Socrates** and **Nicephorus**, **Saint Augustine** in Book 10 of ***The City of God***, chapter 28, expressly affirms that he, swollen with the tumor of vain knowledge, recoiled from the most healthy humility of **Christ**. And let it be so. But who from this will show that he passed from **Judaism** to **Christianity**? Nor are those points concerning the testimonies sought from **Moses** and **Flavius Josephus** of greater weight, since in that place he cites **Moses** not in his own words, but in the words of **Numenius**: who was most well-versed in reading the Prophet, as many other passages of his testify, both in **Eusebius's** ***On the Preparation for the Gospel*** and **Theodoret** in the <term>θεραπ.</term> <note>The *Therapeutika*, or *Healing of Greek Maladies*, a work by Theodoret of Cyrus comparing Christian and Pagan philosophy.</note>, and especially that noble saying: *For what is Plato, but Moses speaking Attic Greek?* <note>original: "τὶ γάρ ἐστι Πλάτων, ἢ Μωσῆς ἀττικίζων;" This famous quote by the Neopythagorean Numenius suggests that Platonic philosophy was derived from Mosaic law.</note> And let us grant that **Moses** was cited primarily <note>original: "προηγουμένως" (proēgoumenōs), meaning "principally" or "directly."</note> by him: what could be achieved from that for proving this opinion? Since it is admitted that almost all the philosophers of that age read the sacred scriptures. For even **Longinus**, the teacher of **Porphyry**, in his book *On the Sublime* <note>original: "περὶ ὕψους" (*peri hypsous*)</note>, adduces a passage from that same beginning of *Genesis* with a most honorable mention of the **Prophet**. But we fight in vain with little arguments of this kind, since it is certain that **Porphyry** read not only **Moses**, but the whole of the sacred scripture, and indeed most diligently: for the purpose of showing the faith of the divine oracles to be false and uncertain: which **Eusebius** in his books *On the Preparation* does not...

He also explains that Porphyry's familiarity with Moses and the Bible came from scholarly study and a desire to critique Christianity, rather than Jewish origins.</summary>

<page-num>30</page-num>

testify in one place, as does **Theodoret** in the seventh discourse of his *Healing of Greek Affections* <note>original: *θεραπευτική* (therapeutikē). Theodoret was a 5th-century Christian bishop and theologian.</note>. Truly, those things which he brings forward concerning the **Jews** in his books *On Abstinence* clearly show that he himself had discovered nothing of their affairs through personal use or experience. Indeed, he relies so much on the testimony of others that anyone can see he is like a guest wandering in a foreign land. Even **Eusebius** <note>Eusebius of Caesarea, the "Father of Church History."</note> recognizes this, who, after citing passages from **Porphyry** in Book 9 of his *Preparation for the Gospel* <note>original: *προπαρασκευὴ* (proparaskeuē)</note>, adds: *“These testimonies regarding both the piety and philosophy of the Jewish men Porphyry appears to have offered from the reading of ancient records.”* <note>original: ταῦτα μὲν ὁ Πορφύριος ἐκ παλαιῶν, ὡς εἰκὸς, ἀναγνωσμάτων τῇ τῶν Ἰουδαίων ἀνδρῶν εὐσεβείᾳ τε ὁμοῦ καὶ φιλοσοφίᾳ ἐμαρτύρησεν.</note> Who would believe that **Eusebius** would have overlooked such a point in so convenient a place? 

But how I might apply a remedy to the words of **Saint Jerome**, I do not easily see. For my part, I do not think anything in the writings of the ancients should be changed rashly, if the sense can be extracted in any way. Therefore, turning my mind in various directions, this first occurred to me: among the places which fell by lot to the tribe of **Asher** in the division of the Holy Land (Joshua 19:25), there is listed **Beten**, whether it be a village or a town, which more recent describers of Palestine place between **Ptolemais** <note>The ancient city of Acre (Akko).</note> and **Tyre**. I suspected that perhaps this was believed by **Saint Jerome** to be the homeland of **Porphyry**, and that Porphyry had preferred to take his ancestral surname from the nearby, most famous city [Tyre] rather than from an ignoble place—just as we know the most famous authors **Virgil** and **Livy** did. 

However, I could think of no reasons by which to persuade myself of this. For **Eusebius**, in his book *On the Names of Places in Holy Scripture* <note>Commonly known as the *Onomasticon*.</note> (which we are soon to publish), writes thus: *“**Bathnai** (for so he calls this place, not Beten, as the Hebrew sources and the Vulgate edition do) belonging to the tribe of **Asher**. And it is now called the village of **Bebeten**, at the eighth milestone from **Ptolemais** toward the East.”* <note>original: Βαθναὶ... φυλῆς Ἀσήρ. καὶ νῦν καλεῖται κώμη Βεβετὲν, ἀπὸ ὀγδόου σημείου Πτολεμαΐδος ἐς ἀνατολὰς</note> And **Saint Jerome**, his translator, writes: *“Batnae: in the tribe of Asher. And it is now called the village of Bethbeten at the eighth milestone of Ptolemais toward the East.”* 

From this, we know by the authority of **Eusebius** and **Jerome** himself that the place had lost its original name at that time; thus **Porphyry** could not have been called a “Batanaeote” <note>A term Jerome used to label Porphyry, possibly suggesting he was from Bashan or a Jewish area.</note> from **Batnae**, or **Bebeten**, or **Bethbeten**. I pass over the fact that it was an obscure place, which the Holy Scriptures mention in only one single spot, and which was unknown to everyone else. And even if we grant it was the homeland of **Porphyry**: what disgrace, I ask...

<meta>catchword: disgrace</meta>

<summary>Holstenius argues that Porphyry was not Jewish, citing Porphyry's own lack of personal knowledge about Jewish customs and the geographical inaccuracies in Saint Jerome's claim that Porphyry was from the village of Beten.</summary>

<meta>Continuity: Lucas Holstenius continues his philological investigation into the insulting labels applied to Porphyry by Saint Jerome, specifically questioning the origin and meaning of the word "Batanaeota."</meta>

<page-num>31</page-num>
<header>The Philosopher Porphyry.</header>

...reproach from the mention of it? Or if **Jerome** objected to it as a matter of shame, why did he not attack with more words a matter known to few or none? Then another thought occurred to me, which the sense of the passage seems to suggest of its own accord: that "Batanaeote" was used as a term of abuse and reproach. Although I have observed no such thing until now, this name perhaps became shameful among the ancients due to some mark of infamy from his people, just as the names of the **Sodomites** and **Capernaites**, and others in the Holy Scriptures. Indeed, it is so certain that no one would doubt that if I called someone by those names, I would not be criticizing their homeland, but rather the crime of impiety or <term>unholiness<note>original: ἀνοσίας (anosias); refers to a violation of divine law or natural piety.</note></term>. 

Nor are examples lacking among secular authors, who called the **Abderites** stupid and blockheads, the **Bergaeans** fabulists and triflers, the **Melians** impious, the **Egyptians** imposters, and the **Cretans** liars. But since neither explanation satisfied me, I finally consulted the **Vatican** manuscripts, as if they were the leaves of the Sibyl <note>A reference to the Sibylline oracles, which were written on leaves and often became scattered and confusingly disordered.</note>; yet these left me even more uncertain than before. For some read the word in that passage as *Bethaniothes*, others as *Biothaniotes*; nor is there any greater variation than that some add the aspirated letter <note>The letter "h".</note> while others omit it. 

From this, fleeing toward conjectures, nothing seemed more suitable than that it should be read, with a slight change, as <term>one who dies a violent death<note>original: βιοθάνατος (biothanatos); often used in antiquity specifically to refer to those who committed suicide.</note></term> and "*that wicked Porphyry*." For it is well known that **Saint Jerome** did not shy away from the use of Greek words; and **Porphyry** himself testifies in his *Life of Plotinus* that he, driven by madness and black bile <note>original: atra bile; in the medical theory of the four humors, an excess of black bile was thought to cause "melancholy" or suicidal madness.</note>, wished to lead his soul out of his body by a violent death. 

For this is the greatest proof of impious recklessness and a clear sign of a deranged mind—an act which not only our holier religion, but even that very Philosophy which he professed, and the exercise of right reason, condemns and turns away from. But if these conjectures should please anyone less, I will add a third from the illustrious **Jacques Sirmond**, a Jesuit theologian and a man of the greatest learning and judgment, who very kindly explained his own opinion on this passage to me, showing, however, that he would not be unwilling to exchange it for a better one. He conjectures that perhaps **Jerome** wrote <term>a bather<note>original: Βαλανεώτης (balaneōtēs); literally a bath-attendant or one who frequents public baths.</note></term>; that is, as **Suidas** <note>A 10th-century Byzantine encyclopedia and dictionary.</note> explains, <term>a meddler and a busybody<note>original: περίεργος καὶ πολυπράγμων (periergos kai polypragmōn).</note></term>. But although it seems that this <term>epithet<note>original: ἐπώνυμον (epōnymon).</note></term> could not unreasonably be attributed to **Porphyry**, and fits not inaptly with the word "wicked" which follows, I nevertheless [say] that **Porphyry**, even though he is said to have driven a demon out of a bath, was called a "bather"...

...unless ancient books teach it, I shall not easily believe it. I have explained these things more diffusely regarding an obscure passage, not because I have striven to contradict a man of the highest erudition, or wish to detract anything from his greatest merits toward the Church of God—whose most pious memory <note>original: *pijssimos manes*; literally "pious shades," a classical way of referring to the deceased's spirit or legacy.</note> I venerate with my whole heart—but so that I might propose material for thought to sharper intellects. And indeed, he who brings forth any certain light in this obscure matter will have me bound to him by a great favor. But the love of examining individual points has led me further from my purpose. Now I shall follow the rest in order.

## CHAPTER V.

->*The mark of a true Philosopher. Porphyry properly*<-

Themistius, in that speech which he calls the "Tester" <note>original: βασανιστὴν (*basanistēn*); a term for one who tests the purity of metals, here applied to the character of a philosopher.</note> or the "Examiner," intending to describe a philosopher according to the mind of Plato, set down several marks, which he calls "types," "symbols," "tokens" <note>original: γνωρίσματα (*gnōrismata*).</note>, and "tests" <note>original: βασανιστήρια (*basanishteria*).</note>; for by the benefit of these, one may test a true and genuine philosopher and distinguish him from the false and adulterated. Alcinous <note>A Middle Platonist philosopher whose *Handbook of Platonism* was a standard introductory text in the Renaissance.</note> also prefixed these to his Platonic introduction, drawing from the sixth book of the *Republic*. Applying this Platonic examination, we too shall discern what is to be thought regarding Porphyry’s genius and his method of philosophizing, and whether antiquity rightly and deservedly placed him in the number of the leading philosophers.

And first indeed, Plato wished philosophers to be "divine-born" and "divine-nurtured" <note>original: διογενεῖς καὶ διοτρεφεῖς (*diogeneis kai diotrepheis*); terms Homer used to describe kings, which Plato here applies to the nobility of the philosophical mind.</note> no less than Homer wished for kings; nor did he think any should be admitted to the secret mysteries of wisdom unless they were born from a "sacred marriage"—that is, from the best parents and a legitimate lineage—as...
