<header>Porphyry the Philosopher</header>

so that their reverence and majesty might remain certain for them: lest, being made public to the profane crowd, they should become cheapened, and, being entrusted to base minds as if to impure vessels, they should be defiled. We have claimed this mark of an upright and legitimate lineage for Porphyry, as far as was permitted, in the previous discourse—although little would be lost of the praise of his genius and wisdom even if those external things <note>Referring to noble birth or social status.</note> were lacking, since Pythagoras, Socrates, and even Plato himself admitted slaves and foreigners <note>original: *barbaros*; in this context, it refers to non-Greeks of outstanding talent.</note> of outstanding talent to the inner shrines of philosophy.

But sharpness of wit, a teachable cleverness, a memory tenacious of what is deposited within it, and the other gifts of the mind—which the Platonists encompass under the name of "natural talent" <note>original: εὐφυΐας (*euphyias*)</note> or "cultivated harmony" <note>original: εὐμουσίας (*eumousias*)</note>—are so necessary that one would aspire to the noble praise of wisdom and affect the ambitious name of "Philosopher" in vain if deprived of these aids. Without them, the mind can by no means be raised to the investigation of heavenly things, nor to the contemplation of that supreme and most blessed Being <note>A reference to "The One" or the Neoplatonic concept of the Divine.</note> which, separated from the erratic flux of matter and withdrawn from the senses, is known by the intellect alone. And in this excellence of genius, I know not whether any of the ancients (I except only those first princes of the philosophers) can be compared with Porphyry. His writings, of which only a few survive out of many, testify to the sharpness of his sublime genius and his exceptional method of philosophizing. The consensus of antiquity confirms this; for among so great a crowd of those practicing philosophy, it bestowed the title of "the Philosopher" as proper and peculiar to Porphyry alone.

For the curious ambition of men in devising titles for the learned did not begin to run riot only in our age or that of our fathers; rather, better antiquity distinguished each of the philosophers with some illustrious surname. To say nothing of Pythagoras the Philosopher, Socrates the Wise, the divine Plato, Aristotle the inspired <note>original: *dæmonium*; in this context, it implies a superhuman or spirit-like intelligence rather than a demonic entity.</note>, the great Hippocrates, and other sages of the major nations; that "golden chain" of the more recent Platonists, as Damascius calls it, is adorned by Proclus and other later writers with a similar variety of names. They call Ammonius "taught by God" <note>original: θεοδίδακτον (*theodidakton*)</note>, Plotinus "the Great" <note>original: μέγαν (*megan*)</note>, Longinus "the most critical" <note>original: κριτικώτατον (*kritikotaton*)</note>, Amelius "the noble" <note>original: γενναῖον (*gennaion*)</note>, Theodorus "the wonderful" <note>original: θαυμαστὸν (*thaumaston*)</note>, Iamblichus "the divine" <note>original: θεῖον (*theion*)</note>, but with a certain religious observation they everywhere call Porphyry alone "the Philosopher" <note>original: φιλόσοφον (*philosophon*)</note>, which indeed...

<meta>Continuity: Building on the previous page's discussion of Porphyry’s natural genius, Holstenius now compares Porphyry’s status among pagan philosophers to that of St. Gregory of Nazianzus among Christian theologians, emphasizing Porphyry's role as a vital interpreter of Plato and Aristotle.</meta>

<page-num>34</page-num>
<header>On the Life and Writings</header>

It is no small praise for Porphyry among **those on the outside** <note>original: τοὺς ἔξω (*tous exo*); a term used by early Christian writers to refer to non-Christian or pagan philosophers.</note> that he earned that surname <note>The title "The Philosopher."</note> after Pythagoras; just as among our own, it was for Gregory of Nazianzus <note>A 4th-century Archbishop of Constantinople and one of the most significant figures in early Christian theology.</note>, who among such a great number of theologians alone obtained the proper name of "Theologian" after Saint John. Even Blessed Augustine, the prince of Latin theologians, called Porphyry the *most learned of philosophers, and a man of no ordinary talent*: and Proclus, the most subtle of the Platonists, admired the **venerable conceptions** <note>original: τὰ ἱεροπρεπῆ νοήματα (*ta hieroprepi noemata*); thoughts worthy of sacred status.</note> of Porphyry in more than one place. Isidore the Philosopher also affirmed that he had collected a great treasure of more divine science. And indeed, to one considering the matter more closely, it will easily appear that the judgment of the ancients concerning Porphyry in no way wandered from the truth. For if we define wisdom according to their own mind as **the science and comprehension of Being, and the only knowledge of the truth within it**, <note>original: τὴν τοῦ ὄντος ἐπιστήμην καὶ κατάληψιν, καὶ μόνην τὴν ἐν τούτῳ γνῶσιν τῆς ἀληθείας</note>—*this is, the science of true Being and the unique knowledge of the truth which lies hidden in this Being*—it certainly cannot be denied that Porphyry is rightly called a philosopher, that is, a seeker of that wisdom. All of his writings are occupied with this: that they might prepare the soul, which is clouded in darkness, for that more divine science through the knowledge of more human letters and arts, as if by a certain **preparatory training** <note>original: προπαιδείᾳ (*propaideia*); the foundational education required before higher philosophical study.</note>; or that they might purge it from the contagion of the body and mortality by the precepts of moral philosophy; or, once purged and released, they might elevate it by a certain more sublime doctrine to the investigation and union with true Being. This will be easily perceived from the arguments of his books, which I shall recount in order. Added to this is another reason: that he illustrated almost all the works of Plato and Aristotle with brilliant commentaries, and for a more certain understanding of them, he laid down a plain and easy path for others with certain introductions, which they call **Introductions and Elements** <note>original: εἰσαγωγὰς (*eisagogas*) & στοιχειώσεις (*stoicheioseis*)</note>. For in a true interpreter, there is required not only the most exact knowledge of those things which he undertakes to explain, but also a certain faculty for illustrating obscurely stated things with clear and transparent speech, establishing doubts, correcting falsehoods, and either reconciling or refuting disagreeing opinions. With what zeal and with how much success this method of illustrating philosophy was handled by Porphyry will be known even more clearly from what follows. Then, Plato established another mark: that a philosopher should be temperate by nature, and in that part of the soul which

<meta>Continuity: This page continues Holstenius's analysis of Porphyry’s character, specifically how his disciplined lifestyle and his psychological temperament (influenced by the "humors") shaped his philosophical focus and his eventual opposition to Christianity.</meta>

<page-num>35</page-num>
<header>*Porphyry the Philosopher.*</header>
<meta>catchword: Porphyry</meta>

is moved by the disturbances of the passions, being instead relaxed and well-composed; this ensures that the lower faculties do not hinder the intellect in its supreme investigation of the truth. For the intellect, by its very nature, grows dim before 그 divine and blessed light while it is immersed in this earthly and bodily mass. 

And since the brute or irrational part of the soul consists of two faculties—one of which seethes with anger, while the other is distended by the gluttony and lust of the belly and its adjacent parts—Porphyry suppressed the latter through a dry and sober way of life, established according to the precepts of the **Pythagoreans**. <note>The followers of Pythagoras were famous for their strict dietary restrictions and self-discipline, intended to purify the soul.</note> This was done so that these urges might not call the mind away from serious thoughts or shake off the reins of reason. 

Yet he did not so easily restrain his anger and the fury of a bilious mind. This may have been because he applied less care to that part of himself, or rather because *passion conquered the health of his mind* <note>original: *ira mentis sanitatem vicerit*</note>—which is what **Medea** affirms happened to her in the Tragedy <note>A reference to Euripides' play *Medea*, where the protagonist famously laments that her passion (*thymos*) is stronger than her reason.</note>, and what often happens in those with a bilious temperament of the body. 

For it appears that Porphyry possessed not only a sublime and powerful genius, but one that was also vehement and mixed with a certain frenzy. This is clear from what he revealed about himself in the *Life of Plotinus* regarding the violent extraction of the soul from the body. <note>Porphyry describes a period of such deep depression and ascetic intensity that he contemplated suicide, until his teacher Plotinus intervened.</note> We see this trait in almost all those into whose hearts **Saturn** has poured much **black bile**. <note>original: *atra bilis*; in the medical theory of the time, "black bile" was the humor associated with melancholy, which was thought to produce either madness or extraordinary genius under the influence of the planet Saturn.</note> This bile boils over even more sharply if, in addition to a dry and meager way of life, nightly studies and long vigils are added along with continuous contemplation. 

Nor would I say there was any other cause for that rabid hatred with which he raged against the **Christian religion** more than others, than that his mind—swelling with the ferment of black bile and inflated by an excessive abundance of learning—could not contain itself. Thus, by his example, it became clear that the "wisdom of the world is foolishness before God"; and that the humility of incarnate wisdom displeased the pride of the Greeks, who considered it folly. 

Therefore, it is less surprising if *Porphyry*, despite such a great love for wisdom—which *Plato* established as a primary mark of a *Philosopher*—and in such an anxious investigation of things, nevertheless vanished in his own thoughts. He could not reach the goal, having strayed from the very doorway. Nor can I help but pity the fate of those most excellent geniuses who lived in those times when the Christian religion raised up a new and unaccustomed light for the minds of mortals: I mean **Numenius**, **Plotinus**, and the rest...

<page-num>36</page-num>
## On the Life and Writings

Of Porphyry, Amelius, Hierocles, and their like. These men, having tasted divine truth from the Sacred Scriptures and from the teachers of the burgeoning Church—or having heard it from the mouths of Christian masters—then diverted the purest streams of truth into the stagnant pools of Pythagoras and Plato; thus, they obscured the sparks of divine light with the smoke and darkness of falsehoods. For what could they not have achieved in the solid defense of truth, when they refined even a few thin rays of it with such admirable genius and such abundant elegance of speech that they drew even the most learned bishops <term>antistites: literally "overseers" or bishops of the Church</term> of the Christian religion into admiration for them? 

But this also shows that Porphyry’s zeal for truth was no light matter. When he turned his mind to philosophy, he did not—as often happens—rashly cling to any single sect as if to a jagged rock, nor did he swear an oath to the words of any one master. For such a <note>The original manuscript contains a correction here where the word "mind" was crossed out and replaced</note> mind and spirit is greatly hindered in the investigation of truth, as if by a cataract over the eye, once it has bound itself by contract or slave-bond to the defense of others’ opinions. 

Nevertheless, he chose two guides above all others whose footsteps he would follow: **Plato** and **Aristotle**. He even undertook a voluminous work to prove that they agreed with one another on the primary principles of doctrine; more will be said about this in the appropriate place. Yet he followed them with a cautious step, so that he might freely depart from their opinions when he perceived better ones. For this reason, **Saint Augustine** praises him in his tenth book of *The City of God* (Chapters 30 and 31), noting that by rejecting Plato’s opinion regarding the soul’s cycle through the "circle of necessity" <note>A Platonic concept suggesting the soul must endlessly reincarnate through fixed cycles.</note>, Porphyry embraced a sounder view. 

> ->*Behold,* he says, *a Platonist disagrees for the better with Plato. Behold, this man saw what the other did not see; nor, after such a great master, did he shy away from correction. Instead, he preferred truth to the man.*<-

Nor did he bind himself to Aristotle with any greater religious devotion; we shall later show that Porphyry refuted Aristotle's opinion on the nature of the soul in an entire book. He most approved of this very free manner of philosophizing, which had been introduced shortly before by **Ammonius** <note>Ammonius Saccas (c. 175–242 AD), the teacher of Plotinus, credited with reconciling the philosophies of Plato and Aristotle in Alexandria.</note> into the Alexandrian school. For the later **Academics** <note>The "New Academy" or later followers of Plato who often veered into skepticism or overly technical interpretations.</note>, divided by the various disagreements of sects and opinions while each strove to pull Plato’s words to their own side, had reduced philosophy to subtle and difficult trifles through a great diversity of interpretations.

<summary>This page discusses Porphyry’s intellectual independence, noting how he synthesized Platonic and Aristotelian thought while remaining free to correct them, earning him the respect of even Christian critics like St. Augustine.</summary>

### 37 Of Porphyry the Philosopher

...and so that each might color their own opinions—or rather their delusions—they devised various <note>original Greek: καλλωπίσματα καὶ σοφίσματα</note> adornments and sophistries, as Eusebius testifies in the third book of *Preparation for the Gospel*, <note>original Greek: μηδ' ὄναρ τοῖς παλαιοῖς εἰς ἐνθύμησιν ἐλθόντα</note> which never even entered the minds of the ancients in a dream. *Pigments and sophistries which never, even in a dream, came to the mind of the ancients.* Added to this were controversies against Aristotle, undertaken as if <term>pro aris & focis: a Latin idiom meaning "for altars and hearths," or fighting for one's very existence and home</term> for life and limb; these were argued with great mental strain on both sides, but with little care for the truth. From this arose a type of philosophizing tangled and confused by perpetual allegories and monstrous chimeras of words and thoughts.

This continued until finally the wisdom of Ammonius of Alexandria <note>Ammonius Saccas (c. 175–242 AD), the teacher of Plotinus, credited with reconciling the philosophies of Plato and Aristotle.</note> shone upon the world. He purified the opinions of the ancient philosophers and cut away the trifles that had grown up on both sides <note>original: "lolij instar," referring to tares or weeds growing among wheat</note> like weeds; he restored the pure and natural appearance of philosophy and showed (as Hierocles testifies) that those two princes of philosophers <note>Plato and Aristotle</note> did not disagree in the slightest <note>original Greek: ἐν τοῖς ἐπικαίροις τε καὶ ἀναγκαιοτάτοις τῶν δογμάτων</note> regarding the most critical and necessary of dogmas.

Herennius, Origen, and Plotinus drank this purified philosophy from the mouth of Ammonius; later Porphyry, Iamblichus, Hierocles, and the other philosophers <note>original Greek: τῆς ἱερᾶς γενεᾶς</note> of the sacred lineage received it. And when Plutarch of Athens brought this same method of philosophizing, learned from Iamblichus, back to his homeland, a continuous succession of men from Asia—Syrianus, Proclus, Marinus, Isidorus, and Damascius—subsequently benefited the restored Academy of Plato there. These men, not content with the cleverness of their exuberant genius, mixed a great mishmash of ancient opinions back into their own commentaries. While they studied to over-adorn Platonic Theology with trappings—driven by a rivalry with the Christianity that was then spreading far and wide—they once again buried the truth (which had been brought back to light shortly before by the work of Ammonius) <note>original Greek: πολυλογίᾳ καὶ ἀλλοτρίων δοξασμάτων ἱστορίᾳ</note> with excessive talk and a history of foreign opinions.

I have recounted these things to show from which sources most of Porphyry's dogmas flowed: especially those which he wrote so excellently regarding the flight from the body and senses, the care of the soul, and the cultivation of the mind and its elevation toward God; and also to show what value they should be held in. For waters are usually purer the closer they are to their origin. From this, if I am not mistaken, it can be seen how it happened that in the writings of the later Platonists, especially Plotinus,

<meta>Continuity: This page transitions from discussing the "over-adornment" of philosophy by later Neoplatonists to the specific historical claim that these philosophers—specifically Plotinus and his circle—secretly borrowed their most profound "mystical" ideas from the Christian teachings of their master, Ammonius Saccas. It contrasts the accounts of Porphyry with those of the Church historian Eusebius.</meta>

<header>Of the Life and Writings</header>

In the works of Iamblichus and Proclus, so many things are found scattered here and there that seem to be drawn not from the common and ordinary dogmas of our religion, but from the innermost and most hidden shrines of **mystical theology**. These ideas are not merely sketched out briefly or in passing, but are expressed so clearly and with such a powerful style of speaking that they have provided no small amount of material for doubt to great minds. They have even driven some into the error of asserting—in a reversal of the true order—that the divine writings of the oldest Church authors were actually borrowed from the Platonists I mentioned, like <note>The phrase "spoils of the Egyptians" refers to a common early Christian metaphor based on Exodus: just as the Israelites took Egyptian gold to build the Tabernacle, Christians were seen as "plundering" pagan philosophy to serve the truth of the Gospel.</note> **spoils of the Egyptians** converted to pious uses. By this very argument, they even attempted to tear down the faith and authority of those most excellent works. 

However, it is certain that Ammonius <note>Ammonius Saccas, the influential Alexandrian teacher who died c. 242 AD.</note> communicated the secrets of our religion to his disciples under a religious vow of silence. Porphyry himself testifies that Herennius, Origen, and Plotinus bound themselves to one another by an oath not to reveal these teachings. When Herennius was the first to break that bond, neither Origen nor Plotinus stood by their promises; instead, through both writings and the living voice, they brought into the public sphere those things they had received from **Ammonius, the Christian philosopher**. 

Indeed, I believe there was no other reason they celebrated Ammonius with the title <term>theodidaktos</term> <note>original Greek: θεοδίδακτον, meaning "taught by God"</note>—as Hierocles testifies in the works of Photius—than because he had handed down to his disciples the secrets of a more divine science, which until that time had been unusual and unheard of in the schools of the philosophers. 

This captured the mind of Plotinus at his very first hearing. Having left the schools of other philosophers in a state of sadness, upon hearing Ammonius, he immediately said to his friends: <term>hunc ipsum quærebam</term> <note>original Greek: τοῦτον ἐζήτουν, "this is the very one I was looking for."</note>. 

Nevertheless, Porphyry, driven by a certain blind and headlong hatred of the Christian religion, claimed with obvious slander that Ammonius turned from a Christian into a Gentile <note>In this context, "Gentile" refers to a practitioner of traditional Greco-Roman paganism.</note>. This claim is abundantly refuted by Eusebius, who lived closest to those times, in the *Ecclesiastical History*, Book VI, Chapter 19. In this matter, I find Porphyry lacks that mark of a "good philosopher" which requires students of wisdom to be fair and truthful, and not at all prone to slander. Nor was he any more fair-minded toward his teacher Origen, whom he no less shamelessly slandered by claiming he had crossed over from Hellenism to the sacred rites of the Christians; whose zeal for interpreting the sacred scriptures...

<summary>This page argues that the "divine" insights of Neoplatonists like Plotinus were actually secret Christian teachings learned from Ammonius Saccas. It also accuses Porphyry of slandering Ammonius and Origen to distance them from their Christian roots.</summary>

<keywords>Ammonius Saccas, Plotinus, Porphyry, Origen, Neoplatonism, Christian theology, secrecy, religious influence, Eusebius, slandering of teachers</keywords>
